# PREFACE.

A 2
<meta>catchword: but,</meta>

<image-desc>A large square ornamental woodcut initial 'C' featuring a central multi-petaled flower, likely a rose, surrounded by intricate scrolling foliage and floral motifs set against a dark, textured background within a decorative border.</image-desc>Since our age has turned its diligence and industry toward illuminating Church History <note>original: *Historiam Ecclesiasticam*</note>, then especially that part of it which sets forth the origin, growth, fate, destruction, and likewise the doctrines and evil arts of the Heretics <note>original: *Haereticorum*</note>, has seemed worthy to be called under consideration and examination. Because of this, some indeed have consulted all the records of Church antiquity and, with careful effort and taking the timeline into account, have pursued the narration of all heresies; others, however, have explained one or two selected from the whole body with a more accurate commentary. And the plan of those former authors was so free from all blame—provided they did not indulge too much in emotions, or prejudiced opinions, or freer conjectures (from which some have occasionally failed to guard themselves diligently enough)—that they instead understood, having been received with well-deserved praise, that their industry was kindled and more and more stirred up to press on with the undertaking. 

For there were very few—and those prematurely fearful—who judged that the mind should be called away from such a plan and work, claiming that the memory of both the history and the doctrines pertaining to heretics ought rather to seem abolished and extinguished by every effort, following the example of the ancient Church, which with deliberate mind determined that the writings and remains of men turned away from the truth should be removed from our midst by flames and fire, and withdrawn from the hands of readers. Very few, I say, were those who felt this way, and they found even fewer supporters for their opinion—and not without reason. 

For, to say nothing of the fact that in such great light of saving doctrine there is hardly any fear that the settled opinions of the Heretics should prove themselves to anyone—opinions which are for the most part no more turned away from the prescription of Scripture than from the judgment of a sound mind, if they are reviewed candidly and without sought-after excuses—certainly it is of great interest for managing the cause of truth more rightly to have a clear view of the beliefs <note>original: *placita*</note> of these men who once disturbed the Church. This is done both so that it may be understood what front-rank leaders <term>Front-rank leaders: translated from the Latin *antesignanos*, referring to the elite soldiers who protected the standards of a Roman legion; here it means the original founders of a school of thought.</term> those men had who today are calling back things long since outdated, as well as...

<summary>In this preface, the author argues that studying the history and doctrines of heretics is vital for the Church, rejecting the idea that such records should be destroyed or ignored. He suggests that understanding ancient errors is necessary to identify and refute modern revivals of those same "outdated" ideas.</summary>
<keywords>Church History, Heresy, Scholarly Diligence, Book Burning, Ancient Church, Theology, Polemics, Error and Truth</keywords>

# PREFACE.

->A 2<-

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial C featuring a central rose-like floral motif surrounded by foliage.</image-desc>

**Since** our age has turned its diligence and industry toward illustrating **Church History** <note>original: "Historiam Ecclesiasticam"</note>, that specific part of it which explains the origin, growth, fate, and destruction of **Heretics** <note>original: "Haeretici"; referring here to those whose teachings were officially condemned by the early church</note>, as well as their doctrines and "evil arts" <note>original: "malas artes"; often referring to deceptive methods or sophistry used in arguments</note>, has seemed especially worthy of being brought under consideration and examination. 

As a result, some scholars have indeed consulted all the records of Church antiquity and, with careful effort and a regard for chronology, have pursued a narrative of all heresies. Others, however, have selected one or two from the whole body to explain with more detailed commentary. Indeed, the plan of those authors has been so free from any blame—provided they did not indulge too much in emotions, prejudiced opinions, or overly free conjectures (against which some have occasionally failed to guard themselves carefully)—that they have instead received well-deserved praise and found their industry for pressing on with the project kindled and more and more excited. 

For there have been very few, and those prematurely fearful, who thought that the mind should be called away from such a plan and work. These few argued that the memory of both the history and doctrines pertaining to heretics should rather be abolished and completely extinguished, following the example of the ancient Church, which deliberately decided that the writings and remains of men turned away from the truth should be removed by flames and fire and withdrawn from the hands of readers. 

Very few, I say, were those who felt this way, and they found even fewer supporters of their opinion—and not without reason. For to say nothing of the fact that, in such great light of saving doctrine, there is little to fear that the fixed opinions of Heretics should prove themselves to anyone (being for the most part as much opposed to the prescription of Scripture as they are to the judgment of a sound mind, if they are reviewed honestly and without seeking excuses), it is certainly of great interest for better managing the cause of truth to have a clear understanding of the decrees <note>original: "placita"; formal opinions or doctrines</note> of these men who once disturbed the Church. This is done both so that it may be understood what "standard-bearers" <note>original: "antesignanos"; the primary leaders or originators of a movement</note> those people followed who are today reviving things long ago outdated, as well as...

<summary>In this preface, the author defends the study of heretical history and doctrines against those who believe such records should be destroyed, arguing that understanding past errors is essential for defending the truth today.</summary>
<keywords>Church History, Heresy, historiography, censorship, ancient Church, doctrinal truth, standard-bearers</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's justification for writing a history of the Bogomil heresy. It begins mid-sentence, following the catchword "vero" from the previous page.</meta>

...indeed, so that it may become clear what the Bishops <note>original: "Antistites"; leaders or high-ranking officials of the early church</note> of the ancient Church thought of them. This was the reason why I—moved and led partly by these arguments and partly by examples—took it upon myself to explain the heresy of the **BOGOMILS** <note>The Bogomils were a medieval religious sect originating in the Balkans, known for their dualistic beliefs (the struggle between a good God and an evil creator).</note> more diligently from suitable records. I was well aware that into this heresy, as if into a certain "Camarina" <note>original: "camarinam quandam"; an ancient Sicilian marsh that was notoriously foul-smelling. The author uses it as a metaphor for a collection of "filth" or corrupt doctrines.</note>, all the dregs and filth of the most impure doctrines had flowed. These doctrines—introduced by the Manichaeans (both old and new), the Paulicians, and the Massalians <note>Various early Christian sects labeled as heretical for their dualistic or mystical views.</note>—were judged by the ancients as needing to be completely removed from the Church. Nor are there people lacking today who have undertaken to polish these same opinions and paint them in various colors.

My resolve was confirmed, and a spur was added to my progress <note>original: "currenti calcar addidit"; literally 'added a spur to one already running,' meaning he was further motivated.</note>, when I saw that the history of this heresy had not yet received the light it required, and that almost all historians of heresy <note>original: "Haeresiologos"</note> had either neglected it entirely or touched upon it only with their fingertips <note>original: "summis digitis"; a Latin idiom meaning to treat a subject superficially or briefly.</note>. Certainly, among all the authors who exist on this subject, no one was occupied more laboriously (though even then not fully) in reviewing it than the late **Caspar Sagittarius**, who, in his *Introduction*, brought forward those things from the Byzantine writers that related to this subject. While he was occupied in explaining this, he himself confesses that he was helped not a little by a unique dissertation written about the Bogomils by **Samuel Andreae**, a former Doctor of the Reformed Church, which was praised by **Hermann Witsius** on page 99 of his *Exercises on the Lord's Prayer* <note>original: "Exercitat. in Orat. Dominic."</note>.

For my part, although I searched diligently, it was not granted to me to see that specific work. However, this loss was made up for by another dissertation on the same subject by **Mr. Antonius Winckler**, a most learned man and a very dear friend of mine. This work, consisting of four sheets, was written years ago under the auspices of the highly celebrated **FABRICIUS** <note>Johann Albert Fabricius (1668–1736), a famous German classical scholar and bibliographer.</note> and defended in the Gymnasium of Hamburg with him presiding. 

But my greatest resource was found in the fact that I possessed a Greek transcript of that part of **Euthymius Zigabenus’s** *Dogmatic Panoply* <note>original: "Panoplia Dogmatica"; a 12th-century theological work defending Orthodox doctrines against various heresies.</note> which follows the history of the Bogomils; scholars know that this work has only been available in Latin until now. Access to that manuscript in the **Bodleian Library** at Oxford was granted to me through the kindness of the distinguished **JOHN HUDSON**, a man who is as learned as he is devoted to the advancement of letters with no less labor than happy success...

<summary>The author explains his decision to write a history of the Bogomils, describing the sect as a collection of various ancient "impure" doctrines and critiquing previous scholars for their superficial treatment of the subject. He cites his primary sources, including a rare Greek manuscript by Euthymius Zigabenus obtained from the Bodleian Library.</summary>

<keywords>Bogomils, Manichaeans, Paulicians, Massalians, Caspar Sagittarius, Samuel Andreae, Antonius Winckler, Johann Albert Fabricius, Euthymius Zigabenus, Bodleian Library, John Hudson, church history, heresy</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's description of his sources and methodology. The previous page ended with a discussion on how other historians had only superficially touched upon the Bogomil heresy.</meta>

...permitted, and who should never be mentioned by me without an honorable preface <note>original: "sine praefatione honoris non unquam commemorandi"; a formal Latin expression of deep respect for a colleague or mentor</note>. This Codex of Euthymius’s *Panoplia* <note>Euthymius Zigabenus was a 12th-century monk; his *Panoplia Dogmatica* (Dogmatic Panoply) was a massive defense of Orthodox Christianity against various heresies.</note> is owed to the care of **JOHN FELL**, formerly Bishop of Oxford, who achieved a distinguished celebrity of name among our citizens no less than among foreigners through his many merits in literary matters. He had seen to it that this work be transcribed from a manuscript of an older character at his own expense, with the intention that the entire work might one day see the light of day in print. But since either an untimely death or the difficulties of the times had interrupted this plan, it happened that that transcript <note>original: "ἀπόγραφον" (apographon); a handwritten copy of a manuscript</note> passed into the public library along with other similar monuments—among which are numbered the books of Damascius *On Principles* <note>original: "περὶ ἀρχῶν" (Peri Archon); Damascius was the last head of the Neoplatonist Academy in Athens</note>—and thus the opportunity was given to me to transcribe from it both this part, which I now present, and other matters which I shall explain elsewhere.

I have used this fragment of the work in such a way that, because I saw that Euthymius had handed down the history of Bogomil doctrines without order or connection, I have referred some things, drawn here and there from the Narrative itself, to history; others to Theological Topics <note>original: "Locos Theologicos"; a method of organizing theology by categorized "commonplaces" or themes</note>; and others to Customs and rites. I have omitted nothing at all that is read in Euthymius’s own treatment. However, so that the entire narrative might be restored afterward to the same order that Euthymius followed, I have always carefully cited those Sections which the Latin translation contains.

I have intentionally not added a Latin translation [to the Greek text], but have only presented a summary and compendium of what is to be said. I did this partly because a translation is available both separately and in the *Libraries of the Fathers* <note>original: "Bibliothecis Patrum"; large printed collections of works by early Christian writers</note>, and partly because I did not want this commentary to wander beyond its established limits. Sometimes, however, where the Latin translator seemed to have departed from the Greek context, or failed to grasp its meaning, or followed an incorrect reading, I decided that the Reader should be warned; I did not neglect to do this even when something seemed wanting in both the [existing] version and the transcript.

Equipped, therefore, with these aids, I have handled the matter by dividing it into three parts: in the first, I present those things which look toward history—that is, the origin, growth, and likewise the fate and destruction of this heresy; in the second, I encompass the doctrines; and in the third, finally, I review their customs and way of life. To these—

<sig>A 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: enim (for)</meta>

<summary>The author explains how he acquired a transcript of Euthymius Zigabenus’s work from the collection of the late Bishop John Fell and describes his editorial method of reorganizing the material into historical, doctrinal, and cultural categories.</summary>
<keywords>John Fell, Euthymius Zigabenus, Panoplia Dogmatica, Bogomils, manuscript transcription, methodology, Damascius, heresy history</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s explanation of his methodology in organizing the fragment of Euthymius Zigabenus’s work. He previously mentioned arranging the chaotic material into historical and theological categories; here, he justifies his defense of Euthymius against critics like Gottfried Arnold and introduces his collaborators.</meta>

For I believe that all things required for an inner knowledge of this subject can be conveniently referred to three chapters, with the agreement of those who have ever been occupied in considering either this or other heresies. Since, however, I was aware that certain writers on the history of heresy—specifically Gottfried Arnold <note>Gottfried Arnold (1666–1714) was a radical Pietist historian famous for his "Impartial History of the Church and Heretics," in which he often argued that the so-called heretics were the true Christians and the established Church was the oppressor.</note>—had held the reliability of Euthymius in suspicion, and had pronounced that most things were attributed to the Bogomils not so much out of the truth of the matter as from a lack of self-control, or rather malice, and a zeal for <term>fabricating heresy</term> <note>original: αἱρετικοποιΐας (haeretikopoiïas); the act of inventing or labeling heresies where they may not exist</note> driven by their own emotions, I have set out to assert and defend Euthymius's reliability. I have done this by periodically bringing forward the testimonies of other authors—who are, however, very few—who <term>concur in opinion</term> <note>original: ὁμοψήφων (homopsēphōn); literally "voting with the same pebble," meaning unanimous or in agreement</note>, thereby securing either clarity or some defense for his assertions. Among other things, I saw this objection raised in particular: that these assertions were attributed to the Bogomils whose absurdity is so great that it exceeds all belief that they could ever have been approved. I thought this argument could be conveniently countered if it were shown that men of this kind were not lacking in the Church of old, nor are they missing even now, who on one hand preceded the Bogomils by their example, and on the other hand follow in their footsteps through imitation.

These things suffice for understanding the rationale of this undertaking. Now mention must be made of those who showed themselves to be my strenuous fellow-soldiers in this commentary. They are **JOHN CHRISTOPHER COLER**, of Altenburg, **HERMANN HEEREN**, of Bremen, and **JOHN HENRY WARDENBURG**, of Oldenburg, most distinguished and learned **MEN**, born for the hope of their fatherland, for whom my expectation is so great that it could not be greater. For when they had collected for themselves no common stores of learning in this Academy and had decided to publish some public specimen of their work, they shared their plans with me and encouraged me to undertake this treatment, comprised of three dissertations, to be examined in the manner of a formal debate. Therefore, I believe the Kind Reader will look upon it kindly and will extensively favor our endeavors.

Given at Wittenberg, September 28, in the year 1711. <note>original: "IV. Kal. Octobreis An. clɔ Iɔ CCXI"; the fourth day before the Kalends of October in Roman dating.</note>

# OVERVIEW OF THE ENTIRE
## TREATISE.

Dissertation I works to uncover the *Name, Origin, and History of the Bogomils*. First, it traces the origin of the *Name* to the *Bulgarian* language and examines various spellings of the name in Section I. It demonstrates that the group owes this name not so much to themselves as to other *Authors* in Section II. Other names for these same people are explained in Section III. Their origin is established as being twofold: partly more distant, traced back to the *Manichaeans and Massalians* <note>The Manichaeans and Massalians were earlier dualist groups—those who believed in two opposing powers of good and evil—whom the Church considered ancestors of later heresies.</note>, and partly more immediate, traced back to *Basil* in Section IV. Their consistency with the *Massalians* is discussed more fully in Section V; in passing, the list of <term>chapters</term> <note>original: κεφαλαίων (kephalaion); the main points or headings of a doctrine</note> or dogmas of the *Massalians*, presented by *Cotelier* in Volume I of the *Grecian Monuments*, is illustrated by variant readings excerpted from the *Bodleian Codex* and restored to its original form. It is shown that their more immediate origin falls in the *12th Century*, when the movement began to raise its head, although its slighter beginnings are to be sought in the *11th Century* in Section VI. An account of *Basil*, the leader <note>original: antesignano; literally a "standard-bearer" or someone who stands before the standards of an army</note> of the *Bogomils*, is given in Section VII, who worked hardest at winning disciples for himself in Section VIII; in this section, a certain passage from *Anna* <note>Anna Comnena (1083–1153), a Byzantine princess and historian who wrote the "Alexiad," which chronicles her father’s reign and the trial of the Bogomil leader Basil.</note> is restored to its original form. Here at last the wisdom of *Emperor Alexios* is included in Section IX. Basil eloquently explains his doctrines before the *Emperor* in Section X, but after being caught in his errors by him, he is delivered to prison in Section XI. His remaining disciples are sought out, and then his companions in prison in Section XII. A unique method for distinguishing the guilty from the innocent is employed in Section XIII. *Basil*, found guilty of obstinacy, is condemned to the pyre and burned, a type of punishment the others barely escaped in Section XV. Shortly after, under *John Komnenos*, the same evil flared up again with the help of *Constantine Chrysomallos* in Section XVI. His writings are examined and destroyed by fire in Section XVII; however, those who had read the books professed that their minds were free from his opinions in Section XVIII. After him, *Niphon*, suspected of *Bogomil* doctrine, is likewise condemned in Section XIX, which also happened to *Michael of Thessalonica*. Finally, in the *13th Century*, during the time of *Germanus, Patriarch of Constantinople*, certain remnants of the *Bogomils* survived.

*Dissertation II* investigates the *Doctrines of the Bogomils*. The writers who explained these doctrines are likewise commemorated and defended against the doubt of *Gottfried Arnold* regarding their reliability in Section I. Their errors concerning **HOLY SCRIPTURE** are discussed in Section II. Why did they reject the books of *Moses*? In Section III, they [rejected] certain...

...which books of the Holy Scripture they rejected, and which they occasionally used against others; and how they especially favored allegories in their interpretation. IV. Certain examples of this are presented. V. Errors regarding the nature of GOD, whom they established as having a human form <note>original: ἀνθρωποειδῆ (anthrōpoeidē)</note>, though being without a physical body <note>original: ἀσώματον (asōmaton)</note>. VI. It is shown how this was possible for them. VII. and VIII. Errors concerning the HOLY TRINITY. IX. On the INCARNATION of CHRIST, whose true flesh they denied. X. Which error Euthymius refutes at length. XI. Errors in the doctrine of the HOLY SPIRIT. XII. On CREATION. XIII. Which Euthymius refutes. XIV. Euthymius’s <term>judgment</term> <note>original: epicrisis; a critical evaluation or summary judgment</note>. XVI. Absurdities regarding the FALL of man. XVII. The Euthymian refutation. XVIII. Doctrine regarding evil ANGELS. XIX. To whom they attribute great power, and whom they therefore think should be worshipped. XX. They said one of these demons inhabits every individual person. XXI. Fantasies regarding the plan the demons entered into for their own restoration. XXII. Errors regarding REGENERATION. XXIII. They held a low opinion of the SACRAMENTS, and specifically of BAPTISM. XXIV. Which they administered with a new rite. XXV. They considered the LORD’S SUPPER as nothing. XXVI. And they substituted the LORD’S PRAYER for it. XXVII. Errors regarding JUSTIFICATION. XXVIII. ### *Dissertation III. On the CUSTOMS of the Bogomils.*

Approach and division of the treatment. I. In internal divine worship there was HYPOCRISY. II. In external worship they avoided CHURCHES. III. As well as the sign of the CROSS, and they spoke of the Cross with contempt. IV. They attributed much to FASTING. V. They were devoted to PRAYERS. VI. In reciting these, they followed certain established rules. VII. They neither admitted nor worshipped IMAGES. VIII. Although they would both kiss and adore the Book of the GOSPELS. IX. Certain rites usually employed in their Baptism. X. On their abstinence from meat, cheese, and eggs. XI. On their arrogance and pride. XII. On their affected appearance of Piety. XIII. On their contempt for literature <note>original: literarum; refers to formal education and classical learning</note>. XIV. On the cunning used in their secret discipline. XV. On women drawn into the fellowship of their errors. XVI. On an external appearance designed for deception. XVII. On a depraved will. XVIII. Epilogue from Euthymius. XIX.

### CONCERNING THE
# NAME OF THE BOGOMILS,
## THEIR ORIGIN AND FATE.

->I.<-

As we prepare to revive the memory of the Bogomils <note>A medieval dualist sect that emerged in the First Bulgarian Empire in the 10th century</note>, we shall first establish those facts from the reliable records of proven writers that pertain to a more accurate understanding of this infamous class of men, from their earliest origins through the various turns of their fortune. With this plan in mind, it seems necessary to treat the name itself even more diligently, as its obscurity and its origin—which must be sought in another language—elude easy understanding. The Bogomils did not receive their name from a leader or founder of the sect, as is often the case, but rather from a certain characteristic of their doctrine or, more likely, their customs, which were common among them. In this matter, I find that the writers who lived closest to the times when this "unhappy crop" <note>An agricultural metaphor for the growth of heresy</note> grew up are in agreement. For Euthymius Zigabenus <note>A 12th-century Byzantine monk and theologian</note>, in Part II of his *Panoply* <note>original: Panopliae; a massive work defending Orthodox doctrine against heresies</note>, Title XXIII, provides the following explanation for the name: *For 'Bog' in the language of the Bulgarians means God, and 'milui' means have mercy. Thus, a Bogomil, according to them, would be one who calls down the mercy of God.* <note>original Greek: Βὸγ μὲν γὰρ ἡ τῶν Βουλγάρων γλῶσσα καλεῖ τὸν Θεὸν μίλβι δὲ τὸ ἐλέησον. εἴη δ' ἂν βογόμιλος κατ' αὐτὸς ὁ τοῦ θεοῦ τὸν ἔλεον ἐπισπώμενος.</note> That is, as Francesco Zino, the Latin translator in the Lyons edition of the *Library of the Fathers* <note>original: Biblioth. P. P. (Bibliotheca Patrum)</note>, Volume XIX, page 220, rendered it: ***Bog* in the Bulgarian tongue signifies God; and *Milui*, have mercy, so that a Bogomil among them is one who implores the mercy of God.** Lambecius <note>Peter Lambeck (1628–1680), a noted historian and librarian</note> displays the Greek words in a similar fashion from a manuscript codex in his *Commentary on the Library of Vienna*, book III, page 170. Nor does the Bodleian *transcript* <note>original: ἀπόγραφον (apographon)</note> which I have used disagree, except that the for-

### Dissertation III. Concerning the CUSTOMS of the Bogomils.
#### Introduction and division of the treatise.

*I. In internal divine worship, there was HYPOCRISY. II. In external worship, they turned away from CHURCHES* <note>original: TEMPLA; specifically physical church buildings</note>. *III. As well as from the sign of the CROSS, and they spoke of the Cross with contempt. IV. They attributed much to FASTING. V. They were devoted to PRAYER. VI. In the reciting of which a certain system was followed. VII. They neither admitted nor worshiped IMAGES. VIII. Although they would both kiss and adore the Book of the GOSPELS. IX. Certain rites usually employed in their Baptism. X. Concerning their abstinence from meat, cheese, and eggs. XI. Concerning their arrogance and pride. XII. Concerning the cunning used in their secret discipline* <term>arcani disciplina: the practice of keeping certain doctrines hidden from the uninitiated</term>. *XV. Concerning the depraved will. XVIII. Epilogue from Euthymius. XIX.*

### CONCERNING
# THE NAME OF THE BOGOMILS,
## THEIR ORIGIN AND FATE.

->I.<-

In setting out to recall the memory of the Bogomils, we will first present those accounts from the reliable records of proven authors that help us better understand this infamous group of people, from their earliest beginnings through the various shifts in their fortune. With this goal in mind, it seems necessary to treat the name itself with greater care, as its meaning is easily missed due to its obscurity and its origin in another language. They did not take the name "Bogomils" from the leader or founder of the sect—as is often the case—but rather from a characteristic of their teaching, or perhaps more accurately, from their common customs. On this point, I find that the writers living closest to the time when this "unhappy harvest" first sprouted are in agreement. For Euthymius Zigabenus <note>A 12th-century Byzantine monk and the author’s primary source for understanding medieval heresies</note>, in Part II of his *Dogmatic Panoply* <note>original: *Panoplia dogmatica*, a systematic defense of Orthodox doctrine against heresies</note>, Title XXIII, provides the following explanation for the name: *For in the language of the Bulgarians, "Bog" refers to God, and "milui" means "have mercy." According to them, a Bogomil is one who seeks to obtain the mercy of God.* <note>original Greek: Βὸγ μὲν γὰρ ἡ τῶν Βουλγάρων γλῶσσα καλεῖ τὸν Θεὸν μίλοι δὲ τὸ ἐλέησον. εἴη δ᾽ ἂν Βογόμιλος κατ᾽ αὐτοὺς ὁ τοῦ Θεοῦ τὸν ἔλεον ἐπισπώμενος.</note> That is to say, as Francesco Zino, the Latin translator in the Lyons edition of the *Library of the Fathers* <note>original: *Bibliotheca Patrum*, a collection of early Christian writings</note>, Volume XIX, page 220, rendered it: *Bog, in the Bulgarian tongue, signifies God; and Milui, have mercy; so that among them a Bogomil is one who implores the mercy of God.* Peter Lambeck <note>Lambecius (1624–1680), a scholar and librarian of the Imperial Library in Vienna</note> presents the Greek text in a similar way from a manuscript codex in his *Commentary on the Library of Vienna*, Book III, page 170. Nor does the Bodleian transcript <note>original: ἀπόγραφον (apographon), a handwritten copy of a manuscript</note> which I consulted differ, except that the foreign <note>The text ends here with the catchword "grina," likely part of the word "peregrina" meaning foreign or strange</note>

<summary>This page introduces the history of the Bogomils, focusing on the etymological origin of their name. It establishes that the name derives from the Bulgarian words for "God" and "have mercy," signifying one who seeks God's mercy.</summary>

<page-num>2</page-num>
<header>ON THE ORIGIN OF THE BOGOMILS</header>

the foreign word *Bog* <note>original: βὸγ</note> is represented with only a slight variation in the Latinized ending as *Bogon* <note>original: βόγον</note>. The illustrious former investigator of hand-written manuscripts, Charles du Fresne <note>Also known as Du Cange (1610–1688), a great scholar of medieval Greek and Latin</note>, shared this same passage with the learned community in his *Glossary of Medieval and Late Greek* from the Colbert Manuscript 4097, under the entry "Bogomils" <note>original: βογόμιλοι</note>, using the same reasoning as Lambecius. He confirms the meaning of the first part of the word with another witness, Herodian <note>Aelius Herodianus, a 2nd-century grammarian</note>, who in his work *On Parsing* <note>original: Epimerismo</note>—which is still unpublished—comments on the word *Bog* in this way:

*“Bog” is “God” according to the Bulgarians, from which also comes “Bogomil.”* <note>original: βὸγ ὁ θεὸς κατὰ βυλγάρους, ἐξ οὗ καὶ βογόμιλος.</note>

(The learned scholar wrote *Bog* [βὸγ] instead of *Bol* [βὸλ], which is mistakenly read today due to an error by scribes or printers. The celebrated Montfaucon recently noted in his *Greek Palaeography* (p. 374) that many such errors were left in that excellent work due to the author's absence.)

In the same manner, Harmenopoulos <note>Constantine Harmenopoulos, a 14th-century Byzantine judge and legal scholar</note>, in his *Treatise on the Opinions of the Heretics*, section 19, interprets the word "Bogomils" identically:

*A Bogomil, in the language of the Mysians, would be one who draws down the mercy of God.* <note>original: Ἔιη δ’ ἂν βογόμιλος κατὰ τὴν μυσῶν γλῶσσαν ὁ τοῦ θεοῦ τὸ ἔλεος ἐπισπώμενος.</note>

Harmenopoulos, by tracing the origin of this name to the language of the "Mysians" <note>An archaic name for people of the Balkans, particularly in Moesia</note>, should not be thought to disagree with those who look for it among the Bulgarians. It has long been common knowledge that Moesia was divided into two parts in antiquity: one comprising Serbia and the other Bulgaria. Therefore, the language of the Mysians is not distinguished from the Bulgarian language.

To these I add a passage from Nicetas Choniates <note>A Byzantine historian and theologian (c. 1155–1217)</note>, from his *Thesaurus of the Orthodox Faith*. This work exists partly in Latin, but the Greek text is found in various libraries—specifically an elegantly written copy in the Bodleian Library <note>The main research library of the University of Oxford</note>, about which I have said more in my notes on the *Casauboniana* (p. 253). Just as Nicetas copied Euthymius in other places, he does not stray from his footsteps here either, but repeats his very words. The most illustrious and learned Montfaucon cited this passage in his exceptional work, *Greek Palaeography*, from a manuscript belonging to Étienne Baluze (Book IV, p. 333); and before him, Gretser cited it in his notes to Volume II of the book *On the Cross* (p. 594), from a manuscript in the Bavarian Library. Gretser, however, mistakenly read *miloui* <note>original: μιλουὶ</note> for *miloui* <note>original: μιλούί</note>. In this [origin] of the name—

<page-num>3</page-num>
<header>AND THEIR FATES.</header>

I believe we should rely on reason rather than approving the opinion of Gottfried Arnold <note>Gottfried Arnold (1666–1714) was a radical pietist historian known for his "Unbiased History of Churches and Heretics," which often defended groups traditionally labeled as heretical.</note>, who perhaps here, as elsewhere, is affected by a love of novelty. In his *History of Heresies* <note>original: Haeresiologia</note> on page 206, relying on some unknown author, he declares that the Bogomils are called *“beloved by God”* in the Bulgarian language, but *“imploring God’s mercy”* in the Saracenic tongue <note>Arnold's reference to "Saracenic" (Arabic/Islamic) is unusual here, as the sect is primarily Slavic and Byzantine.</note>. Friedrich Spanheim <note>Friedrich Spanheim the Younger (1632–1701), a Dutch theologian and church historian known for his meticulous research.</note> comes closer to the view of the ancient writers, being a man of no less faith than learning and industry in narrating the affairs of the Church. In his *Christian History of the 12th Century*, page 1601, he judges that they are called Bogomils either as those who "associate with God" or those who "prayed for God's mercy." In the first part of the name, he is undoubtedly looking to the Greek language and its word *homilein* <note>original: ὁμιλεῖν — meaning to converse or be in company with</note>, which carries the sense of familiarity and friendship. Johannes Leuenclavius <note>A 16th-century German scholar who specialized in Turkish and Byzantine history.</note>, in a marginal note explaining the book of Harmenopoulos, and following his example Jacob Tollius in *Notable features of an Italian Journey* page 109, observes that *Bog* means *“God,”* and *Mile* means *“propitious”* <note>original: Bog Deum, Mile autem propitium</note> in the Henetic language, which today is called the Wendish language <note>The "Wends" were Slavic-speaking populations living near Germanic territories; "Henetic" refers to the ancient Veneti of the Vistula.</note> and is a dialect of the Slavonic tongue. With this origin of the name established, it is easily understood that it is more correct to call them *Bogomils* than *Bongomils*, a spelling observed in several manuscripts. For Tollius, in the aforementioned book on page 111, observes that they are called *Bongomils* in a certain letter of Euthymius found in the Caesarean Library <note>The Imperial Library in Vienna.</note>, which he had mentioned earlier on page 106. He also notes the title *“A Refutation of the Messalian Bogomils”* <note>original: Ἐλέγχος τῶν Μασσαλιανῶν Πογομίλων</note> in a certain manuscript of Euthymius’s *Panoplia* <note>The "Panoplia Dogmatica," a massive defense of Orthodox doctrine by Euthymius Zigabenus.</note>. And I well remember that Lambecius <note>Peter Lambeck (1628–1680), the librarian of the Imperial Library in Vienna.</note> cited the title of a certain letter by Euthymius three times in his commentary on the Vienna Library, and twice he provided the spelling *Bougomiloi* <note>original: βουγομίλων</note>. Among more recent writers, Caspar Calvorius in *The Fissures of Zion* <note>original: Fissuris Sionis (1700), a work on church schisms and sects.</note> page 338, preferred to call them *Bongomils*, though he notes there that others call them Bogomils and Bongomiles. To these you may add what the author of the *Nomocanon* <note>A collection of ecclesiastical law; this refers to a specific Greek edition.</note> says, which appears in Volume I of the *Monuments of the Greek Church*

Jean-Baptiste Cotelier <note>Jean-Baptiste Cotelier (1624–1686) was a prominent French scholar of the early Church fathers.</note> calls it *Bogomilia* <note>original: βογομιλίας</note> in his notes on Canon 19 of the Council of Gangra <note>A 4th-century church council that dealt with asceticism and marriage.</note>, which can be read in Volume II of Philippe Labbe’s *Councils* on page 434. Severinus Binius, however, in his notes, refers to this class of men as *Bogomiluci*. Cesare Baronius <note>An Italian cardinal and historian (1538–1607) known for his massive history of the Church.</note> likewise employed the name *Bogomils* in Volume XII of his *Annals* (entry 43), for which he was noted by Peter Lambeck <note>A 17th-century librarian and scholar.</note> in Book III of his work on the Imperial Library.

II. Having thus examined the inner meaning of the name, the next question to be asked is who, according to the reliability of history, should be considered the first author of that title, and whether this group of heterodox thinkers <note>original: "prave sentientium cohors," literally "a cohort of those who think wrongly," a common way of referring to heretics.</note> gave the name to themselves or rather received it from others. I do not believe either possibility can be explained with perfect accuracy or full certainty, though it seems more likely to me that this name was imposed by others upon those who preferred to be called **Christians**. These observers, seeing the group constant in their prayers and perhaps frequently hearing them boasting of the reconciled grace of God, believed they had sufficient reason for establishing this name. This was the same reason why the ancestors of the Bogomils were called by the name of **Massalians**—that is, **Euchites** or **Prayers** <term>Euchites: From the Greek "euchomai," meaning "to pray."</term>. For those who wish to derive the name of the Massalians from the Chaldean root *tsala* <note>original: צְלָא — an Aramaic/Syriac root meaning "to pray."</note>, which the Syrians express 

<margin><image-desc>A small hand-drawn reference mark consisting of three dots and a flourish.</image-desc></margin>

and use with the meaning of "praying," are correct in their thinking; they believe that *Matslin* <note>original: מַצְלִין</note>, that is, "those who pray," were called *Euchitai* in Greek. Theodoret <note>A 5th-century theologian and church historian.</note> achieved this understanding of the name long ago in Book IV of his *Ecclesiastical History*, chapter 10: "They call these people [Euchites]," he says, "translating the name into the Greek tongue" <note>original: "Ευχίτας τούτους προσαγορεύουσι, οἱ εἰς τὴν Ἑλλάδα φωνὴν τοὔνομα μεταβάλοντες"</note>. Compare what Dionysius Petavius <note>A Jesuit theologian and scholar.</note> remarks on that passage, and Jacob Tollius following him. Hence also John of Damascus, in his book on heresies—which Jean-Baptiste Cotelier included in Volume I of his *Monuments of the Greek Church*, page 302—embraces the same interpretation, saying: "Massalians, which means Prayers" <note>original: "Μασσαλιανοὶ, οἱ ἑρμηνεύονοι Ευχῖται"</note>. Just as the Bogomils, therefore, seem to have expressed the customs of the Massalians specifically in their practice of prayer as well as in other matters, it will seem a wonder to no one that they [shared a name] of almost the same...

<page-num>5</page-num>
<header>AND DESTINY.</header>

...entered into society, especially if, by a certain accustomed formula in their prayers, they made mention of divine mercy. For this conjecture is made by Caspar Sagittarius <note>A 17th-century German historian and theologian.</note> in his *Introduction to Ecclesiastical History*, page 891, where he opines that this formula was perhaps such as the Muscovites <note>A historical term for Russians.</note> are accustomed to use in their prayers today: *Hospodi pomiloi* <note>Old Church Slavonic: "Lord, have mercy."</note>, that is, "Lord, have mercy on me," which they repeat quite frequently. If anyone should wish to believe that the Bogomils themselves were the authors of their own name, I would believe him not to be entirely destitute of reason for stating so, since this very name seems to carry something pleasant with it, and it is well known that heretics have affected names of that kind with all zeal. Johann Heinrich Hottinger <note>A Swiss philologist and theologian (1620–1667) specializing in Oriental languages.</note> observed this among others in his *Philological Treasury*, Book I, Chapter I, page 69, and to lend credibility to his assertion, he calls upon the examples of Muhammad, the Gnostics, Mani, etc. <note>The author suggests these groups chose names implying "purity" or "praise" to gain favor.</note>
Whatever the truth of this may be—whether the Bogomils themselves or their orthodox <note>original: "recte sentientibus," literally "those who think correctly."</note> adversaries invented the name—it is certainly not obscure why it was borrowed from a specific quality rather than from the leader and standard-bearer of the sect itself. For it appears sufficiently from the testimonies of Euthymius <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century Byzantine monk and author of the *Panoply of Doctrine*.</note> and Anna Comnena <note>A Byzantine princess and historian (1083–1153) who wrote the *Alexiad*, detailing her father's reign and the trial of the Bogomil leader Basil.</note> that these men sought the salvation of their sect especially in concealment and dissimulation, which without doubt applied not only to their dogmas, but also to their chief defender and leader.

III. Nor is this the only name by which they were distinguished by the ancients; rather, as one observes in other heresies, where they bore various names according to the diversity of their unusual opinions, we find the same happened to the Bogomils. In this, they followed the example of their ancestors, namely the Massalians <note>A 4th-century ascetic sect, also called Messalians, who believed constant prayer was the only way to expel demons.</note>, to whom the very multitude of names they bore imposed the epithet of a **multi-form and many-named heresy** <note>original Greek: αἱρέσεως πολυειδοῦς καὶ πολυωνύμου</note>: see Euthymius’s *Victory and Triumph over the Sect of the Massalians*, pages 106 and 112, in the edition by Tollius. For these were called now *Prayers* <note>original: "euchitai"</note>, now *Enthusiasts* <note>original: "enthousiastai," meaning those possessed by a divine spirit</note>,

<sig>B 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: now</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the etymology of the name "Bogomil," suggesting it derives from a Slavic prayer for mercy, and explains that the sect used multiple names and secrecy to avoid persecution, much like the ancient Massalians.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the various names by which the Bogomils were known in historical records, transitioning from the linguistic origin of "Bogomil" to its synonymous titles in early Christian heresiology. The catchword "gitarum" likely completes the word "Phundagitarum" (Phundagiatas) from the following page.</meta>

<page-num>6</page-num>
# ON THE ORIGIN OF THE BOGOMILS

...at times they were called Encratites <note>Greek for "the self-controlled," a sect practicing strict asceticism.</note>, at other times Marcionists (not *Marcianists*, as Arnold <note>Gottfried Arnold (1666–1714), a German theologian whose *History of Heresies* was influential but sometimes criticized for its accuracy.</note> incorrectly records on page 206 of his *History of Heresies*; for he could have learned, to say nothing of others, from Cotelerius <note>Jean-Baptiste Cotelier (1629–1686), a French scholar of early Church manuscripts.</note> in Volume III of his *Monuments*, page 639, that these names are not to be confused). Sometimes they were known as Phundaites, which is established by the testimony of Euthymius <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century Byzantine monk and the primary source for Bogomil history.</note> from the passage mentioned; at other times they were called Martyrians and Euphemites, Adelphians, Lampetians, Lycopetrians, Satanists, Choreutae <note>From the Greek for "dancers."</note>, Cauchitae, Eustathians, and, if the Augustinian manuscripts are to be believed, they were called Psalmians. This has been proven from antiquity partly by Tollius in his notes on Euthymius, partly by Daneau <note>Lambert Daneau (1530–1595), a French Calvinist theologian.</note> in his commentary on Augustine’s *Book of Heresies*, chapter 59, and partly by Cotelerius in his notes to Volume I of the *Monuments*, page 78 and following.

I conclude by no empty conjecture that these names were common to the Bogomils, for the most part if not in every respect; not only because they seem to have been cast into a fellowship of names just as they were into a fellowship of doctrines, but also because several of these names are customarily attributed to them in the explicit words of those writers. Thus, Euthymius, in the place cited, joins the name of the Massalians and the Bogomils as being of the same kind of heretics, not only in the title but also in the short work itself on page 112. 

Furthermore, we are not permitted to doubt that they were called Phundaitas, Phundaiatas, and Phundagiatas <note>These names derive from the Greek *phunda*, meaning a pouch or bag, likely referring to the traveling bags carried by these wandering preachers.</note>, according to what is read in Peter Lambeck's <note>Librarian of the Imperial Library in Vienna (1628–1680).</note> *Commentary on the Imperial Library*, Book III, Codex VII, and Book V, Codices 213 to 268. These entries discuss a certain letter of Euthymius written against the Bogomils, the title of which is as follows: 

*A Letter of Euthymius, Monk of the Illustrious Monastery* <note>original Greek: "της Περιβλέπτου μονής" (the Peribleptos Monastery in Constantinople).</note>, *sent from Constantinople to his own homeland, exposing the heresies of the most godless and impious deceivers, namely the Phundagiatas, also called Bogomils and Massalians, as well as the profane and abominable heresy of the Armenians, and other lawless and utterly profane heresies.* 

That is, as Tollius translates it: *The Letter of Euthymius, a Monk of the Illustrious Cloister, sent from Constantinople to his homeland, refuting the Phundagia...* <note>The text cuts off here, continuing the translation of the letter's title on the next page.</note>

<summary>This page catalogs the numerous historical names attributed to the Bogomils, such as Massalians and Phundagiatas, citing various academic and ecclesiastical authorities to prove they were viewed as part of a long tradition of ascetic dissenters.</summary>

<page-num>7</page-num>
->AND FATES.<-

...gitae <note>Completing the word "Phundagiagitae" from the previous page.</note>, who are called both **Bogomils** and **Messalians** <note>The Messalians were an older ascetic sect from the 4th century whose name means "praying people"; they were often conflated with the Bogomils by later Byzantine writers.</note>, as well as the profane and abominable **Armenian** heresy, and likewise other various sects of that kind, no less impious and execrable than these. Regarding this interpretation, I should mention in passing that the illustrious Cave <note>William Cave (1637–1713), an English church historian.</note>, in his *Literary History*, page 567 of the newest edition, correctly observed that **Tollius** <note>Jacobus Tollius (1633–1696), a Dutch classicist.</note> had slipped up. Tollius interpreted the words <note>original: "τῆς περιβλέπτου μονῆς"</note> *of the Illustrious Monastery* in a general sense, whereas according to the common usage of that time, one should only have thought of the Monastery of the **Mother of God** <note>original: "Deiparae"</note>. **Charles du Fresne** <note>Also known as Du Cange (1610–1688), a founding figure in Byzantine studies.</note> clearly demonstrated from reliable writers in his *Christian Constantinople*, Book IV, Chapter II, number 36, that this place was sacred to that Monastery.

As for the matter itself, the origin of the name **Phundagiagitae** is very obscure, and perhaps will never be fully cleared up today, since it seems not unreasonable that the name was taken from the foreign language of the **Bulgarians**, who called them **Bogomils**. This is the reason why I believe **Cotelerius**—a man otherwise most sharp-witted—employed a less successful conjecture here. He suspects that this name arose from *phunda* <note>original: "φούνδᾳ"</note>, that is, a pouch or purse, which they supposedly carried to collect alms; he notes this in Volume I of his *Monuments*, page 744. For although I do not deny that this "barbarous" word <note>A term used by scholars for non-Classical Greek or Latin terms.</note> was accepted with that meaning in the era of the **Bogomils** (as the Distinguished **Charles du Fresne** observed in his *Glossary of Medieval and Late Greek*, page 1695), there are nonetheless two things here that invite further thought: first, it is uncertain whether the name itself ought to be derived from the common Greek language; and furthermore, to my knowledge, I have not read any mention of the Bogomils having an interest in begging for alms. Indeed, the name seemed more obscure to **Du Fresne** than for him to risk his intellectual efforts in unearthing its origins in his praised *Glossary*. I do not recall other names being imposed on these men by the ancients, which they themselves certainly seem to have loathed. I deduce this by no empty conjecture from the words of **Euthymius** in his *Refutation of the Messalians* (or **Bogomils**), page 112, phrased thus: *for [they call] themselves* **Christians**...

# ON THE ORIGIN OF THE BOGOMILS

They were sometimes called the **Encratites** <note>original: "Εγκρατηταὶ"; a group name meaning "the continent" or "self-controlled," often used for various ascetic sects.</note>, sometimes the **Marcionists** <note>original: "Μαρκιωνισταὶ"; followers of the 2nd-century dualist Marcion. This is not "Marcianistae," as the scholar **Gottfried Arnold** incorrectly records on page 206 of his *History of Heresies*. Arnold could have learned from **Jean-Baptiste Cotelerius** (in Volume III of his *Monuments*, page 639) that these names must not be confused</note>, and sometimes the **Phundaites** <note>original: "Φουνδαϊταὶ"; likely derived from the Greek word for a "pouch" or "knapsack," referring to their itinerant lifestyle.</note>. This is established by the testimony of **Euthymius Zigabenus** <note>A 12th-century Byzantine monk and theologian who wrote extensively against heresies.</note> from the passage previously cited. They were also called Martyrians, Euphemites, Adelphians, Lampetians, Lycopetrians, Satanians, Choreutae, Cauchitae, Eustathians, and—if the Augustinian manuscripts are to be believed—the **Psalliani** <note>From the Greek word for "psalm-singers."</note>. These names have been traced back to antiquity partly by **Jacobus Tollius** in his notes on Euthymius, by **Lambertus Danaeus** in his notes on Augustine's *On Heresies* (Chapter 59), and by **Cotelerius** in his notes on Volume I of the *Monuments* (page 783 onwards). I have reached the not-unreasonable conclusion that these names were common to the **Bogomils** in most, if not all, cases. This is because the Bogomils seem to have shared both doctrines and names with these groups, and because certain writers are accustomed to attributing these names to them in explicit terms.

Thus, Euthymius, in the location cited above, joins the names of the **Massalians** <note>An ancient ascetic sect from Mesopotamia whose name means "praying people"; Byzantine authors often viewed the Bogomils as their successors.</note> and the **Bogomils** as heretics of the same kind, not only in the title but also in the short work itself (page 112). We are left in no doubt that they were also called **Phundaites**, **Phundaitates**, and **Phundagiagitae** by what we read in **Peter Lambecius**'s *Commentary on the Imperial Library* (Book III, Manuscript VII; and Book V, Manuscripts 213 and 268) regarding a certain letter by Euthymius written against the Bogomils. The title of the letter is as follows: *Letter of Euthymius, a monk of the Peribleptos Monastery, sent from Constantinople and the said monastery to his own country, exposing the heresies of the most godless and impious deceivers called Phundagiagitae, or Bogomils and Massalians, and the profane and abominable heresy of the Armenians, and other lawless and utterly profane heresies.* That is to say, as **Tollius** translates it: *Letter of Euthymius, a monk of the Illustrious monastery, sent from Constantinople <note>original: "C Poli", an abbreviation for Constantinopoli.</note> from the aforementioned monastery to his own country, refuting the Phundagia-*

<meta>catchword: gitarum,</meta>

<summary>This page explores the various historical names and labels applied to the Bogomils, emphasizing their identification with the Massalians and Phundagiagitae in Byzantine literature. The author cites several 17th-century scholars and Byzantine sources to argue that these diverse names often referred to the same sect or shared traditions.</summary>

<page-num>7</page-num>

# AND THEIR FATES

...gites, who are also called **Bogomils** and **Messalians**, as well as the profane and abominable heresy of the **Armenians**, and other similar sects no less impious and accursed than these. Regarding this interpretation—as I note in passing—the illustrious **William Cave** <note>original: "Cl. Caveus"; a prominent 17th-century English church historian.</note> in his *Literary History* (p. 567, newest edition) rightly observed that **Cornelius Tollius** was mistaken. Tollius interpreted the words "of the Peribleptos Monastery" <note>original Greek: "τῆς περιβλέπτου μονῆς"; *Peribleptos* means "The Admired" or "The Renowned."</note> as simply meaning "an Illustrious Monastery." However, based on the common usage of the language at that time, one should have understood this specifically as the Monastery of the Mother of God <note>The Virgin Mary.</note>. The scholar **Charles du Fresne** <note>Also known as Du Cange, a titan of medieval philology.</note> clearly demonstrated from reliable writers in his *Christian Constantinople* (Book IV, chapter 2, number 36) that the monastery in question was sacred to her.

As for the matter itself, the origin of the name **Phundagiagitae** is very obscure, and perhaps it will never be fully clarified today. It seems not unreasonable to suggest the name was taken from the foreign language of the **Bulgarians**, who also provided the name *Bogomils*. For this reason, I believe **Jean-Baptiste Cotelerius** <note>A famous French scholar of early Christian texts.</note>—a man otherwise very sharp-witted—relied on a less-than-fortunate conjecture here. In his notes to Volume I of the *Monuments* (p. 744), he suspects this name arose from the word *phounda* <note>original Greek: "φούνδα"; meaning a pouch, purse, or knapsack.</note>, referring to a money-bag or purse which they supposedly carried to collect alms.

Although I do not deny that this "barbaric" word <note>The author refers to "barbaric" as Medieval Greek or loanwords rather than classical Attic Greek.</note> was used in that sense during the era of the Bogomils—as the distinguished Charles du Fresne observed in his *Glossary of Medieval and Late Greek* (p. 1695)—there are two things that demand further consideration. First, it is uncertain whether the name itself should be derived from the common Greek language; and second, as far as I know, it is not recorded anywhere that the Bogomils had a practice of begging for alms. Indeed, the name seemed too obscure even to Du Fresne for him to risk his intellectual reputation in uncovering its origins in his aforementioned *Glossary*.

I do not recall other names being imposed on these people by ancient writers that they did not seem to reject. I draw this conclusion from a likely conjecture based on the words of **Euthymius Zigabenus** <note>A 12th-century Byzantine monk and the primary source for this sect's history.</note> in his *Refutation of the Messalians or Bogomils* (p. 112), phrased as: "For [they call themselves] Christians..." <note>original Greek: "χριστιανὲς γὰρ". The author implies the sect rejected labels like 'Bogomil' or 'Phundagiagite' in favor of simply being called 'Christians'.</note>

<summary>The author debates the etymological origins of the name "Phundagiagitae," critiquing scholars who suggest it refers to a beggar's pouch and arguing instead for a Bulgarian origin or a simple self-identification as "Christians."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the names and deceptive practices of the Bogomil sect. Having discussed the specific name "Phundagiagitae" on the previous page, the author now examines how these groups adopted the name "Christian" and other titles to hide their true beliefs from the public.</meta>

<page-num>8</page-num>

# ON THE ORIGIN OF THE BOGOMILS

"...calling themselves [Christians], these enemies of Christ mix with the orthodox under that fair-sounding name," etc. <note>original Greek: "ἑαυτὸς ὀνομάζοντες οἱ μισόχρισοι, τῷ εὐφήμῳ τῷ ὀνόματος ἀναμίγνυνται τοῖς ὀρθοδόξοις"</note> This is interpreted by **Tollius** <note>Cornelius Tollius, the Dutch scholar mentioned on the previous page.</note> as: *for since those enemies of Christ call themselves Christians, being covered by that beautiful name, they easily mingle with those who think rightly.* These same people called themselves **Citizens of Christ** <note>original: "christopolitas"</note>, as **Euthymius** <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century monk who wrote a famous refutation of heresies.</note> reports in the previously cited place (p. 122), though he observes they could more truly be called **Antichrists**. Indeed, the Bogomils made it their own policy to adopt a common fraud used by such men: they used a similarity of name to present a pretense of agreement on the main points of doctrine, while in their minds they were departing into entirely different beliefs.

This custom was strongly favored by the heretics who disturbed the Church in the time of **Justin Martyr** <note>A 2nd-century Christian philosopher and "apologist" who defended the faith to the Roman Emperor.</note>; for this reason, we read him complaining against **Marcion** <note>An early heretic who taught that the God of the Old Testament was different from the God of the New Testament.</note> in this way: *and all those who originate from these men, as we have said, are called Christians, just as the name "Philosophy" is common to philosophers even when their doctrines are shared.* <note>The sense here is that "philosopher" is a broad label for people who might disagree wildly; Justin argues "Christian" had become a similarly misused label.</note> That is to say: *And all who spring from these are called Christians, as we said; not unlike how the common title "from Philosophy" falls to philosophers who go off into different opinions.*

In the same way, the **Manichaeans** <note>A major dualist religion that competed with Christianity, teaching a cosmic struggle between Light and Dark.</note> once claimed that they alone were worthy of the name of Christians, leaving the glory of other names to everyone else. For **Photius** <note>The 9th-century Patriarch of Constantinople and a prolific scholar.</note> says this in the first book of his manuscript *On the Manichaeans*: *And those thrice-wretched men call those who are truly Christians "Romans," while they wrap themselves in the title of Christians, a name to which they are entirely foreign.* <note>In the Byzantine Empire, "Roman" was the standard term for an orthodox citizen of the empire.</note> So too did they have the **Gnostics** <note>Early groups known for their "secret knowledge" (gnosis) and complex mythologies.</note> as their predecessors, about whom **Oecumenius** <note>A 10th-century commentator on the New Testament.</note> says the following in his commentary on the Letter of Jude: *By calling themselves Christians, they deceive many with this name.*

I do not wish to recount here those "praises" which the Bogomils received—names as worthy of them as their "virtues"—for mention of them will occur frequently throughout this treatise. For the sake of an example, it will suffice to mention here that **Euthymius**, in the passage already quoted several times (p. 106), calls them **atheists** <note>original: "ἀθέους"</note>. He uses this, I believe, in the sense familiar to Greek writers, who frequently call men infamous for great crimes by the name of "atheists."

<meta>catchword: See</meta>

<summary>This page discusses how the Bogomils and earlier heretical groups like the Manichaeans and Gnostics adopted the title "Christian" to disguise their unorthodox beliefs. The author cites several historical church fathers to show that this "theft" of names was a common tactic used to deceive the faithful and blend into society.</summary>

<page-num>9</page-num>

### AND THEIR FATE.

See the illustrious **Ezechiel Spanheim** <note>A 17th-century diplomat and scholar noted for his work on ancient coins and literature.</note> on Aristophanes' *Plutus*, verse 492. **Germanus**, Patriarch of Constantinople <note>Germanus I, an 8th-century patriarch who defended the use of icons.</note>, called them "demon-faced" <note>original Greek: δαιμονοπροσωπος</note>, meaning those who reflect the face of demons, according to **Cotelerius** (p. 744 of the notes to Volume I of his *Monuments*), a passage which I have also found in the *Orations on Images* (p. 552) in the works of **Gretser**.

IV. The sect of the **Bogomils**, distinguished by so many names, is found not to have had a single origin. Instead, having been driven by various fates, it has at times died out, and at other times been recalled to light by madmen, beginning to raise its head once more. For this reason, I believe a double origin for it should be established: one more remote and ultimate, the other more immediate. If you seek the former, clear traces of it will be shown in the 4th Century after the Restoration of Salvation <note>The 4th century AD.</note> in the **Massalian** heresy itself <note>The Massalians, also known as Euchites or "Praying People," were a 4th-century ascetic sect accused of believing that everyone is born with an indwelling demon that only prayer can expel.</note>. This heresy transmitted to them not only the first threads of most pestilential errors, but the entire substance, like an unhappy inheritance; it left very little for the more recent restorers of the sect to add from other groups, most notably from the **Manichaeans** <note>A major dualistic religion founded by the prophet Mani, teaching a cosmic struggle between the forces of Light and Darkness.</note>. That the matter stands as I have said is placed beyond controversy by a comparison carefully made between the doctrines and internal principles of the Massalians and the Bogomils, as well as by the clearest testimonies of ancient writers.

I shall bring forward a passage from **Euthymius** <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, the 12th-century Byzantine monk commissioned by the Emperor to document these heresies.</note>, who, at the Emperor's command, was more diligent than all others in explaining the nature of this heresy. He begins his discussion in Part II, Title XXIII of the *Panoplia* <note>The *Panoplia Dogmatica*, a defensive "armor" of doctrine against heretics.</note> in this way: "The heresy of the Bogomils did not exist long before our generation and was a part of that of the Massalians, and it agrees in many things with their doctrines, yet it also invented certain things itself and increased the plague." <note>original Greek: ἡ τῶν Βογομίλων αἵρεσις οὐ πρὸ πολλοῦ συνέστη τῆς καθ᾽ ἡμᾶς γενεᾶς, μέρος οὖσα τῆς τῶν Μασσαλιανῶν, καὶ συμφερομένη τὰ πολλὰ τοῖς ἐκείνων δόγμασι, τινὰ δὲ καὶ προσεξευροῦσα καὶ τὴν λύμην ἀυξήσασα.</note> As interpreted by **Zinus**: *The Bogomil heresy existed not much before our time and was a part of the Massalians; it agrees in many matters with their institutions, though it also devised some things itself and increased the pestilence.* **Anna Comnena** <note>Byzantine princess and historian, daughter of Emperor Alexios I.</note> agrees with this in Book XV of the *Alexiad* (p. 486), unless...

<meta>catchword: because</meta>

<summary>This page argues that the Bogomil sect originated from the 4th-century Massalian heresy, inheriting most of its core errors while adding new elements of its own. The author cites Byzantine historians like Euthymius Zigabenus and Anna Comnena to support this historical continuity.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the origins of the Bogomils, transitioning from the 4th-century Massalians to their even deeper roots in 3rd-century Manichaeism. It cites historical accounts of how these two "pestilential" movements merged to form the Bogomil sect.</meta>

<page-num>10</page-num>

-># ON THE ORIGIN OF THE BOGOMILS<-

...which traces the ultimate origin of this Sect even deeper, referring it back to the **Manichaeans** <note>Followers of Mani, a 3rd-century Persian prophet who taught a dualistic struggle between a good realm of light and an evil realm of darkness.</note> themselves, who, around the middle of the 3rd Century, infected the world with their dogmas like a pestilence. From the union of these Manichaeans with the **Massalians**, as if from two different seeds, the deformed offspring of the Bogomil sect grew and was brought into the light, with **Basil** <note>Basil the Physician, a prominent Bogomil leader who was eventually burned at the stake by Emperor Alexios I Komnenos.</note> acting as its midwife. I cannot help but provide the very words here, which read as follows:

*After these things, as the years of his reign were passing, a massive cloud of heretics arose, a new kind of heresy never before known to the Church. For two most evil and worthless dogmas, known from ancient times, came together: the impiety (one might say) of the Manichaeans, which we also call the Paulician heresy, and the foulness of the Massalians. Such is the Bogomil dogma, composed of Massalians and Manichaeans.* <note>original Greek: Μετὰ δὲ ταῦτα τοῦ ἔτους διεπτεύοντος... This passage is from the *Alexiad* by Anna Komnene, daughter of Emperor Alexios I, describing the rise of the sect during her father's reign.</note>

This is interpreted by **Peter Possinus** <note>A 17th-century Jesuit scholar and editor of Byzantine texts.</note> as: *After these things, around the year of Emperor Alexios \*\*\*, a huge cloud of heretics emerged. They were spreading a new kind of wicked dogma previously unheard of by the Church, which was forged from a mixture of two separate, most wicked, and highly detestable errors of old; namely, from the heresy of the Manichaeans, whom we also call Paulicians, and from the abominable madness of the Massalians, combined and solidified into one—the nefarious dogma of the Massalians <note>Possinus likely meant the Bogomils here, as the text refers to the merger.</note>, which we are now discussing, was born.* 

I observe here in passing that **Leo Allatius**, in the second book of his *On the Perpetual Consensus of the Western and Eastern Church* (p. 636), cited this same passage from a manuscript in such a way that it can be significantly corrected by the edition of Possinus which we follow here. For Allatius reads it in a confused and broken sense: *The impiety of the Manichaeans, as one might say, was also the foulness of the Paulicians,* where it is quite clear from the Greek context that something has fallen out, either through the carelessness of a scribe or a printer, which that manuscript originally contained. This can be gathered clearly from the added Latin translation, which renders the words as: *The impiety of the Manichaeans, as someone might say, which we would call the Paulician heresy, and the abomination of the Massalians.*

<page-num>11</page-num>

->...AND FATES.<-

V. Beyond these testimonies from various writers, the surviving records concerning both the Massalians and the Bogomils make their clear agreement regarding doctrines manifest. For someone who—to say nothing of other evidence—examines the *Chapters of the Impious Sect of the Massalians*, taken from their own book, which are found in **John Damascene's** <note>An 8th-century monk and theologian who wrote *The Fountain of Knowledge*, part of which catalogs various heresies.</note> book *On Heresies*, in his *Collected Works*, and more fully reprinted by **Cotelerius** <note>Jean-Baptiste Cotelier, a 17th-century French scholar of early Christian texts.</note> in the first volume of his *Greek Monuments*, page 302 and following, will not fail to see the greatest similarity on both sides. 

And those *summaries* or Chapters, if I may mention this in passing, do not seem to me to be different from that commentary which the learned collectors of the *Leipzig Acts* <note>A scholarly journal founded in 1682, also known as *Acta Eruditorum*.</note> led us to hope for from an old manuscript codex to illustrate the Massalian heresy in the year 1696, on page 299. Their words go as follows: *To illustrate all these things more fully, we might elsewhere present those things from an old manuscript codex, namely,* "The chapters of the impious dogma of the Massalians, taken from their own book," <note>original Greek: Κεφάλαια τῶ τῶν μασσαλιανῶν ἀσεβῶς δόγματος, ἀναληφθέντα ἐκ τῶ βιβλίο αὐτῶν</note> *which seem all the more suitable for decorating this material because there is almost no doubt that by that "book" of the Massalians, from which those principal chapters of their doctrine are said to have been taken, is understood that very book which **Photius** <note>A 9th-century Patriarch of Constantinople and author of the *Bibliotheca*, a collection of summaries of books he had read.</note> testifies in Codex 52 was titled the **Ascetic** <note>original: ἀσϰητιϰὸν; a manual for spiritual discipline.</note> and contained their dogmas.* 

The illustrious **Cave** <note>William Cave, a 17th-century English church historian.</note> approved of this plan in his *Literary History*, page 568; nor was he displeased by the conjecture of those who convinced themselves that this compendium of doctrine was compiled from the *Ascetic* as if from the "Symbolic" <note>Meaning a foundational or creed-like text that defined the sect's identity.</note> book of the Massalians. Whatever the truth of this may be, I certainly do not think it should be doubted that that summary representation of errors is already read in the aforementioned book by Damascene, since its title completely and in every part agrees with the description in that manuscript codex. 

I myself, not remembering that these were read in Damascene, transcribed a short work from the *Barocci Codex* <note>A collection of Greek manuscripts acquired by the Bodleian Library in 1629 from the Venetian Francesco Barocci.</note> of very ancient note, which exists in the **Bodleian Library** <note>The main research library of the University of Oxford.</note> (no. 185), and I found its title so arranged that, if one were to stand by its faith and authority, the conjecture...

<page-num>12</page-num>
### ON THE ORIGIN OF THE BOGOMILS

...the book I mentioned might perhaps have expired. For that work mentions not just one Massalian book, but many, from which those errors were gathered into a summary. For it was composed in this manner: *The chapters of the impious dogmas of the Massalians, taken from their books.* <note>original Greek: κεφάλαια τὰ τῶν Maſſalianῶν δυſσεβῶν δόγματῶ ἀληφθέντα ἐκ τῶν βιβλίων αὐτῶν</note> However, the former conjecture is strengthened by the fact that **Euthymius** <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century Byzantine monk and theologian known for his "Panoplia Dogmatica."</note> in his *Panoplia*, Title XXII, when about to explain the errors of these men, says he will present their principles as if they were drawn from their own book. It appears that Euthymius used no other book than the one from which that Damascene compendium originated—or perhaps even the *Excerpts* of **Damascene** <note>John Damascene (c. 675–749 AD), an influential Syrian monk and scholar who cataloged various heresies.</note> themselves—because sometimes there is such a great agreement between Euthymius and this Damascene compendium that you can observe the same words on both sides. Thus, in page 219 of Euthymius’s *Panoplia*, you may read the following:

> *They say it is possible for a human being, after the state of <term>impatibility</term> <note>From the Greek "apatheia," a spiritual state of being free from worldly passions or suffering.</note> which they themselves teach, to perceive the very person of the Holy Spirit with the senses and in all fullness.*

->In no other way<-
are the words phrased in the little work by Damascene, with which our **apograph** <note>A transcript or copy of an original manuscript.</note> agrees: *that it is possible for a human being to receive the person of the Holy Spirit sensibly in all fullness and in all activity.* <note>original Greek: ὅτι δυνατὸν, δέξασθαι αἰσθητῶς τὴν ὑπόστασιν τοῦ ἁγίου πνεύματος τὸν ἄνθρωπον ἐν πάσῃ πληροφορίᾳ, πάσῃ ἐνεργείᾳ</note>

->Since, therefore, this monument belongs<-
to the most remote antiquity, I believe I will perform a worthwhile task if I briefly record here the variant readings that occur between the edition by **Cotelerius** <note>Jean-Baptiste Cotelier, a 17th-century French scholar of early Christian texts.</note> and the **Baroccian Codex** <note>A collection of Greek manuscripts held at the Bodleian Library, Oxford.</note> which I have employed, especially since I think some things are read more correctly in the Baroccian parchments.

->Thus Cotelerius on page 302 reads<-
"dwells with," <note>original Greek: συνοικεῖ</note> where our manuscript adds "in" <note>original Greek: ἐν</note>; he also has "according to all things" <note>original Greek: κατὰ πάντα</note> while ours has "according to everything" <note>original Greek: κατὰ πᾶν</note>. He provides "of the divine mysteries," <note>original Greek: τῶν θείων μυστηρίων</note> while ours, with the same meaning, [has] "of the holy mysteries." <note>original Greek: τῶν ὁσίων μ.</note> A little later he has "reputed prayer," <note>original Greek: νομιζομένη εὐχὴ</note> while ours has "zealously practiced [prayer]," <note>original Greek: σπουδαζομένη</note> in which it agrees with the editions of Damascene. At the end of this page he reads: "that it is necessary to receive both impatibility and the presence of the Holy Spirit, and to be in all fullness." <note>original Greek: ὅτι δεῖ καὶ ἀπάθειαν λαβεῖν καὶ τουσίαν τοῦ ἁγίου πνεύματος, καὶ πάσῃ πληροφορίᾳ ἔσεσθαι</note> Ours, however, in my judgment, [is] more correct: "that it is necessary to receive both impatibility and parti-..." <note>original Greek: ὅτι δεῖ καὶ ἀπάθειαν λαβεῖν καὶ με-</note>

The author uses textual criticism, comparing the Baroccian Codex with published editions by Cotelerius, to highlight specific linguistic variants regarding the nature of the Holy Spirit and spiritual "impatibility."</summary>
