<page-num>13</page-num>
<header>AND FATES.</header>

...to perceive the presence <note>original Greek: σίαν [the end of the word 'parousian' or 'ousian']</note> of the Holy Spirit in every sensation and in full assurance.
**Page 303.** That edition has "a revelation having occurred," <note>original: ἀποκάλυψις γενομένη</note> to which our text adds "perceptibly." <note>original: αἰσθητῶς</note>
**In the same place, C.** [Cotelerius] reads "the Savior can be manifested," <note>original: δύναται φανερω̃σθαι ὁ σωτὴρ</note> which the version expresses as "the Savior or the Cross can appear"; our text has "Cross," <note>original: σταυρὸς</note> which then prefixes "holy" <note>original: ἁγίω</note> to "the altar," <note>original: τῶ θυσιαστηρίω</note> and uses "to be offered" <note>original: προσενεχθῆναι</note> instead of "to have been offered" <note>original: προσηνέχθαι</note> with the same meaning; and finally "confusedly," <note>original: πεφυρμένως</note> which is more consistent with grammatical analogy than what Cotelerius has, "confused." <note>original: πεφυραμένος</note> In the second to last verse, Cotelerius reads "bringing in, they say," <note>original: εἰσάγοντες φάσκοντες</note> but our text—with sense supporting it—has "they bring in, saying." <note>original: εἰσάγουσι φάσκοντες</note>
**Page 304.** In the first verse, instead of the Cotelerius reading "abandoned," <note>original: καταλελειμμέναις</note> our text exhibits "occupied," <note>original: κατειλημμέναις</note> and the same adds "to those who have used" after "circumstances," <note>original: poft περιστάσεσιν addit χρησαμένοις</note> which is entirely elegant and more clear. **B. 3.** Instead of "houses of prayer," <note>original: εὐκτηρίοις</note> our text reads "ascetic dwellings," <note>original: ἀσκητηρίοις</note> which I think is the correct reading. In the following, "practicing wonders" <note>original: τὸ τερατεύοντες</note> is rightly changed by ours to "they practice wonders." <note>original: τερατεύονται</note>
**Page 305, verse 9.** After "we confess," <note>original: ὁμολογοῦμεν</note> our text—along with the edited Damascene—inserts "to have," <note>original: ἔχειν</note> which Cotelerius lacks. **C.** Instead of "of whom many etc." our text has: "as many also after such [state] among them are outside of perfection." <note>original: ὡς πολλοὶ καὶ μετὰ τὴν τοιαύτην παρ’ αὐ-τοῖς τῆς τελειότητος ἐκτὸς ὄντας</note> In the third to last verse, our text uses the compound "to dissolve" <note>original: διαλύειν</note> instead of the simple "to loosen," <note>original: λύειν</note> just as in the last verse it has "to take beforehand" <note>original: προλαμβάνεσθαι</note> instead of "to take in addition," <note>original: προσλαμβάνεσθαι</note> which I would prefer.
**Page 306, verse 2.** Instead of "rearing of children" <note>original: τεκνοτροφίας</note> in our text you may read "appearance of children," <note>original: τεκνοφανίας</note> and in verse 3, instead of "to bring to," <note>original: προσκομίζειν</note> you may find "to bring forward." <note>original: προκομίζειν</note> **B. 3.** Our text rightly has "it came of their trickery," <note>original: τῆς ἐκείνων κυβείας ἐγένετο</note> which is different in the editions of Damascene and Cotelerius. Instead of "testimony" <note>original: μαρτυρία</note> in verse 9, our text—less correctly—has "witness." <note>original: μάρτυρα</note> **Verse 11.** "...judging worthily outside the honor of the clerical ranks." <note>original: ἐκτὸς τῆς τῶν κληρικῶν βαθμῶν τιμῆς ἀξίως κρίνοντες</note> **C. 8.** Instead of "the day," <note>original: τὴν ἡμέραν</note> our text puts "the hour." <note>original: ὥραν</note> In the second to last verse, our text is less full: "but they fearlessly despise and commit perjury." <note>original: καταφρονῶσι δὲ ἀδεῶς καὶ ἐπιορκοῦσιν</note> And indeed, it was right to note these things in this place, so that even in this way an opportunity might be created for those interested in these matters to deserve well of this uncommon monument of antiquity, and to restore some of its poorly handled passages to their former integrity.

<page-num>14</page-num>
# ON THE ORIGIN OF THE BOGOMILS
C 3

...to be traced back <note>original Latin: repetendam</note>, having brought forward examples of marvelous agreement regarding the matter at hand, we will not seek to investigate more extensively, as this can be done more correctly in the progress of the discussion. Although this heresy was proscribed from the Church with all zeal in various Synods during the 4th century and those following, it nevertheless sprouted up again from time to time, as can be understood from what others have reported: namely, among the ancient authors, Theodoret (Ecclesiastical History, IV, 11) and Photius (Codex 52); and among more recent authors, John Forbes in *Theological Instructions* (II, 8), Lambecius in Book III of his *Commentaries on the Imperial Library*, and Tollius in his *Insignia of the Italian Journey*.

It began to rear its head again especially at the beginning of the 12th century, while Alexios Komnenos <note>Byzantine Emperor from 1081 to 1118, known for his struggle against various heresies</note> was administering the affairs of the empire with the highest authority, at which time it was distinguished by the new name of "Bogomils." For thus Euthymius records, besides Anna Komnena <note>The daughter of Alexios Komnenos and author of the Alexiad, a primary history of her father's reign</note>, whose words we have already summoned as evidence. He says: "It was revealed during the times of Alexios our God-governed Emperor, who, having hunted down its leader skillfully and quite admirably..." <note>original Greek: Διεγνώσθη δὲ κατὰ τοὺς χρόνους ἀλεξίου τοῦ θεοκυβερνήτου βασιλέως ἡμῶν, ὃς ἐντέχνως καὶ πάνυ θαυμασίως τὸν ἔξαρχον ἀυτῆς θηρεύσας</note>—that is, *The Bogomil heresy was revealed during the reign of our Emperor Alexios, who, guided by the Divine Will, hunted down their leader with a prudent and admirable method.* <note>original Latin: Patefacta est autem (haeresis Bogomilorum) Alexio Imperatore nostro regnante, qui Dei Numine gubernatus, prudenti et admirabili ratione principem illius venatus est.</note>

From these words of Euthymius, compared with those mentioned immediately above, it is also established that the Bogomil heresy had already reached some maturity under the rule of Alexios, and thus preceded his reign by several years, though not very many. Anna Komnena left the same testimony, albeit somewhat doubtfully, in Book XV of the *Alexiad*, page 486. She says: "And as it seems, it existed even in the times before my father, but it was hidden." <note>original Greek: Καὶ ὡς ἔοικεν, ἦν μὲν κἀν τοῖς πρὸ τοὐμοῦ Πατρὸς χρόνοις, ἐλάνθανε δέ.</note>—which the scholar Posinus <note>Petrus Possinus (1609–1686), a Jesuit scholar and editor of Byzantine texts</note> interprets, looking more to the sense than the literal words: *that it is indeed likely that it had already been whispering, as if under the ashes, before my father’s time.* <note>original Latin: quod verisimile quidem est jam ante Patris mei tempora quasi sub cinere mussasse.</note> I omit similar passages from Kedrenos, Glykas, Blastares, and the *Typikon* of Saint Sabas <note>These are various Byzantine historians, canonists, and liturgical texts that discuss the Bogomils</note>. Nor should it seem strange that it did not [become known] at the same time at which it arose,

<meta>catchword: ta fuit,</meta>

<summary>This page explores the historical origins of the Bogomil heresy, noting that while it has roots in 4th-century sectarianism, it gained prominence and its specific name during the 12th-century reign of Byzantine Emperor Alexios Komnenos. The author cites several historical authorities, including Anna Komnena and Euthymius, to argue that the sect had been developing in secret for years before being officially "hunted down" and exposed.</summary>

<page-num>15</page-num>

->AND FATE.<-

...was such that it met the eyes of all, since, as we hinted at above in passing, this way of life had been established for them: that they should cover their plans and not rashly lay bare the intentions of their minds. You may understand this from the words of Anna <note>Anna Komnena, the Byzantine princess and historian</note> which immediately follow: <note>original Greek: δεινότατον γὰρ τὸ τῶν Βογομίλων γένος ἀρετὴν ὑποκρίνασθαι· καὶ τρίχα μὲν κοσμικὴν οὐκ ἂν ἴδοις Βογομιλίζουσαν, κέκρυπται δὲ τὸ κακὸν ὑπὸ τὸν Μανδύαν καὶ τὸ κουκούλιον. "For the race of Bogomils is most clever at feigning virtue; and you will not see a Bogomil-follower with worldly hair, for the evil is hidden under the mantle and the hood."</note> that is, according to the same translator: *For that sect especially affects the squalor of religious austerity and penance, and in that craftiness of simulation it takes the prize. Therefore, you would not easily see anyone from those long-haired ones following the secular fashion join the sect of the Bogomils: this plague hides itself much more willingly under the mantle and hood.*

Let me note in passing that the translation of the first words could have been prepared both more briefly and more appropriately to the mind of the writer in this way: *For the nation of the Bogomils, if any other, is most suited to put forward an appearance of virtue.* For this is what the word <term>deinotatos</term> <note>original Greek: δεινότατος; while often meaning "most terrible," in this context it implies being "most skilled" or "clever" at a task</note> means, the sense of which Posfinus <note>Likely referring to Petrus Possinus (Pierre Poussines), a 17th-century Jesuit scholar and translator of Byzantine texts</note> seems to have understood differently here than he should have. See the most elegant Perizonius <note>Jacobus Perizonius, a renowned Dutch classical scholar</note> on Aelian’s *Various History*, Book 1, and Jean Le Clerc <note>A famous Genevan theologian and critic</note> in the *Art of Criticism*, Volume 1, page 168 and following.

Furthermore, there is another reason why these men lay hidden for some time and indulged in their opinions with impunity: for that reason, too, was explained clearly enough by Anna. For the Emperor Alexios was so distracted by administering the affairs of the empire that, with all his cares turned toward them, he was forced—even against his will—to withdraw his mind from thoughts pertaining to sacred matters, until finally, having settled those affairs, he could think seriously about the business of religion. For thus Anna relates in the cited passage: <note>original Greek: ἀπάρτι γὰρ τὸ πολὺ τῆς τῶν ἑσπερίων καὶ ἑώων ἀποθέμενος φροντίδων πρὸς τὸ πνευματικώτερον ἑαυτὸν ἐπέτεινεν. "For having just now set aside the great mass of Western and Eastern cares, he directed himself more intensely toward more spiritual things."</note> Since, therefore, the Bogomils had no reason to fear for their affairs for this reason, it is easily understood that they gradually thought of unfolding the full resources of their doctrines and worked diligently to draw others into a fellowship of their opinions. From which it could not happen...

<meta>catchword: ...but that (quin)</meta>

<summary>This page describes the Bogomils' use of ascetic clothing and feigned piety to remain undetected in Byzantine society. It also explains that Emperor Alexios I Komnenos was too preoccupied with military and state affairs in the East and West to address the growing heresy until later in his reign.</summary>

# ON THE ORIGIN OF THE BOGOMILS

...from their fame spreading more widely, which finally seems to have filled every region. <margin>p. 4</margin> For this reason, Anna <margin>R</margin> <note>Anna Komnena, the daughter of Emperor Alexios I</note> says: *since the fame of the Bogomils had already been scattered everywhere.* <note>original Greek: ὡς δὲ ἡ τῶν Βογομίλων ἁπανταχοῦ ἤδη διέσπαρτο φήμη</note> <margin>238. Basel edition</margin> Zonaras <note>Joannes Zonaras, a 12th-century Byzantine chronicler</note> explains the same thing more clearly in the third volume of his *Annals*, when he records that Basilius, the leader of this unhappy sect, was occupied for fifty-two years in spreading and passing on to many others the depravity of his opinions, which he himself had absorbed over a period of fifteen years. *He,* (speaking of Basilius,) *having learned the destructive dogmas of error in fifteen years, and having taught them for fifty-two years, filled almost the entire inhabited world with his own ruin; then he also stayed in the ruling city.* <note>The "ruling city" refers to Constantinople. Original Greek: ὃς, ἐν ἔτεσι μὲν πεντεκαίδεκα τὰ τῆς πλάνης μαθὼν ὀλέθρια δόγματα, ἐν δὲ δυσὶ καὶ πεντήκοντα διδάξας αὐτὰ πᾶσαν σχεδὸν οἰκουμένην τῆς οἰκείας λύμης ἐνέπλησεν, εἶτα καὶ τῇ τῶν πόλεων βασιλευούσῃ ἐπεχωρίασεν.</note> In this passage, the word <term>oikoumene</term> <note>original Greek: οἰκουμένη; literally "the inhabited world"</note> should undoubtedly be understood to mean the regions subject to the Eastern Empire, much like the same word is thought by Borremansius in his *Vesperae* (Chapter 5) to have been used in Luke 2:1 regarding the Roman Empire. Weight is added by the testimony of Euthymius <note>Euthymios Zigabenos, a monk and theologian</note> which is read in his *Victory over the Massalians*: <margin>112.</margin> *the multi-named sect of the Massalians, or Bogomils, now prevails in every city, country, and province* <note>original Greek: ἡ πολυώνυμος τῶν Μασσαλιανῶν, εἴτουν Βογομίλων αἵρεσις ἐν πάσῃ πόλει, καὶ χώρᾳ καὶ ἐπαρχίᾳ ἐπιπολάζει τανῦν</note> that is: *That many-named sect of the Massalians or Bogomils, having been spread most widely, rules through all cities and all regions and prefectures.*

VII. From these things it remains that this band of men, estranged from the truth, had already taken root in the dwellings of the East by the 11th Century; these roots, having gradually taken on growth, reached a certain maturity at the beginning of the 12th Century, primarily through the efforts of Basilius. He was a crafty man, naturally inclined to contrive new things, and confirmed in this by industry and habit. Concerning his lineage and birth, there is nothing to be found in the records of contemporary writers, either because he was of very obscure origin and thus unworthy of mention, or because the authors of the histories thought that his nobility of birth (if there was any) had been diminished and obscured by the vices of his mind and teaching. Nevertheless, they did not [neglect] the character of the man and the very form of his body <unclear>t</unclear>

### AND FATES.
D
<meta>catchword: devoted,</meta>

and they remained silent regarding the nature of the studies he indulged in. Euthymius describes the man thus: *This was Basilius the physician, a man quite destructive, even a plague, full of corruption, and an instrument of every vice.* <note>original Greek: Βασίλειος ἦν οὗτος ὁ ἰατρὸς, ἀνὴρ ὀλέθριος μᾶλλον, καὶ λοιμὸς, καὶ φθορᾶς μεστὸς, καὶ πάσης κακίας ὄργανον.</note> Anna does not disagree, but confirms the same more eloquently on page 487, saying: *And finally, Basilius appears as the Chief Satrap of Satanaël, a Monk in dress, with a withered face, beardless chin, tall in stature, and most versatile in handling impiety.* <note>original Greek: καὶ δῆτα... ἀναφαίνεται τοῦ Σαταναὴλ Ἀρχισατράπης Βασίλειος... "Satanaël" was the name the Bogomils used for the Devil, whom they believed was the elder son of God. "Chief Satrap" is a term for a high-ranking governor or official.</note> That is, as translated by Allatius <note>Leone Allacci (1586–1669), a scholar and keeper of the Vatican Library</note> on page 639 of *On the Consensus of the Western and Eastern Church*: *And at last Basilius, the Chief Satrap of Satanaël, appears; a Monk in habit, gaunt of face, smooth-chinned, tall of stature, and most cunning in practicing impiety.* Finally, Zonaras gives his support, saying: *Of such a heresy, the herald and great teacher and proponent of their 'Apostles' was a certain man called Basilius, a physician, who wore the habit of a monk, but was himself clothed in Satan.* <note>original Greek: τῆς τοιαύτης αἱρέσεως... κήρυξ ἦν καὶ μέγας διδάσκαλος... Βασίλειός τις λεγόμενος... αὐτὸν δ᾽ ἐκεῖνον ἐνδεδυμένος τὸν Σατανᾶν.</note> One might reasonably conclude that the man's crafty nature, adapted for deceit—which we see attributed to him here—is evidenced by the fact that he presented himself in the habit of a Monk. No doubt he did this with the intention of feigning piety, so that he might more successfully worm his way into the minds of others, who expected only the best from a Monk. For it is manifest that Basilius did not approve of the rules of a solitary life consecrated to God, nor was he ever truly admitted into a society of Monks, but merely imitated that way of life. Perhaps for no other reason did he combine the art of healing with the study of divine doctrine than to pave a way for himself; by appearing to do something else—namely, taking care of the body—he could infect and corrupt the souls of men (who were less on their guard against such danger) with the stain of his most pestilential errors. Indeed, I can hardly be led to believe that Basilius devoted himself to the healing art with a fixed purpose...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Basilius (Basil the Physician), the leader of the Bogomils, and the rapid spread of the sect throughout the Byzantine Empire during the 11th and 12th centuries. It follows from the previous page's description of his character.</meta>

### ->ON THE ORIGIN OF THE BOGOMILS<-

...before their reputation spread more widely, which finally seems to have filled the entire region. Therefore, Anna <note>Anna Comnena (1083–1153), daughter of Emperor Alexios I and author of the *Alexiad*</note> says on page 48:
<margin>p. 48</margin>
*“since the fame of the Bogomils had already been spread everywhere.”* <note>original Greek: ὡς δὲ ἡ τῶν Βογομίλων ἁπανταχοῦ ἤδη διέσπαρτο φήμη.</note> Zonaras <note>Joannes Zonaras, a 12th-century Byzantine chronicler and theologian</note> explains the same matter more clearly in Volume III of his *Annals*, page 238 of the Basel edition, when he mentions that Basilius, the leader of this unfortunate sect, was engaged for fifty-two years in publicizing and passing on the depravity of his opinions—which he himself had absorbed over a period of fifteen years. He <note>Zonaras</note> says of Basilius: *“Who, having learned the destructive doctrines of error in fifteen years, and having taught them for fifty-two years, filled nearly the whole world with his own ruin, and then he also settled in the royal city.”* <note>original Greek: ὅς... ἐν ἔτεσι μὲν πεντεκαίδεκα τὰ τῆς πλάνης μαθὼν ὀλέθρια δόγματα, ἐν δὲ δυσὶ καὶ πεντήκοντα διδάξας αὐτὰ πᾶσαν σχεδὸν τὴν οἰκουμένην τῆς οἰκείας λύμης ἐνέπλησεν, εἶτα καὶ τῇ τῶν πόλεων βασιλίδι ἐπεχωρίασεν.</note> In this passage, the word **world** <term>oikoumenē (οἰκουμένη): literally 'the inhabited world,' often used specifically to refer to the Roman or Byzantine Empire</term> is undoubtedly to be understood as the regions subject to the Eastern Empire, no less than that same word in Luke 2:1 is thought to have been applied to the Roman Empire by Borremans <note>Engelbert Borremans (1641–1686), a Dutch theologian and scholar</note> in his *Vesperae*, Chapter V. Weight will be added by the testimony of Euthymius <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century monk commissioned by the Emperor to write against heresies</note>, which is read in his *Victory over the Messalians*, page 112: *“the many-named heresy of the Messalians, or rather the Bogomils, is now prevalent in every city, country, and province.”* <note>original Greek: ἡ πολυώνυμος τῶν Μαſſαλιανῶν, εἴτ’ οὖν Βογομίλων αἵρεσις ἐν πάσῃ πόλει, καὶ χώρᾳ καὶ ἐπαρχίᾳ ἐπιπολάζει τανῦν</note> That is: *That multi-named sect of the Messalians or Bogomils, spread most widely through all cities and all regions and districts, holds sway.*

**VII.** From these accounts, it is evident that this band of men, estranged from the truth, had already taken root in the lands of the East in the 11th century; having gradually increased in strength, they reached a certain maturity at the beginning of the 12th century, primarily through the efforts of **Basilius**. He was a crafty man, almost naturally prepared for contriving new things, and further strengthened by his own industry and habit. Concerning his lineage and birth, there is nothing to be found in the records of contemporary writers; this is either because he was of very obscure origin and thus unworthy of mention, or because the historians thought that the nobility of his lineage (if there was any) was diminished and obscured by the vices of his mind and his teaching. Nor, however, did they [neglect] the man's character and the very form of his body...

### ->AND FATE<-
**D**

...and they remained silent about the reasoning behind the studies in which he indulged. Euthymius <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century monk and theologian who wrote the *Panoplia Dogmatica*</note> describes the man in this way: *“This was Basilius the Physician, a man more destructive, even a plague, full of corruption, and an instrument of every vice.”* <note>original Greek: Βασίλειος ἦν οὗτος ὁ ἰατρὸς... original Latin: Hic erat Basilius quidam Medicus, homo perniciosus...</note> Anna <note>Anna Comnena, daughter of Emperor Alexios I</note> does not disagree, but confirms the same more eloquently on page 487: *“And indeed,”* she says, *“there appears the Chief Satrap <term>Archisatrapēs (Ἀρχισατράπης): a high-ranking official or governor; here used mockingly by the author to denote a high-ranking servant of the devil</term> of Satanael <note>The Bogomils believed Satanael was the elder son of God who fell from grace and created the material world</note>, Basilius, a Monk by his clothing, with an emaciated face, beardless, tall in stature, and most versatile in practicing impiety.”* <note>original Greek: καὶ δῆτα... ἀναφαίνεται τοῦ Σαταναὴλ Ἀρχισατράπης Βασίλειος...</note> This is interpreted by Allatius <note>Leone Allacci (c. 1586–1669), a scholar and librarian of the Vatican who sought to reconcile the Greek and Latin churches</note> on page 639 of *On the Agreement of the Western and Eastern Church*: *“And at last Basilius appears, the Chief Satrap of Satanael, a monk by habit, lean in face, smooth of chin, tall in height, and most clever at handling impiety.”* Finally, Zonaras <note>Joannes Zonaras, a 12th-century Byzantine chronicler</note> supports this, saying: *“Of such a heresy, the herald and great teacher and the leader of those they call 'Apostles' was a certain man named Basilius, a physician, wearing the habit of a monk, but inwardly clothed in Satan himself.”* <note>original Greek: τῆς τοιαύτης αἱρέσεως... κῆρυξ ἦν καὶ μέγας διδάσκαλος...</note>

From this, one might not pointlessly infer the crafty nature of the man, shaped for deceit, which we see attributed to him here, even from the fact that he presented himself to be seen in the habit of a Monk. Doubtless, he did this with the intention of feigning piety so that he might more successfully worm his way into the minds of others, who expected only the best things from a Monk. For it is manifest that Basilius did not approve of the regulations of a solitary life dedicated to God, nor was he ever truly admitted into the fellowship of Monks, but rather he only simulated that way of life. Perhaps also for no other reason did he join the art of healing with the study of divine doctrine than to pave a way for himself by which—while appearing to do something else and undertaking the care of the body—he might infect and corrupt the souls of men (who were less on their guard against danger) with the stain of most pestilent errors. For I am hardly led to believe that Basilius devoted himself to the healing art with a fixed purpose...

<meta>This page continues the analysis of the life and influences of Basilius (Basil the Physician), the Bogomil leader. It explores the theory that his medical background and outward asceticism were a deceptive "wolf in sheep's clothing" modeled after sects like the Essenes, and suggests his heresy was derived from reading Manichaean texts rather than direct instruction.</meta>

### ->ON THE ORIGIN OF THE BOGOMILS<-

...[he had] given, since from the cited passage of Zonaras <note>Joannes Zonaras, a 12th-century Byzantine chronicler and theologian</note> it is clear to me that he spent fifteen whole years devoted to grasping false doctrine, and that this plan lived primarily in his mind: that he might one day be able to stir up new troubles in the Church. For this reason, our dear friend Winckler <note>Johann Winckler (1646–1705), a German Lutheran theologian</note>, in his dissertation on the Bogomils, interprets Basil's practice not without reason according to the customs of the Essenes <note>An ascetic Jewish sect known for communal living and strict discipline</note>. Although the Essenes labored most especially and seriously in cultivating sacred studies, they nevertheless spent several hours daily in the practice of medicine; they were thus called by the name of *Therapeutae* <note>original Greek: θεραπευτῶν; meaning 'healers' or 'attendants'</note> and those of the female sex, *Therapeutrides* <note>original Greek: θεραπευτρίδων</note>. Thus, however beautiful a form he displayed in his external appearance, he seemed just as deformed in soul to Zonaras, who testifies that he had "put on" Satan himself. Zonaras used a similar manner of speaking to that with which Euthymius <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, the monk mentioned previously</note> outlined the crafty and deceitful character of all the Bogomils on page 112 of the *Insignia Itinerarii* of Tollius <note>Jacobus Tollius (1633–1696), a Dutch classicist and traveler</note>, where he says **"that they hide the wolf with the fleece,"** <note>original Greek: ὅτι τῷ κωδίῳ τὸν λύκον κρύπτουσι</note> meaning they cover the wolf with a lamb’s skin. Indeed, you may see that heretics were called by the name of "wolves" by the earliest Doctors of the Church, a point which Thomas Milles <note>An 18th-century scholar and bishop known for his edition of the works of Cyril of Jerusalem</note> proved among others in his notes on Cyril, page 45.

**VIII.** Equipped with these character traits, Basil was truly suited to win some appearance of truth for his error. It is rightly thought that he drew this error not so much from the living instruction of others as from the study of books written by the Manichaeans <note>Followers of Manichaeism, a dualistic religion that influenced many medieval heresies</note> and the Massalians <note>A sect also known as the Euchites, accused of believing in a personal demon that must be expelled through prayer</note>. For no mention is made of any teacher by Zonaras, who, in the place mentioned above, most clearly of all explained the beginnings of the doctrine first conceived and then preached by Basil. Therefore, relying on his own wings and propelled by the strength of his own intellect, he built upon the foundations of Christian doctrine—which perhaps had been laid only in passing—those ideas suggested by the rash and ill-advised reading of most pestilential books. By this very act, he provided a sad example of a most unhappy—

### ->AND DESTINIES<-

<note>The header "ET FATIS" is the conclusion of a title likely reading "ON THE ORIGIN AND DESTINIES" of the sect.</note>

...[most certain examples] of self-taught knowledge <note>original Greek: αὐτοδιδαχίας</note> in divine doctrine he left to posterity. He himself, who was perhaps the disciple of no living teacher, was nevertheless stung by the gadfly of ambition and immediately thought of gathering and closely attaching disciples to himself. To this end, he chose individuals from both sexes to admit into the inner knowledge of his teachings. In this plan, he again decided—as he had in other matters—to follow in the footsteps of the **Manichaeans** <note>A major dualist religion that influenced later medieval heresies</note>, choosing exactly twelve disciples. These he honored with the title of **Apostles** and employed them as the heralds of his doctrine. It is clear that Mani <note>The 3rd-century founder of Manichaeism</note> also favored this same number, as seen in **Augustine’s** *On Heresies*, chapter 46, and more explicitly in **Peter Siculus’s** *History of the Manichaeans*, page 30, which lists each one by name. Compare also the illustrious **Fabricius’s** *Apocryphal Codex of the New Testament*, page 140. Just as many followers and companions of his inner circle are attributed to Basil by **Anna Comnena** <note>Byzantine princess and historian, author of the *Alexiad*</note> on page 487, whose words are these:

*For a certain Basil, a Monk, laboring [to bring forth] and having twelve disciples, whom he also called apostles, also drawing along certain wicked and most evil little women as disciples, spread his malice everywhere.* <note>original Greek: Βασίλειος γάρ τις Μοναχὸς ὠδίνων δώδεκα μὲν ἔχων μαθητὰς, οὓς καὶ ἀποστόλους ὠνόμαζε, συνεφελκόμενος δὲ καὶ μαθητρίας τινὰ γύναια κακοήθη καὶ παμπόνηρα ἐφήπλωσε τὴν κακίαν ἁπανταχοῦ.</note>

This is interpreted by **Allatius** <note>Leo Allatius (1586–1669), a scholar and librarian of the Vatican</note> on page 639 of *On the Consensus of the Eastern and Western Church* as follows: *For a certain Basil, a Monk in his impiety, having twelve disciples whom he also named Apostles, and bringing with him certain women of depraved and most wicked character as disciples, poured out his evil through all parts.*

Here, in passing, I note that Allatius—instead of what **Possinus** <note>Petrus Possinus (1601–1686), a Jesuit scholar and editor</note> has as "laboring" <note>original: ὠδίνων</note>, which is the reading I have followed—reads "impiety" <note>original: ἀσέβειαν</note>, which he also expressed in his translation, though it entirely clashes with the nature of the language. Possinus’s own reading must also be slightly changed, so that it is read as *laboring*—that is, "giving birth" to an impious dogma, which he desired to bring forth like a conceived fetus. Indeed, if I see clearly, the true reading will finally be restored if we join the versions of Possinus and Allatius together in this way: *For a certain Basil, a monk, laboring with impiety.* For in this way the expression <note>original: Φράσις</note> will be complete, just as **Paul** used it in the **Epistle to the Galatians 4:19**, so that the sense is that Basil attempted to bring forth the conceived fetus of impiety.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Basil the Monk (or the Physician), the leader of the Bogomil sect. It moves from his imitation of the twelve Apostles to the historical account of how his inner circle was exposed by the Byzantine Emperor Alexios I Komnenos.</meta>

### ->ON THE ORIGIN OF THE BOGOMILS<-

Of these twelve disciples of Basil, none are found mentioned by name in the writings of the ancients, although, according to the testimony of **Anna** <note>Princess Anna Comnena, daughter of the Byzantine Emperor and author of the *Alexiad*</note>, a certain **Diblatius**, a follower of the Bogomils, was arrested by the Emperor’s command and, forced by interrogation, revealed the names of those "Apostles." Because Basil judged that the efforts of women as well as men would be useful to him, he fulfilled the very measure of a heretic defined by **Saint Paul** in 2 Timothy 3:6, where it is said of those who put on a show of piety that they are those *who creep into houses and lead captive silly women* <note>original Greek: ἐνδύνοντες εἰς τὰς οἰκίας, καὶ αἰχμαλωτεύοντες τὰ γυναικάρια</note>. Compare also the notes of the famous **Grabe** on **Irenaeus**, page 101.

IX. Thus, when Basil, equipped with these resources, was strenuously pushing his design and the evil was spreading further—no longer confined to the common people but, as Anna records on page 490, seeping like a lethal poison into the more noble families—the Emperor [Alexios I Komnenos] began to feel, partly by anticipation and partly by observing the reality, the most immediate danger to the purity of the Church. He decided that the cause of religion had to be handled with greater care. The same intellectual strength, sharpness of judgment, and constant persistence in accomplishing tasks that his daughter Anna had praised in all parts of the *Alexiad* (page 487), he did not allow to be lacking at this moment. Instead, he successfully frustrated all the cunning of these most crafty men with his own wisdom. He deemed his first task to be dragging the arch-heretic <term>haeresiarcha</term> <note>A leader or founder of a heretical movement</note> himself and his wretched companions out from their hiding places into the light; and the outcome did not fail his expectations. For some of them were captured as desired and thrown into prison, and through the use of severe interrogation and torture, they satisfied the Royal will. Anna explains these things in her usual diligent and eloquent manner on page 487:

> "The Emperor’s soul could not endure it," *she says*, "and for that reason he investigated the matters of the heresy; some of the Bogomils were brought to the palace, and they all denounced a certain **Basil** as the teacher and chief leader of the **Bogomil** heresy." <note>original Greek: Οὐκ ἠνείχετο... ἡ τοῦ Βασιλέως ψυχή, τῷ τοι καὶ διερευνᾷ τὰ περὶ τῆς αἱρέσεως, καί τινες τῶν βογομίλων εἰς τὰ ἀνάκτορα ἤγοντο, ἅπαντες δὲ Βασίλειόν τινα κατήγγειλον διδάσκαλον καὶ κορυφαῖον πρωτοστάτην τῆς Βογομιλικῆς αἱρέσεως.</note>

### ->AND FATES<-

...[of the heresy]. <note>original Greek: σεως</note> From these followers, a certain **Diblatius** was seized. When he was questioned, he was initially unwilling to confess; however, after being handed over to tortures, he then confessed the name of the man called **Basil**, as well as those whom Basil had appointed as his "apostles." Therefore, the Emperor entrusted the search for this man to many people <note>The translator notes that based on the scholar Allatius, the Greek should read "pollois" (to many) rather than "pollos" as found in Possinus's edition</note>. And indeed, **Basil**, the "chief satrap" of **Satanael** <note>The Bogomils believed Satanael was the elder, fallen son of God and the creator of the material world</note>, was finally revealed. 

Once the man was brought to light in this manner, the Emperor pursued his plan. Understanding that Basil had adopted certain behaviors—which were otherwise common among heretics—as a kind of protective shield, and that he would not recklessly reveal his true inner thoughts, the Emperor approached the man with flatteries. He summoned him under the pretext of great goodwill and, using many words of praise, soothed him, thereby making the man completely submissive to him. I have **Euthymius** <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century monk commissioned by the Emperor to document these heresies</note> as my authority for this account: 

"And having brought Basil to himself," he says, "and having received him with honor, the Emperor deemed him worthy of a seat beside him. He shared pleasant conversation and friendly discourse with him, using a sweet grace in their dialogue. By cleverly pretending to be a student, the Emperor more easily deceived the one who had deceived so many others to their destruction. With sharpness of mind <note>The "Fellian" manuscript of this text contains a slight scribal error in the Greek word for 'sharpness' (ὀξύτητι)</note> and natural skill, he tracked down all the wickedness lurking within this rotten and detestable old man, leaving nothing unsearched or unseen. From there, as if dragging all sorts of diverse and multi-formed beasts out of the dark lair of the man's heart, the Emperor drew out his filthy, venomous, and many-faced dogmas through the machinery of his hypocrisy. Having very skillfully outwitted the old fool—that ancient evil—the Emperor commanded us to commit these things to writing and to triumph over the ridiculous mysteries of this mentioned heresy." <note>original Greek: Καὶ πρὸς ἑαυτὸν... τῆς εἰρημένης αἰρέσεως</note>

The meaning of the passage comes down to this: that the Emperor, composing his words and his facial expression to show kindness and grace, surrendered himself as if to the teaching of Basil. By doing this, he became the catalyst for Basil to vomit forth his impure doctrines with a full mouth. The same...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the ruse used by Emperor Alexios I Komnenos to extract a confession from Basil the Bogomil, drawing parallels with historical precedents of using deception to expose heretical teachings.</meta>

## ON THE ORIGIN OF THE BOGOMILS

**Anna** <note>Anna Komnene, the daughter of Emperor Alexios and author of the *Alexiad*</note> relates the story in almost the same words; for the sake of brevity, we will not recount it all here, but only bring forward those details missing from **Euthymius’s** narrative. This illustrious woman reports that **Isaac the Sebastocrator**, the Emperor's brother, feigned the same [interest in the sect], and that **Alexios** addressed the man with these words: "I admire you for your virtue, and I ask you to learn what has been introduced concerning your honor, since our own ways are merely base and bring no virtue." <note>original Greek: ἄγαμαί σε τῆς ἀρετῆς ἕνεκα, ἀξιῶ δέ σε καταμαθεῖν, τίνα τὰ περὶ τῆς σῆς τιμιότητος εἰσηγμένα, ὡς τάγε μὲν τῶν ἡμετέρων μόνον φαῦλα καὶ οὐδεμίαν ἀρετὴν φέροντα.</note> Add to this the account of **Zonaras**, page 238.

The same method by which **Alexios** stripped the mask from **Basil** is found to have been used by many of the ancients, specifically **Flavian, Archbishop of Antioch**, when he invited **Adelphius**, the leader of the **Massalians** <note>An ascetic sect from the 4th century also known as Euchites, often accused of similar errors as the Bogomils</note>, to a profession of his doctrines. **Theodoret** describes a matter entirely similar to our own in his *Ecclesiastical History*, Book IV, Chapter 10. Nor did **Flavian** achieve what he sought with any less success by approaching the man in this way. For **Theodoret** adds: "Charmed by these words, that old man vomited out all the hidden poison." <note>original Greek: Τούτοις ὁ πρεσβύτης ἐκεῖνος τοῖς λόγοις καταθελχθεὶς, ἐξήμεσεν ἅπαντα τὸν κεκρυμμένον ἰόν.</note> *That is: "Charmed by these words, that old man vomited out the entire hidden poison."* <note>original Latin: h. e. His verbis senex ille delinitus latens venenum totum evomuit</note> 

As I mention this in passing, this is a phrasing <note>term: phrasing (Greek: φράσει)</note> used not only by **Euthymius** in his *Victory over the Massalians* of his own age (where on page 116 he calls them "those who vomited out the poison of such a heresy" <note>original Latin: τὰς τὸν ἰὸν τῆς τοιάυτης αἱρέσεως ἐξεμέσαντας</note>), but **Anna** and **Zonaras** also applied it to **Basil**. The former reports that he "vomited out the melancholy of his demonic possession" <note>original Greek: δαιμονιῶντα τὴν μελαγχολίαν ἑαυτῦ ἐξεμέσαι</note>, while the latter says he "vomited out the poison of wickedness." <note>original Greek: τὸν ἰὸν τῆς πονηρίας ἐξεμέσα</note> Indeed, you will frequently observe that the Doctors of the Church compare the heretics of the early Church to venomous serpents. See **Irenaeus**, Book IV, Chapter 1, and those whom **Feuardentius** praises in his notes.

Since, therefore, one must counter their cunning—which is harmful to many—with a circumspect prudence that averts danger from the simple-minded (even following the precept of Christ himself), I do not see by what right the counsel of either **Alexios** or **Flavian** can be rebuked as being full of fraud and deceit. This is a charge that **Gottfried Arnold** <note>A German Pietist historian known for his sympathetic view of "heretics"</note> shamelessly <note>The catchword "denter" at the bottom of the page completes the Latin word "impudenter," meaning shamelessly or impudently.</note> levels against **Flavian** in his *History of Heresies*, Part 1, page 206...

<meta>catchword: shamelessly</meta>

<summary>This page compares Emperor Alexios's use of deception to uncover the Bogomil leader Basil with earlier historical examples, such as Archbishop Flavian’s interrogation of the Massalians, defending the tactic as a necessary pastoral precaution.</summary>
<keywords>Alexios I Komnenos, Anna Komnene, Basil the Bogomil, Flavian of Antioch, Massalians, Euthymius Zigabenus, religious deception, serpent metaphor, Gottfried Arnold, ecclesiastical history</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the description of how Emperor Alexios I Komnenos successfully deceived Basil the Bogomil into revealing his secret teachings. It draws parallels between this event and historical instances where heretics used secrecy to hide their true beliefs, and concludes with the specific architectural ruse—a hidden scribe behind a curtain—used to document the heresy for trial.</meta>

### AND THE FATES.

...prudently, but in his own manner, he pronounced it. Certainly, if wise men concerned for the salvation of the Church had not dealt with them in this way, I believe their impure opinions would never have been clearly revealed to the eyes of others. For it is most true what the author of the *Philosophumena* <note>The *Philosophumena* (or *Refutation of All Heresies*) was historically attributed to Origen, though modern scholars assign it to Hippolytus of Rome</note>—which circulates under the name of **Origen**—notes on page 6 of the Hamburg edition: that heretics, through their silence and the concealment of their unspeakable tenets, achieve the appearance of following the Divine Will with true worship.

**X.** I believe the same kind of success would have befallen **Basil** and his followers, had they not been wisely drawn out from their hiding places to expose their own shame to the rejection of others, as will become more fully clear from what follows. For **Anna** <note>Anna Komnene, the historian and daughter of Emperor Alexios I</note> reports on page 488 that **Basil**, having equivocated for some time, was finally persuaded and sought a more convenient opportunity to discuss his views. In this, **Basil** imitated the **Donatists** <note>A 4th-century schismatic Christian group in North Africa known for their rigorous and often isolationist views</note> and others like them, who, as the ancients record in **B. Ittigius’s** *Appendix on the Heresiarchs* (p. 339), anxiously avoided every opportunity to compare their views with the Orthodox. She says <note>original Greek: ὁ δὲ... τὰ πρῶτα μὲν ἐσχηματίζετο, καὶ τὴν λεοντῆν ὁ κατὰ ἀλήθειαν ὄνος ἐφείλκεν εἰς ἑαυτὸν πανταχόθεν, καὶ ἀπεπήδα πρὸς τὰ λεγόμενα. πλὴν ἐχαυνώθη τοῖς ἐπαίνοις, καὶ γὰρ ὁμοδίαιτον ἐποίησε τοῦτον.</note>: "At first he put on a facade, and like the proverbial ass in a lion’s skin, he drew attention to himself from all sides, and would leap away from what was being said. However, he was puffed up by praises, for [the Emperor] even made him a companion at his table." (I prefer to read this as *epheilken* [drew toward himself] following **Possinus**, rather than *apheilken* [stripped off] as **Allatius** <note>Leo Allatius and Pierre Poussines (Possinus) were 17th-century scholars who edited Byzantine texts</note> does, as the sense requires it). **Anna** continues: "The Emperor's cousin and **Sebastocrator** <note>A high-ranking court title, usually reserved for the Emperor's close relatives</note> was also present with him in all things and assisted in the drama. Then, he [Basil] vomited out the dogmas of his heresy." The Emperor humored the man and saw to it that he was led into a secluded part of the palace. However, before speaking with him, he secretly positioned a **Notary** <note>A legal scribe or secretary trained to take rapid dictation</note> who, sitting behind an unfolded curtain, recorded with his pen everything that the impure mouth uttered. **Anna** continues: "And what was the manner [of this]? For there was a curtain between both the..."

### ON THE ORIGIN OF THE BOGOMILS

<note>original Greek: "γυναικωνῖτιν ἀπετείχιζε..."</note> ...he partitioned off the women’s quarters, and the Emperors sat with this abominable man as he poured out everything he held in his soul, speaking quite openly. A scribe recorded everything being said from behind a curtain; while that foolish teacher believed he was truly making a convert, the Emperor was merely feigning a desire for instruction. The scribe recorded the details of the teaching, and that "God-struck" man <note>The text notes a scholarly disagreement on the Greek term: Possinus reads it as one word, while Allatius reads it as "God-struck" (Theoplegeis)</note> brought together every point, both the speakable and the unspeakable, and spared no part of his God-hated dogma.

**XI.** Here at last the Emperor dropped his persona; he who had until now acted as a disciple now acted as a judge. Once the veil was pulled back, he revealed the situation as it had actually occurred. Admitting the Senate and the Patriarch of that time, **Nicholas**, into the inner chamber, he ordered that **Basil’s** tenets, which had been captured by the pen, be read aloud. **Anna** <note>Anna Komnene, the Emperor's daughter and historian</note> narrates the event thus: <note>original Greek: "ἀλλὰ τί τὸ ἐντεῦθεν;..."</note> "But what followed? The Emperor threw off the mask and revealed the plot; the entire Senate had been assembled, the military rank-and-file gathered, and the high council of the Church was present. Presiding then over the Imperial City <note>The text uses "CPolis" as an abbreviation for Constantinople; the scholar Ezechiel Spanhemius is cited here regarding the history of this name on ancient coins</note> was the most blessed among patriarchs, the scholar Lord **Nicholas** <note>Nicholas III Grammatikos, Patriarch of Constantinople from 1084–1111</note>. The God-hated dogmas were read out, and the evidence was unassailable."

**Basil**, now feeling himself trapped, transformed the modesty he had previously feigned into a stubbornness of spirit. Since he could not deny or distance himself from his dogmas without disgrace, he had the audacity to defend them as if they were consistent with the truth. For his confession had been far too clear—so much so that it could not be softened by clever interpretation, which **Basil** otherwise seemed likely to have attempted for the sake of others...

<meta>This page continues the account of the trial of Basil the Bogomil by Emperor Alexios I Komnenos, following the secret recording of Basil's heretical doctrines. It focuses on Basil's refusal to recant despite threats and his eventual imprisonment.</meta>

->### AND FATES<-

...the example of heretics, who wish to seem to have expressed the opinion held by the Church, but through different words. Basil approached the matter by another path, and with no small measure of impudence <note>meaning boldness or shamelessness</note> he strove to reconcile his own opinions with the truth. For **Anna** <note>Anna Komnene, the Emperor's daughter and historian</note> teaches this on page 489: <note>original Greek: "Καὶ οὐδὲ ὁ τῆς ἐναντίας μοίρας ἔξαρνος ἦν..."</note> "And he did not deny being on the opposing side, but immediately and with 'bare head' <note>a Greek idiom meaning 'openly' or 'boldly'</note> proceeded to contradictions; he promised to stand in battle array against fire, whips, and ten thousand deaths" (on this point, **Allatius** <note>Leo Allatius, a 17th-century scholar</note> reads the text more correctly than **Possinus**, who has "to have stood"). And after a few intervening sentences: "While all of them were many, reproaching his impiety and those who had shared in his destruction, this same **Basil** remained unswerving—a most noble Bogomil. Though a pyre and other torments were threatened, he held fast to the demon and embraced his **Satanael**." <note>Satanael: In Bogomil belief, the elder son of God who rebelled and created the material world; the suffix "-el" was later stripped from his name to show he had lost his divinity.</note>

Nevertheless, the Emperor did not allow himself to be led to strike the man’s stubbornness in error with a thunderbolt <note>meaning a swift execution or divine judgment</note>, but after an interval of time had passed, he gave him the free power to return to the right path. **Anna** says: <note>original Greek: "Ἔμφρουρος δὲ..."</note> "Having been imprisoned many times, and being sent for by the Emperor, and many times being exhorted to abjure <note>renounce</note> his impiety, he remained the same toward the Emperor’s pleas." He was therefore handed over to a certain custody or, as she puts it, "a small house situated somewhere near the imperial residences, just recently constructed for him." He is said to have been vexed by evil spirits and pelted with stones, which **Anna** explains in many words, but is omitted here by us through a fixed plan, as we are eager for brevity. However, this was not to be kept entirely silent here, since such a new example of divine wrath is offered to us—demonstrated by an external and manifest argument against stubborn men—just as the records of Sacred Antiquities report similar things happened to other men of this same sort. **Pierre Bayle** <note>A famous French Enlightenment philosopher known for his skeptical "Historical and Critical Dictionary"</note> in his Dictionary has elsewhere sustained the effort to call such accounts into doubt, along with others, beyond all reason. See him in Volume III, page 2212, and Volume I, pages 590 and 847.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the trial of the Bogomil leader Basil by Emperor Alexios I Komnenos. While the previous page described the Emperor’s initial attempts to persuade Basil to recant, this section details the Emperor's frustration with the growing influence of the sect and his dramatic plan to separate the true believers from the heretics using a trial by fire.</meta>

# ON THE ORIGIN OF THE BOGOMILS

XII. As an interval of time passed in this way, the expectation of a successful outcome regarding his reform did not bear fruit; instead, the evil crept further every day. Basil’s own stubbornness <note>original Latin: *pertinacia*</note> increased, and his followers interpreted it as a sign of his steadfastness, confirming them in their errors or even drawing them closer to him. The Emperor therefore thought it wise to block the path of this spreading evil. With this plan, he ordered that both the twelve disciples of Basil and the others who had joined the same sect be sought out from every quarter and led by many words to recognize the depravity of their opinions. "For the Emperor," says **Anna** at the end of page 490, <note>Anna Komnene, *The Alexiad*</note> "summoning from all over the earth the disciples and fellow-initiates of Basil, and especially the so-called twelve disciples, tested their convictions. And there were many secret disciples of Basil. For the evil had struck deep, and the horror had reached the greatest and most numerous masses." <note>original Greek: "Ὁ γὰρ αὐτοκράτωρ... τὰς ἀπανταχοῦ γῆς μαθητὰς καὶ συμμύτας τῷ Βασιλείῳ μεταπεμψάμενος, καὶ μάλιστα τοὺς δώδεκα λεγομένους μαθητὰς ἀπεπειρᾶτο καὶ τούτων τῆς γνώμης· Καὶ ἦσαν πολλοὶ κρυφιοὶ μαθηταὶ Βασιλείῳ. Καὶ γὰρ ἐνεβόθυνε τὸ κακόν, καὶ εἰς γένη μέγιστα καὶ πολλῷ πλήθει ἥψατο τὸ δεινόν."</note> Once those who were accused—rightly or wrongly—of this crime were gathered, a sentence of death by the pyre <note>term: pyre (a large wooden structure for burning a body as part of an execution)</note> was pronounced upon every one of them. 

To ensure that the innocent were not subjected to the same punishment as the wicked, and that a guilty person did not escape deserved punishment through deception—especially since there were many among the accused who used every means to shake off the suspicion of heresy—a new invention was needed to cut off every opportunity for trickery or deceit on both sides. He ordered the Civil and Ecclesiastical Senate to be convened, and in their presence, he had each of the Bogomils interrogated again as to which side they belonged. When this was done, with some professing the doctrine of Christ and others that of Basil, he ordered two furnaces <note>term: furnaces (here referring to massive bonfires prepared for the execution test)</note> to be prepared, one of which was marked with a prominent cross, while the other was marked with no sign at all. Furthermore, a choice was given to each of those suspected of the religion to approach one or the other of them, with no hope of mercy offered; thus, if anyone fled to the one displaying the form of the cross, they would be seen as making a profession of Christian doctrine...

<meta>This page continues the account of Emperor Alexios I Komnenos’s interrogation of the Bogomil sect. Having gathered the leaders and followers, the Emperor now moves from verbal persuasion to a dramatic psychological test—a staged trial by fire—to distinguish the unrepentant heretics from those who were falsely accused or willing to return to the Church.</meta>

->### AND FATE.<-

...it was believed he would seal it with blood, or if he joined that other group, he would suffer the well-deserved penalties for impiety. These are the words of **Anna** <note>Anna Komnene, the Emperor's daughter and historian, writing in the *Alexiad*</note>: "At that time, many members of the Senate and the Holy Synod <note>The council of high-ranking bishops</note> were present, as well as those among the **Naziraeans** (these are the Monks, who are elsewhere called *Naziraeans* or *Nazoraeans*: see **du Fresne’s** *Glossary* page 983 and page 422 regarding this passage of the *Alexiad*) who were selected for their learning. When all those accused of the Bogomil heresy had been brought together into the center, the Emperor ordered each one to be questioned again. While some confessed to being Bogomils and clung firmly to their heresy, others denied it entirely, calling themselves Christians; even when accused by others, they would not admit to it. Frowning at them <note>original Greek: ἐπιστοξέυσας αὐτοῖς τὰς ὀφρῦς, literally 'shooting his eyebrows at them'</note>, he said: 'Today, two furnaces must be lit; in one of these, let a cross be fixed in the ground. Then, a choice shall be given to all, so that those who wish to die today in the Christian faith may separate themselves from the others and approach the furnace with the cross. But those who cling to the Bogomil heresy shall be cast into the other. For it is better that they die as Christians than be persecuted while living as Bogomils and trouble the consciences of many. Go now, and let each of you proceed wherever you wish.'" This was a discovery truly worthy of the Emperor’s wisdom, the like of which scarcely exists in the records of the ancient Church. Nor should this plan be considered full of deceit, because, though it was framed as a clever pretense <note>original Latin: *ingenua simulatione*</note>, it was intended for the benefit of the majority.

XIII. Nor did the Emperor fail to achieve the goal he had set for himself. Two furnaces had been constructed, separated by a certain distance, yet both located in the <term>Zucanistrium</term> <note>A stadium or court used for playing a Byzantine version of polo</note>, the place where they were accustomed to play at ball on horseback. For this is how **Constantine** <note>Emperor Constantine VII Porphyrogenitus</note> explains this name in the *Life of Basil*, chapter 55, edited by **Allatius**: "the royal courtyard, in which it is customary for Emperors and the sons of the nobility to play ball from horseback." Combine this with the notes of **Charles du Fresne** regarding this...

<meta>This page continues the account of Emperor Alexios I Komnenos’s psychological test for the Bogomil heretics. On the previous page, the Emperor set up two fires—one with a cross and one without—to force the accused to reveal their true loyalties.</meta>

# ON THE ORIGIN OF THE BOGOMILS

Refer to this passage in **Anna**, as well as *Dissertation VIII* on **Joinville**, and Book II of *Christian Constantinople*, section IV, number 30. Both furnaces were lit seven times with fire, if we believe **Anna** <note>Anna Komnene, the historian and daughter of the Emperor</note>: <note>original Greek: Κάμινοι δὲ τηνικαῦτα ἑπταπλασίως κατὰ τὸν μελῳδὸν ἀνήπτοντο εἰς τὸ ὁτωσíπω καλέμενον τζυκανιστήριον</note> "At that time, the furnaces were lit sevenfold, according to the singer, in the place called the *Tzykanisterion*" <note>A stadium or polo ground near the Great Palace of Constantinople</note>. In this passage, by the name "the singer" <note>original: μελῳδῷ</note>, she undoubtedly refers to **David**, the holy prophet, who in Psalm 12:7 mentions silver subjected seven times to the trial of fire. I prefer to interpret **Anna’s** meaning in this way, rather than following **Charles du Fresne** <note>The 17th-century scholar also known as Du Cange</note>, a man otherwise of skillful intellect, who in his note on this passage (page 422) thought that **Cosmas, Bishop of Maiuma**, was intended. For although **Cosmas** is usually distinguished by later Greek writers with the name "the singer" or *The Modulator* <note>original: Modulatoris</note> as a title of excellence <note>original: κατ’ ἐξοχὴν</note>—as the same author rightly noted on page 902 of his *Glossary*—I do not understand for what purpose **Anna** could have appealed to him here. On the other hand, since **David**, inspired by the Divine Spirit to write hymns, bears the same name—as **Du Fresne** himself proved from a famous passage of **Chrysostom** on page 902 of his *Glossary*—and since David's passage, cited above, applies here perfectly, I have no doubt that **Anna** is primarily referring to him.

With matters thus arranged, they departed to their respective sides. Some joined themselves to the furnace that was to be the token of the Christians; but others, stubbornly defending the sect of **Basilius** <note>Basil the Physician, the leader of the Bogomils</note>, turned toward the other. Those who were present raised a cry to heaven, pitying the lot of the Christians <note>The onlookers believed the Christians were actually going to be burned</note>. However, the Emperor soon dismissed the Christians, having praised them magnificently and encouraged them to guard the purity of their doctrine in the future. As for the others, who were most fully convicted of heresy, he did not subject them to the stated punishment <note>the fire</note>. Instead, having separated those twelve disciples of **Basilius** as if they were the "front-line leaders" <note>term: *primipilis* - a Roman military term for the highest-ranking centurions, here used for the inner circle</note> from the rest of the group, he ordered the remainder to be taken into various forms of custody. He then sought to persuade them to renounce their impure doctrine, sometimes through others and sometimes personally. And indeed, the power of truth was so great among some of them that they felt grief for their errors,

<meta>This page continues the account of Emperor Alexios I Komnenos’s treatment of the Bogomil heretics. ->AND FATES.<-

...[they would be] persuaded, and thus released from custody; just as, on the other hand, others had fortified their hearts with such stubbornness that no weight of arguments was great enough to break and overcome those who resisted. Among other things, which we omit here by deliberate plan, Anna <note>Anna Komnene, the historian and daughter of the Emperor</note> uses these words: 

"Then, visiting them every day, he taught some in person, offering much advice to abandon their foul worship <note>original Greek: μυσαρᾶς ἀποσχέσθαι θρησκείας</note>. As for the others, he appointed certain select men from the sacred ranks of the Church to visit them every day and teach them the orthodox faith, urging them to abandon the Bogomil heresy <note>original Greek: Βογομιλικῆς αἱρέσεως</note>. Some of them changed for the better and were released from prison; others, however, died in their heresy while still held in custody, though they were provided with a generous supply of food and clothing."

In this matter, the Emperor has undertaken nothing thus far that does not agree with the institutions and discipline of the ancient Church. For the Church also, in bringing back to the way of truth those who had strayed, used at times arguments and reasoning drawn from heavenly doctrine; at other times, when she saw she was making no progress in this way, she was accustomed to deal with the matter more severely. I will not cite here the testimonies of the ancient Doctors of the Church, nor will I be anxious about searching for examples, since this task has already been excellently performed by **Veiel** <note>Elias Veiel (1635–1706), a prominent Lutheran theologian</note>, of blessed memory <note>original Greek: ὁ μακαρίτης</note>, in his commentary on the various types of <term>Sacred Economy</term> <note>The management of divine or ecclesiastical affairs</note> (especially ecclesiastical), and in two dissertations on the care of the ancient Christians regarding heresies. Similar work was done by **Strauch** <note>Aegidius Strauch II (1632–1682), a German theologian and mathematician</note> on the fairness of the early Church in judging heretics, and by **Spanheim** <note>Friedrich Spanheim the Younger (1632–1701), a Dutch Calvinist theologian</note> on *Theological Prudence*. 

Alexios proposed this example to be imitated by himself: while he first tried to draw away the followers of Bogomil opinions from their purpose with gentle words, he then committed the stubborn ones to closer custody. He did this with no other purpose than, partly, to restrain the further propagation of this impure doctrine,

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Emperor Alexios I Komnenos’s treatment of the Bogomil prisoners. The previous page ended while citing the theologian Elias Veiel regarding the historical precedents for the Church's use of both instruction and severity toward heretics. This page details the Emperor's humanitarian treatment of the prisoners (despite modern criticisms) and the eventual death sentence passed upon their leader, Basil.</meta>

### ON THE ORIGIN OF THE BOGOMILS

...partly indeed so that he might obtain the opportunity of instructing the ignorant and the deceived. For this reason, even in their very custody—as Anna <note>Anna Komnene, the Emperor's daughter and historian</note> wisely advised—he ordered them to be treated liberally, and allowed nothing to be lacking that pertained to showing them humanity. Therefore, Arnold <note>Gottfried Arnold (1666–1714), a historian who often defended those labeled as heretics</note> had no just cause in his *History of Heresies* <note>Latin: *Haeresiologia*</note> in either of the two places where he comments on the Messalians <note>An ascetic sect from which the Bogomils were thought to descend</note> and Bogomils to write an indictment against those who labored to correct these men in the manner we have described, using his usual formulas of "extreme cruelty and impiety."

XIV. However, since the Emperor did not find everyone obedient to his words even by these means, and since Basil <note>the leader of the Bogomils</note> specifically set an example of stubbornness for the rest, a sentence was pronounced against him by the Holy Synod and Patriarch Nicholas <note>Nicholas III Grammatikos</note>, and then by the Emperor himself. This sentence judged him worthy of death because, having disregarded all warnings, he always maintained his old ways and seemed never likely to adopt better counsels. For so Anna relates at the end of page 492: *“Basil, however, as a true leader of the heresy <note>original Greek: αἱρεσιάρχην</note> and altogether unrepentant, was judged worthy of the fire by all the elite members of the Holy Synod and the monks <note>original Greek: Ναζιραίων λογάδες</note>, and by the Patriarch Nicholas himself; with whom the Emperor also cast his vote, having spoken with him many, many times and having recognized him as a perverse man who would not depart from his heresy.”* <note>original Greek: τὸν μέντοι βασίλειον, ὡς ὄντως αἱρεσιάρχην καὶ ἀμεταμέλητον παντάπασιν ἅπαντες τῆς ἱερᾶς συνόδου καὶ τῶν Ναζιραίων λογάδες, καὶ αὐτὸς δὲ ὁ τότε Πατριάρχης Νικόλαος, πυρὸς ἄξιον ἔκριναν, οἷς καὶ ὁ αὐτοκράτωρ σύμψηφος ἦν, πολλὰ πολλάκις προσομιλήσας αὐτῷ, καὶ ἄνδρα σκαιὸν διαγνοὺς καὶ τῆς αἱρέσεως μὴ ἀφισάμενον.</note> By this very act, Alexios seems to have punished him quite severely, as he was a man who could have been prevented from spreading errors by imprisonment or some other means. Nor can it be said that he judged his lack of restraint should be curbed by this type of punishment not so much because of the depravity of his opinions, but because of plots harmful to the Empire or disturbances expected to arise, since none of these things are mentioned by the writers. Therefore, a suitable place for this punishment was chosen in the Hippodrome of Constantinople <note>The great stadium and social center of the Byzantine capital</note>, a pit having been excavated and filled with wood, which for the construction of a sufficiently high pyre...

--- Page 55 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the trial and execution of Basil, the leader of the Bogomil sect, under Emperor Alexios I Komnenos. It follows the narrative of Anna Komnene regarding the dramatic choice offered to Basil between the pyre and the cross.</meta>

-># AND FATE.<-

...so that he might be of service; on the other side, in turn, a cross was erected, leaving an unhappy choice: whether he preferred to embrace the cross while alive, or to climb the pyre to be burned. Anna <note>Anna Komnene (1083–1153), the daughter of Emperor Alexios I and author of the *Alexiad*, our primary source for these events.</note> explains the matter thus on page 493:

*“For this reason, having lit a massive pyre in the hippodrome <note>The Great Hippodrome of Constantinople, usually used for chariot races but also for public spectacles and executions.</note>—a huge pit having been dug, and a multitude of logs, all of them tall trees, being piled up so that the stack looked like a mountain—then, when the pyre was ignited, a great crowd flowed in little by little, both onto the floor of the stadium and along the seating tiers, all watching expectantly for what was to come. On the other side, a cross was set up, and a choice was given to the impious man <note>original Greek: τῷ ἀσεβεῖ (tō asebei)</note>: if, terrified by the fire and changing his mind, he would approach the cross, he would then be set free from the furnace.”*

Nor did Basil change his plans because of this, but with his soul strengthened against the threats of danger and death, he allowed himself to be led, because he was convinced that an Angel would snatch him even from the midst of the flames. "The crowd of heretics was also present," says Anna, "watching their leader Basil. But he appeared contemptuous toward every punishment and threat, and standing further back from the fire, he mocked and spoke of wonders, saying that certain angels would snatch him from the middle of the fire. And he softly sang that verse of David: *It shall not come near you, but with your eyes you shall look on.*" <note>A reference to Psalm 91:7-8: "A thousand shall fall at thy side... but it shall not come nigh thee. Only with thine eyes shalt thou behold and see the reward of the wicked."</note>

And although later, as the force of the flame came into his sight, he was not a little troubled in spirit—of which thing he gave more than one sign—he soon returned to his old ways, resumed the same spirit, and could not be moved by the Emperor's entreaties, which were then repeated once and again through interpreters.

*“For neither did the fire soften his iron soul, nor did the Emperor's messages sent to him through intermediaries charm him. But either a great madness had seized him due to the pressing necessity and misfortune, and his mind was so perplexed that he could no longer discern what was in his best interest; or, as seems more likely, the devil had taken possession of his soul, casting a very deep darkness over it...”*

<summary>This page describes the final moments of Basil the Bogomil in the Hippodrome of Constantinople, where he refused to recant his beliefs even when faced with a massive pyre and the alternative of the cross. Anna Komnene attributes his stubbornness to either psychological breakdown or demonic possession.</summary>

### ON THE ORIGIN OF THE BOGOMILS

<meta>catchword translation: ...fire</meta>

...poured out, that detestable Basil <note>Basil the Physician, the leader of the Bogomil heretics in Byzantium during the 12th century.</note> stood helpless against every threat and every fear.

XV. However, while the unhappy man clung to that conviction, and confirmed to those present with every assertion that he would escape the deadly flames, the executioners—fearing the illusions of Satan for themselves and thinking a test of the matter should be made—threw his outer garment into the flame. Although the garment felt the fire’s force, it seemed to Basil (if his own words are to be believed) to fly up into heaven. These are Anna’s <note>Anna Komnene, the Byzantine princess and historian whose *Alexiad* is the source for this account.</note> words: 

"Taking up his cloak <note>original Greek: τὸν μανδύαν (*ton mandyan*). This refers to a specific type of garment; the Latin text cites Charles du Fresne’s Glossary (p. 867), which defines it as 'a kind of garment, specifically the mantle worn by monks'</note> the executioners said, 'Let us see if the fire will touch your holy vestments.' And immediately they cast it into the middle of the furnace. Basil, however, was so greatly deluded by the demon deceiving him that he said: 'Look! Do you see the cloak rising into the air?'" 

Once this was done, moved by the Emperor’s command, the same men seized Basil by his waist and threw him into the fire, which the flame immediately seized and delivered to death. Anna says on page 494: 

"The executioners, noticing the fabric of his garment by the hem, lifted him and pushed him, clothes, shoes, and all, into the middle of the furnace. And the flame, as if angry with him, devoured the impious man so thoroughly that there was no smell of burnt flesh <note>original Greek: κνίσσαν (*knissan*), referring to the smell of roasting meat or sacrifice</note>, nor any unusual change in the smoke, but only a thin smoky line appeared in the middle of the flame." 

Thus Basil perished, and it was nearly the case that his followers were removed from the living by a similar kind of death. For the surrounding crowd would scarcely have restrained themselves from throwing them into the same shared punishment, had not the Emperor’s stern voice intervened in the reasoning of this plan. 

"As for the remaining number, as many as shared Basil's foolishness, while the standing crowd was seething and forcing themselves to throw them also onto the..."

->AND FATES<-

...fire, the Emperor did not allow. <note>original Latin: *non concessit*. The Emperor refused to allow a mass execution of all the heretics, choosing instead to execute only the leader, Basil.</note> Indeed, so that the seeds of their doctrines would not be scattered more widely, they were kept closely guarded in various places within the porticos and courtyards of the palace. There they remained hidden for a long interval of time, clinging stubbornly to their errors until they finally died. This is evident from these words of Anna <note>Anna Komnene, the historian and daughter of Emperor Alexios.</note>: "Having ordered them to be enclosed in the porticos and walkways of the Great Palace <note>original Greek: *en tais tōn megistōn anaktorōn stoais*. Regarding this term for 'palace,' see the notes of the illustrious Spanhemius on Callimachus's *Hymn to Apollo*, verse 77, and on Julian's *The Caesars*, p. 358.</note>, the 'theater' <note>The 'theater' refers to the public spectacle or the assembly gathered for the trial.</note> was then dissolved. After this, another very secure guard took over the godless ones; having been cast there and spending a great deal of time, they died in their impiety."

Let us hear Euthymius <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a monk and theologian commissioned by the Emperor to document and refute the Bogomil's secret doctrines.</note> as he briefly explains the punishment taken upon Basil in Section XXII: 

"That death for them is not painless, as if occurring in sleep or the shedding of a garment, was demonstrated by the leader of their heresy. He was subjected to many pains after the sentence of death by fire was passed against him. He was further consumed by fear and received mortal blows to his heart when he realized, once the 'drama' was stripped bare, that he had been outwitted. The mysteries of his faith were made public and committed to writing for a 'triumph' <note>A 'triumph' in this context is a public display of the Emperor's victory over the heretics' secrets.</note>. His disciples—and especially his relatives and those who were his most genuine followers—had been caught in a net and locked away in prisons. All help from every side had perished for him, and his hopes had vanished. For seeing his disciples and relatives in such a pitiful state, contrary to all expectation—since until the final day he thought everyone was in good standing according to the Emperor's promise—and being accused of betrayal, he let forth springs of bitter tears. He sent up deep sighs that revealed the flame within his heart. Struck by these calamities as if by a typhoon, he fainted; and collapsing again, he was carried down, unable to stand, until those ordered to lead him lifted and carried him..."

# ON THE ORIGIN OF THE BOGOMILS

> ...was led to the fire, and as his voice failed him from his thick gasping of breath, he was cast into the flame and became a whole burnt offering <term>holocaust: a sacrifice completely consumed by fire</term> to the demon he worshipped. He himself departed to the place of darkness and other torments prepared for him; yet the body of this thrice-wretched old man will surely rise on the day of the common resurrection, according to the decree of the Savior, who said that even those who have done evil will rise to eternal punishment—even though he himself <note>Basil the Bogomil, the leader previously discussed.</note> dogmatically asserted that there is no resurrection of the dead, pushing aside as false the many testimonies and noble proofs of the Apostle regarding this matter. <note>original Greek: This passage concludes the description of Basil's execution, emphasizing the theological irony that he would face a physical resurrection for judgment despite his sect's denial of the body's resurrection.</note>

XVI. By this means, the roots of this sect could have appeared to be utterly torn up; however, not long afterward, they began to sprout again under John Komnenos <note>John II Komnenos (reigned 1118–1143), the son and successor of Alexios I.</note>. For there had been a certain Constantine Chrysomalus, who had left behind certain commentaries of his own filled with opinions very closely approaching those of the Bogomils—indeed, even surpassing them in wickedness. These were found in the Monastery of the Lord Nicholas, who perhaps should not be distinguished from that Patriarch of Constantinople whom we mentioned above. One copy of these reached Peter the Monk, the Superior of the Monastery of Saint Athenogenes; another reached George the Prelate, distinguished by the surname Pamphilus; and finally, a third was received from Euthymius, living in the Gerocomium <note>A hospital or home for the elderly, often managed by a monastery.</note> Monastery, by those monks who stood as the first messengers and interpreters of this matter. Therefore, a Synod <note>A formal council of church leaders convened to decide on matters of doctrine or discipline.</note> was convened, in which, under the presidency of Patriarch Leo <note>Patriarch Leo Styppes of Constantinople, who presided over the synod in 1143.</note>, and with various Princes, Bishops, and Doctors of the Church in attendance, those copies of Constantine’s writings were compared with one another, subjected to examination, and finally marked with black coal <note>original Latin: *atro carbone notata*. A classical Roman idiom meaning to be formally condemned or marked as evil.</note>.
