# AND FATES <note>Likely the conclusion of a chapter title such as "On the Sect's Origin and Fates."</note>

We shall repeat the words in which the sentence passed on these writings is contained: "Many of the things contained in these writings," they say <note>original Greek: "πολλὰ δὲ... εὕρομεν," referring to the findings of the Church Synod</note>, "having examined them both individually and investigated them as a council, we have found to be full not only of idle chatter, mere innovations, and foolishness, but clearly also of heresies—even more so than the other Enthusiasts <note>A term often used for the Messalians, a sect that heavily influenced the Bogomils</note> and Bogomils." Nor indeed did this judgment deceive them in their belief that they had detected traces of Bogomil doctrine in the commentaries of Chrysomalus. 

For those traces are evident even in this assertion, when he attributes no efficacy to baptism in itself without prior instruction <term>catechesis: formal religious instruction given to a candidate before baptism</term>. [The synod condemned] "the saying (of Chrysomalus) that every Christian baptized in infancy—which, as he says, is done without instruction according to prevailing custom—is not even a Christian in truth, even if he be called one." And shortly after: "And saying again, that as many as are called Christians but have not understood their own spiritual reconstruction <note>original Greek: "anastoicheiōsin," meaning a fundamental re-ordering of one's elements or nature</note> from divine baptism, even if they have attained to the rank of high priest—they have benefited nothing." 

The sense of these words was not grasped by Allatius <note>Leone Allacci (1586–1669), a prominent scholar and librarian at the Vatican</note>, or whoever is the author of the translation which he presents in the place cited <term>l. c.: "locum citatum," meaning "in the place already mentioned"</term>, which was formulated in this way: *Likewise, whoever, although they are called Christians, do not perceive their transelementation from divine baptism, nor have they reached the rank of High Priest, etc.* For the sense of the words should have been represented thus: *All who, while they are called by the name of Christians, do not feel the reformation of themselves from divine baptism, even if they have attained to the high-priestly dignity, will perceive no benefit therefrom.* 

It will appear from what follows that the Bogomils taught this same doctrine. Chrysomalus also followed these same heretics when he dared to attribute two souls to a Christian. "Moreover, in addition to all the other things, he introduces the idea that every Christian has two souls: one of them sinless, and the other prone to sin. And if one has only one soul, he has not yet become a Christian." 

Therefore, the authors of the sentence themselves thought this assertion to be a clear proof of Bogomilism. "But also," they say...

# ON THE ORIGIN OF THE BOGOMILS

...he was led to the pyre of fire, and as his voice failed him, suffocated by the gasping of his breath, he was hurled into the flame and was entirely consumed by the demon he worshipped <note>This describes the execution of Basil the Physician (c. 1118), the Bogomil leader burned at the stake by Emperor Alexios I Komnenos</note>. For there had been a certain Constantine Chrysomalus, who had left behind certain commentaries of his, filled with opinions very closely approaching those of the Bogomils—indeed, even exceeding them in depravity. ->AND FATES. 35<-

We shall repeat the words by which the sentence passed on these writings is contained: "Many of the things," they say, "contained in these [writings], having been scrutinized both individually and examined synodically <note>Examined by a 'synod' or official council of church leaders</note>, we found to be full not only of quibbles, mere novelties, and foolish talk, but also clearly full of heresies—even more so than other Enthusiasts <note>original: "ἐνθουσιαστῶν" (enthusiasts); a term often used for the Messalians, a sect that believed in direct experience of the divine through prayer rather than sacraments</note> and Bogomils." Nor indeed did the judgment fail them in this: that they believed they had detected traces of Bogomil doctrine in the commentaries of Chrysomalus. For these are evident even in this assertion, when he attributes no efficacy to baptism in itself without prior catechesis <note>Instruction in the faith before receiving a sacrament</note>. "But to say," (as Chrysomalus does), "that every Christian baptized in infancy—who, according to the prevailing custom, is baptized without instruction—is not even a Christian in truth, even if he were so named..." And a few lines later: "And again he says, that as many as are called Christians but have not understood their own reformation <note>original: "ἀναστοιχείωσιν" (anastoicheiōsin); literally a 're-ordering of elements' or transformation</note> resulting from divine baptism, even if they have reached the rank of high priest—they have gained nothing."

Leo Allatius <note>A prominent 17th-century Greek-Catholic scholar and librarian at the Vatican</note>, or whoever is the author of the version he presents in the passage cited, did not grasp the sense of these words, which was rendered in this way: *Likewise, whoever, though they are now called Christians, do not perceive their transelementation <note>original: "transelementationem"</note> from divine baptism, nor have reached the high-priestly rank, etc.* For the sense of the words should have been represented thus: *All who, though they are called by the name of Christians, do not feel their reformation through divine baptism, even if they have reached high-priestly dignity, will perceive no benefit from it.*

That the Bogomils taught this same doctrine will appear from what follows. He also followed these very people when he dared to attribute two souls to a Christian. "And in addition to all the other things, he introduces [the idea] that every Christian has <gloss>read "has" instead of "to have"</gloss> two souls: one of them sinless, and the other sinful. If they have only one soul, they have not yet become a Christian." Therefore, the authors of the sentence themselves thought this assertion was a clear proof of Bogomilia. "But also that," they say, "either two...

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the 12th-century condemnation of Constantine Chrysomalus, focusing on the specific Bogomil doctrines found in his work and the subsequent tradition of burning heretical books.</meta>

->### ON THE ORIGIN OF THE BOGOMILS ###<-

"...either a Christian has two souls, as they say, or he is not a Christian at all—a point found explicitly dogmatized by the utterly profane religion of the Messalians <note>A sect originating in the 4th century that emphasized constant prayer and believed every person was born with an indwelling demon that only prayer could expel</note>, otherwise known as the Bogomils (I write it thus instead of *Bogomēlōn*, which Allatius <note>Leo Allatius, a 17th-century scholar and librarian of the Vatican</note> uses several times)."

**XVII.** These filthy opinions appeared so worthy of condemnation to the Fathers assembled at the Synod that they caused the writings of Chrysomalus themselves to be consigned to the flames. "We have decreed," they said, "that the books themselves be immediately handed over to the fire, and we place all those who hold such views under anathema <note>original: "ἀναθέματι" (anathema); a formal curse or excommunication by the Church</note>." In this way, the ancient Church clearly believed it could best provide for the purity of doctrine if, by using flames, it abolished from the sight and memory of men writings full of errors. 

Thus when Arius <note>The 4th-century priest whose views on the nature of Christ led to the Council of Nicaea</note> was condemned, not only did the same fate befall his writings, but those who dared to keep his books were ordered by a special edict of Constantine the Great to fear capital punishment. Socrates <note>Socrates Scholasticus, a 5th-century Christian historian</note> testifies to this in Book 9 of his *Ecclesiastical History*; compare this with Pierre Bayle in Volume I of his *Historical-Critical Dictionary*, page 354. More examples were collected by Sixtus Senensis in Book II, page 63 of his *Sacred Library*, and by Wagenseil in the preface to *The Fiery Darts of Satan* <note>original: "Tela ignea Satanae"; a collection of Jewish anti-Christian polemics compiled by Johann Christoph Wagenseil in 1681</note>, page 7, who also shows that the writings of the Gentiles <note>Here referring to Pagan or non-Christian classical authors</note> experienced the same fate under the Christian Emperors. 

Certainly John of Salisbury, in Book II, chapter 26 of *The Trifles of the Courtiers*, relates that Gregory the Great destroyed a no small abundance of Gentile writings with flames. As for the commentaries of heretics, I would further suggest consulting Theophilus Raynaud in his *Inquiries on Good and Bad Books*, Hartknoch in his *Ecclesiastical History of Prussia*, and the specific dissertations of M. Wilhelm Schütz on the *Burning of Heretical Books*.

**XVIII.** With punishment having been meted out to Chrysomalus in this manner even after his death <note>original: "post cineres" (after his ashes); likely referring to his posthumous condemnation or the burning of his books</note>, those in whose possession his books were found would not have escaped all penalty either, had it not been that regarding the doctrines...

->### AND DESTINIES ###<-

...doctrines <note>The Latin text begins mid-word: "trinis", likely completing "doctrinis"</note> which were handed down in those books; when questioned, they loathed them all from their hearts. For thus the cited Acts <note>The "Acta," or official records of the Church Synod</note> explain the matter:

> It was necessary that these men—the monks Peter and Pamphilus—should also be questioned as to whether they held to any of these things <note>original: "εἴ τι" (ei ti); the translator suggests this reading over "εἴτε" (eite)</note>, or whether they instead spat upon them as erroneous and impious, and anathematized these slippery dogmas, intending to think nothing more of them than what was synodically determined by us today in the hearing of all. They spoke out, saying that even if they previously possessed the books themselves and, reading them without caution or discernment, had accepted them as being sound, they now—having looked at them more scrutinizingly as they were read aloud and having understood them to be rightly rejected—confessed to seeking pardon.

After this profession of faith was issued, Pamphilus escaped free from all punishment. Peter, however—who had previously been appointed to govern other monks—was removed from his position, as he seemed unfit by both temperament and learning to lead others. Furthermore, his administration could not continue without causing offense to those who had heard of this matter. He was therefore transferred to another monastery, where he might obey the directions of others rather than being able to abuse his own authority and power to the deception <note>original: "fraudem"; implying leading others into spiritual error</note> of others.

**XIX.** Later it was also discovered that a certain Niphon and other *false bishops* <note>original: "Pseudo-Episcopos"</note> with him, namely Clement and Leontius, had fallen into the same errors. This Niphon, therefore, being ordered to appear before the Synod, did not withstand the examination of his doctrine as was proper. He was thus condemned by Michael Oxita <note>Michael II, Patriarch of Constantinople from 1143 to 1146</note>, the Patriarch of Constantinople, and moreover suffered this punishment for his imprudence: his beard, which he kept long even down to his feet, was cut off, and he was committed to prison. For in that age, a shaved beard <note>original: "barba rasa"</note> still carried a mark of disgrace, a status it also held in even more remote centuries: See Martin Lydius on *Military Matters*, Book VI, chapter 6, page 237; Hotman in *Francogallia*...

->### ON THE ORIGIN OF THE BOGOMILS ###<-

France chapter IX and Volume X of the *Select Observations of Halle*, Observation IV. John Cinnamus <note>Joannes Cinnamus (c. 1143–1185), a Byzantine historian who wrote about the reign of Manuel I Comnenus</note> explains the matter itself as it happened in Book II, chapter X, from which we shall transfer a few points here for our purpose. 

"There was a certain man," he says, "who had taken up the monastic life, named Niphon; though he had not even reached the point of experiencing a general education or secular studies, he had devoted himself from childhood to the sacred oracles <note>The Holy Scriptures</note>. This Niphon—while Michael <note>Michael II Oxites, Patriarch of Constantinople from 1143 to 1146</note>, a holy man who governed the ecclesiastical throne with much virtue, was still in power—gave many an unhealthy impression of himself regarding Christian doctrine. For this reason, he was condemned by a synodal judgment; then his beard was shorn until it reached down to his ankles, and he was handed over to prison." <note>original Greek: "ἦν τις ἀνήρ... παρεδόθη"</note>

Not long after, Niphon’s affairs almost flowed according to his wishes, when Cosmas <note>Cosmas II Atticus, who succeeded Michael as Patriarch</note> was substituted in Michael’s place. Captivated by this man’s character, Cosmas dealt with him familiarly and left him free power to associate with others. 

And when the Emperor was led by the complaints of others to see that Niphon was returned to prison, Cosmas—even then, despite everyone else protesting—defended his cause in the assembly quite vehemently. As a result, not only was Niphon returned to his former state of life again, but Cosmas himself was deprived of his Patriarchal dignity. 

We shall not bring forward here those things which comprise the sentence pronounced against Niphon himself, and Clement, Bishop of Sasima, and Leontius, Bishop of Balbissa, by Michael Oxita, in the presence of other men of the Sacred Order. He <note>The author being cited, likely John Cinnamus or the editor of the 'Select Observations'</note> presents these from a manuscript at length, including the very formula by which Cosmas was pronounced unworthy of the Patriarchal prerogative. 

The nature of our project does not now permit us to investigate whether more trust should be placed in this narrative or in the words of Nicetas Choniates <note>A prominent Byzantine historian (c. 1155–1217)</note>, who in Book II of his *Annals* testifies that it was not so much a suspicion of corrupt opinions that caused him to be deprived of his dignity, but rather the slanders of malicious men, who [suggested] some sort of treason...

<meta>This page follows the discussion of the downfall of Patriarch Cosmas II and transitions into other 12th-century figures accused of Bogomil-like heresies.</meta>

->### AND FATES. ###<-

...of the rebellion committed against Emperor Manuel, they had transferred the nature [of the crime] to him.

**XX.** Among those who rendered themselves suspect of **Bogomil** opinions, **Michael of Thessalonica**—active around the middle of the 12th century—is also usually included. He was the Master of Rhetoricians and the <term>Protecdicus</term> <note>the chief legal protector or advocate of the church</note> of the Great Church <note>Hagia Sophia</note>. Regarding this type of office, **Charles du Fresne** <note>Also known as Du Cange, a famous 17th-century scholar of the Byzantine world</note> comments on page 361 of his *Glossary*, drawing from Codinus, Symeon of Thessalonica, and others. Michael, along with others—namely **Nicephorus Basiliaca**, **Eustathius of Dyrrachium**, and **Soterichus Panteugenus**—was accused and convicted of errors and paid the penalty of exile. Eventually, however, after changing his opinion, he professed pure doctrine. **Leo Allatius** <note>A Greek scholar and librarian at the Vatican (1586–1669)</note> presents this confession from a manuscript codex at the end of Chapter XII, page 691. Compare this with the work of the illustrious **William Cave** in his *Literary History*, page 589. 

Yet, if judgment is to be made from his retractation, he seems to have been condemned not so much in the name of **Bogomil** opinions, but for a singular opinion unique to himself—unless, besides this, he made other assertions his own (not mentioned there) which approached closer to <term>Bogomilism</term> <note>original Greek: "Βογομιλίαν"</note>. The error mentioned in the retractation is contained in his statement: <note>original Greek: "ὅτι ὁ μέγας καὶ πρῶτος ἀρχιθύτης χριστὸς προσφέρων μὲν καὶ προσφερόμενός ἐστιν, οὐ προσδεχόμενος δὲ καὶ προσλαμβάνων ὁ αὐτὸς τὸ θῦμα, ἀλλὰ προσδεχόμενος ἤτοι προσλαμβανόμενος παρὰ τοῦ πατρός."</note> **"that the great and first high-sacrificer Christ is indeed the one offering and the one being offered, but He himself is not the one receiving or accepting the victim; rather, it is received or accepted by the Father."** <note>This was a significant 12th-century theological dispute regarding whether the sacrifice of Christ was offered to the whole Trinity or only to the Father.</note>

**XXI.** However, it must not be thought that the **Bogomils** completely perished in this century. For certain homilies of **Germanus**, Patriarch of Constantinople <note>Germanus II, Patriarch from 1223 to 1240</note>, stand in the way of that idea; these were delivered specifically against their opinions. In these, he furthermore testifies that an exchange of views took place between himself and a **Bogomil** who was present. Thus, in his *Oration on the Restoration of Holy Images*, page 553, you may read these words of his: <note>original Greek: "Καὶ γὰρ αὐτοῖς ἐγὼ πολλάκις κατὰ πρόσωπον ἀντέστην, καὶ τὰ μιαρὰ τούτων κατῄσχυνα πρόσωπα, συνεργíᾳ τοῦ παρ’ αὐτῶν ὑβριζομένου"</note> **"For indeed, I often stood against them face to face, and I put their foul faces to shame, by the cooperation of Him who is insulted by them..."**

<meta>catchword: ...insulted [by them]</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the 12th-century trials of high-ranking Byzantine officials like Michael of Thessalonica for theological errors and highlights the continued presence of Bogomilism into the 13th century as documented by Patriarch Germanus.</summary>

<keywords>Michael of Thessalonica, Bogomils, Nicephorus Basiliaca, Eustathius of Dyrrachium, Soterichus Panteugenus, Leo Allatius, William Cave, Germanus of Constantinople, Christology, Eucharist, heresy, Great Church</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of historical figures who opposed the Bogomil heresy, focusing on Patriarch Germanus II of Constantinople and the timeline of the sect's influence into the 13th century.</meta>

### ON THE ORIGIN AND FATES OF THE BOGOMILS. ###

...by Christ alone. <note>original Greek: "μένῳ χριστῷ"</note> That is, according to the interpretation of **Jacob Gretser** <note>A 16th-century German Jesuit scholar and historian</note>: *I have frequently stood against them to their face, and I have silenced their shameless countenances with the help of Christ, who is treated with injury and insult by them.* Indeed, in that same Speech at the place mentioned, he complains that:
> many now walk about and sneak into houses, traveling in darkness, and they seize many with a form of piety; for being devils of dark form, they transform themselves into angels of light. <note>original Greek: "ὅτι πολλοὶ ἀρτίως περιπατοῦσι καὶ τὰς οἰκίας εἰσδύνουσιν ἐν σκότει διαπορευόμενοι, καὶ σχήματι εὐλαβείας πολλοὺς συναρπάζουσι, σκοτεινόμορφοι γὰρ ὄντες διάβολοι εἰς ἀγγέλους φωτὸς μετασχηματίζονται." This is an allusion to 2 Corinthians 11:14.</note>

The age of this **Germanus** <note>Germanus II, Patriarch of Constantinople from 1223 to 1240</note> falls in the 13th century. At its beginning, around the year 1222—as appears from **Nicephorus Gregora**, Book III of his *Roman History*, page 39—he began to preside over the Church of Constantinople. He was a "prudent man," in the judgment of **Gregora**, "who adorned his life with both virtue and speech." <note>original Greek: "ἀνὴρ συνετὸς... καὶ ἀρετῇ καὶ λόγῳ τὸν βίον κοσμήσας."</note> The same is proved by the fact that in his writings throughout, he mentions not only the Great Schism between the Greeks and the Latins, but also the wars waged between the Italians and the Emperors of Constantinople. Indeed, to settle the matter, there exists in the Vienna Imperial Library, <term>Codex</term> <note>a manuscript book</note> 276, a letter from **Manuel Ducas** addressed to him, in which Manuel begs him to help reconcile him with **John Ducas**. **Germanus** not only took this business upon himself but also finished it successfully beyond expectation, as he himself indicated in a reply letter written to **Ducas**, which is read in the same Codex. He lived his life until the middle of the 13th century, at which time **Theodore Lascaris** held the <term>fasces</term> <note>a Latin term for the symbols of high office, here meaning imperial power</note> of the empire. 

On this matter, see—besides **Freher**—**Jacob Gretser** in his notes to Volume II of *On the Cross*, page 592, and the Illustrious **William Cave** in his *Literary History*. Since, therefore, the opinions of the **Bogomils** still infested the Church in the age of **Germanus**, so that the minds of others had to be fortified against them by teachers, it appears that they troubled the pure streams of the Church for about a century and a half. Just as in our own memory, there have not been lacking those who, by a very bad example, brought back into the light some of the errors of those men—taken either from the sectarians themselves or from other like-minded <note>original Greek: "ὁμοψήφοις"</note> individuals.

->THE END.<-

<summary>This concluding page establishes that the Bogomil heresy persisted into the 13th century, as evidenced by the writings of Patriarch Germanus II of Constantinople. It also notes that similar errors have occasionally resurfaced in more recent times.</summary>

# ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE BOGOMILS.

->I.<-

We shall now turn to a more diligent investigation of the Bogomils' <note>A medieval dualist sect that emerged in the Balkans, often considered precursors to the Cathars</note> opinions regarding the core tenets of divine doctrine. By doing so, we may understand that the *defenders* and *vindicators* of Christian doctrine within the Church of that era were driven by serious and compelling reasons to devote every effort to clearing away these foul errors. *Euthymius Zigabenus* <note>A 12th-century Byzantine monk and theologian who compiled the "Panoplia Dogmatica" to defend Orthodoxy</note> clearly laid these out in his work, the *Panoplia* <note>original: "Panoplia", a Greek word meaning a "full suit of armor," used here to describe a defensive work against heresy</note>, from which we shall primarily recount them here. This is especially necessary because, until now, it has only been possible to read them in Latin through the translation of *Francesco Zini* <note>A 16th-century Italian scholar</note>. While his version is not without style, it does not always capture the full force and nuance of the original Greek context.

Besides him, there is hardly anyone who has preserved the misguided beliefs of these people for posterity, with the exception of the few points that *Harmenopulus* <note>Constantine Harmenopoulos, a 14th-century Byzantine jurist</note> briefly touched upon—rather than fully explained—in his short treatise on the opinions of heretics. *Nicetas Choniata* <note>A famous Byzantine historian and theologian</note> did not neglect to mention them, yet he completed this task quite briefly; he followed in the footsteps of *Euthymius* so closely that he appears to have relied on no other source. *Zonaras* <note>Joannes Zonaras, a 12th-century historian and canon lawyer</note> says nothing at all about their beliefs, while *Anna Comnena* <note>The daughter of Byzantine Emperor Alexios I Komnenos and author of the "Alexiad"</note> only briefly touches upon a few points as examples. She confesses on page 490 of her *Alexiad* that she was prevented by a certain natural modesty from turning her efforts toward "stirring this marsh." <note>original Latin: "movendam hanc camarinam"; an idiom referring to a dangerous or unpleasant task, like draining the stagnant Camarina marsh in Sicily which was said to cause a plague</note> Therefore, it is primarily upon his [Euthymius'] testimony—

<summary>This page introduces a detailed study of Bogomil doctrines, highlighting the 12th-century theologian Euthymius Zigabenus as the primary historical source due to the scarcity or brevity of other accounts like those of Anna Comnena or Nicetas Choniata.</summary>

<page-num>42</page-num>
<header>ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE BOGOMILS.</header>

... mony <note>original: "testimonio," continuing from the final word "nio" on the previous page</note> we must acquiesce, to which the illustrious woman herself <note>Anna Comnena, the daughter of Emperor Alexius I and author of the *Alexiad*</note> refers the reader. This is the same Euthymius whom we have praised, who was closest to these events. According to the reliability of all history, he must be referred to the reign of our Alexius, the subduer of the Bogomils—not, as was the opinion of Marquard Freher <note>A German jurist and historian (1565–1614)</note>, to another Alexius an entire century later, a view Allatius <note>Leo Allatius (c. 1586–1669), a Greek scholar and librarian at the Vatican</note> addresses in *On the Consensus of the Western and Eastern Church*, p. 641. I see no reason to doubt either his good faith or his diligence, especially since he first undertook this entire labor under the auspices and command of the Emperor himself. He devoted this work to enumerating the specific beliefs of the Bogomils and refuting them using the assertions of the Doctors of the early Church. You may understand this both from his own preface to the *Panoply* and from the introduction by Niketas Choniates <note>A Byzantine historian and theologian</note> to the *Treasury of the Orthodox Faith*, and most especially from the testimony of Anna, which is read on page 490 of the *Alexiad*. From this passage, it is clear who the author of the project was and what the reasoning was for its title. Since we find this latter point explained more clearly by Euthymius himself, we shall repeat his words here from the preface of the work, as they appear in Fell's <note>John Fell (1625–1686), Bishop of Oxford, who produced copies of Greek manuscripts</note> Greek transcript in the Bodleian Library.

"And the name of this book," he says, "shall be the **Dogmatic Panoply** <note>original: "Panoplia," meaning a full suit of armor</note>, or **Arsenal** <note>original: *hoplotheke*; I have corrected this, as a negligent scribe had written *splotheke*</note> of dogmas. Thus, when the time calls us to battle and rouses the spiritual soldiers, they may unfold this work and protect themselves against every kind of warfare with the weapons stored within. For these weapons are manifold and abundant, and sufficient for every variety of war. Since all discourse is twofold—as Gregory the Theologian <note>Gregory of Nazianzus, one of the influential "Cappadocian Fathers" of the 4th century</note> says—with one part establishing one’s own position and the other overturning that of the opponent, we too, having first set out our own position, shall then attempt to overturn the arguments of the adversaries. We shall do both through the all-wise Fathers, as has been said, who have previously labored on behalf of the Word."

<meta>catchword: such as</meta>

It includes a translated excerpt from Euthymius's preface explaining the work's purpose as a spiritual armory for defending Orthodox faith.</summary>

...and just as if they [the holy fathers] had left us their own words as an inheritance, including the various entanglements against their opponents and the methods of their heroic deeds. These malicious heresies—selected like a choice collection of evils—will be set forth in chronological order, accompanied by their subsequent refutations. We will show those who excelled against each heresy, running them down and striking them through with their own counter-arguments. As for the older heresies, we have passed over them as they have long since rotted and been entirely destroyed. But we have set up the more notable ones—those whose coals still trouble the Church even if their flame has died out—as targets to be struck and shot through with arrows. <note>original: "καὶ καθάπερ τινὰ κλῆρον..." This is a quote from the Greek preface of Euthymius Zigabenus's *Panoply of Doctrine* (*Panoplia Dogmatica*).</note>

I prudently omit the Latin translations of the above, which can be read on page 6 of Volume 19 of the *Great Library of the Fathers* <note>original: *Bibliotheca Maxima Patrum*, a massive 27-volume collection of early Christian writings published in Lyon in 1677.</note>.

Since this "Sparta" <term>Sparta: Referring to the Greek proverb "You have been given Sparta, now adorn it," meaning a duty or province one is obligated to manage well.</term> was committed to ***Euthymius*** above all others by the judgment of the Emperor himself, it seems very likely to me that he did not allow either the Emperor himself or others—who had personally witnessed the plots and disturbances of the Bogomils—to find a lack of either honesty or diligence in his commentaries. 

I thought it necessary to give this warning at the very start of this treatise, so that no one, following ***Gottfried Arnold*** <note>A radical Pietist historian (1666–1714) famous for his *Unbiased History of the Church and Heresy*, which argued that "heretics" were often the true Christians.</note>, should dismiss the accounts that follow as mere fictions or complain about "Orthodox frauds." Arnold relies on a seemingly strong defense: he argues that the writers who record this sect's beliefs do not agree with one another, and that they dared to accuse the Bogomils of the most absurd and unheard-of opinions. 

These are the main accusations which ***Arnold*** recklessly hurls at the defenders of the pure doctrine in his *History of Heresies* (*Haeresiologia*), Part 1, p. 356—acting with no more truth than he usually does in his other defenses of heretics. Regarding his first point: if applied specifically to the Bogomils, it is clearly false. There can be no contradiction between writers on this subject because only one man, namely ***Euthymius***, investigated it with deliberate effort; others, by their age...

[authori]ty <note>The text begins mid-word with "-tate," likely completing "auctoritate" (authority) from the previous page.</note> to those lower in standing, or those following in his footsteps, or those passing over the doctrines in silence, or certainly those interpreting based on his work; but if the matter must be explained according to the mind of **Arnold** <note>Gottfried Arnold (1666–1714) was a radical German historian who argued that "heretics" were often more pious than the established Church.</note> regarding the **Messalians** <term>Messalians: Also known as Euchites, this was a 4th-century ascetic sect that the Bogomils were accused of being descended from.</term> in general—of whom the Bogomils were the offspring—even then it will not satisfy the expectations of sensible men. For Arnold has not yet demonstrated the disagreements among writers when listing the errors of the Messalians; nor, even if he had demonstrated them, could he be seen to have achieved his goal. For if there are any disagreements, they relate more to specific rituals or are settled by different ways of speaking—reasons that certainly cannot be held against the evidence itself. I pass over what the Blessed **Ittig** <note>Thomas Ittig (1643–1710) was a prominent Lutheran theologian and scholar of early church history.</note>—an exceptionally skillful investigator of Church antiquity—wisely warned against Arnold in his *Selected Chapters of Ecclesiastical History, Century I, page 265.*

I recall, indeed, that **Friedrich Spanheim** <note>Friedrich Spanheim the Younger (1632–1701) was a Calvinist theologian known for his massive works on church history.</note>, in his larger work *History of Christianity*, Volume I of his Works, page 1601, says in his review of Bogomil dogmas that those who weigh the matter accurately must distinguish certainties from doubtful or fabulous claims. He acknowledges that some points are highly ambiguous or were fabricated by "Greeklings" <note>original: *Graeculis*. A diminutive and often dismissive term for Byzantine Greeks, implying they were prone to clever but dishonest invention.</note> according to their own wit and biases. For my part, I would not stubbornly deny that the matter *could* have happened that way; however, neither Spanheim nor anyone else would dare to affirm with certain faith that it *actually* happened. In fact, I judge that Spanheim was led by arguments that were neither sufficiently fit nor weighty to suspect fraud or a "lust for slandering" here. It seems he made this conjecture because of the disagreement observed among historians, and because the opinions attributed to them [the Bogomils] exceed belief. Regarding the first point, it seems to us to have been sufficiently discussed just before and will be further refuted in this treatise; the second argument [that the beliefs are too strange to be true] does not provide a suitable proof. As the old saying goes, there is no opinion so far from the truth that it does not find someone to support it, and that applies here as well. <header>### ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE BOGOMILS.</header>

To ensure that no cause for doubt remains hereafter, we shall endeavor in the following pages to demonstrate both the consensus of historians and the agreement of the Bogomils' opinions with other sects of both recent and ancient memory. Furthermore, to show that **Euthymius** <note>Euthymius Zigabenus (c. 1050–1122), a Byzantine monk whose "Panoplia Dogmatica" is the primary source of information on the Bogomils.</note> himself is consistent on this subject, we shall call upon his work *Triumph over the Sect of the Messalians* <note>original: "Triumphum de Secta Massalianorum"</note>, which **Tollius** <note>Jacobus Tollius (1633–1696), a Dutch classicist and traveler.</note> included in his *Remarkable Works from an Italian Journey* <note>original: "Insignibus suis Itinerarii Italici"</note>; for in that writing, the beliefs of the "modern Messalians"—that is, the Bogomils—are enumerated. **Lambeck** <note>Peter Lambeck (1628–1680), a historian and librarian to the Holy Roman Emperor.</note>, in his *Commentaries on the Imperial Library of Vienna, Book III, page 170*, thought this *Triumph* was an appendix to Euthymius’s *Dogmatic Panoply* <note>The "Panoplia Dogmatica," an encyclopedic defense of Orthodox Christianity against various heresies.</note>, and he held out hope for its publication in a supplement to the *Corpus of Byzantine History*, a task which Tollius finally fulfilled. While this undertaking is rightly held in the highest esteem—since many unusual details not found elsewhere are learned from this commentary—I do not personally think it was composed by Euthymius for the purpose of being added as an appendix to the *Panoply*. Instead, I prefer to agree with the opinion of the most illustrious **Cave** <note>William Cave (1637–1713), an English church historian.</note>, to whom it quite rightly seems to take the form of either a letter or an oration prepared for a formal gathering of listeners, as noted in his *Literary History, page 567*.

III. Having thus shown and revealed the sources from which the impure doctrine of the Bogomils flows, nothing remains but for us to draw from them what can be said about the group. **HOLY SCRIPTURE** was the first to be exposed to the impious attacks of **Basil** <note>Basil the Physician, the leader of the Bogomils, who was executed by Emperor Alexios I Komnenos.</note>; for he was clever enough to realize that the Scriptures were so contrary to his own assertions that if their authority remained intact and protected <note>original: "sarta tecta," a Latin legal idiom meaning "in good repair" or "undamaged."</note>, his own beliefs could never be approved. However, so as not to bear the mark of excessive impiety, he did not reject the entire Bible; rather, he accepted certain books as being written under divine auspices, while with the most shameless tongue, he attributed the rest to Satan as their author. He therefore approved the Psalter, the sixteen

...by authority to those who are inferior, or those following in his footsteps, or those passing over the doctrines in silence, or certainly interpreting from him; if the matter concerning the **Messalians** <note>Also known as Massalians or Euchites, a sect whose origins were in 4th-century Mesopotamia and were often linked by medieval writers to the Bogomils.</note> in general—of whom the **Bogomils** were the offspring—is to be explained according to the mind of **Arnold** <note>Gottfried Arnold (1666–1714), a radical Pietist historian famous for his "Impartial History of the Church and Heretics," which often defended those the church labeled as heretics.</note>, it will not even then meet the expectation of prudent men. Eccles. Saec. I."</note>, page 265.

I certainly remember that **Friedrich Spanheim** <note>Friedrich Spanheim the Younger (1632–1701), a Dutch Calvinist theologian and historian.</note> in his major work *Christian History* <note>original: "Historiae Christianae"</note>, Volume I of his *Works* <note>original: "Opp." (Opera)</note>, page 1601, in his review of Bogomil dogmas, says that the things which are more certain must be distinguished from the doubtful or fabulous by one who considers the matter accurately; he acknowledges that some things are very ambiguous or were fabricated by "little Greeks" <note>original: "Graeculis"; a dismissive term for Byzantine writers, implying they were prone to exaggeration or deceit.</note> according to their own talent and passions. Indeed, I would not stubbornly deny that the matter *could* have been handled in such a way, but neither Spanheim nor anyone else would dare to affirm with certain faith that it *was* so handled. On the contrary, I judge that Spanheim was led by arguments neither sufficiently fit nor weighty to suspect fraud and a lust for slandering here.

However, he seems to have made this conjecture because disagreement is observed among historians, and because the opinions ascribed to the Bogomils exceed belief. Regarding the former point, it seems to have been sufficiently discussed by us a little earlier and will be further refuted in this treatise; as for the latter point, it does not provide a suitable argument, since the saying commonly used elsewhere—that "there is no opinion so foreign to the truth that it does not have some supporter"—finds its place here as well. <header>### ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE BOGOMILS.</header>

However, so that no cause for doubt remains hereafter, we will strive in what follows to demonstrate both the consensus of historians and the agreement of Bogomil opinions with other sects of both recent and ancient memory. Finally, so it may appear that **Euthymius** <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century Byzantine monk and theologian whose *Dogmatic Panoply* is the primary source for our knowledge of the Bogomils.</note> himself is consistent in this argument, we will call upon his *Triumph over the Sect of the Massalians*, which **Tollius** <note>Jacobus Tollius (1633–1696), a Dutch classicist and traveler.</note> included in his *Notable Contents of an Italian Journey* <note>original: "Insignibus suis Itinerarii Italici"</note>. In that writing, the tenets of the more recent **Massalians**—that is, the **Bogomils**—are enumerated. **Lambecius** <note>Peter Lambecius (1628–1680), a librarian and historian at the Imperial Library in Vienna.</note>, in his *Commentary on the Library of Vienna* <note>original: "Commentar. de Biblioth. Vindob."</note>, book III, page 170, thought that this *Triumph* was an appendix to **Euthymius's** *Dogmatic Panoply*, and he gave hope for publishing it in a supplement to the *Corpus of Byzantine History*, a task which **Tollius** finally fulfilled. While this undertaking is rightly held as most worthy of praise—since many things, neither common nor found elsewhere, are learned from this commentary—I do not think it was composed by **Euthymius** for the purpose of being added as an appendix to the *Panoply*. Instead, I would prefer to agree with the opinion of the Distinguished **Cave** <note>William Cave (1637–1713), an English church historian.</note>, to whom the work seems, not without reason, to take the form of either a letter or an oration prepared for a solemn assembly of listeners, as noted in his *Literary History* <note>original: "Historia Literar."</note>, page 567.

**III.** Having thus shown and opened the sources from which the impure doctrine of the **Bogomils** flows, nothing remains but to draw from them what can be said about the sect. **HOLY SCRIPTURE** was the first to be exposed to the impious insults of **Basil** <note>Basil the Physician, the Bogomil leader who was famously interrogated by Emperor Alexios I Komnenos and later burned at the stake in 1118.</note>. He realized that the Bible so strongly contradicted his assertions that as long as its authority remained intact, his own tenets could never be proven. However, lest he bear the mark of excessive impiety, he did not reject the entire Code of Scripture; rather, he accepted certain books as written under divine guidance, while referring the others to Satan as their author with the most shameless talk. Thus, he approved the **Psalter**, the sixteen books...

# ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE BOGOMILS.
<meta>catchword: hypo</meta>

...of the Prophets, the four Gospels, the Acts and all the Epistles of the Apostles, along with the Apocalypse of John. And indeed, he <note>Basil the Physician, the Bogomil leader.</note> arranged these in such a way that seven volumes were formed from them, by which he persuaded himself and others that the structure of his doctrines, like a house, rested upon as many pillars, misusing a passage from Solomon's Proverbs 9:1. I shall cite the words of **Euthymius** <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, the 12th-century theologian.</note> from Section I, which **Nicetas Choniates** <note>A Byzantine historian and theologian (c. 1155–1217) who wrote the "Treasury of the Orthodox Faith."</note> retains entirely in his *Treasury of the Orthodox Faith*, as found in **Montfaucon’s** <note>Bernard de Montfaucon (1655–1741), a famous French scholar of ancient texts.</note> *Greek Palaeography*, book IV, page 333: 

"They reject," he says, "all the Mosaic books <note>The first five books of the Bible, known as the Pentateuch.</note> along with the God described in them and the righteous men who were pleasing to Him. Indeed, they also reject all the books after these as having been written according to the intention of Satan. 'May the Lord be merciful to us' <note>original: "Ἵλεως ἡμῖν εἴη Κύριος" - a pious interjection by the author to distance himself from the heresy.</note> (the reading is 'to us' [*hēmon*] in the illustrious **Montfaucon** by a printer's error), they say as they repeat those people's words. They accept and honor only seven, which they memorize to the highest degree. I mean the Psalter, the sixteen Prophets, the Gospel according to Matthew, according to Mark, according to Luke, and according to John; the seventh being the book (I prefer to read this with the illustrious **Montfaucon** rather than following the **Fellian** <note>Referring to the edition by John Fell (1625–1686), Bishop of Oxford.</note> copy which has 'of the book') of the Acts along with all the Epistles and the Apocalypse of John the Theologian (Montfaucon reads 'the Apocalypse' in the accusative case, but I prefer the former reading since the Bogomils included the Apocalypse in the seventh book of Scripture). For they say, 'Wisdom has built a house for herself and supported it with seven pillars,' misinterpreting 'house' as their own utterly defiled <note>original: "παμμίαρον"</note> congregation (this word is absent in **Montfaucon**), and the 'seven pillars' as the books enumerated above." 

The same **Euthymius** in Section X relates that the Bogomils traced that entire institution of Moses—which pertained to leading the Jewish nation out of Egypt and instructing them with laws and precepts—back to a malignant spirit, appealing to **Paul**, who said that when the law came, sin came to life <note>A reference to Romans 7:9.</note>. Let us hear **Euthymius** himself: "They say that Moses, having been deceived..."

<meta>This page continues the transcription of Euthymius Zigabenus’s critique of Bogomil theology. # ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE BOGOMILS.

...concerning Satanail <note>The Bogomil name for the Devil. They believed he was the elder son of God who fell from grace and created the material world.</note> going again into Egypt, and deceiving the people of the Jews, and leading them out by performing wonders and signs through the power of Satanail, and ascending Mount Sinai, and receiving the law as dictated by Satanail, and destroying uncounted myriads through it. They claim the Apostle <note>St. Paul.</note> bears witness to this, saying: "I would not have known sin, except through the law." <note>Romans 7:7.</note> And again: "But when the commandment came, sin revived." <note>Romans 7:9. The Bogomils twisted these verses to argue that the Law was evil because it brought sin to life.</note> **Euthymius** <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century Byzantine monk and theologian who wrote the "Panoply of Dogma" to refute heresies.</note> repels this audacity of fabrication thus: 

"But, O sophists of wickedness, why have you maliciously hidden what follows? For it is written immediately after, that *the law is holy, and the commandment is holy, and just, and good.* <note>Romans 7:12.</note> If indeed, according to the testimony of the Apostle, the law is holy, and the commandment holy, and just, and good, then these legislations and commands cannot belong to the impure, unjust, and evil Satanail. For a rotten tree will have rotten fruit, but a good tree surely has good fruit, just as the Lord declared. <note>A reference to Matthew 7:17-18.</note> Furthermore, so that the words of the Apostle might be clarified, one must note that he did not say the law was the *producer* of sin, but the *knower* of it, as it points sin out and commands one to flee from it as something destructive. For before this written law was given to forbid it, even if sin was active, it was not recognized by men as so harmful and something to be avoided. 

But as for how 'sin revived when the commandment came,' listen: because before the legal commandment, sin was weak and, as it were, dead, unable to bring about such great condemnation for those who sin as it brings after the law, and for this reason, it did not wage much of a war. But when the law was given to men as the destroyer and enemy of sin, then it was especially stirred up and, as it were, 'revived.' That is to say (here there was a gap of several letters in the copy <note>original: "ἀπογράφῳ"</note>, showing only the final letters *-geu* of the word; therefore I think it should be read as *baleful* <note>original: "λυγρὸν" (lugron)</note>), it breathed forth a **baleful** breath and raged madly against those who received the legal commandments. But Moses was not deceived by Satanail; rather, He-Who-Is <note>A translation of the divine name, the Septuagint's "Ho On."</note>, God, sent him, and He Himself gave him the law; He did not destroy him, but guided him..."

It features a point-by-point rebuttal from the theologian Euthymius Zigabenus, who uses the Epistles of Paul to argue that the Law is inherently good but reveals the nature of sin.</summary>

# ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE BOGOMILS.

...he led those many thousands of people through him <note>Referring to the agent of Satanail—often identified as Moses in Bogomil myth—mentioned on the previous page.</note>, as you will find in the section against the **Paulicians** <note>A dualist Christian sect that preceded and influenced the Bogomils.</note>, just as we discussed in the previous chapter. **Harmenopulus** <note>Constantine Harmenopulus, a 14th-century Byzantine jurist and theologian.</note> agrees closely with this in his book *On the Opinions of the Heretics*: "they reject the books of the Old and New Testaments along with the God described in them, as having been written under the inspiration of Satan, except for only seven, among which is the Gospel." <note>original Greek: τὰς τῆς παλαιᾶς καὶ καινῆς ἀθετῶσι βίβλους... τοῦ Σατανᾶ</note>. These words are indeed so consistent with those of **Euthymius** <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, the primary source for Bogomil history.</note> that Harmenopulus seems to have merely repeated them, except perhaps for the word "inspiration" <note>original Greek: ἐπίπνοιαν</note>—a term which the Fellian manuscript and the translation by **Fr. Zini** <note>Pietro Francesco Zini, a 16th-century Italian scholar known for translating Greek patristic texts.</note> (who translates quite craftily) also display. A little further on, we will present an illustrious passage from a certain oration of **Germanus**, Patriarch of Constantinople <note>Germanus I, an 8th-century Patriarch who opposed early iconoclasm and heresy.</note>, which likewise shows us the Bogomils holding such views. 

Harmenopulus indeed mentions that the Bogomils rejected books of the New Testament alongside the Old, though I suspect this may have been an oversight on his part—unless, perhaps, one considers that there were some among them who, following the example of the ancient **Manichaeans** <note>A major dualist religion from the 3rd century that viewed the world as a struggle between light and darkness.</note>, wished to exclude certain books from the Canon, such as the *Acts of the Apostles* or the *Epistles of Peter*. From the fifth Anathema found in the *Refutation of the Massalians* <note>The Massalians were a sect accused of "praying incessantly" and holding dualist views similar to the Bogomils; they were often conflated by orthodox writers.</note> in the work of **Tollius** <note>Jacobus Tollius (1633–1696), a Dutch classicist and editor of historical texts.</note> on page 116, one might conclude that they, following the example of other heretics, substituted other books that better suited their views for the rejected Scriptures. As Tollius rightly notes, the **Massalians** once established their own *Asceticum* <note>A lost heretical book of the Massalians used as their spiritual guide.</note> as a kind of canon. The judgment stands thus: "To those who introduce other scriptures than those handed down to us by the Holy Spirit and the Holy Fathers: **Anathema.**" <note>original Greek: Τοῖς παρεισάγουσιν ἑτέρας γραφάς... ἀνάθεμα. 'Anathema' is a formal curse or excommunication by the Church.</note>

## II.
Nor is the reason obscure as to why they especially rejected the books of **Moses**. They saw that these books most strongly opposed their opinions—opinions which they held partly regarding creation (which they believed derived from an evil principle) and partly regarding the **Decalogue** <note>The Ten Commandments.</note>, which they believed was repeated or authored by Satan.

# ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE BOGOMILS.

### H

For this reason I wonder all the more that **Spanheim** <note>Friedrich Spanheim the Younger (1632–1701), a Calvinist theologian and church historian.</note>, in his *Ecclesiastical History* p. 1601, records that the Bogomils do not receive the Mosaic books <note>The Pentateuch, the first five books of the Bible attributed to Moses.</note> and certain other Canonical ones, while yet admitting the **Decalogue** <note>The Ten Commandments.</note> and the Prophetic books. I do not doubt that this slipped the mind of this Man (who was otherwise rightly accomplished in these studies) by a fault of memory; when he intended to mention the **Psalter** <note>The Book of Psalms.</note>, he mentioned the Decalogue in its place. 

Furthermore, **Euthymius** <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century Byzantine monk and the primary source for this sect's history.</note> observes that the Bogomils, regarding the rejection of the writings of Moses, followed in the footsteps of the **Paulicians** <note>A dualist Christian sect originating in Armenia in the 7th century, often seen as ancestors to the Bogomils.</note>. "Therefore," he says, "they learned the rejection of the Mosaic books and those following them from the heresy of the Paulicians. One must look under the title against them for the chapters inscribed thus: 'that the ancient legislation and those who shone within it <note>Referring to the Old Testament prophets and figures.</note> belong to the good God.' And then one must first read the first chapter, and the following seven, which clearly pillory such error; and almost all things after those are in agreement with these seven, but the ones indicated alone suffice." <note>original Greek: Τὴν μὲν οὖν ἀποβολὴν... τῶν Μωσαϊκῶν βίβλων... ἀρκεῖ μόνα τὰ δηλωθέντα.</note> This passage from his *Panoplia* <note>The *Panoplia Dogmatica*, Euthymius's massive work refuting heresies.</note> which Euthymius points out here, where he refuted that Paulician error (which the Bogomils later made their own), is found in Title XX, p. 205. 

It is also clear from other sources that those Paulicians—named after **Paul and John** who restored **Manichaeism** <note>An ancient dualist religion teaching a struggle between a good spiritual world of light and an evil material world of darkness.</note> in the 9th Century, and for that reason called **Paulojohannists** <note>original Greek: παυλοϊωάννας</note> by **Blastares** <note>Matthew Blastares, a 14th-century monk and legal scholar.</note>—borrowed this error along with the rest from the Manichaeans. Examine them, if you please, in **Suidas** <note>The Suda, a 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia.</note> under the entry *Manes*, **Cedrenus** Volume II. p. 480, **Anna Comnena** p. 50 of the *Alexiad*, and **Zonaras** in his *Annals* where he explains regarding Michael. Among more recent authors, consult **Bossuet** in his *History of the Variations* (as he called it), Book II no. 13 p. 128, **Petavius** XV. 7 *On the Incarnation*, **Jean-Baptiste Cotelier** in Volume I of *Greek Monuments* p. 738, and **Bayle** in Volume III of his *Dictionary* p. 2322. 

For my part, I will add nothing more in this place concerning these forerunners of the Bogomil doctrine once I have cited the passage from Euthymius in Greek, which confirms the reasoning for the name I previously gave. "From these two," he says, "Paul and John, to their disciples..." <note>original Greek: Ἀπὸ τούτων τῶν δύο... Παύλου καὶ Ἰωάννα τοῖς μαθηταῖς αὐ-</note>

# ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE BOGOMILS.

...[how he] led those many thousands through him, you will find under the title against the **Paulicians** <note>A dualist Christian sect originating in Armenia in the 7th century, often seen as theological ancestors to the Bogomils.</note>, just as we discussed in the chapter before this. **Harmenopoulos** <note>Constantine Harmenopoulos (c. 1320–1383), a Byzantine jurist whose "Epitome of Heretical Opinions" is a major source here.</note> agrees closely with this in his book *On the Opinions of Heretics*: <note>original: "τὰς τῆς παλαιᾶς καὶ καινῆς ἀθετῶσι βίβλους μετὰ τοῦ ἐν αὐταῖς γεγραμμένου Θεοῦ, ὡς κατ' ἐπίπνοιαν γραφείσας τοῦ Σατανᾶ, πλὴν μόνον ἑπτὰ, ὧν καί ἐστι τὸ Εὐαγγέλιον."</note> "They reject the books of the Old and New Testaments along with the God written about in them, as having been written under the inspiration of **Satan**, except only seven, among which is the Gospel." These words are indeed so consistent with those of **Euthymius** <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, the 12th-century monk whose "Panoplia Dogmatica" is the primary source for this study.</note> that Harmenopoulos seems merely to have repeated them, except that instead of <unclear>ἐπ...</unclear>νοιαν—a word which the Fellian manuscript and the interpretation of Euthymius himself (through the cunning of the translator) displays—he reads *epipnoian* <note>Greek: ἐπίπνοιαν, meaning "divine inspiration" or "breathing upon."</note> with the same meaning. A little later we shall bring forward an illustrious passage from a certain Oration of **Germanus, Patriarch of Constantinople** <note>Germanus II (r. 1223–1240), who wrote against the Bogomils during the Second Bulgarian Empire.</note>, which likewise presents the Bogomils to us as holding these views.

Harmenopoulos indeed mentions that besides the books of the Old Testament, the new ones were also rejected by the Bogomils; however, I think this slipped his mind while he was not paying attention, unless perhaps you consider that there were some among them who, following the example of the ancient **Manichaeans** <note>Followers of the prophet Mani (3rd century), who taught a dualistic struggle between light and darkness.</note>, wished for certain books of the New Testament—for instance, the **Acts of the Apostles** or the **Petrine Epistles**—to be excluded from the Code. From the Fifth Anathematization, which appears in the *Refutation of the Massalians* in **Tollius** <note>Jacobus Tollius (1633–1696), a Dutch scholar who published Byzantine texts.</note> on page 116, you may gather that they, following the example of other heretics, rejected books of Scripture and substituted others more in line with their views; just as Tollius rightly notes in his remarks, the **Massalians** <note>A 4th-century mystic sect, also called Euchites, often accused of the same dualistic errors as the Bogomils.</note> once established their own *Asceticon* <note>A manual of spiritual exercises.</note> as a kind of canon. The decree states: <note>original: "τοῖς παρεισάγωσιν ἑτέρας γραφὰς, ἢ τὰς ὑπὸ τοῦ ἁγίου πνεύματος καὶ παρὰ τῶν ἁγίων Πατρῶν ἡμῖν παραδοθείσας, ἀνάθεμα."</note> "**Anathema** to those who introduce other scriptures besides those handed down to us by the Holy Spirit and from the Holy Fathers."

II. Nor is the reason obscure as to why they rejected the books of **Moses** above all. For they saw these as especially opposing those opinions which they had embraced, partly concerning creation—which in their view was to be derived from an evil principle—and partly concerning the **Decalogue** <note>The Ten Commandments.</note>, which they believed originated likewise from Satan.

# ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE BOGOMILS.

For this reason, I am the more surprised that **Spanheim** <note>Friedrich Spanheim the Younger (1632–1701), a professor of theology and church history at Leiden whose works were standard in the 17th century.</note> reports in his *Ecclesiastical History*, page 1601, that the *Bogomils do not receive the Mosaic books and certain other canonical ones, though they admitted the Decalogue and the Prophetic books.* I have no doubt that this escaped that man—who was otherwise well-trained in these studies—due to a failure of memory; when he intended to mention the **Psalter** <note>The Book of Psalms, which many dualist groups like the Bogomils did accept, unlike the rest of the Old Testament.</note>, he mentioned the **Decalogue** <note>The Ten Commandments.</note> in its place. Moreover, **Euthymius** <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century monk and the primary source for the author's information on these sects.</note> observes that, regarding the rejection of the writings of Moses, the Bogomils followed in the footsteps of the **Paulicians** <note>A dualist Christian sect that appeared in Armenia in the 7th century, often viewed as the theological precursors to the Bogomils.</note>. 

*He says*: <note>original: "Τὴν μὲν οὖν ἀποβολὴν τῶν Μωσαϊκῶν βίβλων καὶ τῶν μετ’ αὐτὰς ἐκ τῆς τῶν Παυλικιάνων αἱρέσεως μεμαθήκασι. ζητητέον δὲ ἐν τῷ κατ’ ἐκείνους τίτλῳ τὰ κεφάλαια, οἷς ἐπιγέγραπται, ὅτι τοῦ ἀγαθοῦ Θεοῦ εἰσιν ἥγε παλαιὰ νομοθεσία καὶ οἱ ἐν αὐτῇ λάμψαντες. Καὶ ἀναγνωστέον τότε πρῶτον κεφάλαιον, καὶ τὰ ἑξῆς ἑπτὰ, στηλιτεύοντα φανερῶς τὴν τοιαύτην πλάνην, καὶ τὰ μετ’ ἐκεῖνα δὲ πάντα σύμφωνα σχεδὸν τοῖς ἑπτὰ τούτοις εἰσὶν, ἀλλ’ ἀρκεῖ μόνα τὰ δηλωθέντα."</note> "They learned the rejection of the books of Moses and those that follow them from the heresy of the Paulicians. One should seek the chapters in the section [of my book] against them [the Paulicians], which are titled, 'That the Ancient Legislation and those who shone within it belong to the Good God.' And one should then read the first chapter and the next seven, which openly denounce such error; almost all those following are in agreement with these seven, but the ones mentioned are enough." 

This passage of his *Panoplia* <note>The "Panoplia Dogmatica," a massive theological work by Euthymius written to defend Orthodox Christianity against various heresies.</note> is indicated here by Euthymius, where he refuted that Paulician error which the Bogomils later made their own; it is found in Title XX, page 205. It is established from other sources as well that those Paulicians—named after **Paul and John** who restored **Manichaeism** <note>A major dualist religion founded by the Persian prophet Mani (3rd century AD) which taught that the material world is evil and the spiritual world is good.</note> in the 9th century, and therefore called <term>παυλοϊωάννης</term> <note>Paul-Johns: a compound name used by later Byzantine writers to mock the sect's alleged founders.</note> by **Blastares** <note>Matthew Blastares, a 14th-century monk and legal scholar.</note>—borrowed this error, along with others, from the Manichaeans. 

Look into them, if you please, in **Suidas** <note>The Suda, a massive 10th-century Greek encyclopedia.</note> under the word *Manes*, **Cedrenus** <note>George Kedrenos, an 11th-century historian.</note> Volume II, page 480, **Anna Comnena** <note>A Byzantine princess and historian whose "Alexiad" chronicles the reign of her father, Alexios I.</note> page 50 of the *Alexiad*, and **Zonaras** <note>John Zonaras, a 12th-century chronicler and legal expert.</note> in his *Annals* where he explains about Michael; from more recent authors, add **Bossuet** <note>The influential French Catholic bishop and writer (1627–1704).</note> in the history of "Variations," as he called it, Book II, number 13, page 128; **Petavius** <note>Denis Pétau, a 17th-century Jesuit theologian.</note> XV. 7 on the *Incarnation*; **Jean-Baptiste Cotelerius** in Volume I of *Greek Monuments*, page 738; and **Bayle** <note>Pierre Bayle, author of the "Historical and Critical Dictionary" (1697).</note> in Volume III of his *Dictionary*, page 2322. I will add nothing further in this place regarding these precursors to the Bogomil doctrine, once I have provided the Greek passage from Euthymius, which confirms the reasoning for the name I previously gave. *He says*: "From these two, Paul and John, to their disciples..."

# ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE BOGOMILS.

...of the leaders, the name of the **Paulicians** was applied, composed in a rather barbaric manner; and instead of "Paul-Johns," they are called Paulicians. <note>original: "γῶν, ἡ προσωνυμία τῶν Παυλικιάνων ἐπετέθη, συντεθεῖσα βαρβαρικώτερον, καὶ ἀντὶ τοῦ Παυλοϊωάνναι παυλικιάνοι κατονομάζονται." The text suggests the name is a corruption of the names of two early leaders, Paul and John.</note> **Cotelerius** <note>Jean-Baptiste Cotelier (1629–1686), a French scholar of early Christian Greek texts.</note> also provided this passage in Greek. Indeed, that this was once the opinion of the **Manichaeans** <note>A major dualist religion founded by the prophet Mani, which taught that the physical world was evil and created by a lesser deity.</note> is evident—to say nothing of other evidence—from what **Archelaus**, Bishop of Mesopotamia, sternly warned against **Mani** in that dissertation which the illustrious **Zacagnius** <note>Lorenzo Alessandro Zaccagni (1657–1712), a Vatican librarian who published collections of ancient Greek and Latin ecclesiastical works.</note> recently and very wisely published in Volume I of *Ancient Monuments of the Greek and Latin Church*. See especially pages 25, 48, as well as 70 and 71.

To this I will add a passage from **Photius** <note>A 9th-century Patriarch of Constantinople and a massive figure in Byzantine scholarship.</note> regarding the Manichaeans, which is read in Book I of his work against the Manichaeans, which has not yet been published, and of which a copy <note>original: "ἀπόγραφον" (apographon)</note> exists in the Johannine Library in Hamburg. He says: *But indeed, they also turn away from the holy prophets and the whole of the Old Testament and the other saints, calling them robbers and thieves.* <note>original: "ἀλλὰ γὰρ, inquit, καὶ τὰς ἱερὰς προφήτας καὶ πᾶσαν τὴν παλαιὰν γραφὴν καὶ τοὺς ἄλλους ἀποστρέφονται ἁγίους, λῃστὰς αὐτοὺς καὶ κλέπτας ἀποκαλοῦντες."</note> I do not wish to mention others from the choir of ancient heretics who "blew the same pipes," <note>An idiomatic way of saying they repeated the same false teachings.</note> rashly rejecting now more, now fewer writings of the **Old Covenant**. **Johann Gerhard** <note>A prominent 17th-century Lutheran theologian.</note>, a theologian of immortal merit to the Church, counted their number in his theological disputation in which the passage of **1 John 5:7** <note>Known as the "Johannine Comma," a phrase used to support the doctrine of the Trinity that was often missing from early manuscripts.</note> is defended.

### IV.
However, the habit of those who hold perverse views is often to be inconsistent with themselves. You may observe this no less among the **Bogomils**, who, as if forgetting themselves, were accustomed to summon those same writings—which they had previously pierced with an **obelus** <term>obelus: a symbol (÷) used by ancient scholars to mark text as suspicious or spurious</term>—as if they were worthy of agreement whenever they seemed to bear witness to their own opinion in some way. Unless, perhaps, you would rather say that they drew "arguments tailored to the man" <note>original: "κατ' ἄνθρωπον" (kat' anthrōpon) — a rhetorical technique of using an opponent's own beliefs against them.</note> from those books of Scripture. The same **Euthymius** <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, the author's primary source for Bogomil practices.</note> mentions this in the place cited, with these words: *It must be known that, while they reject the books of Moses, they often craftily adopt sayings from them to serve as their own defense.* <note>original: "χρὴ δὲ γινώσκειν, ὅτι ἀθετοῦντες τὰς Μωσαϊκὰς βίβλους ῥήσεις ἐξ αὐτῶν πολλάκις πανέργως εἰς συνηγορίαν ἑαυτῶν προσλαμβάνουσι."</note> 

Of those books which they did accept, the holy songs of **David** <note>The Book of Psalms.</note> were most often on their lips when they disputed against those of right belief. This appears clearly from the sermon by **Germanus**, Patriarch of Constantinople, on the first Sunday of Lent, which is read in **Gretser**, Volume II, *On the Cross*, page 549, since from the Davidic...

->### ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE BOGOMILS.<-

...especially with the **Davidic Oracles** <note>The Psalms of David</note> they strive to establish the cult of images in that place. Indeed, **Germanus** <note>Germanus I, Patriarch of Constantinople (c. 634–740), a staunch defender of icons</note> brought this point as a prejudice against their cause on page 559. "And how is it," he says, "that you, a **Bogomil**, while casting out the books of the **Old Law** <note>The Old Testament</note> and saying that its legislation is from the evil one and not from the good God, and having condemned all these things to silence, extract only this small portion of verses and offer them to us? For you ought to accept the whole Law, or if you do not accept it, you should not accept even this short-syllabled interlude." <note>original: "Καὶ πῶς... ὁ Βογόμιλος σὺ τὰ βιβλία τοῦ παλαιοῦ νόμου ἐξοβελίζων... μηδὲ τὸ βραχυσύλλαβον τοῦτο διάψαλμα καταδέξασθαι." Germanus is pointing out the logical inconsistency of the Bogomils using the Psalms to support their arguments while rejecting the rest of the Old Testament as the work of an evil creator.</note> 

This argument would have carried greater weight if the Bogomils had attempted to argue from any other book of the Old Testament besides the **Davidic songs**, which they held in high regard. But their perverse opinion regarding Scripture did not stop here; instead, since they realized that the falsehood of their doctrines could be demonstrated from it even so, they desired to use the **allegorical method** of interpretation as a defense. By its power, they could twist the words of Scripture brought against them—even from the books which in their own judgment were written under divine auspices—into a different meaning. 

For **Euthymius** <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century Byzantine monk and author of the "Panoply of Dogma"</note> continues thus: "And whenever they are cornered by certain sayings of the **seven books** <note>The Bogomil canon typically consisted of the four Gospels, the Acts of the Apostles, the Epistles, and the Psalms—totaling seven volumes in their reckoning.</note> mentioned and are driven toward the truth, they immediately turn to allegory, attempting to escape." <note>original: "Καὶ ὅταν δὲ πρὸς τίνων ῥητῶν... εἰς ἀλληγορίαν τρέπονται..."</note> **Harmenopulus** <note>Constantine Harmenopoulos, a 14th-century jurist and theologian from Thessaloniki</note> confirms the same, saying: "They misinterpret these as well, and with blasphemous and loathsome ideas they transform them to suit their own opinions." <note>original: "παρερμηνεύουσι δὲ... μεταφέρουσιν."</note> 

Indeed, they owe this practice to the **Manichaeans** <note>Followers of Mani, a 3rd-century dualist religion that heavily influenced later medieval heresies</note>, concerning whom **Photius** <note>A 9th-century Patriarch of Constantinople and scholar</note> left the same testimony in Book 1 of his manuscript against the Manichaeans. Speaking of the **Pauline epistles**—which they had otherwise received into their canon—he says: "in which there are some things hard to understand, which the ignorant and unstable twist, as they do the other scriptures, to their own destruction." <note>original: "ἐν οἷς ἐστι δυσνόητά τινα... πρὸς τὴν ἰδίαν αὐτῶν ἀπώλειαν." Photius is quoting 2 Peter 3:16 to describe the heretics' methods.</note> And after a few interjected words: "These are the ones who truly twist and distort to their own destruction." <note>original: "ὗτοί εἰσιν, οἱ ὡς ἀληθῶς στρεβλοῦντες..."</note> 

Evidently, this was the defining characteristic of heretics: to [distort] the sense of Scripture through allegories and far-fetched interpreta-

# ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE BOGOMILS.

...to pervert the Scriptures with **allegorical interpretations**, as the author of *The Vanquished Moses* <note>original: *Mosis Vincati*; likely a reference to a polemical work by Caspar Heunisch (1684) criticizing various sectarian interpretations</note> proved on page 112 using various examples of both ancient and more recent heretics. For further context, see also the distinguished **Buddeus’s** <note>Johann Franz Buddeus (1667–1729), a prominent Lutheran theologian</note> dissertation on the *Allegories of Origen* and **John Spencer’s** *On the Ritual Laws of the Hebrews*, Book I, chapter 11, section 5. We shall cite one especially illustrious passage from **Irenaeus** <note>A 2nd-century bishop and theologian famous for his work against Gnosticism</note> concerning the **Simonians** <note>Followers of Simon Magus, often considered the first heretics of the Christian era</note>, whom the **Bogomils** imitated in other matters. This passage is found in Book III, chapter 12 of *Against Heresies*: *"All of them,"* he says, *"puffed up by the false name of Knowledge <term>Gnosis; the intellectual "insight" claimed by heretical groups</term>, indeed acknowledge the Scriptures, but they distort the interpretations, as we showed in the first book."* It is manifest, even if we remain silent, how closely those people in our own memory approach this example—those who have dared to snatch the prophecies of the Old Testament by **allegorizing** <note>original: ἀλληγοροῦντες (allegorizing)</note> them in whatever way possible to stabilize their own opinions.

V. **Euthymius** <note>Euthymius Zygabenus, a 12th-century Byzantine monk and the primary historical source for Bogomil beliefs</note> does not merely hint that this same practice greatly pleased the Bogomils, but he demonstrated it with clear examples set forth in section XXVIII and those following. Euthymius paves the way for these examples with the following words: *"They misinterpret,"* he says, *"all seven of the aforementioned books <note>The books of the Old Testament that the Bogomils partially accepted, such as the Psalms and the Prophets</note>, twisting their words and turning them away from their correct meaning. They shift the meaning to suit their own views. Whatever is said in those books concerning sinners, the impious, and idolaters, they apply to the faithful of our time; but whatever is said about those who are pleasing to God, they claim for themselves. They assert with full certainty that they are the elect, the righteous, and the heirs of God. It would be a long task, requiring much time, to write out their explanation—or rather, their perversion and total corruption of the established text—of every book. It would be a clear waste of effort and a labor resulting in nothing useful. For after reading only a little way, the readers would be immediately exhausted by the absurdities of their blasphemies. Just like those suffering from seasickness, they would become full of dizziness and vertigo and would, I know well, immediately cast away such writings, spit upon them, and turn away with great loathing..."*

# ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE BOGOMILS.

...pouring down their nonsense <note>original: μωρίαν (folly/nonsense)</note> upon us. But lest we seem to be inventing a defense and trying to escape the labor involved in these matters, we shall explain a few sayings from the Gospel according to Matthew interpreted according to their madness—not to say their distortion—so that we may show the whole jar from a single taste, the source from the stream, and the fabric from the hem. We shall not, however, apply refutations to these twisted interpretations, as they are self-refuting. For the discourse that follows is not meant to be argumentative, but merely indicative of their distorted explanation of the books they have accepted.

I omit the Latin version out of a desire for brevity, as it can be read in the *Library of the Fathers* <note>original: *Bibliotheca Patrum*; a large collection of early Christian writings</note>, though it would be corrected in no small part by someone intelligent and knowledgeable in the language. Euthymius, having made this introduction, follows the Gospel of Matthew in order and reviews certain notable examples of their allegories, some of which we will present here, leaving the rest for our *Treatise on Morality*.

Regarding Rachel, who is mentioned in Matthew 2:18, they imagine that she was a widow who had two daughters whom she presented to Herod dressed as males, and then wept for them when they were carried off with the other boys. By her, they want to represent the Heavenly Father, whose "daughters" are the soul of Adam and the soul of Christ; they claim these were snatched away by the Ruler of this world <term>the "Kosmokrator," often identified by Bogomils as the evil Demiurge or Satan who rules the physical realm</term>, casting the Father into intense grief. The words of **Euthymius** <note>Euthymius Zygabenus, a 12th-century Byzantine monk</note> in Section 29 are as follows:

"They weave a myth that Rachel was a certain widow who had two daughters of an infant age. When Herod was gathering male infants, she supposed they would be granted honor and favors by him, so she disguised them as males and brought them forward as sons. But when these too were slaughtered along with the others as males, the other mothers simply wept, but Rachel wept inconsolably because, in her haste to outwit the King, she was herself outwitted, and lost her daughters in turn. Allegorizing the passage, they say that Rachel is the Heavenly Father, and his children are the soul of Adam and that of Christ. When these were destroyed by Herod—meaning the Ruler of this world—the Father wept inconsolably due to their destruction."
