<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Bogomils' allegorical methods, specifically focusing on how they reinterpret New Testament figures to fit their anti-Old Testament and dualist worldview. It transitions from the story of Rachel to the story of the woman with the flow of blood.</meta>

# ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE BOGOMILS.

These same people say that the woman with the **flow of blood** <note>original: *αἱμοῤῥοοῦσαν* (haemorrhoousan)</note>, of whom we read in Matthew 9:20, is the Church of Jerusalem; they claim she finally ceased to suffer from the effusion of blood from the victims' sacrifices throughout the twelve tribes only when the city itself was overturned. *They understand the woman,* says Euthymius in Section 51, *who had a flow of blood for twelve years to be the Church in Jerusalem, bleeding with the blood of sacrifices poured out among the twelve tribes of Israel. For they say Christ stopped this flow of blood by destroying Jerusalem shortly thereafter. But how could "twelve years" be understood as "twelve tribes"? For a year is a movement and interval of time, while a tribe is a race of men. How great is the difference between a person and time!* <note>original: Γυναῖκα νοοῦσιν... καὶ χρόνου</note> With this **madness for allegory** <note>original: *ἀλληγορομανία* (allegoromania)</note> they joined another opinion that is no more acceptable, which served to excuse or make their foolish interpretations of Scripture seem plausible. For they said that those who have not yet put off the flesh cannot understand the Oracles of Scripture. Euthymius observed this error in Section 39. Since the Bogomils loathed the use of marriage, yet could not answer the Savior’s words—where He said a wife should not be divorced except for the sake of fornication—they retreated to this defense, saying that *these matters are secret and uninterpretable, and known only to those who lay aside the flesh.* <note>original: *ἀπόῤῥητα εἶναι ταῦτα καὶ ἀνερμήνευτα, καὶ μόνοις γνώριμα τοῖς ἀποτιθεμένοις τὴν σάρκα*</note> It is evident, even without my judgment, that this same sentiment is being proposed in our own age by some using these very same formulas of speech.

VI. Since they have run wild through the Holy Book with such unbridled audacity and a reckless passion for interpretation, one can certainly expect nothing but all sorts of insolent opinions from those who never find themselves lacking the means to prove from Scripture whatever they have previously conceived in their minds. This will be further understood if we follow the other articles of faith in order according to their opinion. Regarding **GOD**, they think and speak profanely and almost after the example of the Pagans. God

<meta>This page continues the investigation into Bogomil theology, shifting from their allegorical interpretations of the New Testament to their specific, controversial physical descriptions of the Trinity.</meta>

# ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE BOGOMILS.

For they conceive of Him in human form, yet they do not wish to be seen as establishing Him as corporeal. It appears from the things Euthymius <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century Byzantine monk and critic of heresy.</note> comments on regarding their beliefs that they made a distinction between a corporeal <term>corporeal</term><note>Having a physical, material body.</note> and a human form. <margin>Section III.</margin> "They say," he writes, "that the Son and the Holy Spirit were dissolved back into the Father from whom they proceeded, and that He—having appeared as three persons from the year 5500 <note>In the Byzantine calendar, the world was believed to have been created in 5508 BCE; 5500 marks a significant prophetic milestone.</note> until thirty-three years [later]—became once more a single person: incorporeal indeed, but in human form." <note>original: "Λέγουσι... ἀνθρωπόμορφον δέ."</note>

Euthymius asserts that a conflict exists between these claims, adding this judgment: "Alas for such great stupidity! Oh, what broad laughter! For if he is incorporeal, how can he be human-shaped?" <note>original: "Φεῦ τῆς πολλῆς ἀναισθησίας! ὢ τοῦ πλατέος γέλωτος! εἰ γὰρ ἀσώματος, πῶς ἀνθρωπόμορφος;"</note>

Moreover, according to the testimony of Euthymius in Section II, they outlined that human form more fully in this way: "Indeed," he says, "they put forward a pretense to deceive the more simple-minded by claiming to believe in the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit. But they attach these three names to the Father, and they imagine Him to be human-faced, with a ray shining out from either temple—one for the Son, and the other for the Spirit. And so, their faith ends up in some sort of bodily and monstrously-shaped God, and thus one who does not exist." <note>original: "Πρόσχημα μὲν... καὶ οὕτως ἀνύπαρκτον."</note>

Harmenopulus <note>Constantine Harmenopulus, a 14th-century Byzantine jurist and theologian.</note> expresses the same opinion when, according to the mind of the Bogomils, he attributes the appearance of an old man with a long beard to God the Father, a youth coming into manhood to the Son, and a beardless adolescent to the Holy Spirit. "They say," he writes, "that they see not only in dreams but also while awake the Father as an old man sitting with a substantial beard; the Son as resembling one just passing into his first beard; and the Holy Spirit as a youth with cheeks utterly smooth." <note>original: "λέγουσι... τὰς παρειάς."</note>

Similar to these are the words of Euthymius found in Section 23: "They say," he says, "that often, not only in dreams but also while awake, they see the Father as a deep-bearded old man, the Son as a man with a beginning beard, and the Holy Spirit as a smooth-faced youth—the demons easily transforming themselves into these forms and deceiving them, teaching them that the Holy Trinity is unequal." <note>original: "Λέγουσι... τὴν ἁγίαν Τριάδα"</note>

<summary>This page describes the Bogomils' anthropomorphic view of God, where they visualize the Trinity as three distinct ages of a human male, a belief their critics mocked as absurd and demonic.</summary>

# ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE BOGOMILS.

...sowing among them a trinity and a distinction based on the variety of shapes, along with the Arian <term>Arian</term><note>Referring to Arianism, a 4th-century belief that the Son was distinct from and subordinate to the Father, rather than being of the same essence.</note> division of the single Nature. <note>original: "τριάδα καὶ διάφορον κατὰ τὴν τῶν σχημάτων διαφορὰν καὶ τὴν Ἀρειανικὴν κατατομὴν τῆς μιᾶς Φύσεως τούτοις ἐγκατασπειρόντων."</note>

VII. Indeed, this opinion concerning God seemed so absurd to some that they thought it was not so much truly attributed to the Bogomils as it was fabricated out of the weakness of a malicious mind. Among these is Friedrich Spanheim <note>Friedrich Spanheim the Younger (1632–1701), a Dutch Calvinist theologian known for his works on church history.</note>, who here accuses the temperament and prejudices of the "little Greeks" <note>A disparaging term for Byzantine writers, implying they were prone to exaggeration or deceit.</note>. He finds an ally in Gottfried Arnold <note>Gottfried Arnold (1666–1714), a radical pietist whose "Heresiology" famously argued that the so-called heretics were often more "Christian" than the established Church.</note> in his *Heresiology*, who also, in his usual manner and without citing an authority, was about to complain with a truly impudent mouth about the "impudence of the Orthodox," as he is accustomed to speak. 

I, for my part, see no reason why we should doubt that the Bogomils established that God was clothed in human form and appearance, since other no less unusual opinions are recorded about them. If we wish to dismiss these likewise as fictions, there is nothing left in ecclesiastical history that we cannot call into doubt. Furthermore, this same opinion does not suffer from such great absurdity that it hasn't found other supporters at different times, who have existed in great numbers among both the Pagans and the Christians. 

As for non-Christian nations, Pierre Bayle <note>A famous French philosopher (1647–1706) known for his skepticism and his "Historical and Critical Dictionary."</note> seems to have pronounced more freely than the facts allow in Volume II of his *Continuation of Thoughts on the Comet*, Section 141, page 706, when he contends with every assurance that all of them imagined a corporeal <term>corporeal</term><note>Having a physical or material body.</note> God. For it is well known from the examples of Plato and Xenophon, to say nothing of others, that formerly some of the Pagan philosophers—and undoubtedly others following their example and imbued with their teachings—were most certainly persuaded of the spiritual nature of God. Certainly, Laërtius <note>Diogenes Laertius, a 3rd-century biographer of Greek philosophers.</note> records this of Plato in *Lives of the Philosophers*, Book III, Section 77; as does Maximus of Tyre in *Dissertation I*, and Alcinous in Chapter 10. Regarding Xenophon, Laërtius left the same testimony in Book IX, Section 19, where he is said to have removed the idea of a body from the Supreme Deity so thoroughly that he is reported to have asserted that nothing similar to a human could be found in Him. Many more of the same opinion...

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 56. The word beginning this page, "patrons" (tronos), completes the sentence from the previous page regarding those who defended the idea of a physical God.</meta>

# ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE BOGOMILS.

...[pa]trons from among the pagans are mentioned by *Petavius* <note>Denis Pétau (1583–1652), a highly influential French Jesuit theologian.</note> in Volume I of his *Theological Dogmas*, Book II, Chapter 1, page 88; and by *Cudworth* <note>Ralph Cudworth (1617–1688), an English philosopher and leader of the Cambridge Platonists.</note> in his *Intellectual System of the Universe*, Book I, Chapter 1, Section 19 and page 771 onwards. However, I would not deny that many others who held differing views are also numbered here, among whom *Epicurus* specifically called the Gods *human-like* or *human-shaped* <note>original: "ἀνθρωποειδεῖς vel ἀνθρωπομόρφους"</note> according to [Diogenes] Laertius (Book 10, section 139) and *Sextus Empiricus* in his work *Against the Mathematicians*, page 312. Others who defended the doctrine of *Epicurus* on this point were diligently praised by *Petavius* in Volume I of his *Dogmas*, page 86; by *Vogelsang* in *Theological Exercises* XII, number 14; and by the celebrated *Grapio* in his *Controversial Theology*, Chapter I, Question XI. Among Christians, there were those during the times of the early Church who maintained the same view, and those who persuade themselves—along with *Petavius*—that the opinion of the <term>Anthropomorphites</term><note>A group of Christians who believed God possessed a physical human body, interpreting the "image of God" literally.</note> only arose during the reign of Emperor *Theodosius*, are entirely mistaken. For it can be proven by a clear passage from the *Clementine Homilies* <note>A 2nd or 3rd-century collection of stories and sermons attributed to Clement of Rome.</note> (page 732) that the <term>Ebionites</term><note>A sect of early Jewish Christians who followed Jewish law and often held distinct views on the nature of God.</note> led the way for them; this was the passage cited in support of this view by *Theophilus Alethinus*—or rather, *Jean Le Clerc*, who wished to remain hidden under that name—in his notes on *Petavius’s Dogmas* at the place cited. I omit others, specifically the *Massalians*, *Seleucus*, and *Hermias*, whom *Petavius* (page 87) and *Thomas Milles* (in his notes on *Cyril of Jerusalem*, page 82) have proved from ancient monuments were not at all averse to this error. I am certain that *Melito* also increases this number, as I have no doubt that the title of his book, *On the Corporeality of God* <note>original: "περὶ θεοῦ ἐνσωμάτου"</note>, should be interpreted in this sense, even though I am aware that *Louis Ferrand*, an industrious and learned interpreter, in his notes on Psalm 5, and *Valesius* <note>Henri Valois (1603–1676), a French historian and philologist.</note>—who eventually changed his mind—and others have entered into different interpretations. See, if you please, *B. Ittig* in his excellent commentary on *The Heresiarchs*, Chapter XI, page 195. And indeed, this particular view of God appeared so absurd to some that they believed it was not so much attributed to the Bogomils out of truth as it was fashioned out of the powerlessness of a malicious mind. For my part, I see no reason why we should doubt that the **BOGOMILS** maintained that **GOD** was clothed in human form and appearance, since other equally unusual opinions are recorded about them. If we were to say that these, too, are merely fictions, there would be nothing left in Church history that we could not call into doubt. Furthermore, this same opinion does not suffer from such great absurdity that it has not found other supporters at different times, who have existed in great numbers among both the Pagans and the Christians.

Regarding the non-Christian nations, **Pierre Bayle** <note>Pierre Bayle (1647–1706), a French philosopher whose "Historical and Critical Dictionary" and "Thoughts on the Comet" were foundational to the Enlightenment.</note> seems to have spoken more freely than the facts allow in Volume II of his *Continuation of Thoughts on the Comet*, section 141, page 706, when he contends with every assurance that they all imagined a corporeal God. Regarding the latter, Laertius likewise left a testament in Book IX, section 19, where he is said to have so removed the idea of a body from the Supreme Deity that he is reported to have asserted that nothing similar to a human can be found in Him. -># ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE BOGOMILS.<-

Those among the pagans who mention divine thrones are recorded by Petavius <note>Denis Pétau (1583–1652), a Jesuit theologian and scholar</note> in Volume I of *Theological Dogmas*, Book II, chapter 1, page 88, and by Cudworth <note>Ralph Cudworth (1617–1688), a leading figure of the Cambridge Platonists</note> in *The Intellectual System of the Universe*, Book I, chapter 1, section 19 and page 771 following. Yet I would not deny that many who hold different views are numbered among them, among whom Epicurus specifically called the Gods **of human form** or **human-shaped** <note>original: ἀνθρωποειδεῖς vel ἀνθρωπομόρφους</note> according to the authority of Laertius (X. 139) and Sextus Empiricus in *Against the Mathematicians* (page 312). Others who defend the doctrine of Epicurus on this point have been diligently praised by Petavius in Volume I of his *Dogmas* (page 86), by Vogelfang in *Theological Exercises* XII (number 14), and by the celebrated Grapius in *Controversial Theology* (chapter I, question XI).

Among Christians, there were those even during the era of the early Church who maintained the same view, and those who persuade themselves, along with Petavius, that the opinion of the **Anthropomorphites** <term>Anthropomorphites: a group of Christians who believed that God possessed a physical human body, often interpreting biblical descriptions of God's "hands" or "eyes" literally</term> only emerged during the reign of the Emperor Theodosius are entirely mistaken. For it can be proven from a clear passage in the *Clementine Homilies* (page 732) that the **Ebionites** <note>A Jewish-Christian movement that flourished during the early centuries of the Christian Era</note> led the way in these matters; this passage was brought forward in support of this view by Theophilus Alethinus—or rather, the man who wished to hide under that name, **Jean Le Clerc**—in his notes to Petavius’s *Dogmas* at the location cited. I omit others, specifically the **Massalians**, Seleucus, and Hermias, whom Petavius (page 87) and Thomas Milles in his notes on Cyril of Jerusalem (page 82) have proved from ancient records were not averse to this error. I hold it for certain that **Melito** also increases this number, as I do not doubt that the title of his book *On the God who is Embodied* <note>original: περὶ θεοῦ ἐνσωμάτου — Melito of Sardis was a 2nd-century bishop; scholars have long debated whether this lost work argued for God having a body or simply discussed the Incarnation of Christ</note> must be interpreted in this sense, although I am aware that Louis Ferrandus, an industrious and learned interpreter, in his notes on Psalm V, as well as Valesius (who eventually changed his mind), and others have entered into a different interpretation. See, if you please, Bartholomaeus Ittigius in his excellent commentary on the **Heresiarchs** <term>Heresiarchs: the founders or leaders of a movement deemed heretical by the established Church</term> (chapter XI, page 195). As these matters are settled, the famous Le Clerc acted inconsiderately in the *Judgments of the Dutch Theologians on Critical History*...

-># ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE BOGOMILS.<-

...mention thrones <note>The author likely refers to the "thrones" or seats of the gods in pagan mythology</note> from among the pagans, as noted by Petavius <note>Denis Pétau (1583–1652), a French Jesuit theologian and scholar</note> in Volume I of his *Theological Dogmas*, Book 2, Chapter 1, page 88, and by Cudworth <note>Ralph Cudworth (1617–1688), a leading figure among the Cambridge Platonists</note> in his *Intellectual System of the Universe*, Book 1, Chapter 1, Section 19, and page 771 onwards. Others who defend Epicurus’s doctrine on this point have been diligently praised by Petavius in Volume I of his *Dogmas*, page 86; by Vogelsang in *Theological Exercises* 12, number 14; and by the celebrated Grapius in his *Controversial Theology*, Chapter 1, Question 11.

Among Christians, there were those even in the early days of the Church who held the same view. Those who persuade themselves, along with Petavius, that the opinion of the Anthropomorphites <term>Anthropomorphites: A group of Christians who interpreted biblical descriptions of God literally, believing He possessed a human body</term> only emerged later during the reign of Emperor Theodosius are completely mistaken. For it can be proven from a clear passage in the *Clementine Homilies* <note>A series of early Christian writings once attributed to Pope Clement I</note>, page 732, that the Ebionites <note>A Jewish-Christian sect that maintained Jewish customs while following Jesus</note> led the way for them. This passage was brought forward to support this view by "Theophilus Alethinus"—or rather, the man who wished to hide behind that name, Jean Le Clerc—in his notes to Petavius’s *Dogmas* in the location mentioned. I omit others, specifically the Messalians <note>A sect that believed constant prayer was the only way to expel the demon that every person was born with</note>, Seleucus, and Hermias, whom Petavius (page 87) and Thomas Milles (in his notes to Cyril of Jerusalem, page 82) have demonstrated from ancient records were not averse to this error.

I am certain that Melito <note>Melito of Sardis, a prominent 2nd-century bishop and writer</note> should also be added to this number. I have no doubt that his book title **On God in a Body** <note>original Greek: *περὶ θεοῦ ἐνσωμάτου* (peri theou ensomatou)</note> should be interpreted in this sense, even though I am aware that Louis Ferrand—an industrious and learned translator—in his notes on Psalm 5, and Valesius <note>Henri Valois, a French classical scholar</note> (who eventually changed his opinion), and others have followed a different interpretation. See, if you please, Barthold Ittig in his excellent commentary *On the Heresiarchs*, Chapter 11, page 195. -># ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE BOGOMILS.<-

...criticism <note>The text likely refers to the *Critical History of the Old Testament* by Richard Simon</note> of Richard Simon <note>Richard Simon (1638–1712) was a French priest and influential biblical critic</note>, I believe he made Moses the patron of the Anthropomorphites <term>Anthropomorphites: A sect believing God possesses a human physical form</term>, a point for which he was rightly criticized by Herman Witsius <note>Herman Witsius (1636–1708) was a Dutch theologian</note> in his *Sacred Miscellanies*, Book 1, Chapter 7, Section 20.

VIII. It seemed these points should be discussed more extensively here, so it would be clear that those who think too kindly of the Bogomils <note>A medieval Gnostic-Manichaean sect from the Balkans</note> are mistaken—those who, having seen the absurdity of the idea of a physical God, believe it was impossible for the Bogomils to attribute a human form to Him. I see nothing that can be brought forward for this cause, except what appears from Euthymius <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century Byzantine monk who wrote against heresies</note>, who says the Bogomils called God **incorporeal** <note>original Greek: *ἀσώματον* (asomaton)</note>, and thus they might seem to overturn or distance themselves from **human-shaped form** <note>original Greek: *ἀνθρωπομορφίαν* (anthropomorphian)</note>. 

For if I see anything, they called God "incorporeal" to signify that He is equipped with a certain subtler matter, analogous to human bodies but not identical in every way. Thus Epicurus <note>Ancient Greek philosopher (341–270 BC)</note> also once said, although he stated his Gods **exist according to number** <note>original Greek: *κατ’ ἀριθμὸν εἶναι* (kat’ arithmon einai)</note>—which Kühn <note>Joachim Kühn (1647–1697), a German classical scholar and editor of Diogenes Laertius</note> interprets in his notes on Laertius (page 554) as **without body, without solidity, without corruption**—he nevertheless represented them to himself as **human-conformed** <note>original Greek: *ἀνθρωποειδεῖς* (anthropoeideis)</note> or **conformed to man**. 

According to Kühn’s interpretation, this comes from a continuous **inflow of similar forms tending toward one point, which never cease to arrive from infinite bodies and individuals, and meet so fortunately that from them the God is born in the shape of a human.** 

So too Audius <note>A 4th-century Christian from Mesopotamia who founded the Audaean sect</note>, the leader of the Anthropomorphites in his time, being afraid to attribute a "body" to God, nevertheless contended that His image was impressed upon the human body, as Petavius correctly advises in Volume I, Book 2, Chapter 1 of his *Dogmas*. However, if anyone should say that even in this way these assertions of the Bogomils, Audaeans, and others cannot be reconciled, I shall not fight them. But I do not doubt that what Cicero <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BC), the Roman statesman and philosopher</note> once wisely pronounced concerning the Epicurean **origin of the gods** <note>original Greek: *θεογονίᾳ* (theogonia)</note> can be adapted to our business: 

**"I would be ashamed to say I do not understand, if those very people who explain or defend these things understood them themselves."**

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Bogomils' theology, transitioning from their views on God's physical nature (anthropomorphism) to their controversial rejection of the traditional Trinity. It builds on the previous page's use of Euthymius Zigabenus as a primary source for understanding these medieval "heretics."</meta>

->## ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE BOGOMILS.<-

IX. With this monstrous opinion concerning the nature of the divine Power viewed absolutely, they joined another even worse, which refers to the **TRINITY** of persons. For while they retained this Trinity in words, in reality they abolished it. We have already cited in the previous sections a passage from Euthymius <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century Byzantine monk and author of the *Panoplia Dogmatica*</note>, who continues thus: 

> "Therefore, they took from the Sabellian <term>Sabellianism: A belief that the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit are not distinct persons, but merely different 'modes' or 'masks' worn by one God</term> heresy the practice of attaching the three aforementioned names to the Father; but the refutation of such a doctrine must be sought in the chapter against the Sabellians. However, the bodily nature and the strange quality of the form in the monstrous shape of the God they imagine will suffice for a rebuttal." <note>Original Greek: "τὸ μὲν οὖν προσάπτειν τῷ Πατρὶ τὰς εἰρημένας τρεῖς κλήσεις, ἐκ τῆς Σαβελλιανῆς αἱρέσεως ἔλαβον... τῇ μέντοι τερατομορφίᾳ τοῦ κατ' αὐτοὺς Θεοῦ σωματοειδὲς καὶ τὸ τῆς μορφῆς ἀλλόκοτον ἀρκέσει πρὸς ἔλεγχον."</note>

Thus, they affirmed that the names signifying distinct persons truly apply only to the Father and express His various manifestations. They shared this error not only with the Sabellians, but also with the Montanists, Samosatenians, Massalians (whom they follow in other matters), and Priscillianists. Concerning these, read Calovium <note>Abraham Calov (1612–1686), a prominent Lutheran theologian</note> in Volume III of his *System*, page 87, and Petavium <note>Dionysius Petavius (1583–1652), a Jesuit scholar known as the "Father of the History of Dogma"</note> in Volume II of his *Dogmas*, Book I, Chapter VI.

Hence it will seem surprising to no one that these same people did not fear to claim an end for the Son and the Holy Spirit, just as they claimed a beginning. For Euthymius records in Section III: 

> "They say that the Son and the Holy Spirit are dissolved back into the Father from whom they proceeded, and that He—having appeared as three-personed from the year 5,500 <note>This refers to the Byzantine calculation of the year of the world when Christ was born</note> until [the age of] thirty-three years—became single-personed again." <note>Original Greek: "Λέγουσι τὸν υἱὸν καὶ τὸ πνεῦμα τὸ ἅγιον εἰς τὸν Πατέρα πάλιν... πάλιν γενέσθαι μονοπρόσωπον."</note>

Regarding the origin of both persons—which they invent despite the protest of all Scripture—these worst of chronologists assigned it to the year 5,500 of the world. Euthymius mentioned this in Section IV: 

> "They say that there was neither a Son nor a Holy Spirit until the year 5,500; but from then on, they took the beginning of their being and their naming." <note>Original Greek: "Λέγουσι μήτε υἱὸν εἶναι, μήτε Πνεῦμα ἅγιον, ἄχρι τοῦ πεντακισχιλιοστοῦ πεντακοσιοστοῦ ἔτους."</note>

The same author refutes this foolish conviction in the following words: "Even though John the Evangelist thunders by saying: 'In the beginning was the Word...'" <note>original Greek: "ἐν ἀρχῇ ἦν ὁ λόγον" (en archē ēn ho logon)</note>.

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the Bogomils' concept of God. While the previous discussion (continued in the next section) addresses their views on the Trinity, this page focuses specifically on "Anthropomorphism"—the belief that God possesses a physical, human-like form. The author examines whether the Bogomils truly held this view or if they used technical language to mask it.</meta>

->## ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE BOGOMILS.<-

...criticism of Richard Simon <note>A 17th-century French scholar known as the father of modern biblical criticism</note>, I believe he has made Moses out to be a patron of the Anthropomorphites <note>A sect believing God has a human body</note>; for this reason, Herman Witsius <note>A Dutch theologian (1636–1708)</note> rightly critiqued him in his *Sacred Miscellanies*, Book I, Chapter 7, Section 20.

VIII. These matters seemed worth mentioning here in greater detail so that it might be clear that those who think too kindly of the Bogomils are mistaken—specifically those who, seeing the absurdity of the idea of a physical God, judge it impossible that the Bogomils could have attributed a human form to the Divine. I see nothing that can be brought forward to support their cause, except that it appears from the writings of Euthymius <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century Byzantine monk and the primary source for Bogomil beliefs</note> that the Bogomils called God <term>incorporeal</term> <note>original Greek: ἀσώματον (asōmaton)</note>, and thus would seem to overturn and push away <term>anthropomorphism</term> <note>the attribution of human traits or form to God; original Greek: ἀνθρωπομορφίαν</note> in this way.

However, as I see it, they called God "incorporeal" merely to signify that He is composed of a certain more subtle matter, analogous to human bodies, yet not similar in every respect. In the same way, Epicurus <note>The ancient Greek philosopher</note> long ago, although he said his gods *exist according to number* <note>original: κατ’ ἀριθμὸν εἶναι; meaning they are distinct individuals</note>—which Kühn <note>Joachim Kühn, a 17th-century philologist</note> interprets in his notes on Diogenes Laërtius (page 554) as meaning *without body, without solidity, without corruption*—nevertheless represented those same gods to himself as *human-like* <note>original Greek: ἀνθρωποειδεῖς (anthrōpoeideis)</note> or *conforming to the human shape*. This arose, according to Kühn’s interpretation, from a *continuous flow of similar images tending toward one point, which never cease to arrive from infinite bodies and individuals, and meet so fortunately that from them God is born in the form of a human.*

Likewise Audius <note>A 4th-century Syrian Christian accused of heresy</note>, the leader of the Anthropomorphites of his age, while fearing to attribute a physical body to God, nevertheless contended that God's image was impressed upon the human body, as Petavius <note>Denis Pétau, a prominent Jesuit theologian</note> rightly observes in Volume I, Book II, Chapter 1 of his *Dogmas*. If anyone should say that even in this way the assertions of the Bogomils, the Audians, and others cannot be reconciled, I will not fight him; but I have no doubt that what Cicero <note>The Roman statesman and philosopher</note> once wisely declared regarding the Epicurean <term>theogony</term> <note>the origin or birth of the gods; original Greek: θεογονίᾳ</note> can be applied to our current business:

> *I would be ashamed to say that I do not understand, if those very people who explain or defend such things understood them themselves.*

<meta>catchword: IX. Cum (IX. With...)</meta>

<summary>This page argues that the Bogomils believed God possessed a subtle, human-like form, despite their claims to the contrary. The author compares their "incorporeal" descriptions to the complex theories of Epicurus and Audius to show how a group can claim God is spirit while still imagining Him in human shape.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the Bogomil heresy, transitioning from their views on God's physical form (anthropomorphism) to their controversial understanding of the Trinity. The author argues that while they used traditional names, they fundamentally rejected the orthodox understanding of the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit.</meta>

->### ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE BOGOMILS. 59<-

IX. To this monstrous opinion regarding the nature of the Divine Power considered in itself, they joined another even worse view concerning the **TRINITY** of persons. For while they left this concept in words, they effectively abolished it in reality. We have previously cited a passage from Euthymius <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, the primary 12th-century source on Bogomilism</note>, who continues as follows:

<note>original Greek: τὸ μὲν οὖν προσάπτειν... "They took the practice of attaching these three names to the Father from the Sabellian heresy, but the refutation of such a doctrine should be sought in the chapter against the Sabellians. However, regarding the monstrous shape of their God, the physical and strange nature of His form will be sufficient for a refutation."</note>
"They took the practice of attaching these three names to the Father from the **Sabellian heresy** <note>A 3rd-century belief, also called Modalism, which suggests Father, Son, and Holy Spirit are merely different 'modes' or 'masks' of one person rather than three distinct persons</note>, but the refutation of such a doctrine should be sought in the chapter against the Sabellians. However, regarding the monstrous shape of their God, the physical and strange nature of His form will be sufficient for a refutation."

Thus they affirmed that the names signifying distinct persons truly apply only to the Father and express His various manifestations. They shared this error not only with the Sabellians but also with the Montanists, Samosatenians, Massalians (whom they follow in other matters), and Priscillianists; concerning these, read Calovius <note>Abraham Calov (1612–1686), a Lutheran theologian</note> in Volume III of his *System*, page 87, and Petavius <note>Denis Pétau (1583–1652), a Jesuit theologian</note> in Volume II of his *Dogmas*, Book I, Chapter VI.

For this reason, it will seem a surprise to no one that these same people did not fear to claim a beginning as well as an end for the Son and the Holy Spirit. For Euthymius relates it thus:

<margin>Section III.</margin> <note>original Greek: Λέγουσι τὸν υἱὸν... "They say that the Son and the Holy Spirit are dissolved back into the Father, from whom they proceeded, and that He, having functioned as three persons from the year 5,500 until [the age of] 33 years, became a single person again."</note>
"They say that the Son and the Holy Spirit are dissolved back into the Father, from whom they proceeded, and that He, having functioned as three persons from the year 5,500 <note>According to Byzantine chronology, the world was created 5,500 years before the birth of Christ</note> until 33 years [later], became a single person again."

As far as the origin of either person is concerned—which they invent in defiance of all Scripture—these worst of **Chronologists** <note>Scholars who study the timing of historical events</note> cast it into the 5,500th year of the world. Euthymius recorded this:

<margin>Section IV.</margin> <note>original Greek: Λέγουσι μήτε υἱὸν εἶναι... "They say there was neither a Son nor a Holy Spirit until the year 5,500; from that time they took the beginning of their being and their name."</note>
"They say there was neither a Son nor a Holy Spirit until the year 5,500; from that time they took the beginning of their being and their name."

The same author refutes this foolish belief in the following words: "And yet the Evangelist John thunders when he says: *In the beginning was the Wor...*" <note>Referring to John 1:1, "In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and the Word was God."</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the Bogomil heresy, specifically refuting the idea that they did not believe in a physical God. The author argues that even when they used terms like "incorporeal," they meant a subtler form of matter shaped like a human.</meta>

->### ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE BOGOMILS.<-

[...continued from previous page] of Richard Simon's <note>Richard Simon (1638–1712) was a French priest and a pioneer of modern biblical criticism</note> work, I believe he wrongly turned Moses into a patron of the **Anthropomorphites** <term>Anthropomorphites: A group of believers who hold that God possesses a physical human form or body</term>; for this reason, *Herman Witsius* <note>Herman Witsius (1636–1708) was a Dutch theologian</note> rightly criticized him in his *Sacred Miscellany*, Book I, Chapter VII, Section 20.

VIII. These matters seemed worthy of a more detailed account here, so that it might be clear that those who think too kindly of the Bogomils are mistaken—those who, seeing the absurdity of the idea of a physical God, think it impossible that the Bogomils attributed a human form to Him. I see nothing that can be brought forward for this cause, except that it appears from *Euthymius* <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century monk and the primary source on Bogomil beliefs</note> that the Bogomils called God **incorporeal** <note>original Greek: ἀσώματον</note>, and thus they might seem to overturn and push away the idea of **anthropomorphism** <note>original Greek: ἀνθρωπομορφίαν; the attribution of human characteristics to God</note>. If I see correctly, however, they called God "incorporeal" to signify that He is composed of a certain subtler matter, analogous to human bodies, yet not similar in every respect. In the same way, *Epicurus* <note>The ancient Greek philosopher</note> once said his gods existed "according to number" <note>original Greek: κατ' ἀριθμὸν εἶναι</note>, which *Kühnius* <note>Joachim Kühne, an editor of Diogenes Laertius</note> interprets on page 554 of his notes on *Laertius* as meaning they existed *without a body, without solidity, and without corruption*; yet Epicurus still represented those same gods as **human-conformed** <note>original Greek: ἀνθρωποειδεῖς</note> or having the shape of a man. According to Kühnius’s interpretation, this shape arose *from a continuous flow of similar images tending toward one point, which never cease to approach from infinite bodies and individuals, and which coincide so fortunately that from them the God is born in the form of a man.* So too *Audius*, the leader of the Anthropomorphites in his own age, was afraid to attribute a "body" to God, yet he contended that God's image was impressed upon the human body, as *Petavius* <note>Denis Pétau (1583–1652), a Jesuit theologian</note> correctly reminds us in Volume I, Book II, Chapter 1 of his *Theological Doctrines*. If, however, someone should say that even in this way the assertions of the Bogomils, Audaeans, and others cannot be reconciled, I will not argue with them; instead, I would not doubt that what *Cicero* once wisely pronounced concerning the Epicurean **origin of the gods** <note>original Greek: θεογονίᾳ</note> can be adapted to our current business:
> *I would be ashamed to say I did not understand it, if those who set forth or defend these things understood them themselves.*

He draws parallels between this belief and the complex views of Epicurus and Audius regarding the divine shape.</summary>

->### ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE BOGOMILS.<-

IX. To this monstrous opinion regarding the nature of the Divine Power viewed in its absolute sense, they joined another even worse, which relates to the **TRINITY** of persons. For they retained this Trinity in name, but in reality they abolished it. We previously cited a passage from *Euthymius* <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century Byzantine monk and the chief source of information on the Bogomils</note>, who continues as follows:

<note>original Greek: τὸ μὲν οὖν προσάπτειν τῷ Πατρὶ τὰς εἰρημένας τρεῖς κλήσεις, ἐκ τῆς Σαβελλιανικῆς αἱρέσεως ἔλαβον, τὴν δὲ τοῦ τοιούτου δόγματος ἀνατροπήν ζητητέον ἐν τῷ τίτλῳ τῶν κατὰ Σαβελλιανῶν. τῇ μέντοι τερατομορφίᾳ τοῦ κατ᾽ αὐτοὺς Θεοῦ σωματοειδὲς καὶ τὸ τῆς μορφῆς ἀλλόκοτον ἀρκέσει πρὸς ἔλεγχον.</note>
"Therefore, the practice of attaching these three mentioned names to the Father they took from the **Sabellian heresy** <term>Sabellianism: A 3rd-century belief that the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit are not distinct persons, but merely different 'modes' or 'masks' worn by a single God</term>; however, the refutation of such a doctrine must be sought in the chapter directed against the Sabellians. Indeed, the monstrous nature of the body-like God they imagine, and the oddity of His form, will be sufficient proof against them."

In this way, they asserted that the names signifying distinct persons apply truly only to the Father, and merely express His various manifestations. They held this error in common not only with the Sabellians, but also with the **Montanists**, **Samosatenians**, **Massalians** (whom they follow in other matters), and **Priscillianists** <note>These are various early Christian groups labeled as heretical for their divergent views on the nature of Christ or the Trinity</note>. Regarding these, read *Calovius* <note>Abraham Calovius (1612–1686), a prominent Lutheran theologian</note> in Volume III of his *System*, page 87, and *Petavius* <note>Denis Pétau (1583–1652), a Jesuit scholar known as the 'Father of the History of Dogma'</note> in Volume II of his *Dogmas*, Book I, Chapter VI. 

Thus, it will seem a surprise to no one that these same people did not fear to claim for the Son and the Holy Spirit a beginning, and likewise an end. For so Euthymius records in Section III:

<note>original Greek: Λέγουσι τὸν υἱὸν καὶ τὸ πνεῦμα τὸ ἅγιον εἰς τὸν Πατέρα πάλιν, ἀφ᾽ οὗ προῆλθον, ἀναλυθῆναι, καὶ τριπρόσωπον αὐτὸν, ἀπὸ τοῦ πεντακισχιλιοστοῦ πεντακοσιοστοῦ ἔτους ἄχρι καὶ τριάκοντα καὶ τριῶν ἐτῶν χρηματίσαντα πάλιν γενέσθαι μονοπρόσωπον.</note>
"They say that the Son and the Holy Spirit are dissolved back into the Father from whom they proceeded; and that God, having been designated as 'three-personed' from the year 5500 until thirty-three years [after the birth of Christ], became 'single-personed' once again."

As for the origin of both persons—which they invent despite the protests of all Scripture—those wretched chronologists assigned it to the year 5500 from the creation of the world. Euthymius mentioned this in Section IV:

<note>original Greek: λέγουσι μήτε υἱὸν εἶναι, μήτε Πνεῦμα ἅγιον, ἄχρι τοῦ πεντακισχιλιοστοῦ πεντακοσιοστοῦ ἔτους. ἔκτοτε δὲ λαβεῖν αὐτοὺς ἀρχὴν τοῦ εἶναι καὶ ὀνομάζεσθαι.</note>
"They say that there was neither a Son nor a Holy Spirit until the year 5500. But from that time, they took the beginning of their existence and of their naming."

This foolish belief is refuted by the same author in the following words: "And yet the Evangelist John thunders when he says: *In the beginning was the Word...*" <note>original Greek: καίτοι τοῦ Εὐαγγελιστοῦ Ιωάννου βροντῶντος ἐν τῷ λέγειν. ἐν ἀρχῇ ἦν ὁ λό-</note>

<meta>This page continues the debate over the Bogomils' concept of God's form. The previous discussion touched upon how certain scholars identified Moses as a precursor to these ideas; this page concludes the argument that the Bogomils' claim of God being "incorporeal" was a semantic distinction rather than a rejection of his human-like shape.</meta>

-># ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE BOGOMILS.<-

...critique by Richard Simon made Moses the patron of the Anthropomorphites <term>Anthropomorphites: A group of Christians who believed that God has a physical body similar to a human's</term>, a view for which Herman Witsius <note>Herman Witsius (1636–1708) was a prominent Dutch theologian</note> rightly criticized him in his *Sacred Miscellanies*, Book I, Chapter VII, Section 20.

VIII. It seemed necessary to recount these matters more fully here so it would be clear that those who think too kindly of the Bogomils—judging it impossible for them to attribute a human form to God once they saw the absurdity of such a belief—are mistaken. I see nothing that can be brought forward for this cause, except that it appears from Euthymius <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century Byzantine monk</note> that the Bogomils called God *incorporeal* <note>original Greek: ἀσώματον</note>, and thus overturned or pushed away the charge of *anthropomorphism* from themselves. If I see anything clearly, it is that they called God *incorporeal* to signify that He is endowed with a certain finer matter, analogous to human bodies, yet not similar in every respect. 

In this, they resemble Epicurus <note>An ancient Greek philosopher (341–270 BCE)</note> of old. Although he said his gods "existed according to number" <note>original Greek: κατ' ἀριθμὸν εἶναι</note>—which Kühn <note>Joachim Kühn (1647–1697), a German philologist</note> interprets in his notes on Laërtius as meaning "without body, without solidity, without corruption"—Epicurus nevertheless represented those same gods to himself as *human-shaped* <note>original Greek: ἀνθρωποειδεῖς</note> or conforming to the human form. According to Kühn’s interpretation, this shape arose from "a continuous influx of similar forms tending toward one, which never cease to arrive from infinite bodies and individuals, and which concur so fortunately that the form of a human God is born from them." 

Similarly, Audius <note>A 4th-century Syrian Christian who founded the Audaean sect</note>, the leader of the Anthropomorphites in his own age, feared to attribute a physical body to God, yet he contended that God's image was impressed upon the human body, as Petavius <note>Dionysius Petavius (1583–1652), a Jesuit theologian and historian</note> rightly notes in Volume I, Book II, Chapter 1 of his *Theological Dogmas*. If anyone should say that these assertions of the Bogomils, Audaeans, and others cannot be reconciled even in this way, I shall not fight them; rather, I would not doubt that what Cicero once wisely pronounced regarding the Epicurean *generation of gods* <note>original Greek: θεογονίᾳ</note> can be applied to our current business: 

> *I should be ashamed to say that I do not understand, if those very people who explain and defend such things understood them themselves.*

IX. To this startling opinion concerning the nature of the divine Power viewed in itself, they joined another, even worse, which refers to the **TRINITY** of persons. For while they retained this Trinity in name, in reality they abolished it. We cited in the preceding sections a passage from Euthymius <note>Euthymius Zigabenus was a 12th-century monk whose work, the *Panoplia Dogmatica*, is a primary source for understanding medieval heresies.</note>, who continues as follows:

> Therefore, applying these three names to the Father is something they took from the Sabellian heresy <term>Sabellianism: A 3rd-century belief that God is a single person who simply appears in three different modes or "masks" (Father, Son, and Spirit) at different times.</term>; however, the refutation of such a doctrine must be sought in the chapter directed against the Sabellians. Yet, for their monstrous conception of God, the bodily nature and the bizarre quality of His form will suffice as a proof against them. <note>original Greek: τὸ μὲν οὖν προσάπτειν τῷ Πατρὶ τὰς εἰρημένας τρεῖς κλήσεις, ἐκ τῆς Σαβελλιανικῆς αἱρέσεως ἔλαβον... τῇ μέντοι τερατομορφίᾳ τοῦ κατ' αὐτοὺς Θεοῦ σωματοειδὲς καὶ τὸ τῆς μορφῆς ἀλλόκοτον ἀρκέσει πρὸς ἔλεγχον.</note>

Thus, they affirmed that the names signifying distinct persons actually applied only to the Father and expressed His various manifestations. They held this error in common not only with the Sabellians, but also with the Montanists, Samosatenians, Massalians (whom they follow in other respects), and the Priscillianists. Concerning these, one may read Calovius <note>Abraham Calovius (1612–1686), a leading Lutheran theologian of the 17th century.</note> in Volume III of his *System*, page 87, and Petavius <note>Dionysius Petavius (1583–1652), a Jesuit scholar known as the "father of the history of dogma."</note> in Volume II of his *Dogmas*, Book I, Chapter VI.

From this, it will seem surprising to no one that these same people did not fear to claim a beginning for the Son and the Holy Spirit, and likewise an end. For Euthymius records it thus in Section III:

> They say that the Son and the Holy Spirit are dissolved back into the Father from whom they proceeded; and that He, having functioned as "three-faced" from the five thousand five hundredth year until [He was] thirty-three years old, became "single-faced" once again. <note>original Greek: λέγουσι τὸν υἱὸν καὶ τὸ πνεῦμα τὸ ἅγιον εἰς τὸν Πατέρα πάλιν, ἀφ' οὗ προῆλθον, ἀναλυθῆναι... ἀπὸ τοῦ πεντακισχιλιοστοῦ πεντακοσιοστοῦ ἔτους ἄχρι καὶ τριάκοντα καὶ τριῶν ἐτῶν χρηματίσαντα πάλιν γενέσθαι μονοπρόσωπον.</note>

As for the origin of both persons—which they invent despite the protests of the entire Scripture—these poor chronologists pushed it back to the year of the world 5,500 <note>In the Byzantine calendar, the year 5,500 from creation was traditionally the date of the birth of Christ.</note>. Euthymius mentioned this in Section IV:

> They say there was neither a Son nor a Holy Spirit until the five thousand five hundredth year. From then on, they took their beginning of existence and naming. <note>original Greek: λέγουσι μήτε υἱὸν εἶναι, μήτε Πνεῦμα ἅγιον, ἄχρι τοῦ πεντακισχιλιοστοῦ πεντακοσιοστοῦ ἔτους. ἔκτοτε δὲ λαβεῖν αὐτοὺς ἀρχὴν τοῦ εἶναι καὶ ὀνομάζεσθαι.</note>

This foolish belief is refuted by the same author in the following words: "And yet the Evangelist John thunders when he says: *In the beginning was the Wor—*" <note>This refers to the opening of the Gospel of John: "In the beginning was the Word," emphasizing Christ's eternal existence.</note>

It seemed necessary to recount these matters more fully here, so that it might appear that those who, having perceived the absurdity of the opinion regarding a corporeal God, think it impossible that they attributed a human form to God, feel too kindly toward the Bogomils. In this way, Epicurus once, although he said his gods "exist according to number" <note>original Greek: κατ' ἀριθμὸν εἶναι</note>—that is, as Kühn <note>Joachim Kühn (1647–1697) was a German philologist and editor of Diogenes Laërtius.</note> interprets on page 554 of his notes on Laërtius, *without body, without solidity, without corruption*—he nevertheless represented them to himself as "conformed to man" <note>original Greek: ἀνθρωποειδεῖς</note> or human-like. This was, according to Kühn’s interpretation, *from a continuous flow of similar images tending toward one point, which never cease to approach from infinite bodies and individuals, and meet so fortunately that from them God is born in the form of a man*. 

In the same way, Audius <note>Audius (or Audaeus) was a 4th-century Syrian Christian who founded a sect often accused of literalizing the biblical statement that man was made in God's image.</note>, the leader of the Anthropomorphists in his own age, fearing to attribute a body to God, nevertheless contended that His image was impressed upon the human body, as Petavius <note>Dionysius Petavius (1583–1652) was a French Jesuit theologian and scholar.</note> rightly reminds us in Volume I, Book II, Chapter 1 of *Theological Dogmas*. If, however, anyone says that even so these assertions of the Bogomils, Audians, and others cannot be reconciled, I shall not oppose him; but I would not doubt that what Cicero once wisely pronounced regarding the Epicurean "birth of gods" <note>original Greek: θεογονίᾳ</note> could be adapted to our business:

> *I would be ashamed to say I do not understand, if those very people who explain or defend these things understood them themselves.*

# ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE BOGOMILS.

<note>The text begins with a Greek quotation from Euthymius Zigabenus refuting the Bogomils using the Gospel of John.</note>
"...was the Word, and the Word was with God, and the Word was God. But if the Word was always in the Father, then the Holy Spirit was also always present with the Son. For even the word we speak is never found to exist without breath <note>original Greek: *pneuma* (πνεῦμα), which means both "breath" and "spirit."</note>. But those who say the Father was without the Word until the five-thousand-five-hundredth year <note>The Bogomils believed that the Son and Spirit were not eternal, but were only emitted by the Father in the year 5500 after the creation of the world to deal with the rebellion of Satanael.</note> are decreeing that He was without reason <note>original Greek: *alogon* (ἄλογον). This is a theological pun: it means both "without the Logos/Word" and "irrational" or "unreasonable."</note>, unwise, and powerless until that time—to say nothing of their views on the Holy Spirit and the other absurdities that follow their foolish talk." 

If we summarize these points, it remains that the Bogomils’ view regarding the Holy Trinity was that only one person existed from eternity. At a defined time—specifically, when Christ was born of Mary—this person produced both a second and a third person, both of whom later returned like rivers into the Ocean from which they had flowed <note>This "river to the ocean" metaphor describes a heresy where the Trinity is temporary rather than eternal.</note>. In this sense, they said the Father begat the Son, and the Son begat the Holy Spirit, adding the blasphemous opinion that the Holy Spirit produced the traitor Judas in a spiritual manner; this is a phrase which the Latin translation omitted, but which the Greek original clearly shows. 

"They say," he says, "that the Father begat the Son, and the Son begat the Holy Spirit. And this [the Spirit] spiritually begat the traitor Judas and the eleven apostles. They even cite the usage of the Gospel: 'Abraham,' it says, 'begat Isaac, and Isaac begat Jacob, and Jacob begat Judas and his brothers' <note>A reference to the genealogy in Matthew 1:2.</note>, insisting that these things were written to describe the Holy Trinity." 

Euthymius adds this observation to these claims: "First of all, they introduce not only a son but a grandson, and not just a grandson but twelve great-great-grandchildren <note>original Greek: *trisengonous* (τρισεγγόνους). The Latin translator did not capture the force of this word, translating it simply as *genitos* ("those born"), whereas it specifically means "great-great-grandsons" or *abnepotes*</note> in relation to the Father. Furthermore, if the Son is also a Father, and the Holy Spirit is again a Father, look: there are three Fathers. How then can they confess one Father in their teachings? And again, if the Holy Spirit is a Son of God, look: here are two Sons. How then do they agree on one Son in the theology of the Trinity? [So that] Judas and..."

# ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE BOGOMILS.

...let us leave aside his brothers <note>original Greek: τοὺς ἀδελφοὺς αὐτοῦ. This likely refers to the Bogomil belief that Christ and Satanael were brothers.</note> for the present. From these points, it cannot but follow that they established that certain ranks of dignity exist between the persons of the Holy Trinity. This was observed by Harmenopulus, who, having mentioned that "old" and "young" form of the Bogomil Trinity <note>The Bogomils often depicted the Father as an old man and the Son/Spirit as younger emanations.</note>, adds the following: *The demons thus deceiving them and teaching them that the Holy Trinity is unequal, and* [showing them] *the difference between these forms* <note>original Greek: Τῶν δαιμόνων οὕτως ἐξαπατώντων αὐτοὺς καὶ διδασκόντων, ἄνισον εἶναι τὴν ἁγίαν Τριάδα, <term>ϗ</term> τὴν τῶν σχημάτων τούτων διαφοράν. The term <term>ϗ</term> is a shorthand for "and."</note>. Harmenopulus owes these points to Euthymius, and he even retained Euthymius's own words, cited above.

X. Those who held such perverse views regarding the divine nature of CHRIST, judged no more correctly, nor more in accordance with Holy Scripture, regarding His human nature. This will become apparent if we bring their dreams about the **INCARNATION** into the light. Euthymius relates the matter in Section VIII, the summary of which is this: The Father was anxious about the greater part of humanity, which was subject to the power and empire of Satan (as the principle of evil) and stolen from Him by Satan’s deceptions. He therefore thought of means by which he might increase the number of the elect and free souls from the yoke of slavery. Therefore, he brought forth the Word, or Son, who is also called Michael the Archangel, and likewise Jesus and Christ. "They say," says Euthymius, "that while men were still being bitterly tyrannized and cruelly perishing, only a few became part of the Savior's portion" (so says the Fell manuscript <note>Refers to an edition by John Fell, a 17th-century scholar.</note>, though the Latin translator reads "of the Father's portion," which is more correct, since at that time when God the Father is said to have thought these things, the Son did not yet exist according to the Bogomils' opinion, and thus neither did the Savior) "and entered into the order of the Angels. These are the ones mentioned in the genealogies <term>according to</term> <note>original Greek: <term>ϰͭ</term>, shorthand for *kata* (according to).</note> the Gospel of Matthew and <term>according to</term> Luke. But finally the Father, having been outwitted and realizing he was being wronged—because, although he had given the more precious part of man and performed the most vital tasks for man's completion, a large portion of the race was being carried away—and at the same time pitying the Soul, his own internal...

...[divine] breath <note>original Greek: Φύσημα. In Bogomil theology, the soul was seen as a divine "breath" or spark imprisoned in the material world by the evil creator.</note>, suffering so miserably and being oppressed, to rise up for defense <note>original: ἄμυναν (amynan). The author notes that Fell’s transcript had the nonsensical ἄμυθαν (amythan), which he has corrected based on Latin translations.</note> and in the five-thousand-five-hundredth year <note>In the Byzantine calendar, the year 5500 from the creation of the world was the traditional date for the Incarnation.</note> to pour out from His heart the Word, and this one is the Son and God. For He says, as the scripture states, "My heart has poured forth a good word" <note>A reference to Psalm 45:1, used by the Bogomils to argue that the Son was literally "poured out" or emitted from the Father's heart at a specific point in time.</note>, and they claim this Word and Son is the Archangel Michael. For He says his name is called "Angel of Great Counsel" <note>Isaiah 9:6. The Bogomils identified Jesus/The Word with Michael the Archangel.</note>. He is called "Archangel" as being more divine than all the other angels; "Jesus," as healing every disease and every infirmity; and "Christ," as being anointed in the flesh.

And so they describe His birth in such a way that you may understand that Christ descended from heaven and entered through the right ear of the Virgin <note>The "conceptio per aurem" (conception through the ear) was a medieval trope, but here the Bogomils use it to explain how Christ could enter the world without physical contact with "evil" matter.</note> and emerged under the appearance of a man, although He bore the shadow of a body rather than its reality. Indeed, just as the attack on the person of Christ is rightly numbered among the marks of heretics—as Bellarmine <note>Robert Bellarmine (1542–1621), an influential Catholic Cardinal and theologian.</note> noted in Book I *On Purgatory*, chapter 5, saying, "*no heresy, however magnificently it speaks of Christ, preaches the true Christ, but another, whom it has fashioned for itself*"—so especially His human nature has been exposed to the insults of men who use ingenuity more than Scripture, from the very earliest origins of the Church. I will not mention here the patrons of that opinion whom I brought onto the stage in *Manichaeism before the Manichaeans* <note>A reference to the author's previous scholarly work.</note>, about whom one can also read what Forbes <note>John Forbes of Corse (1593–1648), a Scottish theologian known for his historical-theological studies.</note> recorded in his *Historico-Theological Instructions*, Book II, chapter 1. It is only relevant to observe here that the Bogomils had the Manichaeans <note>An older dualist sect that believed the world was a struggle between light and darkness; the author sees the Bogomils as their successors.</note> as imitators and almost rivals in this point. For the "fantastic" <note>The author uses "fantastic" in the sense of Docetism—the belief that Christ's body was a *phantasm* or illusion.</note> opinion of these people regarding the human nature of Christ was the same, regardless of what their nearly constant defender Arnold <note>Gottfried Arnold (1666–1714), a historian who often took a sympathetic view of groups labeled as heretics.</note> struggles to interpret more mildly on page 126 of his *Heresiology*, as can be established from the manuscript of Photius's <note>Photius (c. 810–893), a Patriarch of Constantinople and prolific writer.</note> first book *Against the Manichaeans*, whose...

...these are the words: "There are times when they are driven to admit that Christ came forth from the Virgin—whenever they are completely forced to this admission—but they tell marvelous stories <note>original Greek: τερατολογοῦσι (teratologousi), literally "telling wonder-tales" or "speaking monsters." It implies the author finds their claims absurd.</note> that He brought His body down from above, and passed through her as if through a tube <note>original: σωλῆνος (solenos). A common metaphor used by Gnostic and Docetic groups to argue that Christ did not take any physical substance from Mary, but merely passed through her like water through a pipe.</note>, and that this spotless and pure Virgin, after the saving birth, bore other sons by Joseph."

Anyone who has read Euthymius <note>Euthymius Zigabenus (d. 1118), a Byzantine monk and the primary source for the Bogomil trial under Emperor Alexios I Komnenos.</note> will not doubt that the Bogomils spoke in a similar way. He says: "And that He descended from above and flowed in through the right ear of the Virgin, and put on flesh that appeared material and like a human body, but in truth was immaterial and worthy of God; and that He exited again from where He entered, so that the Virgin knew neither His entrance nor His exit, but simply found Him lying in the cave wrapped in swaddling clothes."

Nor, indeed, are the causes obscure that made this error so common. For it follows directly and with a natural impulse from that opinion by which they convinced themselves that matter in the true sense of the word proceeded from an evil principle and is the origin of all evil, as will be shown a little later. Since they understood that, in this way, Christ’s <term>sinlessness</term> <note>original: ἀναμαρτησίαν (anamartesian). The state of being without sin, which the Bogomils believed was impossible for anything made of physical matter.</note> could neither be reconciled with nor exist alongside true flesh, they preferred to deny the reality of His body rather than involve themselves in insurmountable difficulties.

See Athanasius <note>A 4th-century Church Father and defender of orthodoxy.</note>, *On the Incarnation of Christ*, Volume I, page 27; and Fulgentius <note>A 6th-century North African bishop.</note>, book I, chapter 5; and—the one to whom I owe these testimonies of antiquity—the Blessed Neumann <note>Caspar Neumann (1648–1715), a German theologian and hymn writer mentioned here with great respect.</note>, a theologian most worthy of a longer life, in his *Disputation on Good Intentions: The Asylum of Heretics*, section 27. Thus, just as the Bogomils denied that Christ had true flesh following the example of those earlier heretics; so they also wished that His entire way of life, all His actions and emotions, be considered merely appearances of actions. For Euthymius continues: "And that He fulfilled the incarnation <note>original: οἰκονομίαν (oikonomian). In theology, this refers to the "economy" or divine plan of salvation through the Incarnation.</note>, and did and taught what is recorded in the Gospels, except that He was subject to human passions only in appearance <note>original: ἐν φαντασίᾳ (en phantasia). This is the root of "Docetism," from the Greek dokein, "to seem."</note>, and that He was crucified and died, and..."

...to rise again, appearing to dismantle the stage and strip away the drama, and setting aside the mask, to cast down the rebel. Once these events were completed in this imaginary way (as they believe), they teach that Christ—having driven Satan from the throne of Majesty—claimed for Himself the dignity that Satan had held until then, being seated at the right hand of the Father; and finally, they say He was completely dissolved back into the essence of the Father from which He had first come forth.

For these are the words of Euthymius <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century monk whose "Panoply of Doctrine" is our main source for Bogomil beliefs.</note> that follow immediately: *“And to bind him with a thick and heavy collar and shut him up in Tartarus <note>original Greek: Ταρτάρῳ (Tartaro). A deep abyss or dungeon used in Greek mythology and adopted by early Christians to describe the prison of fallen angels.</note>, having also stripped from his name the ‘el’ as being angelic—for though he was called Satanael, He left him to be named Satan—and finally, having fulfilled the ministry entrusted to Him, to run back to the Father and sit on the right hand on the throne of the cast-out Satanael, then to enter where He came out from, and to be dissolved again into the Father, in whom in the beginning He was enclosed within His womb.”* <note>The Bogomils believed Christ was an "extension" of the Father who eventually ceased to have a separate identity, a view similar to ancient Sabellianism.</note>

From these points, explained here at length and with great care, we can understand the truth of what Anna Comnena <note>A Byzantine princess and historian who detailed her father's persecution of the Bogomil leader Basil the Physician.</note> summarized in Book XV of the *Alexiad* (p. 488) with these words: *“but also he [Basil] disregarded our theology, and made a phantom of the whole economy.”* 

Possinus <note>Petrus Possinus (1590–1679), a Jesuit scholar and translator of Byzantine texts.</note> correctly translated this as: *“Indeed, the impious man not only mocked our belief in the divine nature of Christ, but he also prattled that the very humanity of the incarnate Word was nothing real, but merely a phantom of a spectral body.”* For the terms <term>theology</term> <note>In this context, *theologia* refers specifically to the nature of God and Christ's divinity.</note> and <term>economy</term> <note>In early Christian thought, *oikonomia* refers to the Incarnation and the physical life of Jesus on Earth as part of God's "management" of salvation.</note> are used with this specific meaning, as proven by Valesius in his notes on Eusebius, Suicer in his *Ecclesiastical Thesaurus*, and du Fresne in his *Greek Glossary*.

XI. These opinions, which Euthymius has reported so extensively, he also solidly refuted with more long-winded words; yet they are worthy of being read here from the Greek context, so that by this illustrious example we may know the diligence the ancients used in asserting the pure doctrine. *“But,”* he says, *“we must proceed to the refutation of the things already mentioned...”*

<meta>This page continues the refutation of Bogomil Christology. <header>ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE BOGOMILS.</header>

...absurdities. First, not all of those mentioned in the genealogies of the Evangelists, both that of Matthew and that of Luke, were beloved of God; rather, except for a few, the rest were completely unproven, impious, and lawless. And this is clear from the books that record their lives. For even if the Bogomils reject these books, they could not say that the Evangelists drew their records from any other sources. And how is it that those whose lives were unworthy even of the earth, ascended into heaven and inherited the places of the fallen angels? <note>The Bogomils held a specific myth that human souls were created to fill the "vacancies" in heaven left by the angels who fell with Satanael.</note> And how did He who knows all things before they happen ever fail to understand, being outwitted by Satanael? <note>original: Σαταναὴλ (Satanael). The suffix "-el" signifies an angelic status which, according to the Bogomils, was later stripped away to leave only "Satan."</note>

But so that we may pass over the following points which do little or nothing to harm the truth: if He begat the Word and Son in the five thousand five hundredth year <note>This refers to the "Byzantine Era" dating, where the incarnation was believed to have occurred 5,500 years after the creation of the world.</note>, how did the Father do so according to the divine and ineffable birth? And how does the Apostle John cry out, "In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and the Word was God"? And how did all things come to be through Him, and without Him not one thing came to be that has been made, as the same Apostle says again? And how did the Apostle Paul say that through Him the Father also made the ages?

I am also astonished by the surpassing folly of their next claim. How are they not ashamed to say that the Archangel Michael is by nature the Son of God, and that he is the one who became man as the Lord and worked the salvation of mankind? For if the Archangel Michael is a created being and a servant, just as all the heavenly powers are, how can he be a Son by nature and of the same essence <term>consubstantial (homoousios)</term> with the Father? Unless they would say that the Father Himself is a created being—which is obviously both paradoxical and self-contradictory.

He who is the Son by nature—namely our Lord Jesus Christ—is called the "Angel of Great Counsel," <note>original: μεγάλης βουλῆς ἄγγελος. A title for the Messiah derived from the Septuagint version of Isaiah 9:6.</note> because just as the spoken human word announces the movements of the mind that generated it, so too did He announce to the disciples the great counsel of His Father—that is, the mystery of His incarnation, the evangelical commandments, and the salvation of those who believe through them. Therefore He said, "I speak nothing of myself, but whatever I have heard from the Father, these things I announce to you." Having been demonstrated—

<header>ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE BOGOMILS.</header>

Once it was finally established that the Archangel Michael is a servant, while the one who became man is the Lord and the Son by nature, the titles of both "Jesus" and "Christ"—which had been wrongly attached to the Archangel Michael—fell away from him and returned to the Son. As for their idea of an "incorporeal flesh" <note>The Bogomils were Docetists, believing Jesus did not have a physical body of matter, but a spiritual or "apparent" one that only seemed to suffer.</note> and all the fantastical consequences that follow it (which effectively deny his true birth from the Virgin), these were sown among them by the madness of the Manichaeans <note>Manichaeism: an older dualistic religion that heavily influenced later medieval heresies like Bogomilism.</note> and other such heresies that came before them <note>original Greek: περὶ αὐτῆς (concerning her/it). The text notes that while the manuscript says "before her," the sense and the Latin translation suggest "concerning her."</note>. If you read the seventh chapter under the title "Against the Armenians" and follow where it leads, you will find no small number of these perversions. 

It is also laughable and not even worth mentioning how they strip the suffix "-el" from the name "Satanael." <note>The suffix "-el" means "of God" in Hebrew. The Bogomils believed Satan was originally "Satanael" (God's first son) and lost the "-el" after his rebellion.</note> For even before his tyranny was cast down, the Son who became man said: "I saw Satan fall like lightning from heaven," even though the "-el" had not yet been stripped from him in their timeline. Furthermore, it is pointless to argue against their claim that the "session at the right hand" <note>The "session" refers to Christ sitting at the right hand of God the Father after the Ascension.</note> of the incarnate one is actually the throne of Satanael. It has already been proven that Satanael was neither the Son by nature, nor the firstborn, nor did he possess a throne, nor did he ever sit at the right hand of the Father.

**XII.** In this manner, they established their views concerning Christ and his two natures, acting against the prescriptions of the Sacred Code <note>The Bible.</note>; they also recklessly neglected these same rules in their doctrine of the Holy Spirit. For they explicitly denied that the Holy Spirit is equal to God the Father in either time or dignity, as is clear from what was said above. From those same points, we recall how they completely removed the distinction between the "characteristic properties" <note>term: characteristic properties <gloss>distinctive traits that define the persons of the Trinity: begetting for the Father, being begotten for the Son, and proceeding for the Spirit</gloss></note> of the Son and the Holy Spirit, which the Scriptures deliver so carefully in many places. They declared that the Holy Spirit was begotten from the Son in the same way the Son was begotten by the Father. In this, they erred not only by confusing "generation" with "procession," <note>In orthodox theology, the Son is "begotten" (generation) while the Spirit "proceeds" (procession). Bogomils simplified this into a chain of births.</note> which must be distinguished by various arguments, as Zimmermann shows in his *Analects* (page 72) and Dorotheus in Part II...

<page-num>67</page-num>
# ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE BOGOMILS.

The fifth disputation of Zacharian Theology <note>Refers to the work of Gotthelf Traugott Zachariä, an 18th-century theologian.</note>, section 40, has proved this, but in this matter they also hallucinated gravely: they denied that the Holy Spirit proceeded from the Father.

XIII. Regarding **CREATION**, they dreamed up things that never even entered the minds of the Pagans themselves—although even those Pagans seem to have struggled significantly to corrupt and defile the older doctrine (which they had received by tradition or reached by conjecture) with their own fictions. We will summarize the matter and afterwards interpret it more fully using the words of Euthymius <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century Byzantine monk whose "Panoplia Dogmatica" is a primary source for understanding medieval heresies.</note> himself. They maintained, namely, that Satanael—after being cast down to the earth, which was then invisible and unformed—entered into a plan with the Angels who were his partners in rebellion. He sought to emulate God the Father and became the author of a new heaven and a new earth, following exactly the same order outlined in the Mosaic tablets <note>The "Mosaic tablets" refers to the account of creation in the Book of Genesis.</note>. These are Euthymius's words from Section VII:

-> They say that Satanael, having been cast down from above and being unable to rest upon the waters—for the earth, he says, was invisible and unformed <note>original Greek: ἀόρατος, καὶ ἀκατασκεύαστος—the Septuagint's phrasing for "without form and void."</note>, since it was still surrounded by the divine form and clothing and had been called to the creative power—called together the powers that fell with him. He instilled courage in them and said: "As God made the heaven and the earth—for it says, 'In the beginning God created the heaven and the earth'—I too shall make a second heaven, as a second god, and so on in order." And he said, "Let there be a firmament," and it was so. "Let this and that be made," and everything was made. And after decorating the second heaven, and removing the water from the face of the earth, and arranging it in the places he approved, just as the book of the cosmogony <note>original Greek: κοσμογονίας—referring here to the Book of Genesis.</note> describes, and after beautifying and adorning her, and creating all things growing from the earth, and the animals, he also set apart this place as a dwelling for himself <gloss>read: for himself <note>The translator suggests correcting the Greek text's reflexive pronoun.</note></gloss> and for the rebellious powers. <-

Indeed, this is the same error committed long ago by the Manichaeans <note>Followers of Mani who believed in a total struggle between a good realm of spirit and an evil realm of matter.</note>, and after them the Paulicians; it was by their example, according to Euthymius’s own judgment, that the Bogomils allowed themselves to be incited.
