<page-num>93</page-num>
<header># ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE BOGOMILS.</header>

...very day, and held the form of sound words <note>original: "sanorum verborum"</note> entrusted to them. For it appears from this that they dreamed sweet dreams of their own singular holiness, and displayed such an innocence of life that they seemed to themselves incomparable to others and beyond the reach of any stain. They did this because they claimed to represent, by reason of their brightness, the **lilies**, whose beauty, in their judgment, the "impure" Solomon did not attain. For so says Euthymius <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, 12th-century Byzantine monk and critic of heresy.</note> in Section XLIII: "They understand the **birds of heaven** <note>original: "Πετεινὰ τοῦ οὐρανοῦ"</note> as the monks who practice asceticism on pillars <note>A reference to "Stylites," Christian ascetics who lived on top of high pillars to separate themselves from the world.</note>, whom they say lead an idle life and are fed in vain by the Heavenly Father. But they call themselves the **lilies of the field**, as being white in the purity of their soul and fragrant <note>original: "μυριπνόους" — literally 'breathing forth perfume.'</note> in virtues; they claim that Solomon could never be compared to them, as he had the 'garment of his soul' stained." Here, the Translator did not quite grasp, or at least did not conveniently express, the word **fragrant** <note>original: "μυριπνόους"</note>, in which the primary basis for the comparison with lilies is placed. For just as lilies emit a most fragrant and sweet scent, so they said that they themselves breathed out the scent of virtues, more excellent than any ointments. For this is what the word *fragrant* means: its second part is formed from **ointment** <note>original: "μύρον" — myron</note>, a name which, being derived (not inaptly) from the Hebrew word for **myrrh** <note>original: "מור" — mor</note>, is well known to be used by the Greeks for an unguent. See the Illustrious Spanheim on Callimachus' *The Bath of Pallas*, verses 13 and 16; Samuel Bochart in *Hierozoicon* Part I, page 260; and Casaubon on Athenaeus, book XV, page 688.

**XXXI. Regarding MARRIAGE**, which they considered to be full of impurity, they wished those who would prove themselves worthy of their society to abstain from it, claiming they were following God's own command to be holier. This can be understood from the words of Euthymius quoted above, where they mention **celibacy** <term>coelibatus</term> <note>The state of abstaining from marriage and sexual relations, typically for religious reasons.</note> among other proofs of a purer life. However, since the words of our Savior—which permitted a wife to be sent away only on the grounds of adultery—seemed not so much to condemn the institution of married life as to define it by certain laws,

<page-num>94</page-num>
<header>### ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE BOGOMILS.</header>

...they took care, as was their custom, not to appear to contradict themselves through a far-fetched interpretation. Euthymius <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century monk and theologian.</note> explains the matter thus in Section XXXIX: "Since the saying: '*Whoever divorces his wife except for the cause of unchastity*,' <note>original: "Ἐπεὶ τὸ· Πᾶς, ὃς ἂν ἀπολύσῃ τὴν γυναῖκα αὑτοῦ παρεκτὸς λόγου πορνείας κ. τ. λ." Referring to Matthew 5:32.</note> etc., regarding the scriptural command not to be joined to a woman, is contrary to their doctrine, they say these things are secret and uninterpretable to all, and known only to those who have set aside the flesh. And confirming the doctrine of celibacy <note>original: "ἀγαμίας"</note>, they bring forward the word of the Lord, who said that in the resurrection they neither marry nor are given in marriage, foolishly <note>original: "ἀνοήτως"</note> understanding 'resurrection' <note>original: "ἀνάστασιν"</note> to mean repentance and the Evangelical way of life." The translator, in rendering the final words, omitted the word **foolishly**, which the accepted style of Euthymius's writing requires to be present—either because he did not find it in his manuscript or because he did not notice it. As for the matter itself, this assertion is such that it once seemed worthy of agreement even to Mani <note>The 3rd-century founder of Manichaeism, a dualistic religion that heavily influenced later sects like the Bogomils.</note> and his followers. We have as witnesses Augustine in *Heresy XLVI*, Theodoret in Volume IV of his *Works*, page 304, and the *Formula for the Reception of Manichaeans* in Tollius, page 141. Thus Theodoret observes in the mentioned place that the Manichaeans waged war against the Creator of this universe, fearing, of course, that his handiwork might be increased through marriage. Here, these premature haters of marriage have the impure origin of their opinion; they, along with Weigel <note>Valentin Weigel (1533–1588), a German theologian whose mystical writings were often viewed as heretical.</note> in Part III, page 69, do not fear to call marriage ***respectable prostitution*** <note>original: "honestam scortationem"</note>.

XXXII. Just as they detested marriage because, through its benefit, a significant addition would be made to matter—which they attributed to the Evil Principle <note>In dualist thought, the "Evil Principle" or Demiurge is often identified as the creator of the physical, material world, while the "Good Principle" is the creator of the spirit.</note>—so for the same reason they denied the **RESURRECTION** of bodies to be reunited with souls hereafter. For they said that the body, like an impure garment of the soul, is cast off through death, and in its place, the soul is clothed with the divine robe of Christ...

<page-num>95</page-num>
<header>### ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE BOGOMILS.</header>

...and they believe that once they have laid it down, the body is left forever to the ashes and is never restored to its former state. Euthymius <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century Byzantine theologian and primary source for Bogomil beliefs.</note> comments thus in Section XXII: 

> They say such people do not die, but are transformed, stripping off this clay and fleshly covering as if in sleep without pain, and putting on the incorruptible and divine garment of Christ, becoming of one body and form with Him, passing through the escort of angels and apostles into the Kingdom of the Father; but the body that was cast off is dissolved into ash and dust, never to rise again. <note>original: "λέγουσι τοὺς τοιούτους μὴ ἀποθνήσκειν, ἀλλὰ μεθίστασθαι, καθάπερ ἐν ὕπνῳ τὸ πηλινὸν τουτὶ καὶ σαρκινὸν περιβόλαιον ἀπίνως ἐκδυομένους καὶ τὴν ἄφθαρτον καὶ θείαν τοῦ χριστοῦ στολὴν ἐνδυομένους, καὶ γινομένους συσσώμους καὶ συμμόρφους αὐτοῦ, μεθισταμένους διὰ προπομπῆς ἀγγέλων καὶ ἀποστόλων εἰς τὴν βασιλείαν τοῦ Πατρός, τὸ δ' ἀποδυθὲν σῶμα χυτὸν εἰς τέφραν καὶ κόνιν διαλύεσθαι μηκέτι μηδαμῶς ἀνισάμενον."</note>

In this, the Bogomils remain consistent with themselves, following the example of the Marcionites, Manichaeans, and others who side with them, approving this error as a necessary consequence of their primary principle. For the late <note>The "B." stands for "Beatus," a common academic courtesy for a deceased author.</note> Thomasius <note>Christian Thomasius (1655–1728), a prominent German jurist and philosopher of the early Enlightenment.</note> rightly remarked in his *Draft published under the title "Of Philosophical Origins," page 27*, when discussing the denial of the resurrection of the body by Simon Magus:

> *Nor was this inconsistent with his most wicked principle of two Gods, of whom the evil one created our body. For thus the state of the soul in the body would always be one of misery, never of blessedness.*

I do not wish to repeat here what Euthymius added by way of refutation. We cited that full passage above when we were explaining the ill-fated death of Basil, the leader of the Bogomils. <note>Basil the Physician, the leader of the Bogomils, who was burned at the stake in the Hippodrome of Constantinople around 1118 AD.</note> Regarding his body, though it was entirely reduced to ashes and consumed, Euthymius declares there, against the Bogomils, that it would eventually be restored to its original form.

XXXIII. There was no respect among them for the **ECCLESIASTICAL MINISTRY**, so much so that they did not fear to call the Teachers of the Church flourishing at that time by the names of **Pharisees** and **Sadducees**. To them pertain the things that Euthymius records in Section XXXI. They [denounced] these same two types of men, namely Monks and Clerics, through two...

<page-num>96</page-num>
<header>### ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE BOGOMILS.</header>

They claimed that those men possessed by demons, who came forth from the tombs <note>A reference to the Gerasene demoniacs in Matthew 8:28.</note>, represented these people <note>the Catholic and Orthodox clergy</note>. They drew the argument for this interpretation from the fact that these men always spend their lives in temples—which might more correctly be called tombs—and are so hostile to their own group <note>the Bogomils</note> that no one ought to guard themselves more carefully from anyone than from them. The matter is more fully understood from these words of Euthymius:

> They say the two men possessed by demons coming out of the tombs are the order of the Clergy and the order of the Monks; for they always linger in handmade temples, which they say are tombs hoarding the bones of the dead. For this is what those impious ones call the relics of the saints. They claim these two orders are exceedingly terrifying to the Bogomils <term>Bogomils: A medieval dualist sect that rejected the physical world and the institutional Church as creations of an evil spirit.</term>, so much so that none of them has the strength to pass by that way. They further claim the great herd of feeding pigs represents the common, ignorant, and pig-like people, into whom these [clerics and monks] enter and teach, casting them headlong into the sea of sin. <note>original: "Δύο δαιμονιζομένους λέγουσιν ἐκ τῶν μνημείων ἐξερχομένους, τό τε τάγμα τῶν Κληρικῶν καὶ τὸ τάγμα τῶν Μοναχῶν· ἀεὶ γὰρ ἐν τοῖς χειροποιήτοις ναοῖς διατρίβειν, οὕστινας εἶναι τάφους θησαυρίζοντας ὀστέα νεκρῶν. Οὕτω γὰρ οἱ ἐναγεῖς ὀνομάζουσι τὰ λείψανα τῶν ἁγίων· εἶναι δὲ τὰ δύο ταῦτα τάγματα φοβερὰ λίαν τοῖς βογομίλοις, ὥστε μηδένα τούτων ἰσχύειν παρελθεῖν διὰ τῆς ὁδοῦ ἐκείνης, ἀγέλην δὲ χοίρων πολλῶν βοσκομένην ὑπάρχειν τοὺς ἀγελαίους καὶ ἀμαθεῖς καὶ χοιρώδεις, εἰς οὓς ἀπέρχεσθαι τούτους καὶ διδάσκειν, καὶ κατακρημνίζειν αὐτοὺς ἐν τῇ θαλάσσῃ τῆς ἁμαρτίας."</note>

We have produced other passages in the preceding sections by which this same point is confirmed.

XXXIV. And indeed, these things drawn from the commentary of Euthymius <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century monk and theologian who wrote the "Panoplia Dogmatica" against heresies.</note>—deliberately organized according to Theological Topics <term>Locos Theologicos: A method of organizing theology by "common places" or major themes.</term>—are what I have been able to recount concerning the opinions of the Bogomils. I do not doubt that more remains which has escaped the memory of posterity, because Euthymius himself, by a set purpose, neglected to mention them. Indeed, the eloquent words of his final Section indicate this clearly enough; in them, he confesses that he was prevented by shame and religious scruple from bringing all the foolishness of the Bogomils into the light of day, especially since the rest can easily be judged from the specimens provided. Accordingly, I too shall make an end of speaking about their doctrines, leaving it to each reader to pursue through suitable conjecture whatever remains that might be seen as necessarily linked to the opinions already mentioned.

## CONCERNING THE
# CUSTOMS OF THE BOGOMILS.

### I.

It might have seemed a wonder if the Bogomils—whose impure and detestable opinions are so glaringly obvious—had demonstrated a sense of holiness in their customs and way of life, or a purity free from the filth of a corrupt life. Indeed, the same thing happened to them that Photius <note>Photius I (c. 810–893 AD), a Patriarch of Constantinople and a prolific scholar known for documenting heresies.</note> recorded regarding the Manichaeans <note>An ancient dualistic religion that taught a struggle between a good spiritual world of light and an evil material world of darkness.</note>, whom the Bogomils bring back to life in most of their primary tenets. For in their teachings, you might occasionally notice the greatest inconsistency and an irreconcilable conflict; however, in their lives—which abound in the worst outrages—you will find nothing that does not perfectly correspond to the principles they have established. For thus Photius teaches in Book I of his manuscript *Against the Manichaeans*:

> As far as their doctrines are concerned, they are so impious that they are utterly inconsistent with the truth and even with themselves. But their way of life is full of licentiousness, full of unspeakable pollutions and unimaginable filth. For they engage in sexual unions of both natures <note>original Greek: "χρῶνται μὲν γὰρ μίξεσιν ἑκατέρας φύσεως"; this likely refers to both heterosexual and homosexual acts, which the author views as perverse.</note>, showing no shame for any kind of obscenity. <note>original Latin: "Quod itaque ad doctrinas eorum attinet, tam profligatae sunt impietatis, et tam a veritate, quam a se ipsis, prorsus dissentiunt. Vivendi autem ratio intemperantia laborat, et plena est impuritatibus nefandis, et flagitiis, quae mente vix concipi possunt. Venere enim utraque utuntur, nulloque obscoenitatis ullius pudore ducuntur."</note>

Here, the Bogomils can be conveniently compared with the Manichaeans...

<page-num>98</page-num>

# ON THE CUSTOMS OF THE BOGOMILS.

### I.

...can be endured, this discussion—which will be directed toward explaining their customs more fully—will prove with clear examples. Indeed, it seems this subject can be most thoroughly exhausted if we divide their customs into distinct chapters, so that some regard God more closely, others the people with whom they shared the commerce of life, and others the Bogomils themselves.

## II.

We call those "customs toward God" which reveal themselves partly in external and partly in internal worship. No one who has examined the foundation upon which their entire method of worshipping the Supreme Being was built will doubt that these were most wretched and far removed from divine prescription. Their worship was entirely **HYPOCRITICAL**, since they did not profess what they believed in words, but rather brought forth anything else to the public. Therefore, among their established opinions—which they hammered into their disciples with every effort—this one held the primary place: that all arts and frauds must be employed to escape the hands of those who were likely to avenge the proclaimed doctrines with well-deserved punishments. 

Furthermore, lest that instruction seem to anyone to overturn the entire framework of religion as it is owed to the Divine Being, those ill-advised people—ready to teach the deceit they recommended by their own example—claimed that there existed in the Gospels a counsel of the Savior <note>A title for Jesus Christ, from the Latin *Servator*.</note> by which they were commanded to look out for their own safety by every means. They interpreted this saying in such a way as to claim they should and could, with every art and deceit, feign the faith of those they opposed. They also retreated to Matthew 23:3 <note>"So you must be careful to do everything they tell you. But do not do what they do, for they do not practice what they preach."</note>, though they had interpolated <note>To interpolate is to alter a text by inserting new material, often deceptively.</note> it and expanded it with their own fabrications. For they said the Savior had commanded in that passage that whatever others—who were outside their own sect—might say, they should perform it with simulation <term>Simulation: the act of feigning or pretending to follow a belief or practice while secretly holding another.</term>, but in reality, they should never express those deeds by truly imitating them. Indeed—and this may surprise you—they even ordered people to look at the example of Christ himself, whom they claimed [had handed down] the art of simulation and...

<meta>The catchword "dasse" is the end of the Latin word "tradidisse," meaning "had handed down" or "had taught."</meta>

<summary>This page categorizes Bogomil morality into three parts and details their "customs toward God," specifically their practice of religious hypocrisy and their belief that Jesus authorized the use of deceit to avoid persecution.</summary>

<keywords>Bogomils, religious hypocrisy, simulation, Matthew 23:3, secrecy, persecution, Savior's counsel, theological deceit</keywords>

--- Page 141 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the Bogomils' use of hypocrisy, specifically how they justified their deceit by claiming that Jesus himself used parables to hide the truth from the unworthy.</meta>

<page-num>99</page-num>

-># ON THE CUSTOMS OF THE BOGOMILS.<-

they thought they should offer one thing to others and apply another, whenever, under the shroud of parables, [Christ] presented one thing while keeping another hidden in his mind. So clever and at the same time corrupt was the character of these men when it came to increasing and amplifying their heap of wickedness. Let us hear Euthymius <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century Byzantine monk and author of the *Panoplia Dogmatica*, our primary source for Bogomil beliefs.</note>, Section 21, speaking thus: <note>original Greek: "Ἔτι προσεγγεγράφθαι καὶ ταύτην ἔλεγε τὴν Κυρίου φωνὴν: Παντὶ τρόπῳ σώθητε..."</note> *Furthermore, he [the Bogomil leader] used to say that this saying of the Lord was also written: "Save yourselves by every means," that is, by trickery and deceit, feigning the faith of those who compel you, and thus being saved from the danger and death brought by them. For they also apply this verse to the same end: "Whatever they tell you to do, do it"—in hypocrisy, clearly—"but according to their works do not do it"—in truth. For they claim that even the Lord himself spoke rightly to the disciples, but to the unbelievers in parables, so that the unbelievers, seeing the outward appearance of hypocrisy, would not see what is hidden in your hearts, and hearing they would not hear—thereby calling deceit and hypocrisy a "parable."* Indeed, all who have fallen away from the truth have shared this fraud throughout every age of the Church: to deceive others and feign a friendly agreement of minds through words and voices. Such a person in former times was Acacius <note>Acacius of Caesarea (died 366 AD), a bishop known for his fluctuating theological positions during the Arian controversy.</note>, whom Philostorgius for that reason called "one thing in opinion, but another in tongue" <note>original Greek: "ἕτερον μὲν τὴν δόξαν, ἕτερον δὲ τὴν γλῶσσαν"</note> in Book 4, final chapter. So too it was charged against the Monothelites <note>A 7th-century theological group that believed Christ had two natures but only one will; they were condemned as heretics at the Council of Constantinople.</note> by Bishop Martin I in the Lateran Synod, that they primarily act to persuade others that they feel the same as the ancient Teachers of the Church, while in fact holding an internal disposition most foreign to those doctrines. Nor are there lacking examples of those who, having adopted terms accepted in the Church, substituted far different meanings, so that, playing with words, they might hide more securely. Examples were brought forward by Balthasar Menzer <note>A prominent Lutheran theologian (1565–1627) known for his polemical works.</note>, a theologian of immortal fame, in a singular commentary in which he explained the *Lutheran Language and Calvinist Mind of the Synod of Dort <note>The Synod of Dort (1618–1619) was an international assembly of the Reformed Church to settle the controversy over Arminianism.</note> regarding Election*. Add to this L. Wilckius’s excellent dissertation on the "form of sound [words]..."

The author compares this behavior to other historical groups like the Monothelites and the participants of the Synod of Dort who allegedly used orthodox language to hide heterodox beliefs.</summary>

<page-num>100</page-num>

->## ON THE CUSTOMS OF THE BOGOMILS.<-

<meta>catchword: -gois (Greek: λόγοις, part of "words")</meta>

...of words held at Leipzig, 1698, and William Forbes’s *Historical-Theological Instructions*, Book VIII, 27, 6 and Book 20. We have understood above, from the conversation he held with Alexius Comnenus <note>Alexius I Comnenus (reigned 1081–1118), the Byzantine Emperor who famously staged a fake conversion to Bogomilism to trick Basil into revealing his secrets before having him executed.</note> concerning the chapters of religion, that Basil, the leader of the Bogomils, showed himself to be a most skilled craftsman in this regard. For being a cunning man, he did not express the true opinion of his mind at the very first opportunity; rather, only when he seemed to have sufficiently ensured his own safety and the good faith of the Emperor did he finally dare to pour out <note>original: "eructare," literally "to belch forth," implying the release of something long suppressed.</note> that which he had kept hidden in his mind. 

However, Basil learned through experience what Gregory Nazianzen <note>Gregory of Nazianzus (c. 329–390 AD), an influential Archbishop of Constantinople and one of the "Cappadocian Fathers."</note> wisely warned in his *First Oration against Julian*: <note>original Greek: "ὅτι οὐδὲν προσποιητὸν μόνιμον"</note> *that nothing feigned is permanent*—that is, nothing achieved through simulation and outward appearance is stable or everlasting—once he was dragged from his hiding places and forced to come into the light. Nevertheless, the Bogomils held the counsel of their leader <note>term: "Antesignanus" (leader/standard-bearer), here referring to Basil.</note> as highly recommended to them, even though his perversity could easily be demonstrated. 

Certainly, Euthymius <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, the primary Byzantine chronicler of this sect.</note> employs all his effort in the aforementioned passage to refute him, where he recounts his own debate on this subject held with a certain follower of Basil as follows: 

*And I, taking into my hands the book of the Gospels belonging to the one teaching these things, and searching through it accurately, found neither the saying mentioned concerning this matter, nor the present one. But such things were as they were, and I reported this fact. But he, looking gloomy and distressed as one who has been convicted, confessed that he himself had never encountered these two sayings, even though he frequently read the Gospel. "But," he said, "when I purchased this present book of the Gospels, I met an old man in a certain desert place, and calling me by name, he said to me: 'You have purchased a great treasure; for this book alone, having escaped the hands of John Chrysostom'"*—for these impure men [the Bogomils] called Chrysostom <note>John Chrysostom (c. 347–407 AD), an Early Church Father known for his eloquence ("Golden-Mouthed"). The Bogomils apparently viewed him as a censor who deleted "true" passages from the Bible.</note> by this name—*"contains the two aforementioned sayings written in it, which were cast out from the other Gospels." Believing his words...*

->## ON THE CUSTOMS OF THE BOGOMILS.<-

<note>The text begins mid-sentence in Greek, quoting Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century monk commissioned by Emperor Alexius to document and refute the Bogomil heresy.</note>
...words I preach, and these things as they are written in my book. He then made such a defense, but I quickly understood that it was Satan who was impersonating that false-voiced old man. The point comes down to this: that Bogomil [Basil] appealed to a certain Manuscript of the Gospels, in which both passages he had previously cited in support of his opinion were allegedly written in specific words. Euthymius, however, leafing through it carefully, could find neither passage. When the other realized this, he complained of a fraud committed against him by a certain old man, who had asserted that this was the only Manuscript that had escaped the corrupting hands of John Chrysostom <note>John Chrysostom (c. 347–407 AD) was the Archbishop of Constantinople and one of the most prolific authors in early Christianity. The Bogomils apparently believed he had edited the Gospels to remove their specific doctrines.</note>.

From this, it is gathered that the Bogomils—following the example of other heretics—diligently sought opportunities to complain about the corruption brought upon the Manuscripts of Holy Scripture, and they judged that the suspicion of this crime should specifically be transferred to John Chrysostom.

Furthermore, in those words with which this accusation is brought forward, some error seems to me to be hidden in the text, although the interpretation by Francisco Zino and the Greek transcription <note>original: "ἀπόγραφον" (apographon)</note> I used agree with each other. For while it is said: <note>original Greek: "ταύτην γὰρ μόνην τὴν βίβλον διαφυγοῦσαν τὰς χεῖρας Ἰωάννου τοῦ χρυσοστόμου"</note> *for this was the only book to escape the hands of John the Golden-Mouthed*, I think that instead of "Golden-Mouthed" <note>term: "Chrysostom" (Greek: χρυσόστομος) means "golden-mouthed," a title given to John for his eloquence.</note>, it should read "Dung-Mouthed" <note>original: "κοπροστόμου" (Koprostomos)</note>, or some other similar word joined with a slight against the Holy Doctor. Unless we conclude this, I do not see why Euthymius would have punished the Bogomils—who were seemingly identifying this John with his accepted title of "Golden-Mouthed"—in his added observation: <note>original Greek: "τὸν γὰρ χρυσόστομον οὕτως οἱ ἀκαθαρτόστομοι προσωνόμαζον"</note> *For the "Impure-Mouthed" ones named the "Golden-Mouthed" thus.* This means the men of impure speech mocked Chrysostom’s name with a pun.

Nor is it otherwise an unusual thing to twist names into insults through a slight change of letters. For in the past, the Orthodox <note>The "Orthodox" refers to the state church of the Byzantine Empire.</note> toward those with wrong opinions, and these heretics in turn toward the Orthodox, showed evidence of a hostile and unkind spirit through such wordplay. In this way:
*   **Photinus** <note>Meaning "Light."</note> was called **Scotinus** <note>Meaning "Darkness."</note>.
*   **Eudoxius** <note>Meaning "Good Glory."</note> was called **Adoxius** <note>Meaning "Ignoble" or "Without Glory."</note>.
*   **Eutyches** <note>Meaning "Fortunate."</note> was called **Atyches** <note>Meaning "Unfortunate."</note>.
*   **Eusebius** <note>Meaning "Pious."</note> was called **Asebius** <note>Meaning "Impious."</note>.
*   **Athanasius** <note>Meaning "Immortal."</note> was called **Sathanasius** <note>A pun on "Satanic."</note>.
and...

...an example which fits very well here: Cyprian was called by the name Coprianus <note>A common polemical pun in the early church; "Coprianus" is derived from the Greek word for dung, *kopros*, used to insult the target's character or origins.</note>. See the most celebrated Fabricius in his notes on the *Apocryphal Codex of the New Testament*, p. 344. Whatever the case may be, it is clear that the Bogomils seemed to have managed their affairs quite cleverly if they could somehow hide the practices of their religion and provide some appearance of goodness to their doctrines, which were full of impiety. They achieved this even through their outward appearance, about which we will be given the opportunity to say more later.

III. Their policy regarding **EXTERNAL WORSHIP** is no more to be approved; they showed themselves so negligent of it that they abominated all temples <note>In this context, *templa* refers to consecrated church buildings.</note> intended for divine worship and called them by unworthy names. They shamelessly asserted that Satan and evil spirits—each according to their rank—had found permanent seats in these places. They claimed that their prince had once chosen the Temple of Jerusalem as his home, and later the Temple of Sophia <note>The Hagia Sophia in Constantinople.</note> in Constantinople. To support this assertion, they prematurely used that oracle of Scripture in which heaven is called the dwelling place of God <note>A reference to Acts 7:48 or Isaiah 66:1: "the Most High does not dwell in temples made with hands."</note>. These are the words of Euthymius <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, the 12th-century monk and theologian.</note>, Section XVIII:

<note>original Greek: "Λέγουσιν, ἐν πᾶσι τοῖς ἱεροῖς ναοῖς κατοικεῖν τοὺς δαίμονας διαλαχόντας αὐτοὺς ἀναλόως τῆς ἑκάστου τάξεως καὶ δυνάμεως. Τὸν μέντοι Σατανᾶν πάλαι μὲν ἀποκληρῶσαι ἑαυτῷ τὸν πολυθρύλλητον ἐν Ἱεροσολύμοις ναὸν, μετὰ δὲ τὴν ἐκείνου ἀναστροφὴν ἐξιδιώσασθαι τὸν ἐν τῇ βασιλίδι ταύτῃ τῶν πόλεων ὑπερφερῆ καὶ περιώνυμον τῆς τοῦ Θεοῦ σοφίας οἶκον. οὐ γὰρ ὁ ὕψιστος φασιν ἐν χειροποιήτοις ναοῖς κατοικεῖ, τὸν οὐρανὸν ἔχων κατοικητήριον."</note>

"They say that demons dwell in all the holy temples, having shared them out according to each one's rank and power. Indeed, they say that Satan long ago took for his own the much-talked-about temple in Jerusalem, and after its destruction, he took possession of the house of the Wisdom of God—which is superior and famous—in this queen of cities. For they say the Most High does not dwell in temples made by hands, having heaven as his dwelling place." 

I think it is obscure to no one that by "Queen of Cities" <note>original: "βασιλίδα τῶν πόλεων"</note> nothing else is understood than Constantinople. For just as Rome was once called the "Queen" <note>original: "βασιλὶς" or "βασιλεύουσα"</note> in Latin because it was the seat of the reigning Emperors—which has long since been proven from Herodian, Eusebius, and various marble inscriptions—

This was proven by the illustrious Spanheim <note>Ezechiel Spanheim (1629–1710), a noted diplomat and scholar of ancient coins.</note> on page 687 of *On the Use and Excellence of Coins*, and very recently by Jean Masson in the notes to Gruter’s *Inscriptions* on page 1080. Thus, since the seat of the empire was transferred to Constantinople, it was inevitable that the dignity of the same praise <note>Referring to the titles and honors formerly held by Rome.</note> would be conferred upon it. See the same Spanheim in the mentioned place, and especially his notes on the First Oration of Julian on pages 30, 31, and 75.

So, it also had other titles in common with Rome. For it was called the **Great City** <note>original Greek: "μεγάλη πόλις" or "μεγαλόπολις"</note> and, what encompasses all titles, the **Second Rome** <note>original Greek: "δευτέρα ῥώμη"</note>. Étienne le Moyne comments on this subject in his notes on *Varia Sacra*, pages 69 and 70. In this "Queen of Cities," therefore, the prince of demons was seen by the Bogomils <term>Bogomils: A medieval dualist sect that believed the material world was created by an evil deity or a fallen angel.</term> to have chosen his primary temple; they were so repulsed by the worship performed there for the Divine Being. Because of this, they called those man-made temples *tombs treasuring the bones of the dead* <note>original: "τάφους θησαυρίζοντας ὀστέα νεκρῶν"</note>, or *sepulchers in which the bones of the dead are preserved.* See Euthymius <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century monk whose "Panoply of Dogma" is a primary source for Bogomil beliefs.</note>, Section 49. In the *Alexiad* on page 488, Anna Comnena reports concerning Basil <note>Basil the Physician, the leader of the Bogomils who was burned at the stake by Emperor Alexios I Komnenos.</note> that he named the holy temples *dwellings of demons* <note>original Greek: "τοὺς ναὸς οἵ μοι τοὺς ἱεροὺς ναὸς δαιμόνων ὠνόμασε"</note>. Harmenopulus, repeating the words of Euthymius, is a witness to the same doctrine: *For they say,* he writes, *that demons dwell in all holy temples* <note>original Greek: "λέγουσι γὰρ... ἐν πᾶσι τοῖς ἱεροῖς ναοῖς τοὺς δαίμονας κατοικεῖν"</note>.

Therefore, the formula of execration which we read in *On the Victory over the Massalians* <term>Massalians: An early ascetic sect (also called Euchites) that the Orthodox Church often linked to the Bogomils due to their shared rejection of church hierarchy.</term> in Tollius, page 121, refers to the Bogomils. It is established from the same writing on page 119 that they were never present at public assemblies usually established for the sake of religion; they are condemned on that account because *they loathe gatherings in churches, and they preside and teach in private places under the pretext of quietness* <note>original Greek: "ὅτι τὰς ἐν ἐκκλησίαις συνάξεις βδελύττονται καὶ ἐν ἰδιάζουσι τόποις προκάθηνται καὶ διδάσκουσι προφάσει μὲν ἡσυχίας χάριν"</note>. This passage, as I mention in passing, can be added to those which learned men have brought forward with the purpose of showing that the pretext of "quietness" <note>Referring to the Greek concept of *hesychia*, or spiritual stillness.</note> is not a new thing with which some in our own age have tried to cloak their institutions; instead, even the memory of former times records the

<page-num>104</page-num>

## ON THE CUSTOMS OF THE BOGOMILS.

The **Hesychasts** <term>Hesychasts: From the Greek word for "stillness" or "quiet," these were Eastern Orthodox monastics who practiced a tradition of inner silence and contemplative prayer.</term> or **Quietists**, who did not live up to their name in their actions, disturbed the Church. See especially Leo Allatius in *On the Agreement of the Eastern and Western Church* <note>original: "de Consensione Orient. et Occidentalis Ecclesiae"</note>, Book II, chapter XVII; and in *On the Writings of the Simeons*, page 177; Dionysius Petavius in Volume I of *Theological Doctrines* <note>original: "Dogmatum Theologic."</note>, page 84; and the learned dissertation on the revolution of Quietism by Johann Andreas Schmid, a man most knowledgeable in sacred antiquities. 

It appears from Augustine's Book XX of *Against Faustus* that the **Manichaeans** <term>Manichaeans: Followers of a major dualist religion that viewed the world as a struggle between light and darkness; they were often accused of despising material religious structures.</term> formerly led the way by their example for the **Bogomils**, who were despisers of temples. There was no lack of pagan philosophers in the past who—whether from a purely rational judgment of the mind or out of respect for the common people's superstitious views regarding temples—denied that God should be worshiped in temples. Among these are numbered the **Persian Magi**, who said that the spacious world is the temple of God, who should not be confined by narrow walls. See Cicero's *On the Laws* <note>original: "de Legibus"</note>, Book II, page 335; and Macrobius in *On the Dream of Scipio*, Book I, chapter XIV. Others provide the testimonies of Aegidius Menagius in his notes on Laertius's *Lives of the Philosophers*, page 6; Thomas Stanley in the *History of Oriental Philosophy*, Book II, chapter VIII; and Jean Le Clerc in the Index with which he provided his Latin version. 

The celebrated Mayerus, in his *Evangelical Selection* <note>original: "Ecloga Evangelica"</note> for the 11th Sunday after Trinity, shows that others besides the Manichaeans (whom we have already seen) afflicted the Church of Christ with similar judgments. It is more well-known than I need to explain that Valentin Weigel <note>A 16th-century German mystic who criticized external church structures.</note> and others in our own memory have taken up these same dregs of thought. 

How foolishly the Bogomils imitated this is briefly shown by Euthymius in the passage cited: "And indeed," he says, "the Lord says: *My house shall be called a house of prayer.* <note>original Greek: "Καὶ µὴν, ὁ οἶκός µου Φησὶν ὁ Κύριος, οἶκος προσευχῆς κληθήσεται."</note> And again, Christ said: *Do not make my Father's house a house of merchandise,* <note>original Greek: "Μὴ ποιεῖτε τὸν οἶκον τοῦ Πατρός µου οἶκον ἐμπορίου"</note> saying this regarding the man-made temple of Jerusalem. But if the temple in Jerusalem was the house of God, how much more so is the one in the Queen of Cities <note>Constantinople.</note>. And to put it simply, all those [temples] consecrated in every place to God, and to the Mother of God, and to all [the saints]..."

<meta>catchword: to the [saints]</meta>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the Bogomils' rejection of physical churches, transitioning into their specific disdain for the symbol of the Cross. It cites Euthymius Zigabenus and other historical scholars to explain the theological conflict between the Bogomils and the Orthodox Church.</meta>

<page-num>105</page-num>

->## ON THE CUSTOMS OF THE BOGOMILS.<-

To the holy, temples are divine; they are sanctuaries and places from which demons are put to flight. For where divine grace dwells, every evil spirit and every activity of demons is driven away <note>original: "τοῖς ἁγίοις ναοὶ θεῖοί τε εἰσὶ καὶ ἁγιαστήρια καὶ τῶν δαιμόνων φυγαδευτήρια. Οπου γὰρ οἰκεῖ θεία χάρις, ἐκεῖθεν ἀπελήλαται πᾶν πνεῦμα πονηρὸν, καὶ πᾶσα τῶν δαιμόνων ἐνέργεια."</note>. The essence of this response is that this belief of the **Bogomils** <term>Bogomils: A medieval dualist sect that emerged in the Balkans, holding beliefs that often rejected the material world and the formal structures of the Church.</term> is false, since the Savior, when speaking of the temple in Jerusalem, did not judge it as something to be abolished, but rather called it a place destined for prayer.

IV. They completely abstained from the sign of the **CROSS**, nor did they speak reverently of the cross upon which our Savior hung, as was proper. They sought this pretext: that the cross took the Savior's life. They dishonor, says **Euthymius** <term>Euthymius Zigabenus: A 12th-century Byzantine monk and theologian who wrote the "Panoply of Doctrine," a major source for understanding medieval heresies.</term> in Section IV, the divine cross as the killer of the Savior, whom they should rather honor as the destroyer of the Devil. For until that time it functioned as a death-dealing instrument, but from then on it became a life-giving weapon, most royal and most terrifying to enemies, as it was sprinkled with the Lord’s blood and water <note>original: "ἀτιμάζουσι... τὸν θεῖον σταυρὸν, ὡς ἀναιρέτην τοῦ Σωτῆρος, ὃν ἔδει μᾶλλον τιμᾷν ὡς καθαιρέτην τοῦ Διαβόλου. μέχρι γὰρ τότε θανατηφόρον ὄργανον χρηματίζον ἐκ τότε ζωηφόρον ὅπλον ἐγένετο... ἅτε τῷ δεσποτικῷ αἵματι καὶ ὕδατι καταῤῥαντισθείς."</note>. This is also discussed in the section against the **Armenians**, and one should look for chapter 14 there. So too in the *Victory over the Messalians* <term>Messalians: An early ascetic sect (also called Euchites) who believed that constant prayer was the only way to expel the demon every person was born with; they influenced later groups like the Bogomils.</term>, Anathema VII, in **Tollius** <note>original: "apud Tollium p. 119"</note>, they are said to cast away the sign of the cross for the sake of their father Satan. This means: *To please their father Satan, they shrink from the sign of the Holy Cross.* In Anathema XI, page 121, they are reported to call the venerable and life-giving cross a *phulka* <note>original: "φῦλκαν"</note>. That is: *They are said to call the venerable and life-giving cross a fury.*

**Tollius** correctly said that *phulka* <note>original: "φῦλκαν"</note> meant a **gallows** <note>original: "furcam"</note>, but **Meursius** did not satisfy the learned when he explained it as a "noose." Because of this, he was noted by **Holstenius** in his notes on Codinus, and by **Charles du Fresne** <term>Du Cange (Charles du Fresne): A highly influential 17th-century French philologist and historian of the Middle Ages and Byzantium.</term> in his *Glossary*, who among other testimonies brings forward one from the manuscript Lexicon of **Cyril**, which settles the matter: it says a *phulka* is a "scaffold," meaning the upright wood, the cross, or the gallows on which criminals are hung <note>original: "ἰκρίον, τὸ ὀρθὸν ξύλον, ὁ σταυρὸς, ἢ φῦλκα, ἐν ᾧ οἱ κακοῦργοι κρεμῶνται."</note>. There is no one among the ancient writers who reproached the Bogomils for that contempt of the cross more vehemently or with greater intensity of mind than **Germanus**, Patriarch of Constantinople...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Bogomils' rejection of the Cross. It specifically draws on a sermon regarding the Exaltation of the Holy Cross, citing the work of the scholar Jacob Gretser to illustrate how the Bogomils viewed the cross as an instrument of murder rather than a holy relic.</meta>

<page-num>106</page-num>

->### ON THE CUSTOMS OF THE BOGOMILS.<-

[the Metro]politan, both elsewhere and especially in the sermon which he held concerning the **Exaltation of the Holy Cross** <note>The Exaltation of the Holy Cross is a major feast day in the Christian calendar, celebrating the cross as an instrument of salvation.</note> against the Bogomils. It is indeed read in Latin in the *Libraries of the Fathers*, but it exists in Greek only in **Jacob Gretser’s** <term>Jacob Gretser: A Jesuit scholar (1562–1625) who wrote extensively on the history and theology of the Cross.</term> commentary on the Cross, Volume II, page 157 and following. Since that book is rare, I hope I shall perform a worthwhile service if I repeat the passage which pertains to this matter here, although it is somewhat long.

"For," he says, "although there are many enemies of the cross—Jews, **Agarenes** <term>Agarenes: A medieval term for Muslims, whom Christian writers often claimed were descendants of Hagar.</term>, and others—these **Bogomils** are more loathsome and impious than all of them; they are clearly the mouth of the Devil, the precursors of the **Antichrist**, the manifest heralds of his imminent presence, and the unambiguous signs of the end of the age." <note>original Greek: "Πολλῶν γὰρ, inquit, ὄντων τῶν ἐχθρῶν τοῦ σταυροῦ, Ἰουδαίων, Ἀγαρηνῶν, καὶ ἑτέρων, οἱ τοιοῦτοι Βογόμιλοι βδελυκτότεροι καὶ δυσσεβέστεροι πάντων τούτων εἰσὶ, καὶ στόμα τοῦ Διαβόλου προδήλως, καὶ πρόδρομοι τοῦ Ἀντιχρίστου, καὶ τῆς ὅσον ἤδη παρουσίας αὐτοῦ κήρυκες ἔκδηλοι, καὶ τῆς συντελείας τοῦ αἰῶνος ἀναμφίβολα σήμαντρα."</note>

Then on page 162, he explains their opinion of the cross more eloquently, citing the argument they are accustomed to using, in these very words: "They say one must not worship or kiss the cross, but rather dishonor and turn away from it, as the thing that destroyed and killed Christ. They lead us into what they claim is disbelief, saying: 'If someone killed your Father by hanging him on a piece of wood, would you have honored and worshipped that wood? Surely not! Instead, you would have turned away from it and hated it, and seeing it from afar you would flee from it as an enemy, as the thing that killed your beloved Father.'" <note>original Greek: "οὐ χρή φασι, τὸν σταυρὸν προσκυνεῖν καὶ ἀσπάζεσθαι, ἀλλ᾽ ἀτιμάζειν καὶ ἀποστρέφεσθαι, ὡς τὸν χριστὸν ἀνελόντα καὶ θανατώσαντα, καὶ ἐπάγουσιν εἰς ἀπιστίαν δῆθεν ἡμᾶς ἐμβαλόντες· εἴ τις, λέγοντες, τὸν σὸν Πατέρα ἐπὶ ξύλου κρεμάσας ἀνεῖλεν, ἐτίμησας ἂν καὶ προσεκύνησας ἐκεῖνο τὸ ξύλον; πάντως οὐχί. ἀλλὰ καὶ ἀπεστράφης αὐτὸ, καὶ ἐμίσησας, καὶ πόρρωθεν βλέπων ἀπέφευγες ἂν, ὡς ἐχθρὸν, οἷα τὸν σὸν Φίλον Πατέρα ἐναποκτείναν."</note>

The meaning of this is that the cross is so far from being worthy of worship that it is instead worthy of abomination, since it brought an end to the Savior’s life. On the following page, he notes that the cross should be highly valued by those who have drawn many benefits from it; conversely, however, it seems abominable to one who has received no small harm from it. From this he finally concludes that the **Devil** must absolutely pursue it with hatred, as it brought about his destruction, and that the Bogomils, by their hatred for the cross, profess themselves friends of **Satan**.

"It is necessary," he says, "for the devil to hate the cross and flee far from it, as one flees from fire. For through it came his crushing and weakening, and total..." <note>original Greek: "τῷ δέ γε διαβόλῳ, inquit, ἀνάγκη μισεῖν τὸν σταυρὸν, καὶ φεύγειν μακρὰν ἐξ αὐτοῦ, ὡς φεύγει τις ἐκ πυρός. δι᾽ αὐτοῦ γὰρ ἡ συντριβὴ αὐτοῦ καὶ ἐκνεύρισις, καὶ παντε-"</note>

<meta>catchword: ...destruction (Greek: λὴς / -lēs)</meta>

<summary>This page quotes historical sources to explain the Bogomil argument against the Cross: they viewed it not as a holy symbol, but as the murder weapon used against Christ, comparing it to a piece of wood used to kill one's own father.</summary>

It transitions from Germanus's harsh rhetoric to a more critical historical analysis of how cross-worship developed in the early church.</meta>

<page-num>107</page-num>
<header>->### ON THE CUSTOMS OF THE BOGOMILS.<-</header>

...total destruction. Therefore, as many as hate the cross and turn away from it, give a clear sign of their devotion to the devil; and being recognized as his faithful servants, they are bound to consider his enemy as their own, just as they likewise love his friends. Consequently, and in accordance with their disposition toward the devil and their unbreakable faith in him, the Bogomils say that it is an unjust thing for the wood upon which someone's father or friend was put to death to be worshiped by his children or friends. <note>original Greek: "λῆς ἐξολότρευσις... τοὺς Φίλους αὐτοῦ." This passage illustrates the Bogomil argument that the cross was a tool of execution—an instrument of murder—and therefore a symbol of tragedy rather than a holy relic.</note> The same author, in a Sermon delivered on the third Sunday of Lent <note>Specifically the "Sunday of the Veneration of the Cross."</note> in praise of the Cross, which appears in the same work on page 295 and following, after having brought forward many arguments regarding the excellence of the cross, turned his speech toward those who despise the cross and said: "I would wish now that both Jews and Bogomils were present to listen to this discourse, which refutes their blasphemy against the all-praiseworthy cross. But what does the church of God have in common with these 'dumb dogs,' as the prophet says? <note>A reference to Isaiah 56:10.</note> And furthermore, the Psalm prevents me from inviting these profane men, declaring that a 'foolish' Jewish man shall not know, and a 'senseless' Bogomil shall not understand." <note>original Greek: "ἤθελον ἄρτι... οὐ συνήσει." Germanus is paraphrasing Psalm 92:6 to target his contemporary theological enemies.</note>

Certainly, if the truth be told, ***Patriarch Germanus*** <note>Germanus I (c. 634–740 AD) was the Patriarch of Constantinople who initially opposed the first wave of Byzantine Iconoclasm.</note> could have refrained from that entire refutation, composed in such harsh words. For although the Bogomils themselves were not free from error here, having cast so many and such great insults against the cross, he himself was stuck in a greater error—he who labored not so much to defend the dignity of the holy cross as to insist upon its cult and adoration. 

To be sure, just as some efficacy and power began to be attributed to the figure of the cross in the 5th century after our salvation was restored <note>i.e., the 5th century AD.</note>, so in the 7th century and subsequent times its worship was received into the Churches. This is briefly shown by ***Friedrich Spanheim*** <term>Friedrich Spanheim (1632–1701): A Dutch Calvinist theologian and historian who was often critical of Roman Catholic and Orthodox traditions.</term> both on page 1177 of his *Ecclesiastical History* and in his specific book *On Images*; but more extensively and with dedicated effort—though not always in the best faith, being carried away by the prejudice of his subject matter <note>original Greek: "ὑποθέσεως" (hypotheseos)</note>—by ***Jacob Gretser*** in Volume I of *On the Cross*. This abuse of the cross [led to] greater...

The previous page established that the Bogomils viewed the cross as a tool of execution rather than a holy object. This page traces the historical development of cross-veneration in the early Church and contrasts it with the "blasphemies" of the Bogomils and their predecessors, the Manichaeans.</meta>

<page-num>108</page-num>
<header>->### ON THE CUSTOMS OF THE BOGOMILS.<-</header>

...it gradually increased, and it seemed to have reached its full development especially during the time when Germanus <note>Germanus I, Patriarch of Constantinople (c. 634–733 AD), was a key figure in the defense of icons during the Iconoclastic period.</note> flourished. This is clearly understood from his three Orations and other discourses on the same subject given by others and cited by Gretser. <note>Jacob Gretser (1562–1625) was a Jesuit scholar who compiled extensive histories on the veneration of the Cross.</note> To illustrate this with an example, I turn to the Oration delivered by him on the Feast of the Holy Cross, where among other things on page 326, he addresses the listener thus: "Worship this wood, O man, as one who is steered and governed by it." <note>original Greek: "τότῳ προσκύνει τῷ ξύλῳ πηδαλιομένῳ καὶ κυβερνωμένῳ, ὦ ἄνθρωπε."</note> This means: *Now adore this wood, O man, as if directed and governed by a rudder.* See also page 330.

The Bogomils ought not to have been blamed for turning their minds away from this superstition; however, they were not careful enough regarding their "neglect of the counter-pull," <note>original Greek: "ἀμελείᾳ τῆς ἀνθολκῆς." This is a rhetorical term suggesting that by pulling too hard in the opposite direction to avoid superstition, they fell into the opposite vice of disrespect.</note> because they went so far as to label the cross with names full of disgrace. They even considered it a matter of religious duty to avoid the use of its wood, which pious antiquity had accepted in memory of the suffering Christ. Indeed, that Tollian record <note>A reference to the writings of Jacobus Tollius (1633–1696), a Dutch scholar who published Byzantine manuscripts.</note> which we just mentioned relates this in these very words.

Furthermore, they had the Manichaeans <note>A dualist sect that believed the world was a struggle between light and darkness; the Church frequently accused the Bogomils of being "Neo-Manichaeans."</note> as the leaders and authors of this action. Regarding them, Photius <note>Photius the Great (c. 810–893 AD), Patriarch of Constantinople, known for his vast learning and opposition to heresy.</note> explains in Book I of his manuscript: "Blaspheming the life-giving cross, these deceivers and sorcerers say they worship and accept a cross, but they fashion it themselves after their own imagining of Christ. For they say that he himself stretched out his hands in the shape of a cross; but as for the true cross, they say it is merely wood and an instrument of evil-doing, lying under a curse, and therefore should not be worshiped or kissed." <note>original Greek: "Καὶ τὸν ζωοποιὸν δὲ σταυρὸν δυσφημούντες... οὐ δεῖ προσκυνεῖν καὶ ἀσπάζεσθαι."</note>

These men, I say, and any others who ever held such views, spoke far too despicably and contemptuously of the Holy Cross, which was held in great esteem in the early Church, even if that esteem was free from all superstition. I have a suitable witness in Tertullian, in chapter III of his book *On the Crown* (*De Corona*), who observes that Christians *at every entry and exit, when putting on shoes or going to the baths, at tables, at the lighting of lamps, at their beds and seats, were accustomed to mark their foreheads with the sign of the cross.* Cyril of Jerusalem <note>Bishop of Jerusalem (c. 313–386 AD) and a Doctor of the Church.</note> testifies in his *Catechetical Lecture* IV.10 that this custom was still accepted in his own time...

XIII. 1. He calls the cross the "boast of boasts." <note>original Greek: "τὸν σταυρὸν καύχημα τῶν καυχημάτων"</note> *Tertullian*, whom I have just praised, records in his book *On Prayer*, chapter 11, that Christians were accustomed to spread their arms in the form of a cross [during prayer]. Add to this the same author's *Apology*, chapter 30, and *Eusebius* <note>Eusebius of Caesarea (c. 260–339 AD), the first great historian of the Christian Church.</note> in his *Life of Constantine the Great*, book IV, chapter 15. However, let us not be led away into using these as arguments for cultic worship and veneration—practices which were utterly foreign to the most holy customs of a congregation consecrated to God. For although the Gentiles <note>A term used here for non-Christians, specifically pagans, who often accused early Christians of "staurolatry" or cross-worship.</note>, following their usual desire to slander Christian institutions, dared to bring them under suspicion of this crime—as is clear from the work of *Minucius Felix* <note>Marcus Minucius Felix was one of the earliest Latin apologists for Christianity; his work *Octavius* defends Christians against pagan accusations.</note> at the very beginning—he nevertheless shows in that same passage, along with other leaders of the Church, that this vice was unjustly attributed to them. *Aringhi* himself, in *Subterranean Rome* <note>Paolo Aringhi (1600–1676) was an archaeologist who studied the Roman catacombs. His *Roma Subterranea* documented early Christian art and burials.</note>, Volume II, book VI, chapter 14, admits that the cross was only exposed for veneration after the battle successfully waged by Constantine the Great against Maxentius. <note>The Battle of the Milvian Bridge (312 AD), after which Constantine supposedly saw a vision of a cross in the sky.</note>

Thus, while the Bogomils very correctly reflected the customs of the ancient Church in denying worship to the Cross, they must nonetheless be judged to have abandoned the Church's example beyond all reason, as they did not restrain themselves from profane and insulting words. Meanwhile, there is something to wonder at: these very Bogomils, as if forgetful of themselves, sometimes chose for themselves the very cross they held in contempt as a kind of certain protection to which they might run when afflicted. For *Euthymius* <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century Byzantine monk whose *Panoplia Dogmatica* is the primary source for Bogomil beliefs.</note> mentions in Section XV that when they were harassed by demons, they were accustomed to betake themselves to the cross with a loud voice, and they gave this reason for their custom: that the cross is the work of demons, who manufactured it for the killing of Christ, and now they [the demons] do not cease to inhabit it.

"When asked again by us," he [Euthymius] says, "the aforementioned leader <term>exarch|A high-ranking official or leader within the Bogomil sect.</term> of the heresy, regarding how those possessed by demons run to the cross and bark at it, he replied: 'The cross is especially loved by the demons inhabiting them, as if it were their own work. For they themselves,' he says, 'constructed it at that time for the destruction of the Savior.' And sometimes they even insult it in..."

...thereafter it gained momentum, and especially at that time when Germanus <note>Germanus I (c. 634–733 AD) was the Patriarch of Constantinople; he was a staunch defender of the use of icons and religious symbols before the Iconoclastic period.</note> flourished, it seemed to have been brought almost to full maturity. To provide an example of this, I refer to the Oration delivered by him on the Feast of the Holy Cross, where among other things on page 326, he addresses the listener thus: "Worship this wood, O man, as you are steered and piloted [by it]," etc. <note>original Greek: "τότῳ προσκύνει τῷ ξύλῳ πηδαλιούμενος καὶ κυβερνώμενος, ὦ ἄνθρωπε"</note> that is: *Worship this wood now, O man, as if directed and governed by a tiller.* etc. Add also page 330.

The Bogomils ought not to have been blamed for calling the mind away from this superstition; however, in doing so, they failed to guard themselves against a neglect of proper reverence <note>original Greek: "ἀμελείᾳ τῆς ἀνθοκλῆς"—this phrase likely refers to a lack of care in how they addressed sacred objects.</note>, in that they dared to characterize the cross with words full of disgrace. Indeed, that Tollian monument <note>A reference to a manuscript or record published by the scholar Jacobus Tollius.</note> which we just mentioned hand down this very thing in precise words.

For he himself, they say, stretched out his hands in the shape of a cross; but the true cross, since it is wood and an instrument of a criminal and lies under a curse, they say one must not worship or embrace." <note>original Greek: "Καὶ τὸν ζωοποιὸν δὲ σταυρὸν δυσφημοῦντές φασιν αὐτὸν προσκυνεῖν καὶ ἀποδέχεσθαι σταυρὸν, οἱ πλάνοι καὶ γόητες αὐτὸν ἀναπλάττοντες τὸν χριστόν..."</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 108 regarding the Bogomil sect’s rejection of the cross. While the previous page established that the Bogomils were reacting against a later medieval "superstition" of cross-worship, this page provides historical evidence from early Church Fathers like Tertullian and Eusebius to show that early Christians respected the symbol without worshipping it. It also highlights a strange contradiction in Bogomil behavior: they officially hated the cross but allegedly used it to drive out demons.</meta>

# ON THE CUSTOMS OF THE BOGOMILS.

XIII. 1. He calls the cross the <term>boast of boasts</term> <note>original Greek: τὸν σταυρὸν καύχημα τῶν καυχημάτων. This is a reference to Galatians 6:14, where the Apostle Paul boasts in the cross of Christ.</note>. Tertullian, whom I just praised, in his book *On Prayer* chapter 11, records that Christians were accustomed to stretch out their hands in the form of a cross. Add to this the same author's *Apology* chapter 30 and Eusebius in *The Life of Constantine the Great* IV. 15. Let it be far from us, however, to seize upon these things as an argument for cultic worship and veneration—practices most alien to the customs of the most holy assembly, consecrated to God. For although the Gentiles <note>The "Gentiles" here refers to non-Christian Romans or pagans.</note>, following their usual desire to slander Christian institutions, dared to bring them under suspicion of this crime <note>The "crime" of *staurolatry*, or cross-worship, which pagans often mocked Christians for.</note>—as is evident from Minucius Felix <note>Marcus Minucius Felix was one of the earliest Latin Christian apologists, likely writing in the 2nd or 3rd century AD.</note> at the beginning of his work—the same author shows in that same place, along with other Leaders of the Church, that this vice was undeservedly reproached upon them.

Aringhus himself, in *Subterranean Rome* Volume II, Book VI, Chapter 14, confesses that the cross was finally exposed for veneration only after the battle successfully waged by Constantine the Great against Maxentius <note>The Battle of the Milvian Bridge (312 AD). Paolo Aringhi (1600–1676) was a scholar of the Roman catacombs who acknowledged that the formal "veneration" of the cross was a later historical development.</note>. Thus, although the Bogomils very correctly reflected the customs of the ancient Church in denying worship to the Cross, they must nevertheless be judged to have abandoned its example beyond reason when they did not restrain themselves from profane and insulting words.

In the meantime, there is cause for wonder that these very Bogomils—as if forgetting themselves—sometimes chose the cross, which they held in contempt, as a kind of most certain protection to which they would run when afflicted. For Euthymius <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century Byzantine monk and the primary source for our knowledge of Bogomil beliefs.</note>, in Section 15, records that when they were harassed by demons, they were accustomed to retreat to the cross with a loud voice, and gave this reason for their custom: that the cross is the work of demons, who manufactured it for the killing of Christ, and now do not cease to inhabit it. He says:

"When the aforementioned leader of the heresy was asked again by us why those possessed by demons run to the cross and bark [at it], he replied that the cross is especially loved by the demons dwelling within them, as it is their own work. For they themselves, he says, constructed it at that time for the destruction of the Savior. Sometimes they also insult it in..." <note>original Greek: Ἐρωτηθεὶς, inquit, αὖθις παρ’ ἡμῶν ὁ δηλωθεὶς ἔξαρχος τῆς αἱρέσεως, πῶς οἱ δαιμονῶντες προστρέχουσι τῷ σταυρῷ, καὶ καθυλακτοῦσιν, ἀπεκρίνατο, Φιλεῖσθαι μάλιστα τὸν σταυρὸν ὑπὸ τῶν ἐνοικούντων αὐτοῖς δαιμόνων, ὡς οἰκεῖον ἔργον. Αὐτοὶ γάρ, φησι, τοῦτον τότε κατεσκεύασαν εἰς ἀναίρεσιν τοῦ Σωτῆρος. Ἔστι δ’ ὅτε καὶ καθυβρίζειν τοῦτον ἐν</note>

# ON THE CUSTOMS OF THE BOGOMILS.

...in hypocrisy, and he often flees it [the cross] voluntarily, so that people seeing him might honor him more as an enemy and pursuer of demons. <note>original Greek: ὑποκρίσει καὶ φεύγειν αὐτὸν πολλάκις ἑκουσίως... This refers to the belief that demons feign fear of the cross to trick observers into thinking someone is holy.</note> But among us, the truth is not so. For they are dragged even against their will by the power of the cross, and they bark like dogs, so that their madness may be revealed and they may be tormented. For the barking and the distortions of the bodies of the demon-possessed, along with their groans, are proof of the torment of the demons. They insult it [the cross] as an enemy and flee from it as a pursuer. The Latin version by Francesco Zini <note>Francesco Zini was a 16th-century Italian scholar who translated many Greek ecclesiastical works into Latin.</note> does not express the words, "and so that they might be tormented" <note>original Greek: καὶ ἵνα βασανίζωνται</note>, that is, so that they might be tortured or vexed, even though the sense of the passage requires it. The same interpreter reads the final words as: "they insult it as an enemy and pursuer," omitting the word "flee."

V. They attributed much importance to **FASTING**, and they made it a primary part of their divine worship, so that they were accustomed to spend Monday <note>original Latin: Lunae, the day of the Moon.</note>, Wednesday <note>original Latin: Mercurii, the day of Mercury.</note>, and Friday <note>original Latin: Veneris, the day of Venus.</note> fasting until the ninth hour <note>The "ninth hour" in the ancient Roman timekeeping used by the Church corresponds to 3:00 PM.</note> in the afternoon. Euthymius <note>Euthymius Zigabenus was a 12th-century monk whose "Panoply of Dogma" is a primary—though hostile—source for Bogomil beliefs.</note> testifies to this in Section XXV: "Every week," he says, "they command fasting on Monday, Wednesday, and Friday until the ninth hour." <note>original Greek: πάσης ἑβδομάδος... δευτέραν καὶ τετράδα καὶ παρασκευὴν παραγγέλλουσι νηστεύειν ἕως ὥρας ἐννάτης.</note> Yet they did not think the law of this institution was so severely dictated to them that they would not depart from it when the occasion arose. For if they were invited to a banquet, they are said to have gorged themselves with food and drink as if they had forgotten their own rules. Thus Euthymius continues: "But if someone should call them to a table, they immediately forget the command and eat and drink like elephants." <note>original Greek: Εἰ δέ τις αὐτοὺς ἐπὶ τράπεζαν καλέσειεν... ἐσθίουσι καὶ πίνουσιν ὡς ἐλέφαντες.</note> To understand this custom of the Bogomils, it should be noted that in the early Church, and specifically in the second century, fasts began to be celebrated on the fourth and sixth days of the week. Tertullian <note>An early Christian author from Carthage (c. 155 – c. 220 AD).</note> calls these "half-fasts" <note>original Latin: semijejunia</note> in chapter 13 of *On Fasting*, because the duration of that fast was not until evening, but only extended to the ninth hour of the day. <meta>This page continues the discussion on the religious practices of the Bogomils, moving from their views on the cross to their specific rituals regarding fasting. # ON THE CUSTOMS OF THE BOGOMILS.

...early Christians were to observe <note>The Latin structure "sunt... obeunda" suggests these fasts were to be performed or attended to.</note> these fasts first by their own choice, and later by law. Epiphanius provides the reason for this in his *Exposition of the Faith*, no. 22, stating that both weekdays were passed in fasting <note>original Greek: ὅτι ὁ Κύριος τῇ ἐπιφωσκώσῃ τετράδι συνελήφθη, καὶ τῷ προσαββάτῳ ἐσταυρώθη</note> "because the Lord was captured on the dawning Wednesday <note>literally "the fourth day."</note> and was crucified on the day before the Sabbath <note>Friday.</note>." Both days are also mentioned in the so-called *Clementine Constitutions* (VII. 23), which add that on Wednesday, the Jews took counsel regarding the death of Christ. It is not necessary to bring up other relevant matters here; anyone who wishes to know more should consult the ancients, such as Balsamon in his letter *On Fasts*, which appears in Volume II of Cotelerius’s *Monuments of the Greek Church*, page 492, and the *Nomocanon* of Cotelerius, no. 429. Among more recent authors, one should look to Henri Valesius’s notes on Eusebius, page 106; Tobias Pfanner in Part II of his *Ecclesiastical Observations*, Observation VII; and the distinguished men <note>original Latin: VV. CC., abbreviation for Viri Clarissimi.</note> William Cave in Part I, Chapter VII of *Primitive Christianity*, and John Ernest Grabe in Volume I of his *Spicilegium* of the Fathers, page 48 and following. To these two days of fasting, the Bogomils added a third, the second day of the week <note>Monday.</note>, undoubtedly with the intention of gaining for themselves the praise of a holier life. This is similar to how Montanus once used to—not so much recommend as—command new, more frequent, and stricter <term>fasts (nēsteias)</term> <note>original Greek: νηστείας</note> to be observed without ceasing, as if imposing a kind of yoke, according to the testimony of Theodoret in *On Heretical Fables* (III. 2). Perhaps the Bogomils also wished to distinguish themselves from the rest of the Christian community by this very practice, following the example of the Manichaeans, who were accustomed to fast even on the Lord's Day <note>Sunday.</note>, as witnessed by Augustine in Letter 74. That this was contrary to accepted custom appears from a clear passage of Epiphanius: <note>original Greek: τὰς Κυριακὰς ἁπάσας τρυφερὰς ἡγεῖται ἡ Ἐκκλησία, καὶ οὐ νηστεύει</note> "The Church considers all Sundays as days of delight and does not fast." Nevertheless, they [the Bogomils] assigned an equal span of hours to their fasting, following the custom of the Church, from which they were otherwise departing...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the religious practices of the Bogomils, moving from their fasting habits to their specific customs regarding prayer. It explores their rejection of traditional liturgy in favor of a strictly limited use of the Lord's Prayer.</meta>

# ON THE CUSTOMS OF THE BOGOMILS.

...the <term>Montanists</term> <note>An early Christian movement (2nd century) known for its emphasis on prophecy and strict asceticism.</note> used to do, whom Tertullian, in his celebrated book *On Prayer*, records as having extended these practices until the evening.

VI. They devoted themselves diligently to pouring out prayers to God, so much so that, as we hinted at above, it is well-established that the name <term>Massalians</term> <note>Derived from a Syriac word meaning "those who pray." This group was often conflated with the Bogomils by medieval writers.</note> was given to them for this reason. Yet, even here, they could not hide their passion for innovation. For I believe it happened that, having neglected all other formulas of prayer which the Church had introduced through long usage, they judged that only the Lord’s Prayer should be used. Euthymius, in Section XIX, left this testimony: "They call only that prayer which was handed down by the Lord in the Gospels—namely, the *Our Father*—by the name of 'prayer'" <note>original Greek: μόνην ὀνομάζωσι προσευχὴν, τὴν ὑπὸ τοῦ Κυρίου παραδοθεῖσαν ἐν τοῖς Εὐαγγελίοις ἤγουν τὸ Πάτερ ἡμῶν.</note>. Harmenopulus agrees with these words: "They give the name of 'prayer' only to the *Our Father*; the others they reject, calling them babbling" <note>original Greek: Μόνην ὀνομάζωσι προσευχὴν τό· Πάτερ ἡμῶν. τὰς δὲ ἄλλας ἀθετοῦσι, βαττολογίαν καλοῦντες.</note>. You may understand the same from Euthymius’s *Refutation of the Massalians*, page 119 in the edition by Tollius, where it is also mentioned that they did not admit the final words of the Lord’s Prayer. The words go as follows:

> For on account of this [namely, Satan], they also reject the sign of the cross and the final concluding phrase added by the divine Luminaries <note>A term for the great Teachers or Fathers of the Church.</note> and the leaders of the Church for the glory of the consubstantial and undivided Trinity—that is: *For thine is the kingdom, and the glory of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit*—they cannot even bear to hear it. <note>original Greek: Τούτου γάρ, (nempe Satanae) χάριν καὶ τὴν σταυρικὴν σημείωσιν ἀποβάλλονται, καὶ τὸ παρὰ τῶν θείων Φωστήρων καὶ τῆς ἐκκλησίας καθηγητῶν προσεθὲν ἀκροτελεύτιον ἐπιφώνημα, εἰς δόξαν τῆς ὁμοουσίου καὶ ἀδιαιρέτου τριάδος, τὸ ὅτι σοῦ ἐστιν ἡ βασιλεία, καὶ ἡ δόξα τοῦ πατρὸς, καὶ τοῦ υἱοῦ καὶ τοῦ ἁγίου Πνεύματος, οὐδὲ ἀκοῦσαι ἀνέχονται.</note>

Indeed, I have not sufficiently determined from these words whether the Bogomils detested the entire <term>doxology</term> <note>A short hymn of praise to God, such as "For thine is the kingdom..."</note> which closes the Lord’s Prayer, or only those specific words which pertain to naming the persons of the Holy Trinity. I am led to believe the latter especially because it is explicitly mentioned that these words were added by the Doctors of the Church—undoubtedly to bring into the light those heretics who held perverse views on the Holy Trinity—since Euthymius did not say this regarding the doxology itself. For although that [doxology] is not found in some manuscripts...

# ON THE CUSTOMS OF THE BOGOMILS.

...which, after Lindanus <note>William Damasus Lindanus (1525–1588), a Dutch bishop and theologian known for his works on biblical interpretation.</note> in his work *On the Best Kind of Interpretation*, and Erasmus, John Mill <note>John Mill (1645–1707), a pioneering English theologian famous for his critical edition of the Greek New Testament.</note> has most diligently shown. Nevertheless, most manuscripts—and those of a better and older quality—confirm it; nor are arguments lacking by which its authenticity <note>original Greek: γνῃσιότης</note> can be defended and established against those who doubt. See especially the notes of John Gregory <note>John Gregory (1607–1646), an English Orientalist and scholar.</note> on certain passages of the New Testament, page 159, and most recently Samuel Werenfels <note>A Swiss theologian (1657–1740) known for his work on biblical hermeneutics.</note> in his *Collection of Theological Dissertations*, the final dissertation. 

Therefore, I judge that only the final words—in which the persons of the Holy Trinity are designated by name to guard against the deceptions of those with perverse opinions, as I have said—were added at a later date <note>original Latin: postliminio, a legal term here used to mean restored or added back later</note>, perhaps not long before the time of Euthymius who wrote these things. This was done for the same reason that the <term>creedal books</term> <note>original: libri Symbolici, referring to the official statements of faith or confessions of a church.</note> of the Christian Church, and even the Creed itself which they call the Apostles' Creed, periodically received various additions whenever a new and objectionable opinion appeared to the Doctors of the Church as likely to corrupt the purity of doctrine. Peter King <note>Peter King (1669–1734), an English lawyer and politician who wrote a famous history of the Apostles' Creed.</note> demonstrated this not long ago with unshakeable arguments in his remarkable book on the Apostles' Creed, originally written in English and translated into the Latin language. 

However, lest the Bogomils should seem to abstain from the use of other prayers without reason and merely by whim, they retreated to the words of Christ, in which He desires that "babbling" <note>original Greek: βαττολογίας — referring to the "vain repetitions" or "empty phrases" forbidden in Matthew 6:7.</note> be foreign to Christians. For Euthymius says in that same place of the *Panoplia*: "They dishonor all other prayers, calling them babbling and part of the 'Gentile portion,' that same babbling mentioned in the Gospels" <note>original Greek: τὰς δ’ ἄλλας πάσας προσευχὰς ἀτιμάζουσι, βαττολογίας αὐτὰς ἀποκαλοῦντες, καὶ τῆς ἐθνικῆς μερίδος, τὴν ἐν τοῖς Εὐαγγελίοις εἰρημένην βαττολογίαν.</note>. 

But Euthymius mocks this trivial excuse of the Bogomils in the same passage and rejects this whole practice, using these words: "The Gospel word recognizes not simply 'much-speaking,' but rather the asking for things that are not fitting" <note>original Greek: οἶδεν ὁ Εὐαγγελικὸς λόγος οὐ τὴν ἁπλῶς πολυλογίαν, ἀλλὰ τὴν περὶ τῶν μὴ προσηκόντων αἴτησιν.</note>. He continues: "For the Lord handed down the *Our Father* prayer to the apostles as the root and form of prayer, not limiting prayer to this alone, but so that, taking their starting points from it, they might be able... <note>original Greek: ὁ δὲ Κύριος παρέδωκε τοῖς ἀποστόλοις τὴν τοῦ Πάτερ ἡμῶν προσευχὴν ῥίζαν προσευχῆς καὶ εἶδος, οὐ ταύτῃ μόνῃ τὴν προσευχὴν περιορίζων, ἀλλ’ ἵνα λαμβάνοντες ἐκ ταύτης τὰς ἀφορμάς δύνανται.</note>

# ON THE CUSTOMS OF THE BOGOMILS.

...they can also create others, as faith grows. For [Christ] did not command them to fast at the beginning, nor did he allow them to be treated with such severity until after his ascension into heaven and his restoration. Wherefore he also said: *Can the wedding guests mourn as long as the bridegroom is with them?* <note>original Greek: Matthew 9:15</note> and so forth. As for the suspicion of <term>battology</term> <note>original: βαττολογία; the practice of "vain repetition" or babbling in prayer, which Jesus warned against in Matthew 6:7.</note>—which the Bogomils apply to the Church's use of sacred prayers modeled after the Lord's Prayer—it is clearly evident that such a charge is very far from them. This is brilliantly shown in the commentary on the nature of that battology by B. Stolberg <note>Balthasar Stolberg (1640–1684), a noted German professor of Greek and theology.</note>, once a distinguished ornament of this Academy, in a unique dissertation reprinted with others on pages 243 and 365; also by Dilherr <note>Johann Michael Dilherr (1604–1669), a German theologian and linguist.</note> in *Academic Disputations*, Volume III, page 190; John Doughty <note>An English clergyman and scholar (1598–1672).</note> in *Analects of the New Testament*, Exercise VII; Fuller <note>Nicholas Fuller (1557–1626), an English Hebraist and philologist.</note> in *Sacred Miscellanies*, II. 16; Salden <note>Willem Salden (1627–1694), a Dutch Reformed theologian.</note> in *Theological Leisure*, book III, Exercise X, paragraph 9; and most recently by Cornelius Adami in *Theological-Philological Observations on Passages of Scripture*, page 108.

**VII.** Up to this point, we have seen the formula of prayer used by the Bogomils <note>The Bogomils were a medieval dualist sect who rejected much of the external ritual of the Church, focusing almost exclusively on the Lord's Prayer.</note>; now we must say something about the rituals usually observed while reciting it. Euthymius <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century Byzantine monk and the primary historical source on the Bogomils.</note>, in Section XIX, whom we called upon earlier, observes that they pronounced this prayer seven times during the day and five times at night. "And this prayer alone," he says, "they pray: seven times during the day, and five times during the night." <note>original Greek: Καὶ ταύτην μόνην... πεντάκις δὲ τῆς νυκτός.</note> However, whenever they uttered it aloud, they did not all do it in one way; rather, some would repeat it ten times, others fifteen, and others still more, while kneeling. Euthymius also reports this in these words: "Whenever they stand for prayer, they say this prayer: some ten times with <term>genuflection</term> <note>original Greek: γονυκλισία; the act of kneeling or prostrating as a sign of worship.</note>, others fifteen times, some more, some less." I would not believe that the Bogomils, in adopting this custom, could have guarded themselves against "vain repetition," since it does not seem likely to me that the same prayer repeated so many times...
