# ON THE CUSTOMS OF THE BOGOMILS.

...requested, could have been brought forward with the same mental effort. Long ago, the author of the *Apostolic Constitutions* <note>A 4th-century collection of Christian precepts and liturgical instructions.</note> (as they are called), in Book VII, chapter 25 and Book VIII, chapter 35, sanctioned by law that Christians ought to use this prayer <note>The Lord's Prayer.</note> three times a day—namely, at the third, sixth, and ninth hours. It is manifest from the very passage of the *Constitutions* I mentioned, as well as from the writings of Tertullian and Cyprian <note>Early Church Fathers from North Africa (2nd–3rd centuries AD).</note>, that these hours were destined for this duty of piety. However, I do not recall it being recorded in the monuments of Church history that anyone used the same formula so frequently <note>The author is contrasting the early Church's thrice-daily prayer with the Bogomils' practice of repeating the Lord's Prayer dozens or even hundreds of times.</note>. Indeed, the practice of certain priests in Spain was quite the opposite; they believed that the prayer recommended by the Savior should not be used daily, but only on the Lord’s Day. See the **Fourth Council of Toledo**, Canon IX.

Euthymius <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century Byzantine monk and the primary source for Bogomil beliefs.</note> mentions that **kneeling** <note>original Greek: γονυκλισίαν (*gonuklisian*)</note> was accepted among the Bogomils during prayer. It seems to me that he called this "lying down upon the earth" <note>original Greek: τὴν εἰς γῆν κατάκλισιν (*tēn eis gēn kataklisin*)</note> in the passage previously cited from the *Refutation of the Massalians* <note>The Massalians or Messalians were an older ascetic sect whose practices were often compared to the Bogomils.</note>. For I do not agree with Tollius <note>Jacobus Tollius (1633–1696), a Dutch classicist.</note> who, on page 119 of his notes, thinks that a falling of the entire body to the ground—such as is attributed to Christ while praying in Matthew 26:39—is hinted at and signified in this place. For although you may observe among other rites of prayer in the writings of the ancients that rite which consists of casting the body onto the ground, I do not believe it is expressed here by the word "prostration" <note>original: *kataklisis*</note>; nor, if Euthymius is to be considered consistent with himself, can anyone else think so.

There is an illustrious passage by Augustine in *On the Care to be Taken for the Dead*, chapter V, in which both rites are encompassed:

> *When praying,* he says, *they do with the members of their body what is appropriate for suppliants: when they fix their knees, when they extend their hands, or even when they are prostrate on the ground.* <note>original: "Orantes, inquit, de membris sui corporis faciunt, quod supplicantibus congruit, cum genua figunt, cum extendunt manus, vel etiam prosternuntur solo."</note>

The Greek Doctors called the former "bending the knee," "supplicating," "falling on the knees," or "kneeling" <note>original: γόνυ κλίνειν (*gonu klinein*), ἱκετεύειν (*hiketeuein*), γονυπέτειαν (*gonupeteian*), γονυκλισίαν (*gonuklisian*)</note>; the latter they called "falling down" and "prostration" <note>original: ὑποπίπτειν (*hupopiptein*) and ὑπόπτωσιν (*hupoptōsin*)</note>. To these is added a third posture of those praying, which Latin writers call by the name of **standing** <note>original: *stationis*; refers to the "stations" or standing prayer vigils of the early Church.</note>. This they used on the Lord’s Day, and at that time which [is] between the so-

# ON THE CUSTOMS OF THE BOGOMILS.

...the period between the festivals of **Easter** and **Pentecost** <note>In the early Church, kneeling was often forbidden during the fifty days of the Easter season to emphasize the joy of the Resurrection.</note> was especially commended. On this matter, one should consult Johannes Hoornbeeck in *Sacred Miscellanies*, p. 606; Joachim Hildebrand in *On the Prayers of the Ancient Christians*, chapter VI; the celebrated Caspar Calvör in Volume II of his *Ecclesiastical Ritual*; and an anonymous French author in a particular book where he extensively tracked that ancient rite through ecclesiastical monuments. I will not omit that gesture which I remember being mentioned only by Eusebius. For here, in his commentary on the Psalms—which we owe to the tireless industry of the celebrated Bernard de Montfaucon <note>A 17th-century Benedictine monk and scholar who founded the study of paleography.</note>—he records that Christians completed their prayers with bent knees and touching their foreheads to the earth. We return to our Bogomils, from whom our discussion has slightly diverted. We read that they also took care in performing their prayers not to fulfill this duty of piety in a "holy building" <note>original: *aede sacra*</note>. They interpreted the Savior’s saying <note>Referencing Matthew 6:6: "But when you pray, go into your room..."</note> in this sense, where He commands those praying to seek out hidden places removed from the eyes of men. Euthymius <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century Byzantine monk.</note> says the following in Section XLII: <note>original Greek: Ταμεῖον λέγουσι τὸν νοῦν καὶ ἀπὸ τοῦδε τοῦ ῥητοῦ κινούμενοι διδάσκουσι, μὴ προσεύχεσθαι μηδένα τούτων ἐν Ἐκκλησίαις, καίτοι σαφῶς ἀκούοντες τοῦ Προφήτου Δαβὶδ, κελεύοντες ἐν Ἐκκλησίαις εὐλογεῖν τὸν θεόν.</note> They say the "inner room" is the mind, and moved by this saying, they teach that none of them should pray in churches—even though they clearly hear the Prophet David commanding them to bless God in the assemblies.

VIII. Just as the Bogomils detested temples, they felt no more kindly toward the **IMAGES** with which those buildings are usually adorned. This is apparent from the testimony of Euthymius, which we cited above from the *Refutation of the Massalians* regarding the rejection of temples. Now we shall learn more of the same from Section XI of his *Panoply* <note>The *Panoplia Dogmatica*, a comprehensive defense of Orthodox doctrine against various heresies.</note>. There you may read these things: They all together reject the hierarchs <note>The Church leadership, such as bishops.</note> and the Fathers as idolaters because of their veneration of icons; they estrange all the pious Emperors from the rank of Christians, and they call only the Iconoclasts <note>original Greek: εἰκονομάχους (*eikonomachous*), those who destroyed religious images.</note>—especially **Copronymus** <note>The Emperor Constantine V (reigned 741–775), a fierce opponent of icons; "Copronymus" was a derogatory nickname meaning "the dung-named."</note>—orthodox and faithful. For they dishonor the

# ON THE CUSTOMS OF THE BOGOMILS.

...venerable icons, calling them "idols of the nations—silver and gold, the works of human hands" <note>A common polemical use of Psalm 135:15 to equate Christian icons with pagan idols.</note>, being ignorant that an idol is one thing and an icon <note>original: εἰκὼν (eikon)</note> is another. For the prototypes of idols are unsubstantial and false; they called those "gods" which were not gods but demons who feigned divinity. But the prototypes of our icons are truly existing and true to their name. Those are idols of the defiled, but these are icons of the saints. More has been written extensively regarding the holy icons in the chapter against the Iconoclasts <note>term: Iconoclasts (from the Greek for "image-breakers"), a movement that sought to ban and destroy religious images.</note>. *Germanus, Patriarch of Constantinople, rails even more vehemently against those holding such views in an Oration he delivered for the restoration of the Images on the first day of the fasts. This can be read in Gretser, Volume II, "On the Cross," page 549; from this, I shall extract those points which serve our purpose.*

"But because," he says, "there are even now some offscourings of the filth of Copronymus <note>A derogatory nickname for the Iconoclast Emperor Constantine V, literally meaning "Dung-named."</note>, and these rabid dogs and filth-loving swine vomit out manure—for so I call their blasphemous words—against those who venerate the sacred likenesses of Christ and all the saints. These are the ones now called Bogomils, the genuine disciples of the Antichrist and fellow-pupils of the traitor Judas; I shall turn the face of my argument toward them. One of these demon-faced Bogomils asked me, 'Why,' he said, 'do you worship walls and boards, and lime, and various colors?' By saying this, he was alluding to the holy icons. And I said to him: 'When have you ever seen one of the children of our Church going to the kilns where the lime is, or to the ruins where the heaps of stones are, or to the paint shops, and worshipping or venerating these things? For if we attributed worship to the stones and colors, we would be compelled to worship them wherever we saw a stone or a board. But this is not the case. Not even you, the enemy of truth, would dare to say this. For we do not offer worship simply to the materials, but to the form manifested in the materials...'"

...and not to every form, but to that of Christ, of the most holy Mother of God <note>original: "Θεομήτορος" (Theometoros), a title for the Virgin Mary</note>, and of the other saints. For the honor of the icon passes over to the prototype <note>The "prototype" is the actual person or divine being that the image represents; the argument here is that the image is merely a window to the divine.</note>. I omit the rest: for the entire oration is composed to refute the Bogomils, who among other things (page 561) are said to worship not God, but a demon, to whom they attribute this universe of things, as its founder and author: "The Bogomils," he says, "ascribe the creation of the world to the devil, so that it follows for them to both consider and worship him as god; and the god of the Bogomils is a little demon, and each of these Bogomils has introduced idols of these things directly against the prototype. For these people are dark-formed and misshapen, and to speak plainly, demon-faced <note>original: "δαιμονοπρόσωποι" (daimonoprosopoi)</note>." From all this, to finally bring the matter to a summary, it cannot be obscure that the Bogomils opposed that superstitious cult of images which, at the time Germanus wrote this, was spreading through the whole Church. This could have provided them with matter for praise, if in other chapters of doctrine they had also decided to protect and reclaim the ancient purity of teaching. Nor indeed will Germanus satisfy a wise reader when he observes—as is commonly brought forward—that this worship is referred not to the image itself, but to its prototype <note>original: "πρωτότυπον" (prototypon)</note>. For in this way, it is nearly the case that we might consider even the idolatry of the Gentiles <note>term: Gentiles (the non-Christian, "pagan" Greeks and Romans)</note> to be somewhat excused, as they were accustomed to cloak the worship of statues with the same reasoning. There is a famous passage from Celsus <note>A 2nd-century Platonic philosopher and a fierce critic of early Christianity.</note>, which Origen preserved in full in Book VII of the work he wrote against Celsus's slanders (page 373 of the English edition). But how slippery and vain this response is, Origen shows clearly in the same place (page 373) when he warns that one must abstain from this adoration of likenesses instituted for divine honor, if only so that the unlearned are not given an occasion to count them among the Gods. I omit similar passages, such as those from Maximus of Tyre <note>A Greek rhetorician and philosopher of the 2nd century.</note> in Dissertation XXXVIII, and others in Eusebius <note>An early Christian historian and bishop.</note>, Book III, chapter 7 of *The Preparation for the Gospel*. See, if you please, Edward Stillingfleet <note>A 17th-century Anglican theologian who wrote extensively on the differences between the Church of England and the Roman Catholic Church.</note>...

...in the English book in which he charged the Roman Church with idolatry <note>original: "εἰδωλολατρείας" (eidololatreias)</note>, page 65; *Samuel Bochart* in Part I of the *Hierozoicon* <note>A famous 17th-century study of animals in the Bible</note>, page 374; *Pfanner* in his *System of Gentile Theology*, chapter XI, section 7; and, he who has most recently of all treated this argument, *Jurieu* in his *History of Worship and Dogmas*, Part III, Tract III, and following. However, just as the early Church did not allow itself to be persuaded into this superstition by that argument, so the Bogomils are entirely to be approved for ordering that the foreign customs of the Image-worshippers <note>original: "Iconolatrarum"</note> be kept far from them. For it is proved—partly from that very *Oration of Germanus*, and partly from other monuments of contemporary writers—that at that time the profane worship of images had been brought to full maturity. A worthy author to be read on this subject is *Friedrich Spanheim* in his *Restored History of Images*, which was published separately at Leiden <note>original: "Lugd. Bat." (Lugdunum Batavorum)</note> in 1686 in octavo, and which we read as inserted into Volume II of his *Works*; and also *John Forbes* in his *Historico-Theological Instructions*, book VII, chapter VI.

IX. Another rite seems to stand in opposition to this "abomination of images," a rite which *Germanus*—in the same Oration we just used—reproaches them for, as if it were akin to the worship of images. This rite consists in the fact that they held the Book of the Gospels in such high value that they did not hesitate to embrace and kiss it. I gather this from the words which are read on page 554:

"You say that the book of the Gospels is holy, and you revere it as a treasure of eternal blessings? Or do you also turn away from it and rank it among things without honor? He [the Bogomil] says: 'By no means do I consider it without honor; rather, I deem it worthy of great honor, and I bow before it and kiss it.' You have said this well. For it is truly most precious and most holy, and truly the book of eternal life. Look, therefore: you shall be condemned by your own mouth, Bogomil! Tell me then, is this book not composed of wooden boards? Is it not of skins? Is it not of the threads that [bind] the skins..." <note>The Greek text here is a dialogue between the Orthodox Patriarch Germanus and a representative of the Bogomil sect. Germanus is attempting to trap the Bogomil in a logical contradiction: if the Bogomil rejects icons because they are material objects (wood and paint), why does he kiss the Gospel book, which is also a material object (wood, leather, and thread)?</note>

<meta>catchword: ...binding [γόντων],</meta>

<summary>This page discusses historical scholarly defenses of the Bogomils' rejection of icons and describes a rhetorical trap set by Patriarch Germanus, who questioned why the Bogomils could kiss the physical material of a Bible while rejecting the material of an icon.</summary>
<keywords>Bogomils, Iconoclasm, Germanus I of Constantinople, Idolatry, Gospel Book, Image worship, Samuel Bochart, Friedrich Spanheim</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument of Patriarch Germanus I of Constantinople, who uses a rhetorical maneuver to suggest that the Bogomils' intense reverence for the physical Book of the Gospels is hypocritical given their rejection of religious icons. It then transitions into describing their initiation rituals as recorded by Euthymius Zigabenus.</meta>

<page-num>120</page-num>

# ->ON THE CUSTOMS OF THE BOGOMILS.<-

...[of living] beings, is it not made of ink and often other colors? Therefore, when you yourself venerate and kiss the book of the Gospels, do you venerate and kiss the wooden boards, the ink, and the skins? Or the words of God written in the book? "Yes," he says, "the words written in the book." <note>original: "...ζόντων, οὐκ ἀπὸ μέλανος καὶ ἑτέρων πολλάκις χρωμάτων; ἆρ' οὖν καὶ σὺ τὸ βιβλίον τῶν Εὐαγγελίων προσκυνῶν καὶ ἀσπαζόμενος, τὰς σανίδας καὶ τὸ μέλαν καὶ τὰ δέρματα προσκυνεῖς, καὶ ἀσπάζῃ; ἢ τοὺς λόγους τοῦ Θεοῦ τοὺς ἐγγεγραμμένους τῇ βίβλῳ; ναί φησι τοὺς λόγους τοὺς ἐγγεγραμμένους τῇ βίβλῳ." This is a quote from Patriarch Germanus I, arguing that if one can honor the "ink and skin" of a Bible for the sake of the Word, one should be able to honor an icon for the sake of the saint.</note> With these words, Germanus—intending, as it were, to slaughter the Bogomils <term>Bogomils: A medieval religious sect that held dualist beliefs and rejected the material trappings of the Orthodox Church, such as icons and elaborate rituals.</term> with their own sword—indicates that they, who venerate the Gospel Codex <term>Codex: An ancient manuscript in book form.</term> so religiously, could be accused of offering worship to the wooden boards and skins in which it is bound. However, they would undoubtedly brush aside such a charge by arguing that this worship is offered not to these materials, but to the Divine Word. It certainly cannot be denied that the Bogomils were excessive here as well, for they pursued the Gospel Codices not only with kissing—which you might perhaps tolerate and interpret as a sign of the singular love they bear for them—but even with adoration. Yet neither practice lacks precedents, which the man most learned in ecclesiastical antiquity, **Johann Andreas Schmidt**, collected in three dissertations on the worship of the Gospels. Add also **Richard Simon** in his *New Observations* illustrating the New Testament, page 121; **Doughty** <note>John Doughty, an English divine and critic</note> in the *Analecta of the New Testament*, Excursion XVI; the famous **Cave** <note>William Cave, author of "Primitive Christianity"</note> in his *Sketch of Nascent Christianity*, page 216; and the famous **Fabricius** <note>Johann Albert Fabricius, a massive bibliographer of the era</note> in book IV of the *Greek Library*, chapter V, page 142.

X. We mentioned above several rites which the Bogomils were accustomed to use when they initiated new recruits <note>original: "tirones," a term for military recruits or beginners</note> into their errors. We add here the one which **Euthymius** <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century Byzantine monk who wrote the "Panoply of Dogma" against heretics.</note> records in his *Refutation of the Massalians* <note>The Massalians were an older sect often confused or equated with the Bogomils by Orthodox writers.</note>, Anathema 13, as having been received among them in the administration of that same sacrament of initiation. <gloss>e</gloss> For from this we learn that the Bogomils were accustomed to spit upon those who wished to join their side, and to wash those same people all over with impure water, doubtless with the intention that they might, by a clear proof, [show] their contempt for those they deemed worthy of it as people alienated from the truth...

<meta>catchword: attested</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the irony of Bogomils venerating the physical Book of the Gospels while rejecting icons, and describes an initiation ritual involving spitting and washing with "impure" water to show contempt for the initiates' former lives.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the Bogomils' religious practices, transitioning from their treatment of the Gospel books to a detailed account by the 12th-century theologian Euthymius Zigabenus regarding their initiation rituals, which he describes as perverse inversions of Orthodox Christian rites.</meta>

<page-num>121</page-num>
<header># ->On the Customs of the Bogomils.<-</header>

...testified. These are the words of Euthymius: "To those who, for the subversion of the entire faith in God, perform other wicked deeds to those unhappily initiated by them, and who—instead of the divine and holy breathing-upon <term>Breathing-upon: Also known as 'insufflation,' a ritual act in the early Church where the priest would blow on the face of the person being baptized to symbolize the gift of the Holy Spirit or the driving out of demons.</term> which we received from the Lord Christ for the mystical inspiration of the Holy Spirit—spit upon those being initiated by them with spittle of which they themselves are worthy. They perform this act upon their initiates just as we do against demons; furthermore, they smear them from bottom to top with filthy water using sponges to subvert holy baptism and the illuminating presence of the Divine Spirit: let them be anathema <term>Anathema: A formal curse by a church leader, excommunicating someone and denouncing their doctrine.</term>." <note>original Greek: "τοῖς ἐπ' ἀνατροπὴν... ἀνάθεμα"; Latin translation: "Iis, qui ad universae... anathema." This passage is from Euthymius Zigabenus's 'Against the Bogomils.'</note>

There is no doubt that the Bogomils established these rituals by deliberate design as opposites to those which the Church had adopted at that time. I believe that the foul expulsion of spit was, as Euthymius himself rightly noted, intended as a mockery of the "breathing-upon" <note>original: "insufflationi"</note> into the face and ears of those being initiated, which was traditionally performed three times. This practice is prescribed by Canon 170 of the Universal Church, as noted by Tollius <note>Jacobus Tollius (1633–1696), a Dutch scholar who studied early Christian rites.</note>. Although this specific ritual belongs to a later age, it was already familiar and widespread several centuries before the Bogomils began their innovations. Conversely, that washing performed with the aid of a sponge and water full of filth seems to have been aimed at the anointing or chrism <note>original: "χρίσμα" (chrisma)</note>, which traditionally followed baptism, and then...

The author cites Euthymius Zigabenus and mentions Canon law to show these were calculated subversions of established Church traditions.</summary>

...and that it precedes him and receives him as a sign of joy, arising from the grace of the Holy Spirit bestowed upon him, and perhaps as a proof of spiritual strength, so that, like athletes anointed with oil, they might enter into combat with adversaries of every kind. See Tertullian’s *On Baptism*, Chapter VII, and the so-called *Clementine Constitutions*, Book VII, Chapter XLV. You may read more regarding these rites in Josephus Vicecomes’ five books *On the Rites of Baptism*, Edmund Martene’s first book *On the Rites of the Church*, and Friedrich Ulrich Calixtus’s dissertation *On Baptism and Its Ancient Rites*.

XI. Let it suffice for us to have touched upon these things; there now remains to be discussed a certain new type of self-chosen worship <term>Self-chosen worship: Derived from the Greek *ethelothreskeia* (will-worship), referring to religious practices or rigors created by human will rather than divine command, often used by critics to describe excessive asceticism.</term> which the Bogomils <note>Bogomils: A medieval dualist sect from the Balkans who believed the material world was created by an evil entity and practiced strict asceticism.</note> fashioned for themselves. For they were not accustomed to eating meat, cheese, or eggs, and they interpreted this as a sign of singular piety. Euthymius mentions this in Section XXXVII, where he says that by their own judgment they **"pursue a more rigorous and pure way of life, abstaining from the eating of meat, cheese, eggs, marriage, and such things."** <note>original Greek: "μετιέναι πολιτείαν ἀκριβεστέραν τε καὶ καθαρωτέραν, ἀπεχομένους κρεωφαγίας, καὶ τυρῶ καὶ ὠῶ καὶ γάμω καὶ τῶν τοιούτων."</note> That is: *they follow a more upright and holy way of living, namely by refraining from the consumption of flesh, from cheese and eggs, from weddings, and similar things.* I see that in the version by Zinus <note>Pietro Francesco Zini (16th century), a translator of Greek theological works into Latin.</note>, no mention is made of cheese. The same Euthymius, in his *Refutation of the Massalians* <note>Massalians: Also known as Euchites, they were a sect that believed in constant prayer to expel a resident demon; they were often linked by historical writers to the Bogomils.</note> on page 119, pronounces a curse upon **"those who say that marriage in the Lord and the eating of meat according to God’s law are abominable to God, and for this reason they overturn both."** <note>original Greek: "τοῖς λέγουσιν, ὡς ὁ ἐν κυρίῳ γάμος, καὶ ἡ κατὰ Θεὸν κρεωφαγία βδελυκτά εἰσι τῷ Θεῷ καὶ διὰ τοῦτο ἀμφότερα ἀνατρέπουσι."</note> I do not doubt that among those things from which the Bogomils abstained was also wine, and one might suspect that for "eggs" <note>original Greek: "ὠοῦ" (oou)</note> perhaps one should read "wine." <note>original Greek: "οἴνου" (oinou)</note> For the Manichaeans <note>Manichaeans: An older dualistic religion that viewed the world as a struggle between light and darkness; their strict "Elect" members avoided meat and wine.</note>—whose footsteps they seem to have followed closely here as constant imitators—carefully guarded themselves against certain foods and especially from wine. This is reported in many places by Augustine, who also observes in his book *On the Morals of the Manichaeans*, Chapter XIX, that **"many of them, unmindful of this precept, [delight] in wine and meat,"**
multos <meta>catchword: many</meta>

### ->ON THE CUSTOMS OF THE BOGOMILS.<-

...that many were discovered washing in the baths. Nor were there lacking others among the disturbers of the Church who made this error—derived from the pagans <note>original: "Gentilibus"</note>—their own. Such was Saturnilus <note>A 2nd-century Gnostic teacher from Antioch who preached extreme asceticism and claimed the world was created by seven angels.</note>, as well as Tatian and the Encratites <note>A radical ascetic sect that rejected marriage, meat, and wine; "Encratite" comes from the Greek word for "self-controlled."</note>, as well as certain disciples of Basilides <note>An early Gnostic teacher in Alexandria who taught that the physical world was a lower realm.</note>. See Irenaeus, books 22 and 30, Epiphanius on heresy 47, and the Author of *Praedestinatus* <note>A 5th-century work that lists and refutes 90 different heresies.</note> on heresy 25. In the time of Bernard <note>St. Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a powerful Cistercian abbot and influential mystic.</note>, there were those who called themselves *Apostolics*, whose minds were occupied by the same opinion, as the esteemed Doctor of the Church relates in Sermon 66 on the Song of Songs <note>original: "in Canticum."</note>. *These people*, he says, *forbid marriage; they abstain from foods.* These same can be compared even more closely with the Bogomils <note>A medieval dualist sect that believed the material world was the creation of Satan.</note>, whom we have seen detest cheese, because, by the testimony of that same Bernard, *they shudder at milk and whatever is made from it, and finally, at everything that is produced from sexual intercourse.*

XII. Now we shall look closely at the customs which the Bogomils demonstrated toward others. Here, in the first place, an intolerable **PRIDE** <note>original: **FASTVS**</note> and arrogance meet the eyes, by which they attributed nothing to others and everything to themselves. To this end belong most of the allegorical interpretations of the Gospels, which they were accustomed to bring forward with great ostentation, for the purpose of making it appear by how great a distance others were separated from their own holiness. Therefore, they call their own assembly <note>original: "Synagogam suam"</note> "Bethlehem," and they call the assembly of the rest "Herod," who tries to kill the Christ—or the word of God—conceived among them. Thus they also call themselves "Magi," but call the Church of those who disagree with them "Jerusalem." Euthymius <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century Byzantine monk and theologian who wrote the "Panoply of Doctrine" against heresies.</note> reports this in Section 28: <note>Translated from the original Greek: "They call their own assembly Bethlehem. For they say that Christ, or the word of God which heralds the truth of the faith, was born among them. But they understand Herod to be our Church, which attempts to destroy the word of truth born among them. And they again address themselves as Magi, being truthful only in this: for they are truly sorcerers, corrupters, and avenging spirits. Again, they call our Church Jerusalem, and the star, the Law of Moses. For they say they were guided by it [the Law] as far as..."</note> "They call their own assembly Bethlehem. For they say that Christ, or the word of God which heralds the truth of the faith, was born among them. But they understand Herod to be our Church, which attempts to destroy the word of truth born among them. And they again address themselves as Magi, being truthful only in this: for they are truly sorcerers, corrupters, and avenging spirits. Again, they call our Church Jerusalem, and the star, the Law of Moses. For they say they were guided by it <gloss>read 'by it' [singular]</gloss> as far as..."

<page-num>124</page-num>
### ### ->ON THE CUSTOMS OF THE BOGOMILS.<-

...of our faith <note>original: "τῆς καθ' ἡμᾶς πίστεως"</note>. Then, they say that they learned from the High Priests and the other scribes and teachers that Christ is born in the aforementioned Bethlehem. For they claim that their first teachers came from among us. In this way, they considered those who were outside their own group to be among the number of the Sadducees and Pharisees, and they displayed a truly Pharisaic pride by hurling insults at them. Euthymius <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century Byzantine monk and author of the *Panoplia Dogmatica*, our primary source for Bogomil beliefs.</note> describes the matter in Section 31. He says: "They address the believers among us as Pharisees and Sadducees coming to the baptism of John. Oh, what shamelessness! Then, insulting us, they call us 'offspring of vipers' <note>original: "γεννήματα ἐχιδνῶν"</note>—that is, the seed of the serpent who lay with Eve, as has been explained before <note>A reference to the Bogomil myth that the material world was corrupted when the fallen angel Satanael seduced Eve, creating the human race from that union.</note>." (The translator rendered this as *as they themselves say* <note>original: "ut ipsi ajunt"</note>, and thus found another reading in his own manuscript, which nevertheless seems entirely inferior to ours, since through it he refers back to what was said earlier.) "And they tell their followers not to be upset, as they were truthfully addressed in this way by John the Baptist."

They were driven by the same spirit of pride when they interpreted Christ’s winnowing fan <note>A tool used to toss grain into the air so the wind blows away the lighter chaff.</note> as the preaching of the Gospel, and the threshing floor as the assembly of Christians (who are very diverse in their habits). They declared that they themselves were the purest wheat, while the rest took the place of the chaff. We owe this account to the same Euthymius in Section 32. He says: "They call the 'sandals' <note>original Greek "hypodemata": sandals</note> of Christ the 'examples' <note>original Greek "hypodeigmata": examples. The Bogomils used the phonetic similarity between these words to create their allegory.</note> of the signs which he showed to the disciples and the crowds, which John was unable to carry, because he was entirely unable to show such things himself. And the 'winnowing fan' <note>original Greek "ptyon"</note> of Christ is the Word of the Gospel as it was 'spat out' <note>original Greek "apoptysthenta". Another pun on the word for fan.</note> through his mouth. The 'threshing floor' refers to the Christians, as they are of various kinds—some being orthodox, while others, on the contrary, are heretical. The 'wheat' they call their own faith, as it is pure and nourishing, while 'chaff' they call our faith, as it is useless and worthy of the fire." Euthymius takes up and abominates this comparison with these words: "What pious ear could endure such blasphemies? My soul swells with indignation at these absurdities, and my senses..."

<summary>This page describes how the Bogomils viewed mainstream Orthodox Christians as modern-day Pharisees and "seed of the serpent," while allegorizing biblical terms like the "winnowing fan" and "wheat" to assert their own spiritual superiority.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Bogomil elitism and their allegorical reinterpretation of the Gospels. The previous page described how they insulted the Orthodox Church as "offspring of vipers"; here, the author explores how they claimed the promises of the New Testament exclusively for themselves while casting outsiders as "dogs and swine."</meta>

<page-num>125</page-num>
### ### ->ON THE CUSTOMS OF THE BOGOMILS.<-

"My heart boils," <note>original: "μαίμασσε"</note> according to the prophet, <note>Likely a reference to the zeal or distress felt by biblical prophets like Jeremiah when witnessing wickedness.</note> "and I wish to cut short this discourse and proceed no further, nor give over to light and memory those things worthy only of darkness and oblivion. But I must force myself a little longer and endure for a while the tiresome talk of these endless babblers, who are as deranged as they are abominable." <note>original: "Παραϐιαστέον δὲ μικρὸν ἔτι... τῶν ἀπερατολόγων τούτων καὶ παραφόρων καὶ ϐδελυρῶν."</note> The Latin version does not contain this final sentence; however, it is necessary here because, although he said in the immediately preceding words that he would put an end to his review of these absurdities, he here changes his mind and sets about weaving the same web once more.

Therefore, in Section 34, he relates that they interpreted their own Church as Capernaum, but the assembly of those who disagree with them as the city of Nazareth—the city which the Savior left in order to move into the other. "They address us as Nazareth," <note>original: "Ναζαρὲτ μὲν ἡμᾶς προσαγορεύουσι"</note> they say, "but themselves as Capernaum, and they claim that Christ now dwells among them, having abandoned our congregation." Just as they do this, they no less claim for themselves alone those types of blessings <note>The Beatitudes from the Sermon on the Mount.</note> which Christ commanded his followers to expect; nor do they admit anyone into their society who has not openly professed their name.

Euthymius puts it this way: "They assert that Christ spoke all the beatitudes only concerning the faithful among them—that is, the Bogomils." <note>original: "τοὺς μακαρισμοὺς πάντας περὶ μόνων τῶν κατ' αὐτοὺς πιστῶν... εἰπεῖν τὸν χριστὸν διαϐεϐαιοῦνται."</note> Lest they seem to have said this recklessly, they applied to themselves the praises of the "poor in spirit," the "mourning," the "hungering," and the "thirsting for righteousness." Indeed, they did not think anything was lacking to them that the Savior intended to be the property of those desiring true happiness. "For they claim to be such people," says Euthymius, "the poor in spirit, the mourning, the hungering, and the thirsting for righteousness, and so on. They claim that they are the ones called the 'salt of the earth' and the 'light of the world,' and whatever else Christ said regarding the apostles." <note>original: "τούτους δὲ κληθῆναι καὶ ἅλας τῆς γῆς, καὶ φῶς τοῦ κόσμου..."</note>

Those words of the Savior, in which he forbids the Holy thing to be opened to dogs or pearls to be cast before swine, <note>A reference to Matthew 7:6.</note> they explain in such a way that the "holy thing" signifies a simpler faith, "pearls" signify their secret doctrines, but the assembly of people alienated from their own sacred rites signifies the "dogs and swine."

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the Bogomils' elitist theology, specifically how they appropriated biblical metaphors—like "holy things" and "pearls"—to distinguish their inner circle from the rest of the Christian world, whom they disparaged.</meta>

<page-num>126</page-num>
### ### ->ON THE CUSTOMS OF THE BOGOMILS.<-

...they say. "They call 'holy,'" <note>original: "Ἅγιον"</note> says Euthymius, "their own simpler faith, but the 'pearls' <note>original: "μαργαρίτας"</note> are the more mystical and costly doctrines of their error. As for the 'dogs and swine'—oh, what surpassing insolence!—they mean the pious among us, whom they view as idolaters. I shall refrain from speaking of their other unspeakable words. Then they add that they receive those coming to them as a 'dog' and a 'swine'; first, they purify them through fasting and prayer, then they baptize them, as we have described previously. Only then, as the person is enlightened by small steps of progress and ascension, do they entrust them with the 'holy' and the 'pearls.'"

They seem to owe this practice to Basilides <note>An early Gnostic teacher in Alexandria (2nd century AD) who claimed a private, superior knowledge of God.</note>, who, with no less pride, as Epiphanius <note>Epiphanius of Salamis, a 4th-century bishop known for his "Panarion," a catalog of heresies.</note> records, is believed to have said: "We are the men, and all others are swine and dogs." <note>original: "ἡμεῖς ἐσμὲν οἱ ἄνθρωποι, οἱ δὲ ἄλλοι πάντες ὗες καὶ κύνες"</note> That is: *We are humans, but all others are swine and dogs.*

So too does Bernard <note>Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a Cistercian monk and influential mystic.</note> in his 65th Sermon on the Song of Songs testify regarding the "Apostolicals"—the fanatics of his own time—saying: *"In accordance with the Gospel, do they take care not to give what is holy to dogs, and pearls to swine? Or is this an open admission that they are not of the Church, because they judge all who are in the Church to be dogs and swine?"*

Even our own age has produced men who belch out similar voices against any assembly that differs from their own opinions—opinions introduced without scriptural authority <note>original: "ἀτερ γραφῆς"</note>. This will be clear to anyone inspecting the writings of the Roemelingians <note>Followers of Christian Römeling (1667–1740), a Lutheran pastor who became a radical pietist and critic of the church hierarchy.</note>, which the celebrated Edzard <note>Sebastian Edzard (1672–1736), a Hamburg theologian known for his staunch defense of Lutheran orthodoxy against both Pietists and Catholics.</note> recently opposed in his usual manly fashion.

But let us listen further to the ornamented words <note>original: "phalerata verba," meaning flashy or deceptive language intended to impress.</note> with which the Bogomils decorated their "Pandora" <note>A mythological reference to a gift that seems attractive but contains all the world's evils; here it refers to the sect's deceptive allure.</note> and offered it for others to gaze upon. It seemed too little to them if they merely appeared superior to their equals and those of their own time; they proceeded to such a point of insolence and rashness that they dragged the ancient Doctors of the Church into the same contempt. For they judged that those men—specifically Basil and Gregory <note>St. Basil the Great and St. Gregory of Nazianzus, 4th-century "Cappadocian Fathers" who defined the orthodox doctrine of the Trinity.</note>, whom they remembered had resisted the heretics attacking the divinity of Christ—should be called by the name of "false prophets." "They call them false prophets," <note>original: "ψευδοπροφήτας"</note> he says...

<header>### ->ON THE CUSTOMS OF THE BOGOMILS.<-</header>

...they say—oh, what absurdity! They speak of **Basil the Great** <note>Basil of Caesarea (c. 330–379), a foundational doctor of the Church and influential monastic leader.</note> in his dogmas, **Gregory** <note>Gregory of Nazianzus (c. 329–389), known as "The Theologian" for his defense of the Trinity.</note> the star of Theology, and **John of the Golden Tongue** <note>John Chrysostom (c. 347–407), the Archbishop of Constantinople famous for his eloquence.</note> [Chrysostom], as if they had taught from their own minds <note>original: "ὡς οἴκοθεν δογματίσαντας" — implying they invented doctrines rather than following divine revelation.</note>. For I shall remain silent regarding the other drunken insults of the "thunderbolt," the "whirlwind," and the "abyss" <note>original: "σκηπτοῦ καὶ πρησῆρος καὶ χάσματος" — these appear to be specific, perhaps metaphorical, epithets the Bogomils used to mock the Church Fathers.</note>, and other words worthy of every punishment, which they hurl against these saints more than any others.

In a similar way, they applied the words of **Matthew 7:22** <note>"Many will say to me on that day, ‘Lord, Lord, did we not prophesy in your name and in your name drive out demons...’"</note>, which are spoken against false prophets, to those whose miracles were celebrated in the Church. **Euthymius** <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century monk and the primary source for Bogomil beliefs.</note> says in Section 46: 

"Interpreting these words of the Gospel, they say these are the holy hierarchs and divine Fathers among us, as many as were deemed worthy of prophetic grace, cast out demons, and performed many other wonders. For they say that the demons dwelling within those saints performed all these miracles to amaze the more foolish. And yet, 'a demon does not cast out a demon.' For **Truth Itself** <note>meaning Christ.</note> says this—you most raving dog!—the 'venom of asps is under your lips.'" <note>A quote from Psalm 140:3, here used by the author to rebuke the Bogomils for their blasphemy against the saints.</note>

That is to say, they wanted all the miracles reported of the saints to be attributed not to divine power but to diabolical tricks, by which the unlearned might be turned toward admiration. Thus, those who sought a greater reputation for themselves by tearing others down learned to interpret everything in the worst possible way. 

Since they directed all their thoughts and plans toward this goal, they also claimed for themselves the praise of wisdom because they built all their own teachings upon the **Lord's Prayer** <note>The "Our Father" (Pater Noster).</note> as if upon a rock, while others instead laid down other prayers as a foundation—slippery like sand and prone to collapse. 

Euthymius says in Section 47: "They call themselves the 'wise man' who built his house upon the rock of the prayer, *Our Father*. But they call us 'foolish,' having built our house upon the sand of other prayers—many in number, but weak and senseless, at least as it seems to those senseless people."

Furthermore, they call their own doctrine "new wine," and in turn call those who do not <meta>catchword: receive,</meta>

<header>### ->ON THE CUSTOMS OF THE BOGOMILS.<-</header>

...they say. "They call 'holy'," says **Euthymius** <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century Byzantine monk whose *Panoplia Dogmatica* is a primary source on the Bogomils.</note>, "their own simpler faith <note>original: "τὴν κατ’ αὐτὰς ἁπλοστέραν πίστιν"</note>, and 'pearls' they call the more mystical and precious doctrines <note>original: "μαργαρίτας δὲ τὰ μυστικώτερα καὶ πολυτελέστερα δόγματα"</note> of their error. But 'dogs' and 'pigs'—oh, the extreme insolence!—they call the pious among us, as if we were idolaters. I will refrain from repeating their other unspeakable slanders. Furthermore, they add that they receive those coming to join them as 'dogs' and 'pigs'; first they purify them through fasting and prayer, then they baptize them, as we have already described. Once the person has been enlightened by these small steps and advancements, they then entrust the 'holy thing' and the 'pearls' to them."

They seem to have inherited this view from **Basilides** <note>A major Gnostic teacher in 2nd-century Alexandria.</note>, who, with no less arrogance according to **Epiphanius** <note>Epiphanius of Salamis (c. 310–403), a bishop famous for his writings against heresies.</note>, we believe said: *"We are the human beings; everyone else is a pig or a dog."* <note>original Latin: "Nos sumus homines, ceteri vero omnes sues et canes."</note> **Bernard [of Clairvaux]** testifies to the same in his 65th Sermon on the *Song of Songs* regarding the "Apostolics," the fanatics of his own time: *"Do they not,"* he asks, *"take care, according to the Gospel, not to give what is holy to dogs or pearls to swine? Is this not an open admission that they are not part of the Church, since they consider all who belong to the Church to be dogs and swine?"*

Our own age has also seen men who spew similar insults against any group that does not share their opinions—opinions introduced without any basis in Scripture <note>original Greek: "ἄτερ γραφῆς" — without writing or scripture.</note>. This will be obvious to anyone looking at the writings of the **Roemelingians** <note>Followers of Christian Römeling (1658–1747), a radical Pietist who claimed mainstream Christianity was corrupt.</note>, which the celebrated **Edzard** <note>Sebastian Edzard (1672–1736), a Lutheran theologian in Hamburg known for his polemics against religious dissenters.</note> recently and vigorously opposed.

But let us listen further to the flowery words with which the Bogomils have adorned their "Pandora" <note>A reference to the mythological Pandora's box—something that looks attractive but conceals many evils.</note> and offered it for others to contemplate. It was not enough for them to appear superior to their contemporaries; they reached such a height of insolence and recklessness that they dragged the ancient **Doctors of the Church** into the same contempt. For they judged that those men—specifically **Basil** and **Gregory**, whom they remembered had stood against the heretics who attacked the divinity of Christ—should be called by the name "false prophets." "They call them false prophets," he [Euthymius] says...

...they say—oh, what absurdity! <note>original: "ὦ τῆς ἀτοπίας!"</note> They speak of **Basil the Great** <note>Basil of Caesarea (c. 330–379), a foundational figure in Eastern monasticism and theology.</note> in matters of dogma, **Gregory** the star of Theology <note>Gregory of Nazianzus (c. 329–390), known for his influential oratory on the Trinity.</note>, and **John the Golden-tongued** <note>John Chrysostom (c. 347–407), named for his legendary eloquence.</note>, as if they were merely teaching their own human inventions <note>original: "ὡς οἴκοθεν δογματίσαντας"</note>. And yet, "a demon does not cast out a demon" <note>An allusion to Matthew 12:24–26.</note>; for this is what Christ, Truth itself, says—*“O rabid dog, the poison of asps is under your lips.”* <note>original: "λυσσωδέσατε κύον, ἰὸς ἀσπίδων ὑπὸ τὰ χείλη σου." A fierce rhetorical rebuttal by the author against the Bogomil claims.</note>

Evidently, they wanted all the miracles reported about the Fathers to be attributed not to divine power, but to diabolical trickery, by which the unlearned were turned toward admiration. Thus, those who sought a greater reputation for themselves through the degradation of others learned to interpret everything in the worst possible way. Since they directed all their thoughts and plans toward this goal, they also claimed for themselves the praise of wisdom because they built all their own works upon the **Lord's Prayer** <note>The "Our Father." The Bogomils famously rejected all formal liturgy and prayers except for the Lord's Prayer.</note> as if upon a rock, while they claimed everyone else laid down other prayers as a foundation—prayers as slippery as sand and prone to collapse.

*"They call themselves 'wise men',"* says Euthymius in Section 47, *"who have built their house upon the rock of prayer—the 'Our Father.' But they call us 'fools' who have built our house upon the sand of other prayers, which are many, but are weak and senseless—or so it seems to those fools."* <note>original: "Ἄνδρα μὲν φρόνιμον... ὀνομάζουσιν ἑαυτοὺς... μωρὸν δὲ ἡμᾶς..."</note> Furthermore, they call their own doctrine "new wine," and in turn, they call "old wineskins" those who do not

<header>### ->ON THE CUSTOMS OF THE BOGOMILS.<-</header>

...should admit [the old wine], but rather new [wineskins], who are ready to receive it. **Euthymius** <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, a 12th-century Byzantine monk and the primary chronicler of the Bogomil sect.</note> says in Section 50: *“They call their own teaching ‘new wine,’ but they call those who reject it ‘old wineskins,’ and those who receive and preserve it within themselves ‘new wineskins.’”* <note>original: "Οἶνον μὲν... καλῶσι νέον τὴν διδασκαλίαν ἑαυτῶν..."</note> In this way, the Bogomils took great care not to be found lacking in **heretical haughtiness and intolerable pride**, a trait which **Augustine** <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430), the influential Western theologian.</note> detested in the Manichaeans <note>Followers of Manichaeism, an ancient dualist religion that the author links to the Bogomils.</note> and other men of that same ilk in Book 5, Chapter 7 of his work *Against Faustus*. Indeed, **Tertullian** <note>An early Christian author (c. 155–220) from Carthage.</note> spoke truly in his *Prescription Against Heretics* when he said: **“all heretics swell with pride.”** This is supported by **Nicetas Choniates** <note>A Byzantine historian and theologian (c. 1155–1217).</note> in his *Treasury of the Orthodox Faith*, Book 4, Heresy 27, where he remarks: **“ambition was common to almost all heretics.”** I will pass over the examples noted by the ancient Church regarding **Novatus**, to whom **Cyprian** <note>Bishop of Carthage (d. 258).</note> in Epistle 49 attributed **“the arrogance and stupor of a proud swelling,”** as well as **Tatian**, **Valentinus**, and **Donatus**. More recent examples include **Kuhlmann**, **Bourignon** <note>Quirinus Kuhlmann and Antoinette Bourignon, 17th-century mystics and controversial religious figures.</note>, and others. See, if you please, the dissertation by the Blessed **Bayer** on ambition as the cause of heresy, and the Illustrious **Jaeger** in Volume 1 of his *History of the 17th Century*, page 200, for the year 1612.

XIII. Yet, lest they appear to be proud without reason, they affected a singular **APPEARANCE OF PIETY**. That this was their strategy and primary method can be known from what we have already mentioned above. However, a greater light, if I am not mistaken, will be shed by the eloquent assertion of the Patriarch **Germanus** <note>Germanus I, Patriarch of Constantinople (c. 634–733).</note>, which is found in his *Oration on Images*, page 553, in Volume 2 of Gretser’s work *On the Cross*: *“For many,”* he says, speaking of the Bogomils, *“walk about miserably, and creep into houses, traveling in darkness, and they snatch many away with a show of piety. For being dark-shaped devils, they transform themselves into angels of light.”* <note>original: "Πολλοὶ γὰρ... ἀθλίως περιπατοῦσι..."</note> That is to say: **“For many now go about, and insinuate themselves into homes, walking in darkness, and they carry many away at once with a show of piety. For since they are dark devils, they transfigure themselves into Angels of light.”** This, of course, is that mask...

...by which this kind of people has always set out to deceive others; those who, to use the phrasing of Paul in 2 Timothy 3:5, **"possess a form of godliness but deny its power,"** <note>original Greek: μόρφωσιν ἔχοντες εὐσεβείας τὴν δύναμιν αὐτῆς ἠρνημένοι</note> or, as **Gregory of Nazianzus** <note>A 4th-century Archbishop of Constantinople and influential theologian.</note> once said of the Pharisees, **"they have painted over themselves with a certain fiction and appearance of piety."** <note>original Greek: εὐσεβείας τι πλάσμα καὶ ὄψιν περικέχρωσαν</note> Most of them were so clever that, following the example of the *Samaritans*—who denied the resurrection of the body under the pretext of piety—they themselves dared to drink in and offer others the most poisonous doctrines. I wisely restrain myself from bringing forward examples from either ancient or more recent memory, since our own age has seen a great number of such things. However, regarding antiquity, the most celebrated **Mayer** <note>Johann Friedrich Mayer (1650–1712), a Lutheran theologian who wrote on the history of "Pietism" and early church sects.</note> has diligently recounted those things which seemed to belong here in any way in his excellent commentary on the *Pietists of the Ancient Church*.

XIV. With these sought-after colors of false piety, they covered and painted their supercilious contempt for **LEARNING**, and they decreed that those who cultivated their minds with the arts and liberal teachings were farthest from the path of truth. They claimed these were the "scribes" whom the Savior did not deem worthy of his teaching. Following his example, they themselves were carefully on guard not to initiate such men into their sacred rites, viewing them as unsuitable candidates whose minds, being full of human erudition, could not be instilled with the principles of a more divine doctrine. Toward this opinion they twisted the words of Christ with which he turned away the Scribe in *Matthew 8:19–20*. **“By 'Scribe',”** says **Euthymius** <note>Euthymius Zigabenus, the primary 12th-century source on Bogomilism.</note> in Section 48, **“they mean every man of letters, and they command one another not to accept a scholar among the disciples, in imitation, as has been said, of Christ, who did not accept the scribe.”** <note>original Greek: Γραμματέα μὲν... λέγουσι πάντα γραμματικὸν...</note> They interpret the rest of his words with a no less accurate fiction. **“But the foxes,”** Euthymius continues, **“they understand to be the ascetics shut up in narrow little dwellings, as if in certain dens; and the 'birds of the heaven' they understand to be the pillar-dwellers, as being unworthy of his visitation.”** <note>original Greek: ἀλώπεκας δὲ... νοοῦσι τοὺς ἐν στενοχώροις οἰκίσκοις... "Pillar-dwellers" or stylites were ascetics who lived on top of pillars to separate themselves from the world.</note> That is, they interpreted the "foxes" to be *Monks* enclosed in narrow cells, but the "birds" to be those who...

<header>### ->ON THE CUSTOMS OF THE BOGOMILS.<-</header>

...who, from among those same Monks, lived on columns and were usually called by the name of *Kionitai* or *Stylites* <note>From the Greek *stylos* (column); these were Christian ascetics who lived on top of high pillars to separate themselves from the world.</note>, whose origin **Theodorus Lector** <note>A 6th-century church historian from Constantinople.</note> attributes to Simeon: *Simeon was from the village of Sisan, and he was the first to make a practice of the columns.* <note>original Greek: Συμεὼν ἀπὸ κώμης ἦν Σισὰν, καὶ πρῶτος τῶν κιόνων ἐπιτήδευμα ἐποίησε.</note> Add to this the *Glossary of Medieval Greek* by **Du Cange** <note>Charles du Fresne, sieur du Cange (1610–1688), a renowned philologist.</note> under the word *kionitai*, and the *Glossary of Medieval Latin* under the same word. **Euthymius** <note>Euthymius Zygabenus, a 12th-century Byzantine monk and the primary source for Bogomil beliefs.</note> corrects these distortions of Scripture in that same place with these words: *Truly an open grave is the throat of the cursed ones, sending forth a great stench, and pouring out destructive venom through a mouth that fights against God.* <note>original Greek: Τάφος ὄντως ἀνεωγμένος ὁ λάρυγξ τῶν ἀλαστόρων, πολλὴν ἀναπέμπων δυσωδίαν, καὶ Φθοροποιὸν ἰὸν προχέων διὰ τοῦ θεομάχου στόματος.</note> Thus the Bogomils, following the example of other "shufflers in the dark" <note>Latin: *tenebrionum*, referring to those who avoid the light of scrutiny or reason.</note>, fled the light of learning, which both in ancient times and in our own memory some have been unable to endure—fearing, no doubt, that doctrines conceived in darkness would not be approved by judges who are observant and fortified by the brilliance of letters. Such were the **Gnosimachi** <note>A sect whose name literally means "fighters against knowledge."</note> in former times, of whom **John of Damascus** <note>An 8th-century monk and theologian.</note> records in Heresy 88: *they oppose all knowledge of Christianity. For God seeks nothing else from a Christian except good deeds.* <note>original Greek: πάσῃ γνῶσει τοῦ χριστιανισμοῦ ἀντιπίπτειν. οὐδὲν γὰρ ἄλλο ζητεῖν τὸν Θεὸν παρὰ χριστιανοῦ εἰ μὴ πράξεις καλάς.</note> These people certainly considered valid that opinion which **Colus** once—according to the testimony of **Rivet** <note>André Rivet (1572–1651), a French Protestant theologian.</note> in Volume 3 of his *Works*, page 13—boldly pronounced in a public assembly: ***I declare to you, ignorance is the mother of piety.*** How close those people are to this view, who in recent years followed **Weigel**, <note>Valentin Weigel (1533–1588), a German mystic who influenced later Pietism.</note> and in our own time followed **Rosenbach**, <note>Johann Georg Rosenbach (1678–1747), a radical Pietist who criticized academic theology.</note> and disparaged either all studies in general, or at least Academic and Scholastic studies, as if they were obstacles to true piety; let the judgment be theirs who have long since complained about such voices.

XV. Furthermore, our Bogomils gave more than one example of **CUNNING** and craftiness designed to ensnare others. I mention here, not without purpose, that they placed those who listened to them onto the peak of impiety not all at once, but gradually and by degrees. For they feared that if their minds were unprepared when they approached these things, they would be seized by horror; thus, at the start of their instruction

<header>### ->ON THE CUSTOMS OF THE BOGOMILS.<-</header>

...they concealed some things, which they later brought forward along with the rest. This appeared from the passage of **Euthymius** <note>Euthymius Zygabenus, a 12th-century monk and the main source for Bogomil history.</note> cited above, in which the more secret and hidden points of their doctrine were said to be held in the place of "pearls, not to be cast before swine." <note>A reference to the biblical warning in Matthew 7:6 against sharing sacred things with those who do not value them.</note> The same Euthymius, in Section 26, explains this **Discipline of the Secret** <term>Arcani disciplina: A practice in the early Church, and here attributed to heretics, of hiding certain higher dogmas from the uninitiated to prevent misunderstanding or mockery.</term> more fully in these words: *“In the beginning they teach the newcomers simply, instructing them to believe in the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit, and to know that Christ was made flesh, and gave the holy Gospel to the holy Apostles. They suggest they keep the Gospel commands: to pray, to fast, to keep themselves pure from all uncleanness, to possess nothing, to endure injuries, to be humble of mind, and to love one another. In short, they teach all things that are good, charming them with useful instruction, but hunting them little by little, and secretly leading them to destruction. For as time goes on, they sow weeds among the wheat, and when they have made the wretched ones tame and obedient and have caught them within their nets, then indeed—then they give them the poisonous mixture to drink, openly blaspheming and initiating them into the doctrines of the devil.”* <note>original Greek: ἐν ἀρχῇ μὲν τοὺς εἰσαγωγικοὺς ἁπλῶς διδάσκουσι... καὶ τὰ δόγματα τοῦ διαβόλου μυσαγωγῶντες αὐτούς.</note>

To be sure, this custom was very familiar to the Pagans, who counted among their "unspeakable things" <note>Greek: *aporrheton*; things forbidden to be spoken aloud.</note> those mysteries of which they were ashamed, and which they were not accustomed to share rashly, except with those proven by long experience. There is a famous passage found in the **Philosophumena** <note>Also known as the *Refutation of All Heresies*, a 3rd-century work long attributed to Origen but now believed to be by Hippolytus of Rome.</note>, which circulates under the name of **Origen**, on page 6, where heretics are said *“through silence to hide their own unspeakable mysteries, so as to be thought by many to worship God.”* <note>original Greek: διὰ τοῦ σιωπᾷν ἀποκρύπτειν τε τὰ ἄῤῥητα ἑαυτῶν μυστήρια νομισθῆναι πολλοῖς Θεὸν σέβειν.</note> The aforementioned writer pursues the same point further on page 8: *“I shall proceed,”* he says, *“to show their unspeakable mysteries, which they hand down to those being initiated with great reliability, not having first confessed them, unless they have deceived the person by keeping him in suspense for a length of time, and prepared him to be blasphemous toward the truly existing God, and [appealing to those] longing for curiosity...”* <note>original Greek: πρόειμι... δείξων αὐτῶν τὰ ἀπόῤῥητα μυστήρια... καὶ περιεργίαν γλιχομένους. The note indicates the last word should be read from the manuscript as "longing for."</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Bogomils’ historical precedents and their rejection of intellectualism, specifically comparing them to the "Stylites" (pillar-dwelling monks) and the anti-knowledge "Gnosimachi" heresy. It transitions into a new chapter regarding their deceptive methods of conversion.</meta>

<page-num>130</page-num>
<header>### ->ON THE CUSTOMS OF THE BOGOMILS.<-</header>

...who among those same monks lived upon columns and were usually called by the name of *Kionites* or *Stylites* <note>original Greek: κιονιτῶν vel συλιτῶν. Both terms refer to "pillar-dwellers," Christian ascetics who lived on top of high pillars to separate themselves from the world.</note>, whose origin Theodorus Lector <note>A 6th-century church historian from Constantinople.</note> attributes to Simeon: *Simeon was from the village of Sisan, and was the first to make a practice of the columns.* <note>original Greek: Συμεὼν ἀπὸ κώμης ἦν Σισὰν, καὶ πρῶτος τῶν κιόνων ἐπιτήδευμα ἐποίησε.</note> Add to this the Mediaeval Greek Glossary of Du Cange <note>Charles du Fresne, sieur du Cange, a famous 17th-century philologist and historian.</note> under the word *kionitai* and the Mediaeval Latin under the same word. In the same place, Euthymius <note>Euthymius Zygabenus, our primary source for Bogomil doctrine.</note> punishes these distortions of Scripture with these words: *The throat of these cursed ones is truly an open grave, sending up a great stench and pouring out life-destroying poison through a mouth that fights against God.* <note>original Greek: Τάφος ὄντως ἀνεῳγμένος... θεομάχου στόματος.</note> Thus the Bogomils, following the example of other avoiders of light <term>tenebrionum: literally "creatures of the shadows." A term used to mock those who hide their beliefs or avoid intellectual scrutiny.</term>, fled from the light of letters—a light which some, both in ancient times and in our own memory, have been unable to endure. They feared, naturally, that doctrines conceived in darkness would not be approved by judges who are observant and fortified by the splendor of learning.

Such were the Gnosimachi <term>Gnosimachi: A name meaning "enemies of knowledge." These were a sect that believed the pursuit of Christian knowledge was unnecessary, as God only cares about actions.</term> of old, whom John of Damascus records in Heresy 88 as *opposing every kind of knowledge of Christianity; for they say God seeks nothing else from a Christian except good deeds.* <note>original Greek: πάσῃ γνώσει... πράξεις καλάς.</note> These men certainly held as valid that opinion which Colus once boldly pronounced in a public assembly (as witnessed by Rivet in Volume III of his Works, page 13): ***I declare to you, ignorance is the mother of piety.***

How close to these are those who, in previous years with Weigel <note>Valentin Weigel (1533–1588), a German mystic whose followers were often accused of being "enthusiasts" who rejected formal theology.</note> and in our own time with Rosenbach <note>Johann Georg Rosenbach (1678–1747), a radical pietist who famously attacked the university system and formal religious education.</note>, have attacked <term>fuggillarunt: an archaic term for mocking or lashing out against something.</term> either all studies in general or at least Academic and Scholastic ones, as if they were obstacles to true piety. Let the judgment of those who have long complained about such voices be the final word.

**XV. OF THE CUNNING** and craftiness prepared for ensnaring others, our Bogomils have provided more than one example. I refer here, not without reason, to the fact that they did not place those who listened to them at the peak of impiety all at once, but gradually and by degrees. For they feared lest their minds, if they approached these things without preparation, might be seized with horror; thus, at the start of their instruction

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 130 regarding the deceptive nature of the Bogomils. The previous page ended with the suggestion that they feared the "light of letters" (education) because it would expose their true doctrines. This page explains their "Discipline of the Secret," where they presented a pious front to recruits before revealing their actual beliefs.</meta>

<page-num>131</page-num>
<header>### ->ON THE CUSTOMS OF THE BOGOMILS.<-</header>

They used to conceal several matters, which they would afterwards suggest along with the rest. This appeared from the passage of Euthymius <note>Euthymius Zygabenus, a 12th-century Byzantine monk and the primary source for this text.</note> cited above, in which the more secret and recondite points of their doctrine were said to be held as "pearls not to be cast before swine." The same Euthymius, in Section XXVI, explains this Discipline of the Secret <term>Arcani disciplinam: A practice in the early church and among certain sects where the most sacred or controversial teachings were hidden from outsiders and only revealed to proven initiates.</term> more fully with these words:

*In the beginning, they simply teach the newcomers, instructing them to believe in the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit, and to know that Christ became flesh, and gave the Holy Gospels to the holy apostles; they advise them to keep the Gospel commandments, to pray, to fast, to be pure from all uncleanness, to be without possessions, to be patient and humble-minded, and to love one another. In short, they teach all good things, enticing them with useful teaching, but hunting them little by little, and secretly leading them to destruction. For as time goes on, they sow tares among the wheat; and when they have made the wretched ones manageable and obedient, and have caught them within the nets, then indeed—then!—they give them the mixture of poison to drink, openly blaspheming and initiating them into the dogmas of the devil.* <note>original Greek: Ἐν ἀρχῇ μὲν τοὺς εἰσαγωγικοὺς... τὰ δόγματα τοῦ διαβόλου μυσταγωγοῦντες αὐτούς.</note>

Of course, this custom was very familiar to the Pagans <note>original Latin: Gentilibus</note>, who numbered among their "forbidden secrets" <note>original Greek: ἀπόρρητα (*aporrheta*)</note> those mysteries of which they were ashamed, and which they were not accustomed to share rashly, unless to those proven by long practice. There is an illustrious passage read in the *Philosophumena* <note>A work titled "Refutation of All Heresies," attributed at the time to Origen but now believed to be by Hippolytus of Rome.</note>, which circulates under the name of Origen, on page 6, where heretics are said *through silence to hide their own unutterable mysteries so as to be thought by many to worship God.* <note>original Greek: διὰ τοῦ σιωπᾶν ἀποκρύπτειν τε τὰ ἀρρητὰ ἑαυτῶν μυστήρια νομισθῆναι πολλοῖς Θεὸν σέβειν.</note> The same praised Author follows this further on page 8: *We declare,* he says, *that we will show their forbidden mysteries, which they hand down to the initiates under great trust, not having admitted them before unless they have kept the person in suspense for a long time, and have prepared them to be blasphemous toward the true God, and those who are greedy for curiosity...* <note>original Greek: πρόφαμεν... δείξων αὐτῶν τὰ ἀπόρρητα μυστήρια... καὶ περιεργίαν γλιχομένους</note> (thus it must be read from the manuscript).

...(of which I shall speak elsewhere) they see the [light] of the promise. <note>original Greek: τῆς ἐπαγγελίας ἰδῶσι. This continues the quote from Euthymius Zygabenus describing the final stage of initiation.</note> And then, having judged the person to be a slave to sin, they initiate them, <gloss>handing down</gloss> the completion of evils, <note>original Greek: παραδιδόντες (handing down/delivering)</note> binding them with oaths neither to speak out, nor <gloss>to hand it over</gloss> <note>original Greek: παραδιδόναι</note> to any chance person (thus I read again from the manuscript). Irenaeus, in Book I, Chapter 23, described the customs of the **Basilidians** <term>Basilidians: A 2nd-century Gnostic sect in Alexandria known for their complex cosmology and secretive practices.</term> with these same colors. Add to this Eusebius, *Church History*, Book IV, Chapter 7. Compare also the excellent commentary of the celebrated Mayer regarding the **Pietists** <term>Pietists: A movement within Lutheranism that began in the 17th century, emphasizing individual piety and living a rigorous Christian life. The author here compares them to ancient secretive sects.</term> of the Ancient Church, page 39; Christopher Sandius in *The Core of Church History*, page 62; and specifically Dodwell’s fourth dissertation on Irenaeus regarding the **Valentinians** <term>Valentinians: One of the major Gnostic Christian movements, founded by Valentinus in the 2nd century.</term>.

XVI. They promoted the expansion of their doctrine no less cunningly by taking care to insinuate themselves into the friendship of those who were powerful in authority and wealth. They were surely persuaded that they would accomplish through the examples of illustrious figures what might be denied to them through either logical arguments or their other deceits. **Anna Comnena** <note>A Byzantine princess and historian (1083–1153) who wrote the *Alexiad*, a vital source for the history of the Bogomils.</note> observed this in the passage where she complains of this most pestilential doctrine spreading far and wide. "For indeed," she says on page 490, "the evil had sunk even into the greatest houses, and the terror touched a great multitude." <note>original Greek: καὶ γὰρ ἐνεβύθυνε τὸ κακὸν καὶ εἰς οἰκίας μεγίσας, καὶ πολλῷ πλήθει ἥψατο τὸ δεινόν.</note> That is: *This evil had crept even into the most distinguished families, and this plague had seized a great number of people.* Very closely related to this is the fact that the leader of the Bogomils drew women especially into his nets, following the custom of those who, from the earliest age of the Church, wanted women to be the primary instruments of their wickedness. Thus, long ago, **Nicholas of Antioch**, the *inventor of all impurities*, led female choirs, according to **Jerome** in his work *Against Pelagius*. An abundance of further examples is provided by Gisbertus Voëtius in *Ecclesiastical Politics*, Part II, pages 208 and 1012; Gebhard Theodor Meyer in his writing against Petersen, page 201; and Johann Andreas Schmid, a man most skilled in ecclesiastical antiquity, in a unique dissertation which he titled *The Woman, the Leader of the Deed* <note>original Latin title: *Feminam facti ducem*, a reference to Virgil's *Aeneid*.</note> or *The Heterodox Woman*. Join with these the notes of the famous Johannes Ernst Grabe...

<header>### ->ON THE CUSTOMS OF THE BOGOMILS.<-</header>

...[commentary] on Irenaeus, page 101, and the work that surpasses the diligence of all others in this matter, the celebrated Feustking’s *Gynaeceum Fanaticum* <term>Gynaeceum Fanaticum: A 1704 work by Johann Heinrich Feustking discussing women involved in "fanatical" or heretical movements throughout history.</term>.

XVII. It remains for us to look briefly at the customs that relate more specifically to the Bogomils themselves. Their external appearance was full of deceit and corresponded perfectly to the hidden designs of their minds. For although they greatly detested the regulations of Monks—as has been shown by more than one example above—they nevertheless did not consider it a religious scruple to use clothing suitable for Monks. This was done with no other purpose than to more successfully gain the familiarity and conversation of others.

*Euthymius* records the matter thus in Section XXIV: <note>original Greek: στολίζονται κτὰ Μοναχοὺς, καὶ τὸ σχῆμα τούτων ὡς δέλεαρ... περιβάλλονται... κ.τ.λ.</note> "They dress like Monks and put on their outward appearance as a lure <gloss>to entice others, of course</gloss>, hiding the wolf under the sheepskin. Thus, being accepted because of their pious appearance, they gain the opportunity for conversation without suspicion, and through 'Christ-talk' <note>original Greek: χριστολογίας (christologia), referring here to using pious Christian language as a facade.</note> they spit their poison into the ears of their listeners." *Anna Comnena* <term>Anna Comnena: A Byzantine princess and historian (1083–1153) who wrote the *Alexiad*, a primary source for the history of the Bogomils.</term> confirms this on page 486. She says: <note>original Greek: Καὶ τρίχα μὲν... κοσμικὴν οὐκ ἂν ἴδοις Βογομιλίζουσαν...</note> "And you would not see worldly hair on a Bogomil; the evil is hidden by a veil." <note>original Greek: κεκρύφαλον (kekryphalon), referring to a hair-net or head covering.</note> That is, according to the translator *Possinus*: *Thus, you would not easily see anyone from among those long-haired worldlings joining the Bogomil sect; this plague hides itself far more willingly under a cloak and cowl.*

This method of deceiving others seemed useful long ago to those who plotted new schemes. Thus the author of the book *Musar* <term>Musar: Likely referring to a Jewish ethical text; the author is comparing Bogomil tactics to ancient examples of religious hypocrisy.</term> in Chapter XIII records that there was a class of men who wore garments like the garments of the Pharisees, so that others would place greater trust in them. So also we read in *Socrates* (Book VI, 22) <term>Socrates Scholasticus: A 5th-century historian of the Christian Church.</term> that *Sisinnius*, a **Novatian** Bishop <term>Novatians: A rigorist Christian sect that emerged in the 3rd century, known for their strict discipline and refusal to readmit those who had renounced their faith under persecution.</term>, used to walk in public dressed in white robes to secure for himself a reputation for singular holiness. He used to hide his practice behind the advice of Solomon, who commanded that garments should be white <note>Ecclesiastes 9:8.</note>. Anna describes the other external habits of the Bogomils in the same passage I mentioned, in such a way that you understand they composed their faces to a look of austerity. Wrapped in their cloaks up to their noses, with downcast...

...[walking] with a lowered head, and emitting a low murmur from the tongue. <note>original Greek: Καὶ ἐσκυθρωπάκει ὁ βογόμιλος, καὶ μέχρι ῥινὸς σκέπεται καὶ κεκυφὼς βαδίζει καὶ ὑποψιθυρίζει... τὸ στόμα, τἄνδοθεν δὲ λύκος ἐστὶν ἀναδεικνύμενος — "And the Bogomil looked gloomy; he covers himself up to his nose, walks stooped over, and mumbles with his mouth, but inside he shows himself to be a wolf."</note> (Allatius <term>Leone Allacci (1586–1669): A Greek scholar and librarian at the Vatican.</term> reads "whispers under" <note>original: ὑποψιθυρίζει</note> less correctly). This is a true image of the Pharisees, whom the Jewish writings represent in the same way. There were seven types of Pharisees, as appears from the *Sotah* <note>A tractate of the Mishnah and Talmud.</note> III. 1, from which Buxtorf <term>Johannes Buxtorf (1564–1629): A celebrated Hebraist.</term> derived the matter in the *Talmudic Lexicon* under the entry *Parush* <note>Hebrew: פריש (Pharisee)</note>, as did Wagenseil in his notes p. 543, Lightfoot on Matthew III. 7, and Drusius *On Sects* II. 22.

XVIII. The internal character of the Bogomils, insofar as it relates to their will, was utterly corrupt—all the more so because they generally tried to hide their depravity through their external way of life. For as often as the opportunity arose, they took no more care to avoid the filth of a polluted life than those who, having cast aside all pursuit of virtue, are carried away with an excited impulse to commit every kind of crime. For Euthymius <term>Euthymius Zigabenus: A 12th-century Byzantine monk and theologian who wrote against heresies.</term> records in Section XXV that although they prescribed for themselves a law of fasting three days a week, when they were invited to a banquet they ignored it so thoroughly that they provided a supreme example of voracity and gluttony. He then adds: <note>original Greek: ἐκ τούτου δὲ πρόδηλον, ὅτι ἀσελγαίνουσιν, εἰ καὶ πορνείαν καὶ τὴν ἄλλην ἀκαθαρσίαν λόγῳ κολάζουσιν, ὡς ἄσαρκοι καὶ ἀσώματοι.</note> that is: *From which it is clear that they also live licentiously, even if they rebuke fornication and every other lust in words, as if they were devoid of flesh and body.* Nor indeed should this seem foreign to their institutions; for if they ever considered the logic of their System, they must be thought to have recalled it to mind here especially. Specifically, they admitted a double principle in the administration of the universe, God and Satan, and they asserted that both were to be pursued and soothed with worship and observance, denying that this could be done unless the body, as if from a 

<meta>catchword: evil [malo]</meta>

<page-num>135</page-num>
