...[proceeded] from the evil Demon, as if it were corrupted and weighed down by every kind of disgrace, while the spirit, in turn, is brought back to God, from whom it flowed forth. This is exactly what the Carpocratians <term>A 2nd-century Gnostic sect known for their belief in reaching salvation through experiencing all worldly things.</term> once taught, if we believe **Eusebius** <term>Eusebius of Caesarea (c. 260–339 AD): A historian of the early Christian Church.</term>: "They teach," he says, "that those who are to enter into the perfection of their initiation—or rather, their 'initiation into filth'—must perform all the most shameful acts; for they say they could not otherwise escape the worldly rulers <note>original Greek: τοὺς κοσμικοὺς... ἄρχοντας — referring to the archons or demonic entities believed to govern the material world.</note> unless they had paid their debts to all of them through these unmentionable deeds." <note>original Greek: πάντα δρᾷν χρῆναι... διδάσκουσι τὰ αἰσχρότατα τοὺς μέλλοντας εἰς τὸ τέλειον τῆς κατ’ αὐτοὺς μυσαγωγίας, ἢ καὶ μᾶλλον μυσαροποιΐας, ἐλεύσεσθαι, ὡς μὴ ἂν ἄλλως ἐκφευξομένους τοὺς κοσμικοὺς... ἄρχοντας, μὴ οὐχὶ πᾶσι τὰ δι’ ἀῤῥητοποιΐας ἀπονείμαντας χρέα.</note> That is to say, they claimed that those seeking perfection in their assembly must perpetrate every kind of baseness, because otherwise they would not be immune from the vengeance of the Princes of this world <note>A common Gnostic and Bogomil term for the demonic creators of the physical realm.</note> unless they had paid them their due through an impious life. **Augustine** testifies to the same regarding the Manichaeans <term>Followers of Mani (3rd century AD) who preached a dualistic struggle between light/spirit and darkness/matter.</term>, as does the *Formula for the Reception of Manichaeans* on pages 141 and 148 in the works of **Tollius**. I deliberately omit other similar examples that accurately reflect the customs of the Bogomils, which have been diligently collected into a summary by the most celebrated **Mayer** in his commentary *On the Pietists of the Ancient Church*, by **B. Neumann** in his dissertation *On Good Intention* (§ XXX), by **Friedrich Ulrich Calixtus** in his dissertation *On the Various States of Man* (§ XIV), and by **John Forbes** in his *Theological Historical Instructions* (Book XIV, chapter II, and Book XII, chapter IX). Anyone who remembers the distinction between the "external" and "internal" man—which was used by **Valentinus Weigelius** <term>Valentin Weigel (1533–1588): A German theologian and mystic whose followers were called Weigelians.</term> (Part I of his *Postils*, page 108, and elsewhere) following in the footsteps of **Mani** and others—will not doubt that this opinion was passed down to the fanatics of a more recent age as if by a right of inheritance. Compare **Böhme** <term>Jakob Böhme (1575–1624): A famous German Christian mystic and theologian.</term> in Book I *On the Testaments of Christ*, page 48, and the points I raised in *Manichaeism before the Manichaeans*, page 293 and following.

XIX. These are the facts we have been able to bring forward regarding the customs and the entire heresy of the Bogomils, following the lead of **Euthymius** <term>Euthymius Zigabenus: A 12th-century Byzantine monk who wrote the "Panoplia Dogmatica" against heresies.</term>. However, we do not doubt—as we have already noted above—that other details remain which Euthymius himself judged should be passed over in silence, as will appear from his own words, with which our [account...] <meta>catchword: mentatio- (likely argumentatio, meaning "argument" or "discussion")</meta>

<meta>Continuing from the previous page, the author concludes the discussion on the Bogomil sect’s moral and spiritual errors by citing the final account of their leader’s downfall and execution under the Byzantine Emperor.</meta>

<page-num>136</page-num>
### ON THE CUSTOMS OF THE BOGOMILS.

...we shall put an end to this commentary. Indeed, the author says in the final section:

"But I do not think," he says, "that I myself am raving while imitating the ravings of those who are deranged. <note>original Greek: παραπαίειν (parapaiein) — a play on words meaning to be delirious or to strike a false note.</note> I am ashamed, believe me, and I speak such things against my will. For this reason, I intentionally passed over most of their deeds and wrote down what was said in a scattered manner. Those who read this must judge from these few examples that the remaining parts of this sect are, as the saying goes, 'from the same pottery.' <note>original Greek: τῆς αὐτῆς... κεραμείας — a common idiom meaning things are made of the same material or are of the same poor quality.</note> For we have opened a vast sea of impiety that would require many days to cross, and we have barely pulled away from the shore, yet I already sense that you are becoming seasick.

Indeed, even before you, I have suffered this myself. I think it is better to lower the sails of this discourse, strike the stern, and take refuge in the harbor of silence. This is especially true since that wicked and talkative tongue has fallen silent, and those musical lips—which taught this cursed babbling—have been closed, and the mouth of those speaking injustice has been stopped. <note>This refers to the silence of death following the execution of the Bogomil leader.</note> For our most wise and high-minded Emperor <term>Alexios I Komnenos (r. 1081–1118): The Byzantine ruler who led the suppression of the Bogomils.</term>, having exposed the hypocrisy of the drama and 'tested' this man <note>original Greek: διακωδωνίσας — literally 'ringing a bell' to test the sound of a coin.</note>, found him sounding like base metal and counterfeit from every side. <note>original Greek: ὑπόχαλκον (hypochalkon) — 'copper underneath,' referring to a fake gold coin.</note> Though he promised change at one moment, he later returned like a dog to his own vomit. The Emperor, after putting the matter to trial and receiving the unanimous vote of all officials—both Ecclesiastical and political—condemned him to undergo punishment by fire: he who had sent many souls into the fire of Gehenna <note>Gehenna is a term for Hell or eternal punishment.</note>.

And now the one burned by fire has departed, carrying his 'piety' as a wicked burial shroud. Who that knows how to mourn could worthily lament him? He learned the doctrines of error for fifteen years, then taught them for more than forty; he pushed ten thousand disciples into the pit of destruction, and finally did not return from his errors. He fell from both lives <note>Meaning both his earthly life and the hope of eternal life.</note>, encountering both this fire and the fire to come, departing through the fire that is quenched toward the fire that is never quenched.

Yet we must take heart regarding those who remain. The head of the dragon has already been crushed; the most vital of his parts and limbs have been seized, others are about to be, and more are reported every day. We are nourished by good hopes that what is left will not escape the diligent search of our God-aided Emperor and his eager investigation. He labors with great care in every way to catch them all within his net and to show the fields of piety to be clear of weeds."
